Research complete. Findings below, in the requested plain-text house style. Per your instruction I wrote no files.

CRITICAL AND SENSITIVE PERIODS IN THE NERVOUS SYSTEM

A research briefing emphasising literature from 2015 to 2026, with older landmark papers included where they define the field.


1. CRITICAL PERIOD VERSUS SENSITIVE PERIOD

The textbook distinction is about reversibility.

A critical period (CP) is a sharply bounded window in which a specific experience is required for normal development, and outside which the outcome is largely irreversible.

A sensitive period (SP) is a broader window in which experience has an unusually strong effect, but plasticity never falls to zero, so later recovery or later learning remains partly possible.

Sources for these definitions include the Oxford Research Encyclopedia entry "Critical and Sensitive Periods in Brain Development" (https://oxfordre.com/psychology/display/10.1093/acrefore/9780190236557.001.0001/acrefore-9780190236557-e-700) and Knudsen, E. I., "Sensitive Periods in the Development of the Brain and Behavior", Journal of Cognitive Neuroscience, 2004, 16(8):1412-1425 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15509387/).

Knudsen's framing is the one most working neuroscientists actually use: experience during a sensitive period sets the architecture of a circuit, and later plasticity can only operate inside the constraints that architecture imposed.

HONEST ASSESSMENT: the distinction is NOT sharp, and the field has been steadily abandoning it.

• Almost every "critical" period turns out to be reopenable in rodents by pharmacological or environmental manipulation (section 5), which by the strict definition disqualifies it from being critical at all.

• Windows are not single events. Even within one system, different features close at different times. In vision, stereopsis, acuity, and ocular dominance have distinct and staggered windows.

• The onset and offset are themselves plastic. Dark rearing delays the whole visual window; thyroid hormone shifts the imprinting window; enriched environments shift onset earlier.

• Many authors now use "critical period" loosely as a synonym for "sensitive period with a steep offset". Reviews such as "Postnatal critical-period brain plasticity and neurodevelopmental disorders: revisited circuit mechanisms" (Neuroscience Bulletin / ScienceDirect, 2025, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S1673852725002036) treat the terms as effectively interchangeable.

• CONTESTED: whether any true irreversible critical period exists in mammals at all. The strong reading is that all of them are metaplastic states that can in principle be re-entered. That claim is well supported in rodents and poorly supported in humans.


2. CLASSIC EXAMPLES WITH REAL TIMING

2.1 Monocular deprivation and ocular dominance, the Hubel and Wiesel work

David Hubel and Torsten Wiesel showed in kittens that suturing one eyelid shut during a limited postnatal window causes cortical neurons in primary visual cortex (V1) to lose responsiveness to that eye, permanently.

Key numbers: in cats the window runs roughly from postnatal week 3 to week 12 or 13, with peak susceptibility around weeks 4 to 8, and as little as 6 to 7 days of deprivation at the peak produces a near-total ocular dominance shift.

The same deprivation in an adult cat produces essentially no shift.

They shared the 1981 Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine for this work.

In the mouse, which is the modern workhorse, the ocular dominance critical period runs approximately postnatal day (P) 19 to P32, peaking around P26 to P28 (Gordon, J. A. and Stryker, M. P., Journal of Neuroscience, 1996).

2.2 Human amblyopia, binocular vision, and stereopsis

Amblyopia (reduced vision in one eye from abnormal early visual experience, commonly from strabismus or anisometropia) is the human analogue.

Real timing numbers:

• Susceptibility to disruption of binocular vision begins at about 3.6 to 6 months of age and peaks at about 12 to 20.4 months. See "The Critical Period for Susceptibility of Human Stereopsis", Investigative Ophthalmology and Visual Science (https://iovs.arvojournals.org/article.aspx?articleid=2124049).

• Stereopsis (depth perception from the two eyes) itself emerges abruptly at about 3 to 4 months of age.

• Ocular misalignment at any point in roughly the first 4 years of life prevents normal stereopsis developing.

• The window of high susceptibility is the first few months to about 3 years; useful treatment responsiveness extends to about 7 to 8 years; residual plasticity persists thereafter but is much weaker. See "Critical periods in amblyopia", https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6047524/ and Mitchell, D. E. and Sengpiel, F., "Critical Periods in Vision Revisited", Annual Review of Vision Science (https://www.maurer.ca/dpubs/Mitchell.pdf).

IMPORTANT REVISION SINCE 2015: the old clinical dogma that amblyopia is untreatable after age 7 or 8 is now known to be wrong.

The Pediatric Eye Disease Investigator Group (PEDIG) trials showed real acuity gains in children aged 7 to 12, and smaller but real gains in teenagers 13 to 17, especially those not previously treated.

Dichoptic therapy (presenting different images to each eye) is now an approved treatment. Luminopia received US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) authorisation in 2021 for ages 4 to 7 and this was expanded to ages 8 to 12 in 2025. Real-world 2025 data report a mean improvement of about 1.8 lines of best-corrected visual acuity over an average of 163 hours of use across 11.8 months (https://consultqd.clevelandclinic.org/real-world-evidence-supports-dichoptic-therapy-for-amblyopia).

So in humans the visual window is a sensitive period with a long shallow tail, not a hard critical period.

2.3 Language phonology and perceptual narrowing

Infants begin able to discriminate phonetic contrasts from any language and lose the non-native ones between roughly 6 and 12 months of age, while native-contrast discrimination simultaneously improves.

This is perceptual narrowing, and the leading account is Patricia Kuhl's Native Language Magnet model.

Primary source: Kuhl, P. K. et al., "Infants show a facilitation effect for native language phonetic perception between 6 and 12 months", Developmental Science, 2006, 9(2):F13-F21 (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/j.1467-7687.2006.00468.x).

Neural confirmation in the modern era: Bosseler, A. N. et al., "Oscillatory Dynamics Underlying Perceptual Narrowing of Native Phoneme Mapping from 6 to 12 Months of Age", Journal of Neuroscience, 2016, 36(48):12095-12105 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/36/48/12095).

Roughly the same 6-to-12-month narrowing appears for face perception, musical rhythm, and audiovisual speech matching, which is why it is treated as a general developmental principle rather than a language-specific one.

CONTESTED: whether narrowing is loss of ability or reweighting of attention. Non-native contrasts can be partially recovered in adults with training, which favours reweighting over true loss.

2.4 First-language acquisition and deprivation

The strongest human evidence for a genuine critical period comes not from second languages but from first-language deprivation.

• Deaf children born to hearing parents who first encounter a signed language late never reach native-like grammatical competence, and the deficit scales with age of first exposure. This is the Newport and Mayberry line of work, and it is the most methodologically credible human critical-period evidence available, because the deprivation is of language itself rather than of a second language.

• Cochlear implantation shows a clear age gradient. Children implanted before 12 months outperform those implanted after 12 months; those implanted before 2 years generally reach or approach hearing peers; outcomes degrade markedly after about 3.5 years, which corresponds to the closing of central auditory maturation as indexed by the cortical P1 response latency (Sharma and Dorman work). See the 2023 systematic review, Wu et al., Otolaryngology-Head and Neck Surgery (https://aao-hnsfjournals.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/ohn.284).

• Case studies of extreme deprivation (Genie, 1970) are consistent with a critical period but are scientifically worthless as evidence, being single cases confounded with abuse, malnutrition, and possible prior disability. Treat any argument that leans on Genie with suspicion.

2.5 Second-language accent and syntax, and the Hartshorne, Tenenbaum and Pinker 2018 study

THE STUDY

Hartshorne, J. K., Tenenbaum, J. B. and Pinker, S., "A critical period for second language acquisition: Evidence from 2/3 million English speakers", Cognition, 2018, 177:263-277 (https://dspace.mit.edu/entities/publication/a52f82e4-59da-416c-96e8-28257c058ed9).

Method: a viral online grammar quiz taken by 669,498 native and non-native English speakers, combined with a computational model that separates the rate of learning from the amount of exposure.

Headline finding: the RATE of grammar learning stays high until about 17.4 years of age and then declines. Because reaching native-like grammar takes roughly 30 years of exposure at that rate, and because the rate collapses at 17.4, native-like attainment in practice requires starting immersion by about age 10 to 12.

This was a genuinely new claim, because it pushed the offset far later than the traditional "puberty" story of Lenneberg, while still preserving a discontinuity.

THE CRITICISMS, which are serious

• van der Slik, F., Schepens, J., Bongaerts, T. and van Hout, R., "Critical Period Claim Revisited: Reanalysis of Hartshorne, Tenenbaum, and Pinker (2018) Suggests Steady Decline and Learner-Type Differences", Language Learning, 2022, 72(1):87-112 (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/lang.12470). Reanalysing the same data separately by learner type, they found that for monolinguals, bilinguals, and early-immersion learners a CONTINUOUS model fits better than a discontinuous one, that is, no critical age at all. Only non-immersion and later-immersion learners showed a discontinuity, at 18.6 and 19.0 years respectively. They argue these are schooling and life-circumstance effects (leaving school, entering work, changing social networks), not neural maturation.

• Sampling and self-selection. The data come from a Facebook-shared quiz. Non-native respondents who take an English grammar quiz for fun are not a random sample, and the sample composition varies systematically with age of acquisition.

• Cross-sectional confound. Age of acquisition is confounded with birth cohort, migration circumstances, education, and socioeconomic status.

• Model-dependence. The 17.4-year figure is a parameter of a specific "exponential learning with sigmoidal decay" model, not a directly observed inflection. Different plausible model families move or remove it.

• Construct validity. A written multiple-choice grammar quiz is not the same construct as spoken syntactic competence.

See also Mayberry, R. I. and Kluender, R., "Rethinking the critical period for language", Bilingualism: Language and Cognition, 2018 (https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/bilingualism-language-and-cognition/article/critical-periods-for-language-acquisition-new-insights-with-particular-reference-to-bilingualism-research/D2AA3626DD6AE4D2B512B12A85B6C505), which argues the critical-period evidence is strong for FIRST language and weak for second language.

STATUS: the 17.4 number is widely cited and should be treated as contested, not established. The robust and uncontested part is that later starters do worse on average and that near-native syntax after adolescence is rare.

Accent specifically: phonological attainment declines earlier and more steeply than syntax, with degradation apparent from roughly age 6 onward. But documented exceptions exist (a small percentage of late learners pass as native), which is itself an argument against a hard critical period.

2.6 Absolute pitch

Absolute pitch (the ability to name a pitch with no reference tone) is acquired almost only by people who begin musical training before about age 6 or 7, and prevalence is far higher among speakers of tone languages such as Mandarin and Vietnamese.

The clean interpretation is a critical period; the confound is that early training, tone language, and genetic predisposition are entangled.

2.7 Filial imprinting

Konrad Lorenz's classic case. In the domestic chick the laboratory sensitive period runs from hatching to about day 3.

The modern mechanism is thyroid hormone.

Yamaguchi, S. et al., "Thyroid hormone determines the start of the sensitive period of imprinting and primes later learning", Nature Communications, 2012, 3:1081 (https://www.nature.com/articles/ncomms2088).

Mechanism: imprinting training itself triggers rapid conversion of circulating thyroxine to 3,5,3'-triiodothyronine (T3) by type 2 iodothyronine deiodinase (Dio2) in brain vascular endothelial cells. The T3 surge opens the window. Giving exogenous T3 makes chicks imprintable on day 4 or day 6, that is, AFTER the natural window has closed, and can extend the window beyond a week, via non-genomic mechanisms.

This is one of the cleanest demonstrations anywhere that a "critical" period is chemically gated rather than fixed by a clock.

GABAergic signalling is also implicated in both opening and closing the chick window: Aoki, N. et al., "GABA-A and GABA-B Receptors in Filial Imprinting Linked With Opening and Closing of the Sensitive Period in Domestic Chicks", Frontiers in Physiology, 2018 (https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/physiology/articles/10.3389/fphys.2018.01837/full). Note the convergence with the mammalian visual cortex story in section 3.


3. WHAT OPENS A CRITICAL PERIOD: THE MATURATION OF INHIBITION

This is Takao Hensch's central contribution, and it is among the best-established findings in developmental neuroscience.

THE CORE CLAIM: a critical period does not open when excitatory circuits arrive. It opens when a specific class of inhibitory circuit matures enough to create a functional excitation-inhibition balance.

The evidence chain:

• Mice lacking the 65 kilodalton isoform of glutamic acid decarboxylase (GAD65), the enzyme that makes the inhibitory transmitter gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), never open an ocular dominance critical period at all. Infusing the benzodiazepine diazepam, which enhances GABA-A receptor function, opens it immediately, at whatever age it is given. Hensch, T. K. et al., Science, 1998, 282:1504-1508.

• The critical trigger is a specific receptor subtype at a specific synapse. GABA-A receptors containing the alpha1 subunit, located at somatic (perisomatic) synapses, are necessary and sufficient. Alpha2-containing receptors are not. Fagiolini, M. and Hensch, T. K., Nature, 2000; Fagiolini, M. et al., Science, 2004, 303:1681-1683.

• The cell type that makes those synapses is the parvalbumin-positive (PV) basket interneuron, which wraps the cell bodies of excitatory pyramidal neurons in a "basket" of synapses. PV cells are late-maturing, fast-spiking, and pace gamma-band oscillations. Their maturation is the pacemaker of the critical period.

• Brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) accelerates PV maturation. Overexpressing BDNF causes both an early opening AND an early closing of the visual critical period. Huang, Z. J. et al., Cell, 1999, 98:739-755. Conversely dark rearing, which slows PV maturation, delays the whole window.

• Otx2 is the messenger that drives PV maturation from outside the cortex. Otx2 is a homeoprotein transcription factor that is NOT made by cortical PV cells. It is made elsewhere, notably the choroid plexus and the retina, is secreted, travels, and is captured specifically by PV cells. Sugiyama, S. et al., "Experience-dependent transfer of Otx2 homeoprotein into the visual cortex activates postnatal plasticity", Cell, 2008, 134:508-520. Otx2 transfer is necessary and sufficient to open, and later to close, the visual critical period.

• The capture mechanism is now molecularly resolved. Otx2 contains a 15-amino-acid domain with an arginine-lysine doublet (the "RK peptide") that binds with high affinity to chondroitin sulphate D and E moieties on perineuronal nets around PV cells. Beurdeley, M. et al., "Otx2 Binding to Perineuronal Nets Persistently Regulates Plasticity in the Mature Visual Cortex", Journal of Neuroscience, 2012, 32(27):9429-9437 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/32/27/9429). Review: Bernard, C. and Prochiantz, A., "Otx2-PNN Interaction to Regulate Cortical Plasticity", Neural Plasticity, 2016 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4736602/).

• The same chondroitin sulphate chemistry controls both ends. Miyata, S. et al., "Chondroitin Sulfate Is Required for Onset and Offset of Critical Period Plasticity in Visual Cortex", Scientific Reports, 2017, 7:1645 (https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-017-04007-x). Continuous chondroitin sulphate accumulation together with Otx2 contributes bidirectionally to opening and closing.

Note the elegant and slightly uncomfortable consequence: the SAME structure (the perineuronal net) is required to open the window, by concentrating Otx2, and then to close it, by acting as a brake. It is not simply a brake.

ESTABLISHED: PV and GABA maturation gates critical period onset in rodent visual cortex, and diazepam-induced precocious onset is robustly replicated.

LESS ESTABLISHED: that the identical mechanism gates human language, social, or emotional windows. That is an extrapolation, widely assumed and not directly demonstrated.


4. WHAT CLOSES A CRITICAL PERIOD: THE MOLECULAR BRAKES

The modern framing, largely due to Hensch and Bavelier, is that closure is ACTIVE. The brain does not run out of plasticity; it installs brakes. This matters because brakes can be released.

4.1 Perineuronal nets and chondroitin sulphate proteoglycans

Perineuronal nets (PNNs) are lattice-like extracellular matrix structures, rich in chondroitin sulphate proteoglycans (CSPGs, principally aggrecan and brevican) cross-linked by hyaluronan and link proteins, that condense around PV interneurons at the end of the critical period.

They physically and biochemically stabilise existing synapses and restrict new ones.

The landmark: Pizzorusso, T., Medini, P., Berardi, N., Chierzi, S., Fawcett, J. W. and Maffei, L., "Reactivation of Ocular Dominance Plasticity in the Adult Visual Cortex", Science, 2002, 298:1248-1251 (https://www.science.org/doi/abs/10.1126/science.1072699).

Current review: "Critical Periods and Beyond: Dynamic Functions of Perineuronal Nets in Cognition, Development, and Disease", Journal of Neuroscience, 2025, 45(46):e1367252025 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/45/46/e1367252025). This review explicitly walks back the simple "PNN equals brake" story, arguing PNNs are dynamic across the whole lifespan and also protect against oxidative stress and support memory.

4.2 Myelin-associated inhibitors

Myelination coincides with the end of critical periods, and myelin carries growth-inhibitory ligands.

The ligands are Nogo-A, myelin-associated glycoprotein (MAG), and oligodendrocyte myelin glycoprotein (OMgp). They converge on the Nogo-66 receptor 1 (NgR1) with co-receptors p75 and LINGO-1, signalling through RhoA and ROCK to collapse growth cones.

McGee, A. W., Yang, Y., Fischer, Q. S., Daw, N. W. and Strittmatter, S. M., "Experience-driven plasticity of visual cortex limited by myelin and Nogo receptor", Science, 2005, 309:2222-2226. In NgR1-null mice ocular dominance plasticity, which normally ends around P32, persists into adulthood.

Later work: NgR1 restricts plasticity partly by limiting synaptic delivery of AMPA-type glutamate receptors (Cerebral Cortex, 2016, 26(1):427-440, https://academic.oup.com/cercor/article/26/1/427/2367399).

4.3 PirB

Paired immunoglobulin-like receptor B (PirB) is a major histocompatibility complex class I (MHC-I) receptor, that is, an immune receptor, expressed on neurons. Its human functional analogue is LILRB2.

Syken, J., Grandpre, T., Kanold, P. O. and Shatz, C. J., "PirB restricts ocular-dominance plasticity in visual cortex", Science, 2006, 313:1795-1800. PirB deletion leaves ocular dominance plasticity enhanced well into adulthood.

PirB also cell-autonomously constrains dendritic spine density (eNeuro, 2016, 3(5):ENEURO.0089-16.2016, https://www.eneuro.org/content/3/5/ENEURO.0089-16.2016).

This is the finding that opened the "immune molecules as plasticity brakes" field, which is now large and includes complement C1q and MHC-I in synaptic pruning.

4.4 Lynx1, the cholinergic brake

Lynx1 is an endogenous, membrane-tethered, three-finger-toxin-like protein, structurally related to snake alpha-bungarotoxin, that dampens nicotinic acetylcholine receptors. Its expression rises sharply in adulthood.

Morishita, H., Miwa, J. M., Heintz, N. and Hensch, T. K., "Lynx1, a cholinergic brake, limits plasticity in adult visual cortex", Science, 2010, 330:1238-1240 (https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1195320).

Lynx1-null adult mice recover from long-term monocular deprivation; the effect is mimicked by cholinesterase inhibitors such as physostigmine or donepezil.

Downstream, Lynx1 removal unmasks proteolytic activity, including tissue plasminogen activator (tPA) and matrix metalloproteinase 9 (MMP-9), which degrade the extracellular matrix. Sadahiro, M. et al., Journal of Neuroscience, 2015, 35(37):12693 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/35/37/12693). Lynx1 also limits dendritic spine turnover (Journal of Neuroscience, 2016, 36(36):9472).

Note the convergence: NgR1, PirB, and Lynx1 all appear to funnel into extracellular protease activity and matrix state. The brakes are not independent.

4.5 Epigenetic brakes

Chromatin becomes less permissive with age. Histone deacetylases (HDACs) remove acetyl groups from histones, compacting chromatin and suppressing plasticity-related gene expression.

Putignano, E. et al., "Developmental downregulation of histone posttranslational modifications regulates visual cortical plasticity", Neuron, 2007, 53:747-759. Visual experience drives histone H3 and H4 acetylation and phosphorylation strongly in juveniles and weakly in adults; HDAC inhibition restores the juvenile pattern.

DNA methylation, the methyl-CpG-binding protein MeCP2, and the transcription factor complex containing Otx2 also feed into this. The epigenetic brake is best regarded as the layer that makes the other brakes durable.


5. REOPENING CRITICAL PERIODS

Everything in this section is strong in rodents. Read section 5.9 before drawing any human conclusion.

5.1 Chondroitinase ABC digestion of perineuronal nets

Chondroitinase ABC (ChABC) is a bacterial enzyme that cleaves chondroitin sulphate chains, dissolving PNNs.

Pizzorusso et al., Science, 2002 (above): intracortical ChABC in adult rats reactivates ocular dominance plasticity.

Pizzorusso, T. et al., "Structural and functional recovery from early monocular deprivation in adult rats", PNAS, 2006, 103:8517-8522 (https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.0602657103): ChABC plus reverse lid suture produced COMPLETE recovery of ocular dominance, visual acuity, and dendritic spine density in adult rats.

Extended since to perirhinal recognition memory (Journal of Neuroscience, 2013, 33(16):7057), fear-memory erasure, and spinal cord injury recovery.

HUMAN STATUS: none. ChABC requires direct intraparenchymal delivery, is immunogenic, is thermally unstable, and non-specifically strips matrix. No approved human use for plasticity. Spinal-cord-injury translation attempts remain preclinical or very early.

5.2 Valproate and HDAC inhibitors

Rodent: HDAC inhibition (valproate, trichostatin A, sodium butyrate) restores ocular dominance plasticity and enables recovery from amblyopia in adult rats and mice.

HUMAN, and this is the one genuine human reopening claim: Gervain, J., Vines, B. W., Chen, L. M., Seo, R. J., Hensch, T. K., Werker, J. F. and Young, A. H., "Valproate reopens critical-period learning of absolute pitch", Frontiers in Systems Neuroscience, 2013, 7:102 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3848041/).

Design: healthy adult men, double-blind placebo-controlled crossover, 15 days of valproate, with an online pitch-class training course. The valproate arm performed significantly better on a pitch-class identification test.

FLAG THIS HARD. The sample was about two dozen men. It is a single small study. Absolute pitch here means performance on a laboratory identification task, not the genuine lifelong perceptual trait. I found no successful independent replication in the 13 years since publication, which for a result this widely publicised is itself informative. Valproate is also a teratogen and a broad-spectrum drug with many non-HDAC actions, so mechanism attribution is weak. Treat as suggestive and unreplicated, not established.

5.3 Fluoxetine and other SSRIs

Rodent: Maya Vetencourt, J. F. et al., "The antidepressant fluoxetine restores plasticity in the adult visual cortex", Science, 2008, 320:385-388. Chronic fluoxetine in adult rats reduced intracortical GABAergic inhibition, raised BDNF, and permitted full recovery of visual acuity and ocular dominance in amblyopic animals.

Mechanism refined: Lesnikova, A. et al., "Chondroitinase and Antidepressants Promote Plasticity by Releasing TRKB from Dephosphorylating Control of PTPsigma in Parvalbumin Neurons", Journal of Neuroscience, 2021 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7880295/). This unified the ChABC and antidepressant stories: PNN CSPGs signal through the receptor phosphatase PTPsigma to keep the BDNF receptor TrkB dephosphorylated in PV cells; both ChABC and fluoxetine relieve that suppression. Related work from Eero Castren's group showed fluoxetine binds TrkB directly.

HUMAN, and it failed. Lagas, A. K. et al., "Fluoxetine does not enhance the effect of perceptual learning on visual function in adults with amblyopia", Scientific Reports, 2018, 8:12830 (https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-018-31169-z). Phase 2, randomised, double-blind, 42 adults with moderate-to-severe amblyopia, 20 mg fluoxetine (n=22) versus placebo (n=20), 10 weeks, all with daily perceptual training and patching. Both groups improved (fluoxetine -0.167 logMAR, placebo -0.194 logMAR) but the between-group difference was 0.027 logMAR, 95 percent confidence interval -0.057 to 0.110, p = 0.524. No drug effect.

A second null: a crossover randomised placebo-controlled trial of citalopram plus patching in adults with amblyopia, Neural Plasticity, 2019, 5857243 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6590556/), also failed to show benefit.

A smaller trial in Iran reported positive results for oral fluoxetine in patients aged 10 to 40 (Eye, 2019, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41433-019-0360-z), but it is the outlier against two better-controlled nulls.

CONCLUSION: the SSRI critical-period-reopening effect is robust in rodents and has NOT translated to human amblyopia. This is the single most important reality check in this whole literature, because SSRIs were the most translatable candidate and they are the one that was actually properly tested.

Also worth noting: levodopa, the other pharmacological candidate, was tested by PEDIG (Amblyopia Treatment Study, JAMA Ophthalmology, 2015) as an adjunct to patching in children and produced no clinically meaningful additional benefit.

5.4 Dark exposure

Rodent: complete darkness for about 10 days reactivates plasticity in adult visual cortex, including at thalamocortical synapses, and enables recovery from chronic monocular deprivation. Montey, K. L. and Quinlan, E. M., Nature Communications, 2011, 2:317 (https://www.nature.com/articles/ncomms1312). Also effective in aging mice and after cortical stroke (Neurobiology of Aging, 2014, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0531556514002575).

Mechanism: it is the LIGHT REINTRODUCTION, not the darkness itself, that triggers plasticity, acting via perisynaptic MMP-9 activation which degrades the extracellular matrix. Murase, S. et al., eLife, 2017, 6:e27345 (https://elifesciences.org/articles/27345).

HUMAN STATUS: none for amblyopia recovery. Short-term monocular deprivation in adult humans (a few hours of patching) does produce measurable, transient shifts in ocular dominance in binocular rivalry, which shows that some homeostatic plasticity persists in adult human visual cortex. That is real but it is a hours-scale homeostatic effect, not a reopened critical period.

5.5 Environmental enrichment

Rodent: Sale, A. et al., "Environmental enrichment in adulthood promotes amblyopia recovery through a reduction of intracortical inhibition", Nature Neuroscience, 2007, 10:679-681 (https://www.nature.com/articles/nn1899). Adult amblyopic rats moved to enriched cages recovered normal acuity and ocular dominance, with reduced GABAergic inhibition, increased BDNF, and reduced PNN density. Enrichment also extends ocular dominance plasticity into adulthood and protects plasticity after stroke (PNAS, 2014, https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1313385111).

HUMAN STATUS: no controlled equivalent. Perceptual learning, video-game training, and physical exercise are the loose analogues and produce modest, task-specific gains. Claims that "enrichment reopens critical periods in humans" are extrapolation.

5.6 Transplantation of embryonic inhibitory neurons

This is conceptually the most striking result in the field, because it dissociates the plasticity clock from the host brain's age.

Southwell, D. G., Froemke, R. C., Alvarez-Buylla, A., Stryker, M. P. and Gandhi, S. P., "Cortical plasticity induced by inhibitory neuron transplantation", Science, 2010, 327:1145-1148. Embryonic medial ganglionic eminence (MGE) cells, the birthplace of PV and somatostatin interneurons, transplanted into host visual cortex induce a SECOND ocular dominance critical period.

The key number: the new window opens when the TRANSPLANTED cells reach a cellular age of about 33 to 35 days, regardless of the host's age. The clock travels with the cell.

Follow-ups: Davis, M. F. et al., Nature, 2015, on transplantation creating a new critical period in adult visual cortex; MGE but not caudal ganglionic eminence cells are effective (Brain Structure and Function, 2017, https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00429-016-1232-y); the vesicular GABA transporter is required, proving the effect is GABA-release-dependent (Journal of Neuroscience, 2019, 39(14):2635, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30705101/); and, importantly, the plasticity is mediated by HOST interneurons that the transplant recruits, not by the transplanted cells directly (Nature Communications, 2021, 12:602, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-021-21097-4). Work continued into 2024 (https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.1101/2024.12.27.630358).

HUMAN STATUS: none for plasticity. Interneuron-progenitor transplantation has entered early human trials only for drug-resistant epilepsy (Neurona Therapeutics NRTX-1001, derived from human pluripotent stem cells), not for reopening sensory or cognitive windows.

5.7 Psychedelics as psychoplastogens

THE HEADLINE PAPER: Nardou, R., Sawyer, E., Song, Y. J., Wilkinson, M., Padovan-Hernandez, Y., de Deus, J. L., Wright, N., Lama, C., Faltin, S., Goff, L. A., Stein-O'Brien, G. L. and Dolen, G., "Psychedelics reopen the social reward learning critical period", Nature, 2023, 618:790-798 (https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-023-06204-3, open version https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10284704/).

Background: Dolen's earlier work (Nardou et al., Nature, 2019) had shown that mice have a critical period for social reward learning in the nucleus accumbens, mediated by oxytocin-dependent long-term depression, which closes after adolescence, and that MDMA reopens it.

The 2023 finding: reopening is a SHARED property of psychedelics as a class. Adult mice given lysergic acid diethylamide (LSD), psilocybin, MDMA, ketamine, or ibogaine all showed restored social reward learning; saline and cocaine did not. So it is not simply a dopaminergic or a serotonin-2A-specific effect.

The most-quoted and most-interesting number: the DURATION of the reopened window scales with the duration of the acute subjective effect reported in humans. Approximately: ketamine about 48 hours, psilocybin about 2 weeks, MDMA about 2 weeks, LSD about 3 weeks, ibogaine about 4 weeks. The acute drug effect lasts hours; the open window lasts days to weeks. This dissociates the trip from the therapeutic window, which is why the field cares.

Mechanism: metaplasticity via restored oxytocin sensitivity in nucleus accumbens medium spiny neurons, plus transcriptomic evidence of extracellular matrix reorganisation, tying the psychedelic story back to the PNN story of section 4.

Commentary: "Psychedelics reopening windows of development in mice", Communications Psychology, 2023 (https://www.nature.com/articles/s44271-023-00012-1).

2025-2026 SYNTHESIS: Dolen, G. and Wilkinson, M. L., "The Emerging Neurobiology of Psychedelics: Critical Periods, Metaplasticity, and Extracellular Matrix Remodeling", Annual Review of Neuroscience, 2026, 49:495-515, DOI 10.1146/annurev-neuro-112723-045129 (https://www.annualreviews.org/content/journals/10.1146/annurev-neuro-112723-045129). The argument is that psychedelics should be reconceived not as correcting a chemical imbalance but as opening a learning window, which explains why psychotherapy accompaniment and set-and-setting matter so much clinically.

Related 2026 work: "Psychedelics and the Extracellular Matrix: Rewiring Neuroplasticity and Metaplasticity for Next-Generation Psychiatric Therapies", Biological Psychiatry, 2026 (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0006322326000880), and Caulfield, A., Young, A. H. and Mehta, M., "Learning as the unifying mechanism of psychedelic action", Journal of Psychopharmacology, 2025 (https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/full/10.1177/02698811251405683).

Earlier framing paper: Lepow, L., Morishita, H. and Yehuda, R., "Critical Period Plasticity as a Framework for Psychedelic-Assisted Psychotherapy", Frontiers in Neuroscience, 2021, 15:710004 (https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnins.2021.710004/full).

FLAGS ON THE PSYCHEDELIC CLAIM:

• All of the Nardou 2023 data are mouse data. There is no human demonstration that psychedelics reopen any critical period.

• "Social reward learning critical period" is an operationalised behavioural assay in mice (conditioned place preference for a social context), not a validated model of human social development, autism, or post-traumatic stress disorder.

• The duration-scaling result is a correlation across five drugs between a mouse measure and human self-report from separate literatures. It is elegant but it is n=5 compounds.

• Ketamine is included, and ketamine is not a classical psychedelic. That strengthens the "not receptor-specific" claim but weakens any tidy serotonin-2A mechanism.

• CLINICAL REALITY CHECK: in August 2024 the FDA issued a Complete Response Letter declining to approve Lykos Therapeutics' MDMA-assisted therapy for post-traumatic stress disorder, citing trial design problems including functional unblinding and concerns about therapy conduct. Whatever the neurobiology, the human clinical evidence base is not currently regarded by regulators as sufficient. Anyone citing "psychedelics reopen critical periods" as an established human therapeutic mechanism is overstating the case.

• There is also an unresolved and important question of whether an artificially reopened window is safe. A window that permits therapeutic relearning also permits maladaptive relearning, and the mouse work does not address the risk of a person being highly impressionable for two to four weeks.

5.8 Other approaches worth knowing, 2015 to 2026

• Otx2 blockade. Infusing the RK peptide to compete with Otx2 capture, or conditionally deleting Otx2 in choroid plexus, reactivates adult ocular dominance plasticity (Beurdeley et al., 2012 and successors). Rodent only.

• Non-invasive brain stimulation. Transcranial direct current stimulation and transcranial magnetic stimulation transiently reduce GABA concentration measured by magnetic resonance spectroscopy in adult human cortex, and adult human GABA levels track learning capacity. This is the closest thing to a human demonstration that the inhibition brake is real and modifiable, but the effects are small and short-lived and the replication record for stimulation studies generally is poor.

• Action video-game training in adults with amblyopia produces modest acuity and stereo gains. Established as a real but small effect; not a reopened critical period.

• PNN modulation by PV activity itself (chemogenetic silencing of PV cells thins PNNs) suggests a feedback loop rather than a one-way brake.

5.9 STRICT LEDGER: HUMANS VERSUS RODENTS

Shown in rodents, robustly, with recovery of function:
chondroitinase ABC; HDAC inhibitors; fluoxetine and SSRIs; dark exposure plus light reintroduction; environmental enrichment; MGE interneuron transplantation; Lynx1, NgR1 and PirB deletion; Otx2 blockade; psychedelics for social reward learning; thyroid hormone T3 for imprinting in chicks.

Shown in humans:
• Nothing on the list above has been demonstrated to reopen a critical period in humans with a well-powered, replicated, controlled trial.

• Valproate for absolute pitch (Gervain 2013) is the only positive human reopening result, and it is a single small unreplicated study.

• Fluoxetine and citalopram for adult amblyopia were properly tested and FAILED. Levodopa as a patching adjunct also failed.

• What IS established in humans is weaker and less exotic: adult amblyopia remains partially treatable well past the supposed critical period, with dichoptic therapy now FDA-authorised to age 12; short-term monocular deprivation shifts adult human ocular dominance transiently; adult humans can partially relearn non-native phonetic contrasts with training. All of this argues for a long-tailed sensitive period rather than for a reopened critical period.

The honest summary is that the molecular biology of critical periods is one of the most beautiful and best-worked-out stories in neuroscience, and its translation record in humans so far is close to zero.

NOTES FOR THE CALLER

Three things are worth surfacing to the user explicitly.

First, the single most important and least-publicised fact here is the fluoxetine translation failure. Two controlled human amblyopia trials with SSRIs were null. This is the field's clearest test of whether rodent critical-period reopening transfers to people, and it did not.

Second, the Hartshorne, Tenenbaum and Pinker 17.4-year figure should be presented as contested. The 2022 van der Slik reanalysis found no discontinuity at all for most learner types.

Third, the psychedelic critical-period work is entirely rodent, and the FDA declined to approve MDMA-assisted therapy in August 2024, so the clinical claim is not yet supported.

1. ELEMENTAL COMPOSITION OF THE HUMAN BODY BY MASS

• The claimed set (O 65%, C 18.5%, H 9.5%, N 3.2%, P 1%, S 0.25%) is PARTLY verified. O, C, H are robust across sources. N, P and S vary materially between sources - this must be stated as a range, not a single number.

• Wikipedia "Composition of the human body" (accessed 2026-08-12) gives O 65.0%, C 18.5%, H 9.5%, N 2.6%, P 0.6%, S 0.3%, citing Emsley 2011 "Nature's Building Blocks: An A-Z Guide to the Elements" (Oxford), Chang 2007 "Chemistry" 9th ed., Frausto da Silva and Williams 2001 "The Biological Chemistry of the Elements", Zumdahl 2000, Cohn et al. 1976 (total-body neutron activation), Aloia et al. 1997. URL: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Composition_of_the_human_body

• Science Notes "Elements in the Human Body and What They Do" gives O 65%, C 18%, H 10%, N 3%, P 1%, S 0.25%, citing Banci 2013 "Metallomics and the Cell", Chang 2007, Frausto da Silva and Williams 2001, and Nelson/Cox "Lehninger Principles of Biochemistry" 5th ed. 2008. It does NOT cite Emsley or the CRC Handbook. URL: https://sciencenotes.org/elements-in-the-human-body-and-what-they-do/

• ICRP "Reference Man" / standard man, 70 kg (ICRP Publication 23, 1975; updated as ICRP Publication 89, 2002, "Basic anatomical and physiological data for use in radiological protection: reference values"): nitrogen 1,800 g (2.6%), phosphorus 780 g (1.1%), sulphur 140 g (0.2%). Confirmed via search of the CRC Handbook table "Chemical Composition of the Human Body" hosted by BioNumbers. URLs: https://www.icrp.org/publication.asp?id=icrp+publication+89 and https://bionumbers.hms.harvard.edu/files/CRC%20Handbook-Chemical%20Composition%20of%20the%20Human%20Body.pdf (the PDF itself returned unparseable binary via fetch - the ICRP values above were confirmed only through search snippets, so treat the 780 g / 140 g / 1,800 g figures as second-hand until the PDF is opened locally).

• EXPLICIT DISAGREEMENT: nitrogen is quoted as 2.6% (Wikipedia/ICRP lineage) versus 3.0-3.3% (textbook lineage). Phosphorus is quoted as 0.6% (Wikipedia) versus 1.0-1.1% (ICRP and textbooks). Sulphur 0.2% versus 0.25% versus 0.3%.

• Root cause of the spread: the "textbook" percentages are rounded teaching values with no traceable primary measurement, while ICRP values derive from cadaver analysis and neutron-activation studies. Composition also genuinely varies with fat, muscle and bone fraction (Wikipedia explicitly notes this), and with hydration - a fatter body has more C and H and less O.

• WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: cite ICRP Publication 23/89 reference-man values explicitly and give the textbook values as a rounded alternative, rather than presenting one set as fact. The single most defensible framing for the book is: O about 65%, C about 18%, H about 10%, N 2.6-3.3%, P 0.6-1.1%, S 0.2-0.3%, together about 99% of body mass.


2. CARBON TETRAVALENCE AND BOND ENERGIES

• Chemistry LibreTexts, "Bond Energies", Table 1 "Average Bond Energies (kJ/mol)" (accessed 2026-08-12), URL: https://chem.libretexts.org/Bookshelves/Physical_and_Theoretical_Chemistry_Textbook_Maps/Supplemental_Modules_(Physical_and_Theoretical_Chemistry)/Chemical_Bonding/Fundamentals_of_Chemical_Bonding/Bond_Energies

• Values retrieved (kJ/mol): C-C 347; C-H 413; C-O 358; C-N 305; C=O 745; C=C 614; Si-Si 340 (see caveat below); Si-O 452; S-S 266; O-H 467; N-H 391.

• The claimed C-C about 348 and C-H about 413 are VERIFIED (347 and 413 in this table; the 348 figure is the same value in other tables).

• CAVEAT AND DISAGREEMENT ON C-C: search results also surfaced 336-337 kJ/mol for C-C from other compilations. "Average bond energy" (a mean over many molecules) and "bond dissociation energy" (BDE, for one specific bond in one specific molecule) are different quantities and are routinely conflated. Ethane's C-C BDE is about 377 kJ/mol; the 347-348 figure is the tabulated average. The book should say "average bond enthalpy" and name the table.

• Reference-grade source for BDEs if a stronger citation is needed: Luo, Y.-R., "Comprehensive Handbook of Chemical Bond Energies" / the CRC BDE tables, PDF at http://staff.ustc.edu.cn/~luo971/2010-91-CRC-BDEs-Tables.pdf

• Tetravalence itself: carbon, ground-state configuration 1s2 2s2 2p2, forms four covalent bonds via sp3, sp2 or sp hybrid orbitals. This is textbook and not in dispute; no number needed.

• Ranking that matters for the book: C-H (413) is stronger than C-C (347); C-O (358) is slightly stronger than C-C; C-N (305) is the weakest of the four. So the carbon skeleton is not the most fragile link in a biomolecule - C-N is, which is consistent with peptide-bond and nucleobase chemistry being the site of much biological making and breaking.


3. WHY CARBON CATENATES - THE SILICON COMPARISON

• DISAGREEMENT, and it is a significant one. The LibreTexts average-bond-energy table gives Si-Si as 340 kJ/mol, essentially equal to C-C at 347. Multiple teaching sources instead give Si-Si as 222-226 kJ/mol, roughly two-thirds of C-C. Search returned "C-C 348 versus Si-Si 226 kJ/mol".

• The 340 kJ/mol value appears to be an outlier or an error propagated in that particular LibreTexts table; the widely used chemistry-teaching value for Si-Si is about 222-226 kJ/mol, and elemental silicon's atomisation energy supports the lower figure being closer to a real Si-Si single bond in silanes. DO NOT state a single Si-Si number in the book without checking a primary table (Luo CRC BDEs, or Greenwood and Earnshaw "Chemistry of the Elements"). This is the weakest-supported number in this whole gathering.

• Mechanistic reasons, verified as consensus: bond length C-C about 154 pm versus Si-Si about 222 pm; longer bonds give poorer orbital overlap and weaker bonds because the bonding electrons sit further from both nuclei. Carbon hybridises effectively (sp3/sp2/sp) and forms strong multiple bonds; silicon does not form stable Si=Si double bonds under ordinary conditions.

• The decisive asymmetry for biology is not Si-Si versus C-C but Si-O (452 kJ/mol) versus C-O (358 kJ/mol). Silicon chains are thermodynamically doomed in the presence of oxygen and water because Si-O is so much more stable - silanes hydrolyse and oxidise readily, whereas hydrocarbon chains are kinetically stable in air and water. That is the real reason life is carbon-based, and the Si-O number IS well verified from the same LibreTexts table.

• Sources: https://chem.libretexts.org/.../Bond_Energies ; https://testbook.com/question-answer/carbon-shows-greater-tendency-for-catenation-than--63709c31c5ff1d079432a665 (teaching source, low authority - use only for the qualitative argument, not the number).


4. BRAIN LIPID CONTENT

• The flat claim "the brain is about 60% lipid by dry weight" is NOT directly supported by O'Brien and Sampson 1965 and should be restated.

• O'Brien, J.S. and Sampson, E.L. (1965), "Lipid composition of the normal human brain: gray matter, white matter, and myelin", Journal of Lipid Research 6(4): 537-544. URL: https://www.jlr.org/article/S0022-2275(20)39619-X/pdf

• Measured lipid as percent of DRY weight, frontal lobe, donors aged 10 months, 6 years, 9 years and 55 years: myelin 78-81%; white matter 49-66%; grey matter 36-40%.

• So "about 60%" is a reasonable whole-brain average only because whole brain mixes grey and white matter; it is not a value any single tissue shows, and grey matter is well below it. The honest statement is: lipid makes up roughly 50-60% of human brain dry mass, ranging from about 36-40% in grey matter to about 78-81% in isolated myelin (O'Brien and Sampson 1965).

• Also note the brain is roughly 75-80% water by wet weight, so lipid is only about 10-12% of the WET brain. The "60% fat brain" claim in popular writing routinely omits the dry-weight qualifier - worth flagging in the book.

• Sastry, P.S. (1985), "Lipids of nervous tissue: composition and metabolism", Progress in Lipid Research 24(2): 69-176 - the standard review; not fetched in this session, so cite it as a review rather than for a specific number.

• Cholesterol point worth adding: the brain holds roughly 20-25% of the body's total cholesterol despite being about 2% of body mass. This figure was NOT verified in this session - verify before use.


5. PHOSPHORUS IN ATP AND PHOSPHOLIPIDS

• Standard free energy of hydrolysis of ATP to ADP plus inorganic phosphate: delta-G-naught-prime = -30.5 kJ/mol (about -7.3 kcal/mol) at pH 7, 25 degrees C, excess Mg2+. VERIFIED. BioNumbers BNID 111336, https://bionumbers.hms.harvard.edu/bionumber.aspx?s=n&amp;v=3&amp;id=111336

• In-cell (physiological) free energy: about -50 to -60 kJ/mol, roughly double the standard value, because cellular ATP/ADP/Pi concentrations are far from equilibrium. Specific verified figures: about -57 kJ/mol as a typical cellular value; -52 kJ/mol measured for human erythrocytes at ATP 2.25 mM, ADP 0.25 mM, Pi 1.65 mM. Source: Milo and Phillips, "Cell Biology by the Numbers", chapter "How much energy is released in ATP hydrolysis?", https://book.bionumbers.org/how-much-energy-is-released-in-atp-hydrolysis/

• The claimed "-30.5 standard, -50 to -60 in cell" is therefore VERIFIED, with the caveat that the in-cell value is cell-type and condition dependent and is properly called the phosphorylation potential (delta-G-p), not a constant.

• Chemistry: the energy is stored in phosphoanhydride bonds (the linkages between the alpha-beta and beta-gamma phosphates). The favourable hydrolysis comes from charge repulsion relief among the negatively charged phosphates, resonance stabilisation of the released inorganic phosphate, and increased solvation entropy - not from any "high-energy bond" in the literal sense.

• Phospholipids: the phosphodiester head group is what makes membranes amphipathic; the same phosphodiester linkage forms the DNA and RNA backbone. Phosphorus is the only one of the six that appears in a form (phosphate) that is charged at physiological pH, which is exactly why it is used both to anchor lipids in bilayers and to keep nucleic acids inside membranes. Not a number, but it is the load-bearing conceptual point.


6. PROTEIN KINASES, PHOSPHATASES, AND THE PHOSPHOPROTEOME

• Manning, G., Whyte, D.B., Martinez, R., Hunter, T. and Sudarsanam, S. (2002), "The protein kinase complement of the human genome", Science 298(5600): 1912-1934. VERIFIED: 518 putative protein kinase genes, of which 71 had not previously been reported as kinases; the sequences of 56 more were corrected or extended. URL: https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1075762

• The 518 splits (standard, from the same paper) into about 478 with a eukaryotic protein kinase (ePK) catalytic domain and about 40 atypical protein kinases. This split was not re-verified from full text in this session - check the paper before printing the breakdown.

• Phosphatases: about 200 protein and lipid phosphatases in the human genome, i.e. kinases outnumber phosphatases roughly 2.5 to 1. Of these, 107 genes encode members of the four protein tyrosine phosphatase (PTP) families (Alonso et al. lineage; see "Protein tyrosine phosphatases in the human genome", https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15186772/). Broader survey: Chen, M.J., Dixon, J.E. and Manning, G. (2017), "Genomics and evolution of protein phosphatases", Science Signaling 10(474): eaag1796, https://stke.sciencemag.org/content/10/474/eaag1796 . The "about 200" figure should be given as approximate - counts differ by whether lipid phosphatases and pseudophosphatases are included. This is the main uncertainty here.

• The kinase/phosphatase asymmetry is real but often over-interpreted: many phosphatases have broad specificity and are targeted by regulatory subunits (PP1 and PP2A alone have dozens of holoenzyme forms), so the functional count of dephosphorylating entities is far larger than 200.

• Human phosphosite counts, VERIFIED: Ochoa, D. et al. (2020), "The functional landscape of the human phosphoproteome", Nature Biotechnology 38(3): 365-373 (online 9 December 2019). 119,809 human phosphosites at 1% site-level false discovery rate, from manual curation of 112 phospho-enriched datasets across 104 human cell types or tissues, re-analysing 6,801 proteomics experiments. Sites map across 14,154 proteins, of which 11,982 (85%) carry at least one validated phosphosite. Only 2,638 phosphosites (about 2.2%) were curated by PhosphoSitePlus as known to regulate protein function. URL: https://www.nature.com/articles/s41587-019-0344-3 ; open version https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.1101/541656v1.full

• That "85% of detected proteins carry at least one phosphosite" is the best-supported version of the "most of the proteome is phosphorylated" claim, and it is stronger than the older Sharma et al. (2014, Cell Reports) figure of over 50,000 phosphopeptides implying about 75% of the proteome. Use Ochoa 2020 as the headline.

• PhosphoSitePlus totals: the 2023 release (v6.7) documents over 1.4 million post-translational modification sites across 18 species, including over 547,000 experimentally verified phosphorylation sites - but that is ALL species, not human. Do not quote 547,000 as a human number. Primary database citation: Hornbeck, P.V. et al. (2015), "PhosphoSitePlus, 2014: mutations, PTMs and recalibrations", Nucleic Acids Research 43(D1): D512-D520, https://academic.oup.com/nar/article/43/D1/D512/2439467 . Human-specific current counts should be read straight off https://www.phosphosite.org because they change with each release - this is a value that has no stable citable number, and the book should say so or use Ochoa's 119,809 with its date.

• The headline framing that survives scrutiny: roughly 518 kinases and roughly 200 phosphatases manage on the order of 100,000-120,000 known human phosphosites, of which only a couple of thousand have an established regulatory function. Phosphorus is the cell's switch element, and most of the switches are uncharacterised.


7. DISULPHIDE BONDS

• Chemistry: covalent S-S linkage between the thiol side chains of two cysteine residues, formed by two-electron oxidation. S-S average bond energy 266 kJ/mol (LibreTexts Table 1) - notably weaker than C-C (347) and C-O (358), which is exactly why the bond is reversible under physiological redox conditions and can act as a switch rather than a permanent staple.

• Where they occur: bacterial periplasm, mitochondrial intermembrane space, and the eukaryotic endoplasmic reticulum and secretory pathway - all oxidising compartments. Secreted and cell-surface proteins (antibodies, insulin, extracellular matrix proteins) are disulphide-rich.

• Formation machinery in the ER: Ero1 generates a disulphide de novo, passing electrons ultimately to molecular oxygen, and donates that disulphide to protein disulphide isomerase (PDI), which transfers it to substrate proteins and also shuffles mispaired bonds. Frand and Kaiser / Tu and Weissman lineage; see "Ero1p oxidizes protein disulfide isomerase in a pathway for disulfide bond formation in the endoplasmic reticulum", Molecular Cell, https://www.cell.com/biophysj/fulltext/S1097-2765(00)80198-7 and the review "Forming disulfides in the endoplasmic reticulum", BBA Molecular Cell Research, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0167488913000657

• Why they are rare in the cytosol - VERIFIED with a numerical correction to the claim in the brief. The cytosol is held strongly reducing by the glutathione system (glutathione reductase continuously regenerating GSH from GSSG, plus the thioredoxin system). Measured cytosolic glutathione redox potentials: about -289 mV in one roGFP2 measurement; -280 to -320 mV across cultured human cell growth conditions; "more negative than -310 mV" in other reports. The -230 mV figure in the brief is TOO POSITIVE for cytosol - -230 mV is closer to ER-type values. Correct this in the book. Sources: https://www.nature.com/articles/nchembio.1142 (Nature Chemical Biology, cytosolic redox homeostasis); https://rupress.org/jcb/article/166/3/337/44072/Monitoring-disulfide-bond-formation-in-the (Journal of Cell Biology, monitoring disulphide formation in the eukaryotic cytosol); https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3863748/ (roGFP2 probes, -289 mV).

• Consequence: at those potentials free thiols are overwhelmingly favoured, so structural disulphides essentially cannot persist in cytosolic proteins. Cytosolic cysteines instead serve as redox sensors, metal ligands (zinc fingers, Fe-S clusters) and catalytic nucleophiles.

• WHAT IS NOT WELL ESTABLISHED: a single canonical cytosolic glutathione redox potential. It varies by cell type, compartment, cell-cycle phase and measurement probe across at least -280 to -320 mV. Quote the range, not a point value.


8. IRON-SULPHUR CLUSTERS IN THE RESPIRATORY COMPLEXES

• COMPLEX I (NADH:ubiquinone oxidoreductase). Eight conserved Fe-S clusters in the mammalian enzyme; bacterial enzymes have 8-9. Seven form a wire from the FMN flavin to the quinone site, along the path NADH - FMN - N3 - N1b - N4 - N5 - N6a - N6b - N2 - Q; the eighth, N1a, sits on the opposite side of the flavin and its function remains debated (proposed antioxidant or electron-buffering role). Cluster N7 is present in some bacteria and is NOT found in eukaryotes. Verified from https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respiratory_complex_I and from Biochemical Journal 456(1): 139 "Investigating the function of [2Fe-2S] cluster N1a, the off-pathway cluster in complex I", https://portlandpress.com/biochemj/article/456/1/139/46420/

• TYPES in complex I: N1a and N1b are [2Fe-2S]; the remainder (N2, N3, N4, N5, N6a, N6b) are [4Fe-4S]. That gives two [2Fe-2S] plus six [4Fe-4S] for the mammalian enzyme. PARTIALLY VERIFIED ONLY - the N1a assignment as [2Fe-2S] is directly verified from the Biochemical Journal title above, and the count of eight is verified, but I could not open the full text of Hirst, J. (2013), "Mitochondrial complex I", Annual Review of Biochemistry 82: 551-575 (paywalled; https://www.annualreviews.org/doi/abs/10.1146/annurev-biochem-070511-103700 , PubMed https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23527692/ ) to confirm the full two-plus-six breakdown from a primary source. WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: read Hirst 2013 Table 1, or Sazanov's structures - Baradaran, R., Berrisford, J.M., Minhas, G.S. and Sazanov, L.A. (2013), "Crystal structure of the entire respiratory complex I", Nature 494: 443-448, https://www.nature.com/articles/nature11871 - or the review "The multitude of iron-sulfur clusters in respiratory complex I", BBA Bioenergetics, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0005272816300433 (returned HTTP 403 to automated fetch; accessible through an institutional login).

• Naming convention note: the "N" designations come from EPR spectroscopy of the NADH dehydrogenase and were systematised by Hirst; the BBA review above states its nomenclature is adapted from Hirst's proposals. Worth explaining in the book, since the names look arbitrary otherwise.

• COMPLEX II (succinate dehydrogenase). THREE Fe-S clusters, all in the SDHB subunit, and they are of three different types: [2Fe-2S], [4Fe-4S] and [3Fe-4S], in that order along the electron path from the FAD in SDHA toward the ubiquinone site formed by SDHC and SDHD. VERIFIED. The [2Fe-2S] takes the first electron from FADH2 leaving a flavin radical, then the second. Note the [3Fe-4S] - it is the one place in the respiratory chain where a three-iron cluster appears, and it is a good teaching detail. Sources: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SDHB ; "The assembly of succinate dehydrogenase: a key enzyme in bioenergetics", https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11105593/ ; "The iron-sulfur clusters in Escherichia coli succinate dehydrogenase direct electron flow", Journal of Biological Chemistry, https://www.jbc.org/article/S0021-9258(19)34941-5/fulltext

• Complex II is also the only respiratory complex that is simultaneously a citric-acid-cycle enzyme, and the only one that pumps no protons.

• COMPLEX III (cytochrome bc1, coenzyme Q - cytochrome c reductase). Exactly ONE Fe-S cluster per monomer: a [2Fe-2S] cluster in the Rieske iron-sulphur protein (ISP) subunit. Plus three hemes per monomer - two b-type hemes in cytochrome b (bL, low potential, and bH, high potential) and one c-type heme in cytochrome c1. VERIFIED from https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coenzyme_Q_%E2%80%93_cytochrome_c_reductase . The functional enzyme is a dimer, so a complex III dimer carries two Rieske clusters and six hemes.

• The Rieske cluster is chemically distinctive: unlike ordinary ferredoxin-type clusters where all irons are cysteine-ligated, one iron of the Rieske [2Fe-2S] is ligated by two histidines and the other by two cysteines. This raises its redox potential to about +300 mV (versus strongly negative potentials for typical ferredoxin clusters) and is what lets it accept an electron from ubiquinol. Confirmed qualitatively by https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00775-014-1185-7 ("The iron-sulfur core in Rieske proteins is not symmetric") and the search result noting cysteine-plus-histidine ligation. The +300 mV figure was NOT independently verified in this session - check before printing.

• The Rieske protein's extrinsic domain physically swings between the cytochrome b and cytochrome c1 positions during the Q cycle; its conformational heterogeneity has been mapped by cryo-EM - see https://journals.iucr.org/m/issues/2023/01/00/rq5008/ (IUCrJ 2023, "Analysis of the conformational heterogeneity of the Rieske iron-sulfur protein in complex III2 by cryo-EM").

• COMPLEX IV (cytochrome c oxidase) has NO iron-sulphur clusters - it uses hemes a and a3 plus copper centres CuA and CuB. Worth one sentence in the book because it breaks the pattern and shows sulphur handing off to copper.

• Running total for the book: the mitochondrial respiratory chain carries eight Fe-S clusters in complex I, three in complex II and one in complex III per monomer - twelve per chain-monomer, every one of them a sulphur-iron assembly, which is the cleanest illustration available that sulphur is not a trace decoration but the wiring of aerobic metabolism.


ITEMS FLAGGED AS UNRESOLVED, IN PRIORITY ORDER

• Si-Si bond energy: 340 (LibreTexts) versus 222-226 (teaching sources). Do not print a number until checked against Luo's CRC BDE tables or Greenwood and Earnshaw. Highest risk item in this set.

• Cytosolic glutathione redox potential: the -230 mV in the brief is wrong; use -280 to -320 mV as a range.

• "Brain is 60% lipid": needs the dry-weight qualifier and the grey/white/myelin breakdown, or it is misleading.

• Complex I two [2Fe-2S] plus six [4Fe-4S]: count of eight verified, type breakdown only partly verified. Confirm against Hirst 2013 Table 1 or Baradaran et al. 2013 Nature.

• Human phosphatase count "about 200": approximate and definition-dependent; the only hard number verified is 107 protein tyrosine phosphatase genes.

• Body composition N, P, S: genuine source disagreement, must be presented as a range with the ICRP and textbook lineages named.

• Rieske cluster redox potential about +300 mV: asserted from background, not verified this session.

• Brain holds 20-25% of body cholesterol: asserted from background, not verified this session.
