IODINE - WEB-VERIFIED QUANTITATIVE FINDINGS (access date 2026-08-12)

1. RECOMMENDED INTAKES - WHO/UNICEF/ICCIDD vs US IOM

WHO/UNICEF/ICCIDD (2007, "Assessment of iodine deficiency disorders and monitoring their elimination", 3rd ed.), daily recommended intake:
• 90 micrograms/day - preschool children 0-59 months
• 120 micrograms/day - schoolchildren 6-12 years
• 150 micrograms/day - adolescents (&gt;12 y) and adults
• 250 micrograms/day - pregnant AND lactating women
Source: https://iris.who.int/server/api/core/bitstreams/82b512a6-57b8-45f3-8539-30b2f7f63564/content

US Institute of Medicine (IOM, 2001; now National Academies), Recommended Dietary Allowance (RDA):
• 150 micrograms/day - ages 14+ (male and female)
• 220 micrograms/day - pregnancy
• 290 micrograms/day - lactation
Source: https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iodine-HealthProfessional/ and https://www.nationalacademies.org/read/10026/chapter/10

EXPLICIT DISCREPANCY (verified, not inferred): the two bodies agree at 150 micrograms/day for non-pregnant adults but disagree in pregnancy and lactation.
• Pregnancy: WHO 250 vs IOM 220 micrograms/day (30 microgram gap). NIH ODS states verbatim that WHO and partners "recommend a slightly higher iodine intake for pregnant women of 250 mcg per day" than the IOM's 220.
• Lactation: WHO 250 vs IOM 290 micrograms/day - here the direction REVERSES, IOM is the higher of the two by 40 micrograms. This reversal is frequently misreported as "WHO is always higher"; it is not.
• Children: WHO gives 90 micrograms/day for 0-59 months as a single band; IOM instead uses an Adequate Intake of 110 micrograms/day (0-6 months) and 130 (7-12 months), then RDA 90 micrograms/day for ages 1-8 years. So the "90 micrograms children 0-5" figure is specifically the WHO band, not the IOM structure.
The gap is a difference in derivation, not in evidence: IOM's figure is built up from thyroid iodine balance/turnover studies, WHO's from the amount needed to keep pregnancy median urinary iodine in the 150-249 micrograms/L range. Neither is "wrong"; the book should quote both with attribution.

Upper limits:
• IOM Tolerable Upper Intake Level (UL), adults: 1,100 micrograms/day (1.1 mg/day). Source: NIH ODS, above.
• EFSA (European Food Safety Authority) UL, adults: 600 micrograms/day. Child ULs: 200 (1-3 y), 250 (4-6 y), 300 (7-10 y), 450 (11-14 y), 500 micrograms/day (15-17 y). Source: EFSA Scientific Opinion on Dietary Reference Values for iodine; summarised at https://www.ages.at/en/human/nutrition-food/residues-contaminants-from-a-to-z/iodine
EXPLICIT DISAGREEMENT: EFSA's adult UL is 600 vs IOM's 1,100 micrograms/day - EFSA is roughly 45% lower. The divergence comes from choice of endpoint: IOM anchored on elevated thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) in adults, EFSA on more sensitive subclinical thyroid changes. A person taking a kelp supplement can legally be "safe" under one framework and over the limit under the other. WHO does not publish an equivalent adult UL; it instead flags population median urinary iodine &gt;=300 micrograms/L as excessive.

2. BODY IODINE POOL

• Total body iodine in an iodine-replete healthy adult: 15-20 mg.
• Fraction in the thyroid: 70-80%.
Verbatim from NIH Office of Dietary Supplements: "The iodine-replete healthy adult has about 15-20 mg of iodine, 70%-80% of which is contained in the thyroid."
Source: https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iodine-HealthProfessional/
Both target values in the brief are CONFIRMED as stated. Note this is a small pool - roughly a hundred days of intake - which is why deficiency shows up in months, not years.

3. URINARY IODINE CONCENTRATION (UIC) POPULATION THRESHOLDS - WHO 2007

UIC is a POPULATION indicator (median of a group), not a diagnostic test for an individual; day-to-day individual variation is large. WHO/UNICEF/ICCIDD 2007 epidemiological criteria, median UIC in micrograms/L:

School-age children and non-pregnant adults:
• &lt;20 - severe iodine deficiency
• 20-49 - moderate deficiency
• 50-99 - mild deficiency
• 100-199 - ADEQUATE
• 200-299 - above requirements
• &gt;=300 - excessive

Pregnant women:
• &lt;150 - insufficient
• 150-249 - ADEQUATE
• 250-499 - above requirements
• &gt;=500 - excessive

Lactating women: &gt;=100 micrograms/L considered adequate.
Sources: https://iris.who.int/server/api/core/bitstreams/a3681638-dbee-4470-8d7b-bdfc9a33cccc/content ; https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iodine-HealthProfessional/
The pregnancy and lactation criteria were first published in 2007 - before that, only the child/adult bands existed. A WHO Expert Group is currently reviewing these indicators (https://www.who.int/groups/expert-group-for-updating-guidance-on-indicators-for-the-assessment-of-iodine-status), so the pregnancy cutoff of 150 in particular should be described as under active review, not settled. Note the 150 pregnancy threshold has been challenged empirically - see Zhang et al. on Chinese pregnant women after long-term universal salt iodisation, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6734293/

4. THYROID HORMONES AND THE DEIODINASES

• Thyroxine (T4) contains 4 iodine atoms; triiodothyronine (T3) contains 3. Confirmed: https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iodine-HealthProfessional/
• The three iodothyronine deiodinases - DIO1 (type 1, D1), DIO2 (type 2, D2), DIO3 (type 3, D3) - are ALL selenoproteins containing a selenocysteine residue in the catalytic centre. This is the mechanistic link that makes selenium status a co-determinant of thyroid hormone metabolism: an iodine chapter that omits selenium is incomplete.
• Reactions catalysed:
◦ Outer-ring (5'-) deiodination = ACTIVATING: T4 to T3. Carried out by DIO2 (exclusively) and by DIO1.
◦ Inner-ring (5-) deiodination = INACTIVATING: T4 to reverse T3 (rT3), and T3 to 3,3'-diiodothyronine (T2). Carried out by DIO3 (exclusively) and by DIO1.
◦ DIO1 is the only one capable of BOTH outer- and inner-ring deiodination.
• DIO2 is expressed in brain, pituitary, placenta and brown adipose tissue. In brain specifically it sits in grey-matter ASTROCYTES and in tanycytes lining the third ventricle, where it converts circulating T4 into locally active T3 for delivery to neurons. This is the key point for the book: the brain largely manufactures its own T3 in glia rather than importing T3 from blood.
• DIO3 is expressed in brain (virtually all neurons), skin and placenta, and is the guard against excess - it inactivates T4 to rT3 and T3 to T2, and placental DIO3 shields the fetus from maternal hormone excess.
Sources: https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/biochemistry-genetics-and-molecular-biology/deiodinase ; https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/biochemistry-genetics-and-molecular-biology/dio2 ; crystal structure and peroxiredoxin-like catalytic mechanism: Schweizer et al., PNAS 2014, https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1323873111
Caveat on citation: the brief asks for "Bianco et al. reviews" - Antonio Bianco is indeed the standard authority (Bianco, Salvatore, Gereben, Berry, Larsen, Endocrine Reviews 2002, "Biochemistry, cellular and molecular biology, and physiological roles of the iodothyronine selenodeiodinases"; and Bianco &amp; Kim, J Clin Invest 2006). I verified the CONTENT above against fetched sources but did not fetch the Bianco Endocrine Reviews full text directly - if the book cites Bianco by page number, fetch that paper before going to press.

5. IODINE DEFICIENCY AND COGNITION - AND THE DISAGREEMENT

The consensus framing:
• Iodine deficiency is described by WHO and UNICEF as the leading preventable cause of brain damage / intellectual impairment worldwide.
• UNICEF/GAIN: nearly 19 million newborns globally each year - about 14% of all births - are at risk of permanent but preventable brain damage and reduced cognitive function from insufficient iodine. Source: https://www.unicef.org/press-releases/newborns-brain-damage-iodine-deficiency
• UNICEF's general statement of magnitude: insufficient iodine in pregnancy and infancy reduces a child's IQ by 8 to 10 points.

The large historical meta-analyses (both find large effects):
• Bleichrodt N &amp; Born MP (1994), "Meta-Analysis of Research on Iodine and Its Relationship to Cognitive Development", in Stanbury JB (ed.), The Damaged Brain of Iodine Deficiency, Cognizant Communication Corp., New York, pp. 195-200: children in moderately-to-severely iodine-deficient areas averaged 13.5 IQ points lower. Reference record: https://www.scirp.org/reference/referencespapers?referenceid=1054032
• Qian M, Wang D, Watkins WE, Gebski V, Yan YQ, Li M, Chen ZP (2005), "The effects of iodine on intelligence in children: a meta-analysis of studies conducted in China", Asia Pacific Journal of Clinical Nutrition: effect sizes of 12.45, 12.3 and 4.8 IQ points depending on the comparison (iodine-sufficient community vs deficient; and different supplementation timings). PubMed: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15734706/ ; full text: https://gwern.net/doc/iodine/2005-qian.pdf
IMPORTANT PRECISION POINT: the headline "12.45 points" is only one of three numbers in Qian et al. The 4.8-point figure - the smallest - corresponds to the more conservative supplementation comparison. Quoting only 12.45 misrepresents the paper.

The methodological criticisms (REPORT THESE - the field does not agree):
• Study quality was mixed: much of Bleichrodt &amp; Born's input was OBSERVATIONAL, and only six of the cited papers were published in peer-reviewed journals.
• The two meta-analyses draw mainly on cross-sectional community comparisons, so it is impossible to separate the contribution of iodine status DURING PREGNANCY from status in later childhood.
• Observational community comparisons are confounded by health, socioeconomic status, and access to and quality of schooling - iodine-deficient regions are typically also poor and remote regions. Some or much of a 13.5-point gap could be poverty rather than iodine.
Source for these criticisms: Zimmermann MB (2012), "The Effects of Iodine Deficiency in Pregnancy and Infancy", Paediatric and Perinatal Epidemiology, https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/j.1365-3016.2012.01275.x

The more recent, more conservative evidence:
• Bath SC, Steer CD, Golding J, Emmett P, Rayman MP (2013), Lancet 382:331-337, ALSPAC (Avon Longitudinal Study of Parents and Children). n = 1,040 UK mother-child pairs; first-trimester urine; children assessed at ages 8-9. Cohort median UIC 91.1 micrograms/L (i.e. mild-to-moderate deficiency in the UK, historically assumed replete). Comparing iodine-to-creatinine ratio &lt;150 vs &gt;=150 micrograms/g, odds of being in the lowest quartile:
◦ Verbal IQ - odds ratio 1.58, 95% CI 1.09-2.30, p=0.02
◦ Reading accuracy - odds ratio 1.69, 95% CI 1.15-2.49, p=0.007
◦ Reading comprehension - odds ratio 1.54, 95% CI 1.06-2.23, p=0.02
Abstract: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23706508/ ; https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140-6736(13)60436-5/abstract
Note this is an OBSERVATIONAL association reported as odds ratios, not as IQ points - it is not directly comparable to a "13.5 points" claim, and it too carries residual confounding.
• Levie D et al. (2019), "Association of Maternal Iodine Status With Child IQ: A Meta-Analysis of Individual Participant Data", Journal of Clinical Endocrinology and Metabolism. Pooled individual data from three European cohorts (Generation R, Netherlands; INMA, Spain; ALSPAC, UK), n = 6,180 mother-child pairs. Findings: NO significant association between low iodine-to-creatinine ratio (&lt;150 micrograms/g) and NONVERBAL IQ. A positive curvilinear association with mean VERBAL IQ, present only up to about 14 weeks' gestation and disappearing afterwards. Conclusion: vulnerability is real but narrow - verbal IQ, first trimester only. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6804415/
• Harding KB et al. (2017), Cochrane Database of Systematic Reviews, Issue 3, "Iodine supplementation for women during the preconception, pregnancy and postpartum period", CD011761.pub2. Conclusion: no clear evidence of either benefit or harm of iodine supplementation in pregnancy for maternal and infant thyroid function or child neurodevelopment. Only two randomised controlled trials reported child outcomes; both found no difference in cognitive scores between iodine and control groups, and both were UNDERPOWERED. https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD011761.pub2/full ; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28260263/

HOW TO STATE THE DISAGREEMENT HONESTLY IN THE BOOK:
There is no serious dispute that SEVERE iodine deficiency in pregnancy causes major, permanent brain damage - cretinism is the proof, and no one contests it. The dispute is entirely about MILD-TO-MODERATE deficiency in otherwise well-nourished populations. There, the observational literature (Bath 2013, Levie 2019) shows associations, mostly with verbal/reading measures and mostly confined to early pregnancy, while the randomised-trial evidence (Cochrane 2017) is genuinely equivocal because the trials are too small. The large legacy figures (13.5 and 12.45 IQ points) come from severe-deficiency settings using observational designs with acknowledged confounding, and should NOT be presented as the expected benefit of supplementing a mildly deficient Western pregnancy.
WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: an adequately powered randomised controlled trial of iodine supplementation started PRE-CONCEPTION or in the first trimester, in a mild-to-moderately deficient population, with child cognitive outcomes at school age and separate reporting of verbal and nonverbal domains. No such trial has yet reported. Existing trials began too late in gestation and enrolled too few children - which is precisely why Cochrane cannot rule benefit in or out.

6. CRETINISM

Two classical forms, both from severe iodine deficiency:
• NEUROLOGICAL cretinism: intellectual disability, deaf-mutism, squint (strabismus), spastic diplegia, and disorders of stance and gait. Thyroid function is near-normal in these individuals, with hypothyroidism only transient postnatally, and they lack clinical hypothyroid features. Damage is PRENATAL, transmitted mother to fetus.
• MYXOEDEMATOUS cretinism: permanent, severe POSTNATAL thyroid hormone deficiency. In addition to neurological damage: dwarfism (short stature), sexual immaturity, retarded skeletal maturation, and marked clinical myxoedema. Thyroid is ATROPHIC on clinical exam and ultrasound, rather than goitrous - a useful distinguishing sign versus the neurological form.
• Unifying hypothesis (Boyages &amp; Halpern 1993, "Endemic cretinism: toward a unifying hypothesis", https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8499765/): the two forms are not separate diseases but a continuum determined by the length and severity of POSTNATAL thyroid hormone deficiency. A consistent pattern and intensity of neurological, intellectual and audiometric deficit is common to and equally present in ALL types of endemic cretin - the prenatal insult is shared; the postnatal course is what differs.
• Comparative studies: Chaouki et al. 1988, Clinical Endocrinology, https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/j.1365-2265.1988.tb03671.x ; Boyages et al. 1988, JCEM (western China), https://academic.oup.com/jcem/article-abstract/67/6/1262/2652023 ; Halpern et al. 1991, Brain 114:825, https://academic.oup.com/brain/article-pdf/114/2/825/732887/114-2-825.pdf
• Historical prevalence anchor: in a studied Algerian endemic goitre area, goitre prevalence 51.3% with endemic cretinism 1.1% of the population (Chaouki et al.). Geographic distribution was classically Alpine, Himalayan, Andean and central African highlands - regions where glaciation and heavy rainfall leached iodine from soils.
• Long-term follow-up: 31 surviving endemic neurological cretins in China after 42 years of iodine supplementation - thyroid condition and residual clinical signs documented at https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9309213/ (residual deficits persist; supplementation prevents but does not reverse).
NOT WELL ESTABLISHED: there is no reliable current global count of living cretins. Endemic cretinism is now rare wherever salt iodisation has been sustained, but historical prevalence figures are region-specific surveys (like the Algerian 1.1%) rather than a global estimate. Do not quote a worldwide cretinism number.

7. CURRENT GLOBAL IODINE STATUS

Iodine Global Network (IGN), Global Scorecard of Iodine Nutrition 2025 (published 22 April 2025) - THIS IS THE LATEST AVAILABLE:
• Covers 194 WHO Member States plus Liechtenstein and Palestine.
• Based on national median UIC in SCHOOL-AGE CHILDREN from epidemiological studies conducted 2009-2023.
• Classification used: insufficient = median UIC &lt;100 micrograms/L; adequate = 100-299; excessive = &gt;=300.
• National-data country counts (from the IGN scorecard map legend): 21 insufficient, 91 adequate, 8 excessive - i.e. 120 countries with usable national data, leaving roughly 74 with sub-national or no data.
Sources: https://ign.org/scorecard/ ; PDF https://ign.org/app/uploads/2025/10/Scorecard_2025_FINAL_22-April-2025.pdf ; map/legend https://ign.org/app/uploads/2025/09/IGN-%E2%80%93-Scorecard-map-download.pdf
CAVEAT ON THESE COUNTS: ign.org returns HTTP 403 to automated fetching, so the 21/91/8 figures come from the indexed legend text of the scorecard map PDF rather than from a page I rendered myself. They are internally consistent and match the stated 194-country/2009-2023 framing, but VERIFY THEM BY OPENING THE PDF IN A BROWSER before printing. There is also a conflicting statement in circulation that "the number of countries classified iodine deficient has risen from 19 in 2017 to 23 today" (IGN commentary, October 2025) - 23 versus the map's 21. The discrepancy is most likely a national-data-only count versus a count including sub-national data, but I could not confirm which. Do not print a single number without resolving this.

Historical trajectory for context (Zimmermann &amp; Andersson, European Journal of Endocrinology 2021, "Global perspectives in endocrinology: coverage of iodized salt programs and iodine status in 2020", https://academic.oup.com/ejendo/article/185/1/R13/6654349): countries with adequate iodine intake nearly doubled from 67 in 2003 to 118 in 2020; 21 countries remained deficient and 13 had excessive intakes (from excess groundwater iodine or over-iodised salt).
IMPORTANT NARRATIVE POINT: the trend is no longer monotonic improvement. Deficient-country counts bottomed out around 2017 (19) and have since risen. Excess is also a real category - 8 to 13 countries - so "more iodine is better" is false at the population level.

Salt iodisation coverage:
• 89% of the global population used salt with some iodine in 2020 (UNICEF). Regional highs: East Asia and Pacific 92%, South Asia 90%.
Source: https://data.unicef.org/topic/nutrition/iodine/
Note the wording: "salt with SOME iodine" is a weaker criterion than "adequately iodised salt" (typically &gt;=15 parts per million). Coverage with ADEQUATELY iodised salt is lower and varies sharply by residence type and socioeconomic status within countries - see https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5404210/ (results from 10 national coverage surveys). The ~88-90% figure in the brief is confirmed at 89% but must be labelled "salt containing any iodine", not "adequately iodised".

8. THYROID HORMONE RECEPTORS AND THE MCT8 TRANSPORTER

Receptors:
• Thyroid hormone receptor alpha, gene THRA (TRalpha) and thyroid hormone receptor beta, gene THRB (TRbeta) are nuclear receptors that bind T3 and act as ligand-dependent transcription factors on thyroid hormone response elements. TRalpha1 predominates in the developing brain and heart; TRbeta predominates in liver, cochlea and the pituitary/hypothalamic feedback loop. Unliganded receptor actively REPRESSES target genes, so the absence of T3 is not neutral - it imposes a repressive state, which is why timing of hormone availability matters so much in development.
• Resistance to thyroid hormone beta (THRB mutations) is the classic and more common syndrome; THRA mutations produce a distinct phenotype with growth retardation, skeletal dysplasia and intellectual disability with relatively normal TSH.
NOTE ON VERIFICATION: the receptor-isoform tissue distribution above is standard textbook endocrinology that I did not fetch a dedicated source for in this pass. If the book states isoform-specific claims, cite Bernal J, "Thyroid hormone receptors in brain development and function" (Nature Clinical Practice Endocrinology &amp; Metabolism, 2007) and verify directly.

MCT8 / SLC16A2 and Allan-Herndon-Dudley syndrome (AHDS) - FULLY VERIFIED:
• Monocarboxylate transporter 8 (MCT8) is the protein product of the SLC16A2 gene, a transmembrane transporter specific for thyroid hormones T3 and T4. It is essential for neuronal T3 uptake and, in endothelial cells, for passage of thyroid hormone across the blood-brain barrier.
• AHDS is X-linked, caused by inactivating SLC16A2 mutations. Because transport into brain fails, the brain is severely hypothyroid even though blood hormone levels are not low - a dissociation that is the diagnostic key.
• Characteristic biochemical signature: serum TSH within the normal age-specific range, normal-to-LOW serum T4, and ELEVATED serum T3. Any child with high T3 and low-normal T4 and normal TSH should prompt SLC16A2 testing.
• Clinical features in affected males: infantile hypotonia and feeding difficulties; developmental delay/intellectual disability ranging from mild to profound; later-onset pyramidal signs; extrapyramidal findings (dystonia, choreoathetosis, paroxysmal movement disorder, hypokinesia, masked facies); seizures, often drug-resistant.
• Myelination and central nervous system development peak in the first two years of life and are severely compromised without correct thyroid hormone transport into the CNS - this is the therapeutic window and the reason for interest in newborn screening.
• Heterozygous females are mostly clinically unaffected but may show minor thyroid test abnormalities; however 8 heterozygous female patients have been reported with variable neurocognitive impairment, behavioural problems and thyroid function abnormalities.
• Mutation spectrum: large deletions, frameshift deletions, single amino acid deletions, insertions and substitutions, both inherited and de novo.
Sources: GeneReviews, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK26373/ ; https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7198743/ (MCT8 Deficiency: The Road to Therapies for a Rare Disease) ; https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12452532/ (early diagnosis and screening) ; https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1521690X07000280
CONCEPTUAL LINK FOR THE BOOK: MCT8 deficiency and iodine deficiency converge on the same endpoint - a brain starved of T3 during the myelination window - by opposite routes. Iodine deficiency means there is not enough hormone to make; MCT8 deficiency means the hormone exists but cannot get in. Together they demonstrate that it is intracellular brain T3, not circulating hormone, that development actually depends on. DIO2 in astrocytes is the third leg: local generation. Supply, transport, local activation - break any one and the outcome is similar.

ITEMS NOT SETTLED / FLAGGED FOR PRE-PRESS VERIFICATION
• IGN 2025 country counts 21/91/8 versus the "23 deficient" figure - resolve by opening the scorecard PDF manually (ign.org blocks automated fetch).
• Bianco et al. deiodinase review - content verified via secondary sources, primary paper not fetched.
• TRalpha/TRbeta tissue-distribution specifics - not fetched this pass; cite Bernal 2007 and verify.
• No reliable current global cretinism prevalence figure exists; use region-specific historical surveys only.
• The magnitude of cognitive effect from MILD deficiency remains genuinely unresolved pending an adequately powered pre-conception/first-trimester randomised trial.</result>

<result>CHLORINE / CHLORIDE - WEB-VERIFIED QUANTITATIVE FINDINGS (access date 2026-08-12)

1. PLASMA / SERUM CHLORIDE REFERENCE RANGE

• Verified: 98-107 mEq/L (= 98-107 mmol/L) for adults. MedlinePlus Medical Encyclopedia, "Chloride test - blood", review date 19 May 2025, reviewed by Jacob Berman MD MPH (Univ. of Washington). https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003485.htm

• Lab-to-lab variation, exact wording from that source: "Normal value ranges may vary slightly among different laboratories." So 98-107 is a conventional, not universal, interval; individual labs publish their own method- and population-specific intervals (commonly 96-106, 98-106, or 98-107).

• Mayo Clinic Laboratories pediatric catalog lists 98-107 mmol/L for ages 18 years and older (https://pediatric.testcatalog.org/show/CL). The Mayo PDF test definition (unit_code 8460) returned HTTP 403 and could not be fetched directly - the age-stratified pediatric values were NOT verified.

• Outcome data (not a reference range, but bounds the "physiological optimum"): discharge serum chloride of 103-105 mmol/L was associated with lowest mortality, with 100-108 mmol/L predicting favorable survival, in a hospitalized-patient cohort. PMC7353470, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7353470/

• Note: mEq/L and mmol/L are numerically identical for chloride because Cl- is monovalent.

2. CEREBROSPINAL FLUID (CSF) CHLORIDE

• Deranged Physiology (Chapter 4.3.1, "Properties and contents of cerebrospinal fluid", https://derangedphysiology.com/main/required-reading/neurological-intensive-care/Chapter-431/properties-and-contents-cerebrospinal-fluid) gives CSF chloride approximately 132 mmol/L, i.e. "about 15-20 mmol/L higher than in the plasma". Its stated rationale: CSF lacks albumin, so there is no large impermeant anionic protein charge; chloride is elevated to preserve electroneutrality. That page attributes the underlying data to "early 1960s dog studies" WITHOUT naming authors - so this specific number has a weak citation trail and should be treated as textbook-level, not primary-source-level.

• Clinical laboratory normal CSF chloride commonly quoted as 120-132 mEq/L (Labpedia.net, "Cerebrospinal Fluid Analysis Part 2", https://labpedia.net/cerebrospinal-fluid-analysis-part-2-cerebrospinal-fluid-csf-normal-abnormal-interpretations/). The commonly cited 118-132 mM band is consistent with this. The claimed "115-130 mM" in the brief is slightly LOW at the bottom end relative to what clinical sources publish; 118/120-132 mM is better supported.

• MECHANISM - IMPORTANT DISAGREEMENT: the Gibbs-Donnan explanation and the active-secretion explanation are both in circulation and they are not equivalent.
◦ Donnan account: plasma contains ~40 g/L impermeant polyanionic albumin; CSF protein is ~0.2-0.5% of plasma protein. A Donnan equilibrium across a protein-impermeant barrier therefore predicts higher diffusible anion (Cl-) on the protein-poor side. See Donnan-effect analysis, PMC3118367, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3118367/
◦ Secretion account: CSF is explicitly NOT a plasma ultrafiltrate, and measured concentrations do not match a pure Gibbs-Donnan prediction. A lumen-positive transepithelial potential of approximately +5 mV across choroid plexus epithelium drives Na+, Cl-, HCO3- into CSF, with water following osmotically. StatPearls, "Physiology, Cerebral Spinal Fluid", https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK519007/ ; StatPearls "Neuroanatomy, Cerebrospinal Fluid", https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK470578/
◦ Honest synthesis for the book: Donnan sets the direction and part of the magnitude; active choroid-plexus transport (NKCC1 at the apical membrane, Na+/K+-ATPase, Cl-/HCO3- exchange) accounts for the fact that CSF composition is regulated rather than passively equilibrated. What would settle the residual quantitative share is a modern isotopic/electrophysiological human measurement of the choroid-plexus transepithelial potential plus paired plasma/CSF ion activity (not concentration) measurements - this does not appear to exist in humans.

• Clinical corollary: CSF chloride falls in tuberculous meningitis (values &lt;120 mmol/L used diagnostically; one cohort cutoff &lt;102.15 mmol/L). https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1155/2022/9692804 ; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9825210/ . Mechanistically attributed to rising CSF protein at fixed osmolarity - i.e. the Donnan relationship running in reverse, which is indirect evidence FOR the Donnan contribution.

3. INTRACELLULAR CHLORIDE - MATURE VS IMMATURE NEURONS

• Mature: [Cl-]i approximately 4-8 mM, inferred from EGABA of -65 to -80 mV in adult (P30) hippocampal slices via gramicidin perforated patch. Reported in Hübner &amp; Holthoff (2013) and in the developmental-chloride literature; see Frontiers in Cellular Neuroscience 7:177, "Anion transport and GABA signaling", https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/fncel.2013.00177/full . This brackets the claimed 5-10 mM; 4-10 mM is the honest range.

• Immature: steady-state [Cl-]i approximately 30 mM in immature rat neocortical neurons (Achilles et al., 2007, J Neurosci), reduced by bumetanide (NKCC1 block) or Na+-free solution - confirming NKCC1-dependent accumulation. Cited in Watanabe M &amp; Fukuda A (2015), "Development and regulation of chloride homeostasis in the central nervous system", Front Cell Neurosci 9:371, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4585146/
• In P2-5 CA3 pyramidal cells, EGABA -61.4 +/- 5.4 mV with Nernst-derived [Cl-]i spanning 7-30 mM - note the very wide spread, which is itself the key point about how contested these absolute numbers are.
• Theoretical bounds (Kaila et al. 2014, Box 2): KCCs can in principle drive [Cl-]i down to 4.4 mM (ECl = -90 mV); NKCCs can in principle drive it above 90 mM (ECl = -8 mV). Real neurons sit far from both limits.

• The commonly quoted "20-40 mM" for immature neurons is a range assembled across preparations, not a single measurement; the best-anchored single value is ~30 mM (Achilles 2007). Report it as "roughly 20-40 mM, anchored at ~30 mM in neocortex".

MEASUREMENT-METHOD DEPENDENCE (this is where the numbers actually break down)
• Whole-cell patch DIALYSES the cell and imposes the pipette chloride - it does not measure native [Cl-]i. Khazipov, Valeeva &amp; Khalilov (2014), CNS Neurosci Ther 21(2):83-91, doi:10.1111/cns.12353, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6495283/ - "whole-cell dialysis artificially alters [Cl-]i, producing unreliable measurements of true EGABA".
• Gramicidin perforated patch preserves native anions (gramicidin pores pass monovalent cations, not Cl-) - the current gold standard, but slow, high-access-resistance, and prone to spontaneous rupture.
• Cell-attached single GABA-A channel recording measures the DRIVING FORCE without perturbing Vm or [Cl-]i - but Zilberter notes it "does not provide quantitative measurements" of absolute values.
• Clomeleon (Kuner &amp; Augustine 2000): IC50/EC50 for Cl- above 160 mM, average KD ~91 mM - i.e. far outside the physiological 5-30 mM range, so it is nearly insensitive where it matters. Kaila et al. 2014: Clomeleon "has a Cl- affinity that is far beyond the physiologically relevant concentration range." Ben-Ari et al. 2012 note Clomeleon studies produced "extreme heterogeneity of reported chloride levels (from 1 to 120 mM between P5 and P7)".
• Cl-Sensor (Markova et al. 2008): IC50 ~30 mM - much closer to physiological.
• ClopHensor (Arosio et al. 2010): IC50 ~40 mM; improved variant IC50 ~20 mM (Mukhtarov et al. 2013).
• MQAE and related quinolinium dyes are collisionally quenched by Cl- and are pH- and loading-dependent; they were widely used but I could not verify a specific quantitative bias figure from a fetched source - flag as not verified.
• Spatial caveat (Kaila et al. 2014): somatic recordings "do not provide correct estimates of EGABA and DFGABA in dendrites during network activities". Khazipov 2014: "GABA(A) reversal potential shifts toward more negative values in dendrites proportionally to the distance from the soma." So a single whole-cell number is not a property of the neuron - it is a property of the compartment.

CONCLUSION ON CONTESTEDNESS: the DIRECTION (immature high, mature low) is not seriously disputed. The ABSOLUTE VALUES are. What would settle it: a genetically encoded ratiometric sensor with KD in the 5-20 mM band, pH-corrected, validated in vivo against gramicidin perforated patch in the same cells - the sensors exist (Cl-Sensor, ClopHensor variants) but a definitive cross-validated in-vivo dataset has not been produced.

4. E_Cl, E_GABA, AND THE DEPOLARIZING-GABA CONTROVERSY

• Mature neurons: EGABA -65 to -80 mV, ECl more negative than Vrest (Kaila et al. 2014). Typical Vrest -60 to -70 mV, so GABA-A opening hyperpolarizes or shunts.
• Immature neurons: ECl and EGABA are set "typically by 10-30 mV" MORE POSITIVE than resting Em (Khazipov, Valeeva &amp; Khalilov 2014). Example values in CA3 pyramidal cells: EGABA around -44 mV (whole-cell) early, moving to -65 to -70 mV by the second postnatal week.
• EGABA is always more positive than pure ECl because of the bicarbonate shunt: EHCO3 is approximately -10 to -20 mV (Kaila et al. 2014; Hübner &amp; Holthoff 2013 give "around -10 mV"), and GABA-A channels are appreciably HCO3--permeable, so EGABA is a weighted mixture pulled positive relative to ECl.
• KCC2b knockout: EGABA/glycine is approximately 20 mV MORE POSITIVE than wild-type at the same neonatal stage under identical in-vitro conditions (cited in Ben-Ari et al. 2012). This is a strong causal argument that the shift is transporter-driven, not slice-condition-driven.

THE CONTROVERSY - BOTH SIDES, EXPLICITLY

Challenge side:
• Zilberter and colleagues argued depolarizing GABA in slices is an artefact of energy-substrate deprivation - slices perfused with glucose alone are metabolically compromised; adding ketone bodies (beta-hydroxybutyrate), pyruvate or lactate was reported to abolish depolarizing GABA.
• Dzhala and colleagues argued instead that it is a SURFACE-DAMAGE artefact of slicing (traumatized, axotomized neurons near the cut face accumulate Cl-).
• Bregestovski and colleagues contributed the optical-sensor critique - that indicator KD mismatch invalidates many absolute chloride claims (this is the one point where both camps largely agree).
• Zilberter Y (2015), Commentary in Front Pharmacol 6:294, doi:10.3389/fphar.2015.00294, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4674808/ - argues that calling a shift of only a few mV "depolarizing GABA" is misleading framing when the functional effect is inhibitory; also that cell-attached patch is not quantitative; questions Kirmse's calcium-transient developmental claim (11/117 cells at P1 vs 6/204 at P3-4).

In-vivo evidence:
• Kirmse K et al. (2015), "GABA depolarizes immature neurons and inhibits network activity in the neonatal neocortex in vivo", Nat Commun 6:7750, doi:10.1038/ncomms8750, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26177896/ . Anaesthetized mice, P3-4 occipital cortex, patch-clamp plus calcium imaging. Findings: GABA depolarized the MAJORITY of immature neurons, BUT the depolarization was very small, did not efficiently activate voltage-gated Ca2+ channels, and did not evoke action potential firing. Blocking GABA-A increased spontaneous network activity; potentiating GABA-A suppressed it. Conclusion: GABA is a depolarizing but NET-INHIBITORY transmitter in vivo at P3-4.
• Note carefully: Kirmse et al. did NOT refute depolarization. They refuted EXCITATION. This distinction is frequently mis-stated in secondary sources.

Rebuttal side (Ben-Ari / Kaila camp):
• Ben-Ari Y et al. (2012), "Refuting the challenges of the developmental shift of polarity of GABA actions: GABA more exciting than ever!", Front Cell Neurosci 6:35, https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/cellular-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fncel.2012.00035/full . Marshals seven independent studies against the energy-substrate hypothesis:
◦ Tyzio et al. 2011 - beta-hydroxybutyrate 4 mM does not alter depolarizing/excitatory GABA in immature slices (dual patch-clamp, two-photon imaging).
◦ Kirmse et al. 2010 - no BHB effect in cortical plate neurons at identical perfusion rates to Zilberter's.
◦ Bos &amp; Vinay 2012 - physiological energy substrates do not change GABA/glycine reversal potentials in neonatal spinal cord.
◦ Waddell et al. 2011 - BHB no effect in cultured neurons.
◦ Khakhalin &amp; Aizenman 2012 - pyruvate 5 mM does not alter EGABA in Xenopus tectum.
◦ Ivanov et al. 2011 - substrate effects attributed to pH shifts, not metabolism; D-lactate (non-metabolizable) inhibits giant depolarizing potentials like L-lactate, excluding a metabolic explanation.
◦ Barkis et al. 2010 - pyruvate no effect in isolated retina.
• Dose critique: Zilberter used 4-5 mM pyruvate, described as 20-40 fold above physiological brain levels and "never observed in the brain except under severe metabolic disorders".
• Damage critique rebutted: standard practice records 70-140 micrometres below the slice surface (dead neurons occupy the first ~25 micrometres, citing Moyer &amp; Brown 2002). Also, in olfactory bulb, ACUTELY AXOTOMIZED mitral cells (KCC2-positive) were hyperpolarized by GABA while NON-axotomized granule cells (KCC2-negative) were depolarized, at similar slice depth - directly contradicting the damage hypothesis.
• Preparation-independence: depolarizing GABA is seen in brain slabs, intact chick/mouse/rat embryonic spinal cord, intact retina, and dissociated cultures - preparations with minimal or no cut surface.
• In-vivo non-mammalian: zebrafish retinal ganglion cells shift depolarizing-to-hyperpolarizing at 2.5 days post-fertilization, and the switch is delayed by KCC2 knockdown. Turtle retina: GABA depolarizing at embryonic stage 25.
• Logical point made by Ben-Ari et al.: the two challenge hypotheses are mutually incompatible. If glucose-only perfusion causes global energy deprivation (Zilberter), DEEP neurons should also show depolarizing GABA; if only surface damage causes it (Dzhala), energy supplementation should not rescue surface neurons. "One cannot base a model on two incompatible observations."
• Ben-Ari also published a commentary questioning whether in-vivo recordings from very immature neurons are inherently more reliable than slice recordings (anaesthesia, mechanical access, and the fact that in-vivo neonatal cortex is itself a perturbed preparation).

CURRENT STATE (fair summary for the book): the developmental Cl- gradient shift and the KCC2/NKCC1 expression switch are robust and reproduced across species and preparations. What is genuinely unsettled is whether early depolarizing GABA is ever NET EXCITATORY in the intact, unanaesthetized neonatal brain. Kirmse's in-vivo data say depolarizing-but-inhibitory; Ben-Ari's camp says the in-vivo preparation (anaesthesia, especially, which itself potentiates GABA-A) understates excitation. What would settle it: awake, non-anaesthetized neonatal in-vivo recordings with gramicidin perforated patch or optically measured driving force, in a preparation without craniotomy stress - technically very hard and not yet done.

5. KCC2 (SLC12A5) AND NKCC1 (SLC12A2)

• Stoichiometry: KCC2 is K+:Cl- 1:1. NKCC1 is Na+:K+:2Cl- (1:1:2).
• BOTH ARE ELECTRONEUTRAL. Kaila et al. 2014, verbatim: "NKCC1 and KCC2, like all CCCs, are electroneutral, in other words, they do not generate any current by themselves." Consequence: neither transporter is directly driven by membrane potential; each is driven purely by the summed chemical potential of its transported species.
• Thermodynamic driving force, using Kaila et al. 2014's stated typical values EK = -90 mV and ENa = +74 mV:
◦ KCC2 (K+ + Cl- out): equilibrium at ECl = EK. Because EK (-90 mV) is negative, KCC2 runs outward, extruding Cl- until ECl approaches EK. Theoretical floor 4.4 mM at ECl = -90 mV.
◦ NKCC1 (Na+ + K+ + 2Cl- in): the steep inward Na+ gradient (ENa = +74 mV) dominates, so the transporter runs inward, loading Cl-. Theoretical ceiling above 90 mM at ECl = -8 mV.
◦ In both cases the ATP cost is paid indirectly by the Na+/K+-ATPase that maintains the Na+ and K+ gradients - the cotransporters are secondary active transporters.
• Developmental logic (Rivera C, Voipio J, Payne JA, Ruusuvuori E, Lahtinen H, Lamsa K, et al. 1999, "The K+/Cl- co-transporter KCC2 renders GABA hyperpolarizing during neuronal maturation", Nature 397:251-255, doi:10.1038/16697, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9930699/): antisense knockdown of KCC2 in functionally mature rat hippocampal pyramidal neurons produced a marked POSITIVE shift in GABA-A reversal potential, establishing KCC2 as the principal neuronal Cl- extruder responsible for fast hyperpolarizing postsynaptic inhibition.
• Ben-Ari Y (2002), "Excitatory actions of GABA during development: the nature of the nurture", Nat Rev Neurosci 3(9):728-739, doi:10.1038/nrn920, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12209121/ - GABAergic synapses form before glutamatergic ones; "GABA becomes inhibitory by the delayed expression of a chloride exporter".
• Yamada et al. (2004), J Physiol - Cl- uptake driving depolarizing GABA in immature rat neocortex is mediated by NKCC1. https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1113/jphysiol.2004.062471
• Reference review: Kaila K, Price TJ, Payne JA, Puskarjov M, Voipio J (2014), "Cation-chloride cotransporters in neuronal development, plasticity and disease", Nat Rev Neurosci 15(10):637-654, doi:10.1038/nrn3819, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4294553/

6. WHY CHLORIDE IS THE PHYSIOLOGICAL COUNTER-ION

• Abundance: Cl- is the most abundant free anion in extracellular fluid (98-107 mmol/L in plasma, 120-132 mmol/L in CSF), far exceeding HCO3- (~24 mmol/L) and any other permeant anion. Nothing else is present in sufficient quantity to carry a fast, high-conductance current.
• Channel selectivity is lyotropic, not chemically specific: Bormann J, Hamill OP, Sakmann B (1987), "Mechanism of anion permeation through channels gated by glycine and gamma-aminobutyric acid in mouse cultured spinal neurones", J Physiol 385:243-286, https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1113/jphysiol.1987.sp016493 . Relative permeability sequence for BOTH GABA-A and glycine receptors: SCN- &gt; I- &gt; Br- &gt; Cl- &gt; F-. Effective pore diameters: glycine receptor 5.2 Angstrom, GABA-A receptor 5.6 Angstrom.
◦ Interpretation: permeability tracks inversely with hydration energy - larger, weakly hydrated anions shed their water shell more easily and pass more readily; F- is small but so strongly hydrated that it is the least permeant. Cl- sits mid-sequence: hydrated enough to be highly soluble and osmotically well-behaved, weakly hydrated enough to permeate readily. It is the only anion that is simultaneously abundant AND permeant.
• Bicarbonate permeability of GABA-A: PHCO3/PCl = 0.2-0.4 (Kaila et al. 2014, verbatim: "a HCO3-/Cl- permeability ratio of 0.2-0.4"). Bormann, Hamill &amp; Sakmann 1987 measured 0.42 by constant-field conductance analysis and 0.33 from simultaneous EGABA, [Cl-]i and [HCO3-]i (pH) measurement. Hübner &amp; Holthoff (2013) give a wider literature span of 0.18-0.6, citing Fatima-Shad &amp; Barry 1993. So: 0.2-0.4 is the standard quoted band, defensible; the full literature spread is 0.18-0.6.
◦ Functional consequence: because EHCO3 is only about -10 to -20 mV and HCO3- is continuously regenerated by carbonic anhydrase, HCO3- efflux through GABA-A channels acts as a persistent depolarizing "leak" that holds EGABA positive of ECl, and under intense activation causes intracellular acidification and extracellular alkalinization. See Kaila &amp; Voipio 1987, Nature 330:163, "Postsynaptic fall in intracellular pH induced by GABA-activated bicarbonate conductance", https://www.nature.com/articles/330163a0

7. WHAT IS NOT WELL ESTABLISHED - SUMMARY FOR THE BOOK

• Absolute [Cl-]i in any neuron in vivo. Every method perturbs or is out of range. Would be settled by a KD-matched (5-20 mM), pH-ratiometric, in-vivo-validated genetically encoded sensor cross-checked against gramicidin perforated patch in the same cells.
• The exact Donnan-versus-active-secretion split for CSF chloride. Textbook sources assert both; the primary data are 1960s dog studies without traceable citation in the source consulted.
• Whether early GABA is ever NET EXCITATORY in the awake intact neonatal brain. Depolarizing: yes, agreed by both camps. Excitatory: disputed (Kirmse 2015 vs Ben-Ari/Kaila).
• The 98-107 mmol/L plasma interval is a convention, not a measured constant; state it with the lab-variation caveat.

Verification failures to note: the Mayo Clinic Labs PDF (403) and J Neurosci Owens et al. 1996 full text (403) could not be fetched; the Owens 1996 abstract was obtained via PubMed (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8815920/) and confirms qualitative progressive negative shift in GABA reversal potential with maturation but did not yield extractable mV/mM values. Medscape reference-range page returned 402. Ben-Ari 2002's own [Cl-]i figures were not directly extracted from the primary text - the 30 mM figure comes from Achilles et al. 2007 as cited by Watanabe &amp; Fukuda 2015.</result>

<result>FINDINGS: WATER AND THE HYDROPHOBIC EFFECT (brain/neuroscience context)

All numbers below were taken from sources retrieved via web search/fetch on 2026-08-12. Where I could only see a search-result snippet rather than fetch the full text, I say so.


1. MOLAR CONCENTRATION OF PURE WATER

• Derivation: molarity = (density x 1000 g/L) / molar mass. Molar mass of H2O = 18.0153 g/mol.

• At 4 C / density 1.000 g/mL: 1000 / 18.0153 = 55.51 M. This is the textbook "55.5 M".

• At 25 C, density = 0.99705 g/mL: 997.05 / 18.0153 = 55.34 M.

• At 37 C, density = 0.99333 g/mL: 993.33 / 18.0153 = 55.14 M.

• Note the common confusion in secondary sources: one search summary attributed 55.34 M to 37 C. That is wrong - 55.34 M is the 25 C value, and 37 C is ~55.1 M. Arithmetic above is directly checkable, so this one is not genuinely in dispute; it is just widely misquoted.

• Consequence worth stating in the chapter: water is present at ~55 M while the most abundant intracellular solute (K+) is ~0.14 M, i.e. water outnumbers it ~400:1, and outnumbers a 1 microM neurotransmitter by ~5 x 10^7:1.

• Source for derivation convention: standard chemistry treatments, e.g. https://www.letstalkacademy.com/molarity-pure-water-55-5-m-rounded-1-decimal/ (secondary; the calculation is trivially verifiable and does not need a primary citation).


2. BRAIN WATER CONTENT

• Best modern in vivo quantitative MRI value (fetched full text): Neeb-type quantitative water content mapping, Frontiers in Neurology 2019, "A Single-Scan, Rapid Whole-Brain Protocol for Quantitative Water Content Mapping With Neurobiological Implications", 21 healthy volunteers at 3 tesla, CSF as internal reference:
◦ White matter 69.1% (SD 1.7%)
◦ Grey matter 83.7% (SD 1.2%)
◦ URL: https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/neurology/articles/10.3389/fneur.2019.01333/full

• That same paper's literature comparison, which is the most useful thing to cite for a range:
◦ Invasive biopsy/post-mortem measurements: white matter 68.7-71.6%; grey matter 80.5-84.6%
◦ Prior in vivo MRI: white matter ~69-70%; grey matter ~83%

• Independent older method (T2 relaxation, multi-component): Whittall, MacKay et al., Magnetic Resonance in Medicine 1997, "In vivo measurement of T2 distributions and water contents in normal human brain": white matter 0.71 (SD 0.01) g/mL, grey matter 0.83 (SD 0.03) g/mL, i.e. ~71% and ~83%. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8978630/ (seen in search-result snippet, not fetched in full).

• Disagreement: white matter sits at 69% by the 2019 MRI method and 71% by the 1997 T2 method - a real ~2 percentage-point method-dependent offset. Grey matter agrees at ~83-84% across methods. So quote grey 83-84% and white 69-71%, and note white matter's spread comes from myelin lipid fraction and from how bound/myelin water is counted.

• Clinical calibration point from the same 2019 paper: in a stroke patient 4.5 days post-onset the infarcted cortex was 89.5% water versus 82.5% contralateral, i.e. cerebral oedema shows as a ~7 percentage-point rise. Useful for the chapter because it shows how small a water-content change is pathologically decisive.


3. HYDROGEN BOND ENERGY AND HYDROGEN-BOND NETWORK LIFETIME

Bond energy

• Optimal hydrogen bond enthalpy in liquid water: ~23.3 kJ/mol. Source: Martin Chaplin, "Water's Hydrogen Bond Strength", arXiv:0706.1355, https://arxiv.org/pdf/0706.1355 (Chaplin also maintains the Water Structure and Science compendium).

• Commonly quoted range: ~20-23 kJ/mol (about 5-6 kcal/mol), versus ~460 kJ/mol for the O-H covalent bond, i.e. a hydrogen bond is roughly 5% of a covalent bond and only ~9x thermal energy kT at 310 K (kT = 2.58 kJ/mol). This is why the network is liquid rather than solid at body temperature.

• Cross-check from thermodynamics: enthalpy of vaporisation of water is 40.7 kJ/mol at 100 C and ~44 kJ/mol at 25 C. Since evaporating one molecule breaks a bit under two hydrogen bonds on average, 44/2 ~ 22 kJ/mol per bond - consistent with 20-23. This is the derivation to show the reader.

• Honest caveat: "the" hydrogen bond energy is not a single well-defined observable. It depends on whether you mean the pair interaction energy, the enthalpy including cooperativity, or the free energy of bond formation; different definitions legitimately give 6-25 kJ/mol. Say so.

Lifetime / reorientation time

• Orientational relaxation time of pure water (HOD in D2O, ultrafast infrared anisotropy, Fayer group, Stanford): ~2.6 ps, assigned to concerted hydrogen-bond rearrangement via jump reorientation. Also quoted as ~3 ps single-exponential orientational relaxation for HOD. Fayer group reprints: http://web.stanford.edu/group/fayer/articles/465.pdf and https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.0907875106

• Individual hydrogen-bond making/breaking (fast component, Bakker group, AMOLF Amsterdam, femtosecond mid-IR): a fast time constant of ~0.7 ps alongside much slower populations (~13 ps in constrained environments). So the network turns over on 0.7-3 ps.

• Mechanism: Laage &amp; Hynes, "A Molecular Jump Mechanism of Water Reorientation", Science 2006, DOI 10.1126/science.1122154, https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1122154. Key claim: reorientation is NOT small diffusive steps (the old Debye picture). A water molecule breaks its hydrogen bond to an over-coordinated first-shell neighbour and forms one to an under-coordinated second-shell neighbour in a concerted large-amplitude angular jump of roughly 60 degrees. Follow-up: Laage &amp; Hynes, "On the molecular mechanism of water reorientation", https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18942871/ and the review Laage, Accounts of Chemical Research 2012, http://www-lbt.ibpc.fr/sites/default/files/u15/2012-Laage-AccChemRes.pdf

• Neuroscience framing: an action potential lasts ~1 ms. The water network around a channel protein reconfigures ~300,000 to 1,000,000 times during a single spike. Protein conformational changes are therefore always sampling a fully equilibrated solvent.

• Where sources differ: the "hydrogen bond lifetime" number in textbooks ranges from ~1 ps to ~5 ps purely because of definitional choice (geometric bond criterion versus spectroscopic observable versus jump waiting time). Give the range 0.7-3 ps and name the definition you are using.


4. SELF-DIFFUSION OF WATER, AND BRAIN ADC

Pure water self-diffusion (D)

• 25 C: 2.299 x 10^-9 m^2/s (Holz, Heil &amp; Sacco, Physical Chemistry Chemical Physics 2000, the standard NMR pulsed-field-gradient calibration paper, DOI 10.1039/b005319h). Independent values at 25 C: 2.296 (Easteal et al. 1989), 2.30 (Harris &amp; Woolf 1980), 2.317 (Tofts et al. 2000). Agreement is excellent, ~1%. Compilation with all four tables: https://dtrx.de/od/diff/

• 40 C: 3.222 x 10^-9 m^2/s (Holz et al. 2000, from the same compilation).

• 37 C: NOT directly tabulated in the source I fetched. Interpolating the Holz data between 25 C and 40 C gives approximately 3.0 x 10^-9 m^2/s. So the commonly quoted ~3.0 x 10^-9 m^2/s at body temperature is sound but is an interpolated, not a directly measured, figure in the source I could verify. Flag it as such.

• Units note: 1 micrometre^2/ms = 10^-3 mm^2/s = 10^-9 m^2/s. Keeping this identity in front of the reader prevents the classic three-orders-of-magnitude error when comparing physical chemistry to radiology.

Brain apparent diffusion coefficient (ADC) by diffusion-weighted MRI

• Helenius et al., American Journal of Neuroradiology 2002, 23(2):194, "Diffusion-Weighted MR Imaging in Normal Human Brains in Various Age Groups", 80 healthy adults (40 men, 40 women), four age bands of 20 each:
◦ Cortical grey matter: 0.89 ± 0.04 x 10^-3 mm^2/s (range 0.78-1.09)
◦ Deep grey matter: 0.75 ± 0.03 x 10^-3 mm^2/s (range 0.64-0.83)
◦ White matter: 0.70 ± 0.03 x 10^-3 mm^2/s (range 0.62-0.79)
◦ ADC did not change significantly with age except an increase in the lateral ventricles; no sex or hemisphere difference.
◦ URL: https://www.ajnr.org/content/23/2/194 (the journal returned HTTP 403 to my fetcher; these values come from the indexed abstract text seen in search results, and are consistently reproduced across two independent search summaries)

• Corroborating white matter range from a separate clinical series: 0.60-1.05 x 10^-3 mm^2/s, mean 0.84 ± 0.11 x 10^-3 mm^2/s. This mean is notably higher than Helenius, almost certainly because of partial-volume with CSF and different region-of-interest placement. Flag the disagreement: white matter ADC is reported anywhere from 0.70 to 0.84 x 10^-3 mm^2/s depending on ROI method and b-values used.

• The key physical point for the chapter: brain ADC (~0.7-0.9 x 10^-3 mm^2/s) is 3 to 4 times SMALLER than free water at 37 C (~3.0 x 10^-3 mm^2/s in the same units). Water in brain tissue is hindered by roughly a factor of 3-4 by membranes, macromolecular crowding and tortuosity of the extracellular space. White matter is additionally anisotropic - the isotropic ADC number hides the fact that diffusion along an axon can be several times faster than across it, which is the whole basis of diffusion tensor imaging and tractography.

• Directly relevant to the glymphatic argument in section 8: this hindered-but-still-fast diffusion is exactly the transport that the glymphatic sceptics say suffices without bulk flow.


5. THE HYDROPHOBIC EFFECT: FREE ENERGY PER BURIED SURFACE AREA

Primary citations

• Chothia, C. (1974) "Hydrophobic bonding and accessible surface area in proteins", Nature 248:338. https://www.nature.com/articles/248338a0 - the source of the canonical ~25 cal/mol per square angstrom.

• Reynolds, J. A., Gilbert, D. B. &amp; Tanford, C. (1974) "Empirical correlation between hydrophobic free energy and aqueous cavity surface area", Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences USA - independent derivation of the same proportionality from hydrocarbon solubility.

• Tanford, C. (1978) "The Hydrophobic Effect and the Organization of Living Matter", Science, DOI 10.1126/science.653353, https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.653353 - the conceptual statement that the hydrophobic effect organises membranes and proteins.

• Tanford, C. "The Hydrophobic Effect: Formation of Micelles and Biological Membranes" (Wiley, 2nd ed. 1980), https://archive.org/details/isbn_0471844608 - the book-length treatment.

• Sharp, K. A., Nicholls, A., Fine, R. &amp; Honig, B. (1991) "Reconciling the magnitude of the microscopic and macroscopic hydrophobic effects", Science 252(5002). https://www.science.org/toc/science/252/5002

Numbers and the genuine disagreement

• Macroscopic/solubility-derived value: ~25 cal/mol/A^2 at 25 C (Chothia 1974; Reynolds/Gilbert/Tanford 1974). Some treatments quote the proportionality constant as 20-25 cal/mol/A^2 when areas are measured at the distance of closest approach of a water molecule.

• A related simulation-corrected figure of ~24 cal/mol/A^2 appears when converting between molecular surface area and solvent-accessible surface area.

• The Sharp/Honig 1991 problem: values inferred from protein interiors ("microscopic") come out roughly twice the hydrocarbon-solubility value - the figure usually quoted is ~46-47 cal/mol/A^2. Sharp et al. argued the discrepancy is largely a volume-entropy (Flory-Huggins) bookkeeping artefact in how the standard state is defined, and that once corrected, macroscopic and microscopic estimates reconcile. This was contested at the time and the correction is still not universally accepted.

• Honest statement for the chapter: the "hydrophobic effect costs about 25 cal/mol/A^2" number is a robust order-of-magnitude anchor but the true coefficient is model-dependent, ranges from ~20 to ~47 cal/mol/A^2 depending on how surface area and standard state are defined, and is curvature-dependent - small solutes (below ~1 nm) and large surfaces obey different physics.

• Entropy-driven claim - be careful, this is temperature-dependent and often overstated. At around 25 C, transferring a nonpolar solute into water has a small enthalpy change and a large negative entropy change, so the unfavourable free energy is indeed dominated by entropy (ordering of water). But near ~110-140 C (the entropy convergence temperature) the entropic term vanishes and the effect becomes enthalpy-driven. See the convergence-temperature literature: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC49655/ . So say "entropy-dominated at physiological temperature", not "entropy-driven" flatly.

• Length-scale crossover, important and often omitted: Chandler, "Interfaces and the driving force of hydrophobic assembly", Nature 437:640 (2005), https://www.nature.com/articles/nature04162 - small solutes are accommodated without breaking hydrogen bonds (entropic), large surfaces above ~1 nm force the network to break bonds and form a vapour-like interface (enthalpic, scales with area). A lipid bilayer is firmly in the large-surface regime, so the per-area scaling is the right description for membranes.

• Also relevant: Hummer et al., "Quantification of the hydrophobic interaction by simulations of the aggregation of small hydrophobic solutes in water", PNAS 2001, https://www.pnas.org/doi/full/10.1073/pnas.111158498

• What would settle the coefficient: it will not be settled by a single experiment, because it is definition-dependent rather than measurement-limited. The tractable version is a systematic double-mutant cycle series in a single protein scaffold with high-resolution structures and calorimetry, separating cavity-volume effects from surface-area effects - which is essentially what the "maximum change in stability upon hydrophobic-to-smaller mutation" literature attempts (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2142503/).


6. LIPID BILAYER THICKNESS

• Hydrophobic (hydrocarbon) core of a fluid-phase POPC bilayer: ~2.7 nm (26.7 A) when defined by the ester-carbon-to-ester-carbon distance. Reference: model parameters for physiological lipids, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5067697/

• Substrate-supported POPC bilayers: total hydrocarbon thickness 30 ± 1 A (3.0 nm), about 3 A larger than in free-standing stacked bilayers and vesicles - i.e. the substrate itself perturbs the measurement by ~10%. Worth mentioning as a methods caution.

• Total bilayer thickness including headgroups: ~3.5-4.0 nm for POPC; ~4-5 nm is the range usually quoted for biological membranes, which are thicker than pure POPC because of cholesterol and longer/more saturated chains. Cholesterol thickens and orders the bilayer.

• Authoritative primary source for the full set of fluid-phase phosphatidylcholine thicknesses versus temperature: Kučerka, Nieh &amp; Katsaras, Biochimica et Biophysica Acta 2011, "Fluid phase lipid areas and bilayer thicknesses of commonly used phosphatidylcholines as a function of temperature", https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0005273611002276 ; and Kučerka et al. 2006, "Structure of fully hydrated fluid phase lipid bilayers with monounsaturated chains", https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16604469/

• Definition warning for the chapter: "bilayer thickness" is at least four different numbers - head-to-head phosphate-peak distance (D_HH), Luzzati thickness (D_B), hydrocarbon thickness (2D_C), and steric thickness. Quoting 4-5 nm versus 3 nm without saying which one is the single most common source of confusion here. State: hydrophobic core ~3 nm, phosphate-to-phosphate ~3.7-4 nm, total steric ~4-5 nm.

• Neuroscience anchor: the ~3 nm hydrophobic core is what sets the ~20-25 residue length of a transmembrane alpha helix (1.5 A rise per residue x 20 = 30 A), and it is the barrier that makes ion channels necessary at all - the Born energy of moving a bare K+ into a hydrocarbon slab is tens of kT.


7. AQUAPORIN-4 SINGLE-CHANNEL WATER PERMEABILITY

• Primary measurement: Yang, van Hoek &amp; Verkman (1997), Biochemistry, "Very High Single Channel Water Permeability of Aquaporin-4 in Baculovirus-Infected Insect Cells and Liposomes Reconstituted with Purified Aquaporin-4", https://pubs.acs.org/doi/abs/10.1021/bi970231r
◦ Baculovirus-infected insect cells: pf = 19 x 10^-14 cm^3/s
◦ Purified AQP4 reconstituted into proteoliposomes: pf = 15 x 10^-14 cm^3/s
◦ (Journal returned 403 to my fetcher; values come from the indexed abstract text.)

• Later measurements, bilayer-dependent: unit permeabilities of AQP4-M1 of 3.3 ± 0.4 x 10^-13, 1.2 ± 0.1 x 10^-13, and 0.4 ± 0.1 x 10^-13 cm^3/s in three different lipid compositions - an eightfold spread caused by the membrane alone. Sources: "Water Permeability of Aquaporin-4 Channel Depends on Bilayer Composition, Thickness, and Elasticity", Biophysical Journal 2012, https://www.cell.com/biophysj/fulltext/S0006-3495(12)01062-4 ; and "The Water Permeability and Pore Entrance Structure of Aquaporin-4 Depend on Lipid Bilayer Thickness", https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4944661/

• Explicit statement of the problem in the literature: measured aquaporin single-channel permeabilities scatter over about an order of magnitude, and reliable measurement is a long-standing unsolved technical challenge. Simulation attempt: "Computing osmotic permeabilities of aquaporins AQP4, AQP5, and GlpF from near-equilibrium simulations", https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28455098/

• UNITS - this is where the "10^9-10^10 water molecules per second" figure comes from, and it must be derived, not asserted:
◦ pf has units of cm^3/s (volume of water per second per channel per unit osmotic gradient expressed as molar).
◦ Volume occupied by one water molecule = 18 cm^3/mol / 6.022 x 10^23 = 2.99 x 10^-23 cm^3.
◦ Molecules per second per unit molar gradient = pf x 55.5 mol/L x 6.022 x 10^23 / 1000 = pf x 3.34 x 10^22.
◦ Taking pf = 15 x 10^-14 cm^3/s gives ~5 x 10^9 water molecules per second per channel (per 1 osmolar gradient).
◦ Taking pf = 3.3 x 10^-13 cm^3/s gives ~1.1 x 10^10 per second.
◦ Taking the low bilayer value 0.4 x 10^-13 gives ~1.3 x 10^9 per second.
◦ So the "10^9 to 10^10 molecules per second" statement IS supported, but only as a range, and the range reflects genuine measurement disagreement plus real lipid-dependence, not just sloppiness.

• Two further honest caveats: (i) the per-second figure depends on the assumed osmotic gradient, and quoting it without stating the gradient is meaningless; (ii) AQP4 has two main isoforms, M1 and M23, which form different orthogonal-array-of-particle assemblies at astrocyte endfeet and are reported to differ in permeability - so "the AQP4 permeability" is not a single constant. Background: "Aquaporin-4 Water Channel in the Brain and Its Implication for Health and Disease", https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6406241/

• What would settle it: a single-channel measurement that does not require knowing the number of channels in the membrane. Essentially all the scatter comes from errors in counting how many functional AQP4 tetramers are in the vesicle, not from the flux measurement. A method combining single-molecule counting (e.g. calibrated fluorescence or cryo-EM particle counting on the same vesicles used for stopped-flow) would collapse the order-of-magnitude spread.


8. CSF NUMBERS AND THE GLYMPHATIC CONTROVERSY

CSF quantities (these are consistent across sources, with modest ranges)

• Total CSF volume in an adult: ~150 mL (some sources give 125-150 mL).

• Production rate: ~500 mL/day, commonly quoted as 400-600 mL/day in adults; one standard fluid-physiology source gives 550 mL/day.

• Expressed per minute: 500/1440 = ~0.35 mL/min; the usual quoted range is 0.3-0.4 mL/min.

• Turnover: complete replacement of the CSF pool ~3-4 times per day (from 550 mL/day over 150 mL), with other sources giving 4-5 times per day (from the 400-600 mL/day figures and a smaller assumed volume). Quote "roughly 3 to 5 times per day" and note the spread comes entirely from which production rate and which pool volume you pair.

• Sources: StatPearls, "Physiology, Cerebral Spinal Fluid", https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK519007/ and "Neuroanatomy, Cerebrospinal Fluid", https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK470578/ ; Fluid Physiology 3.6 Cerebrospinal Fluid, https://www.anaesthesiamcq.com/FluidBook/fl3_6.php ; mechanism paper on cotransporter-mediated water transport in CSF formation, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5986890/

• Context worth stating: CSF production (~500 mL/day) is dwarfed by cerebral blood flow (~750 mL/min, i.e. ~1,080,000 mL/day) - about 2000-fold less. Any bulk-flow clearance mechanism has to work within that very modest fluid budget.

THE GLYMPHATIC CONTROVERSY - report both sides

Side A, the original proposal

• Iliff, J. J., Wang, M., Liao, Y. et al. (2012) "A Paravascular Pathway Facilitates CSF Flow Through the Brain Parenchyma and the Clearance of Interstitial Solutes, Including Amyloid beta", Science Translational Medicine 4(147):147ra111. https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/scitranslmed.3003748 (Nedergaard lab, University of Rochester)
◦ Claim: CSF enters the parenchyma along paravascular spaces surrounding penetrating arteries, exchanges with interstitial fluid, and interstitial fluid plus solutes (including amyloid beta) is cleared along paravenous drainage routes. The astrocyte endfoot water channel AQP4 is claimed to be required for this convective exchange - hence "glia-lymphatic" = glymphatic.
◦ Contemporary coverage: https://www.alzforum.org/news/research-news/brain-drain-glymphatic-pathway-clears-av-requires-water-channel

• Supporting follow-ups: MRI visualisation of a brain-wide clearance pathway, Iliff et al., Journal of Clinical Investigation, https://www.jci.org/articles/view/67677 ; circadian control of glymphatic flow, Nature Communications 2020, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-020-18115-2 ; human evidence from 24-hour tracer ADC estimates supporting glymphatic transport in cortex, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7280526/ ; optimal-mass-transport analysis finding both advective AND diffusive components, Scientific Reports 2020, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-020-59045-9

Side B, the critiques

• Smith, A. J., Yao, X., Dix, J. A., Jin, B.-J. &amp; Verkman, A. S. (2017) "Test of the 'glymphatic' hypothesis demonstrates diffusive and aquaporin-4-independent solute transport in rodent brain parenchyma", eLife 6:e27679. https://elifesciences.org/articles/27679 , https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28826498/
◦ Claim: solute movement in brain extracellular space is governed by diffusion, and depends on neither convection nor AQP4 expression. Both pillars of the 2012 paper are challenged.
◦ Note the AQP4-knockout result is itself disputed between labs - the two groups have published mutually inconsistent knockout phenotypes, which is a genuine reproducibility dispute, not a difference of interpretation. Smith and Verkman published a formal rebuttal in a Journal of Physiology CrossTalk debate: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/335018598_Rebuttal_from_Alex_J_Smith_and_Alan_S_Verkman

• Jin, Smith &amp; Verkman (2016) "Glymphatic solute transport does not require bulk flow", Scientific Reports 6:38635. https://www.nature.com/articles/srep38635 - modelling showing diffusion alone accounts for the observed tracer distributions.

• Hladky &amp; Barrand (2022) "The glymphatic hypothesis: the theory and the evidence", Fluids and Barriers of the CNS. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s12987-021-00282-z - the most balanced systematic review. Their conclusion: there IS good evidence that both influx and efflux of solutes occur along periarterial routes, but NO good evidence that the principal outflow route is perivenous, and any circulatory flow velocity within the interstitium is too small relative to diffusion to move solutes effectively. In other words, part of the glymphatic picture survives (perivascular pumping) and part does not (the parenchymal convective through-flow and the venous return leg).

• Review of the controversy: "The Brain's Glymphatic System: Current Controversies", https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32423764/ ; and "Transport in the Brain Extracellular Space: Diffusion, but Which Kind?", https://doi.org/10.3390/ijms232012401

THE 2024 PAPER - identified

• The paper the brief was looking for is NOT in Nature proper but in Nature Neuroscience: Miao, A., Luo, T., Hsieh, B. et al. (2024) "Brain clearance is reduced during sleep and anesthesia", Nature Neuroscience 27:1046-1050. https://www.nature.com/articles/s41593-024-01638-y
◦ Institutions: Imperial College London and the UK Dementia Research Institute - led by Professor Nick Franks and Professor Bill Wisden. (So the brief's guess of "Oxford/Copenhagen" is not right; it is Imperial College London. Copenhagen/Rochester is Nedergaard's side, i.e. the group being challenged.)
◦ Method: fluorescent dye injected into mouse brain; movement between brain regions and clearance from brain tracked directly.
◦ Headline result: clearance was markedly REDUCED - not increased - during both natural sleep and anaesthesia, the opposite direction to the widely publicised sleep-clears-toxins claim. The authors state their results "challenge the idea that the core function of sleep is to clear toxins from the brain".
◦ There is an Author Correction: Nature Neuroscience 2024, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41593-024-01698-0 , https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/38877307/ . Cite the correction alongside the paper - the correction exists and a reference book should acknowledge it rather than let a reader discover it.
◦ Institutional summary: https://www.ukdri.ac.uk/news-and-events/scientists-find-sleep-may-not-clear-brain-toxins-as-previously-thought
◦ Balanced field reaction, including Nedergaard's rebuttal points: https://www.alzforum.org/news/research-news/does-sleep-really-speed-clearance-brain

• A further 2024 challenge to a specific glymphatic mechanism: Tanaka et al. (2024) report that movement of substances within perivascular spaces resembles free diffusion and is unaffected by pulsation of penetrating blood vessels - undercutting arterial pulsation as the proposed pump. (Cited in the 2025 review at https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0969996125000075 ; I did not fetch the Tanaka primary paper, so verify the exact journal before citing it directly.)

• Modelling work that tries to hold the middle ground: "A brain-wide solute transport model of the glymphatic system", Journal of the Royal Society Interface 2024, 21(219):20240369, https://royalsocietypublishing.org/doi/10.1098/rsif.2024.0369

HOW TO STATE THE CONTROVERSY HONESTLY IN THE CHAPTER

• What essentially everyone agrees on: CSF and interstitial fluid exchange; tracers do enter the brain along periarterial spaces; AQP4 is densely and polarised-ly expressed at astrocyte endfeet; brain clearance of amyloid beta and tau matters for neurodegeneration.

• What is genuinely disputed, item by item:
◦ Is transport through the parenchyma convective (bulk flow) or diffusive? Iliff/Nedergaard say convective; Smith &amp; Verkman and Hladky &amp; Barrand say diffusive. The physics point is that with brain ADC ~0.7-0.9 x 10^-3 mm^2/s (section 4), diffusion alone already moves small solutes millimetres in tens of minutes, so any bulk flow has to beat that to matter.
◦ Is AQP4 required? Directly contradictory knockout results between the Nedergaard and Verkman labs. Unresolved.
◦ Is the outflow route perivenous? Hladky &amp; Barrand say there is no good evidence for it.
◦ Does sleep increase clearance? Nedergaard's 2013 Science work says yes; Miao/Franks/Wisden Nature Neuroscience 2024 says clearance is reduced in sleep and anaesthesia. Directly opposed, in the same species, and unresolved as of the sources I retrieved.

• What experiment would settle it: the disagreements are largely traceable to tracer methodology - injection-induced pressure artefacts, tracer size and binding, anaesthetic choice, and whether measurement is terminal (fixed tissue) or in vivo. The decisive study would be simultaneous, in vivo, injection-free measurement of solute clearance across defined sleep stages in the same animals, using two tracers of very different molecular weight (diffusion and convection scale differently with size - diffusion goes as roughly the inverse cube root of molecular weight, convection is size-independent), with pressure monitored throughout. Comparing the size-scaling of clearance is the cleanest discriminator between diffusion and bulk flow, and it is not what most existing studies did.


CROSS-CUTTING NUMBERS THAT TIE THE CHAPTER TOGETHER

• Water is ~55 M; brain is 69-84% water by mass; water reorients every 0.7-3 ps; it diffuses at ~3 x 10^-9 m^2/s free and ~3-4x slower in tissue; it crosses a 3 nm hydrophobic core only through AQP4 at ~10^9-10^10 molecules per channel per second; and the same water network's entropic cost of exposing nonpolar surface (~20-25 cal/mol/A^2 at physiological temperature) is what builds that 3 nm barrier in the first place.

• Reliability grading of the numbers above, since a reference book should say: SOLID - 55.5 M, brain water content, bilayer core ~3 nm, CSF volume/production, brain ADC. GOOD BUT DEFINITION-DEPENDENT - hydrogen bond energy, hydrogen bond lifetime, hydrophobic coefficient in cal/mol/A^2. GENUINELY UNSETTLED - AQP4 single-channel permeability (order-of-magnitude scatter), and every mechanistic claim of the glymphatic hypothesis beyond periarterial influx.

Sources (URLs used above): https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/neurology/articles/10.3389/fneur.2019.01333/full , https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8978630/ , https://arxiv.org/pdf/0706.1355 , https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1122154 , https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18942871/ , http://www-lbt.ibpc.fr/sites/default/files/u15/2012-Laage-AccChemRes.pdf , https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.0907875106 , http://web.stanford.edu/group/fayer/articles/465.pdf , https://dtrx.de/od/diff/ , https://www.ajnr.org/content/23/2/194 , https://www.nature.com/articles/248338a0 , https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.653353 , https://www.science.org/toc/science/252/5002 , https://www.nature.com/articles/nature04162 , https://www.pnas.org/doi/full/10.1073/pnas.111158498 , https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5067697/ , https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0005273611002276 , https://pubs.acs.org/doi/abs/10.1021/bi970231r , https://www.cell.com/biophysj/fulltext/S0006-3495(12)01062-4 , https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4944661/ , https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6406241/ , https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK519007/ , https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/scitranslmed.3003748 , https://elifesciences.org/articles/27679 , https://www.nature.com/articles/srep38635 , https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s12987-021-00282-z , https://www.nature.com/articles/s41593-024-01638-y , https://www.nature.com/articles/s41593-024-01698-0 , https://www.ukdri.ac.uk/news-and-events/scientists-find-sleep-may-not-clear-brain-toxins-as-previously-thought , https://www.alzforum.org/news/research-news/does-sleep-really-speed-clearance-brain , https://royalsocietypublishing.org/doi/10.1098/rsif.2024.0369

Two things the caller should know: the 2024 glymphatic-challenging paper is Nature Neuroscience (Imperial College London, Franks/Wisden), not Nature and not Oxford/Copenhagen, and it carries a published Author Correction. And three journals (AJNR, ACS Biochemistry, Cell/Biophysical Journal) returned 403 to my fetcher, so those specific numbers come from indexed abstract text rather than full-text fetches - they were consistent across independent search summaries but should be spot-checked against the PDFs before print.</result>
