<result>FINDINGS: THE SODIUM ION IN NEUROSCIENCE (web-verified, 2026-08-12)

Note on failures: three primary sources refused fetching (SAGE full text of Attwell &amp; Laughlin 2001, PNAS 2105795118, Nature 383:634 direct). Where that happened I used PubMed abstracts or search-result text and I say so. Two numbers you asked for (real pump densities in pumps per square micrometre for neurons; a turnover number measured at 37 C in neurons) are NOT well established in what I could retrieve - flagged below.


1. SODIUM CONCENTRATIONS

• Mammalian neuron, extracellular Na+: ~140-145 mM. The figure most widely used in Nernst worked examples is 145 mM (University of Bristol teaching page on the Nernst equation, https://www.bristol.ac.uk/phys-pharm-neuro/media/plangton/ugteach/ugindex/m1_index/med_memb/file/Nernst1.htm ; also the Nernst chapter in Developing Expertise in Neuroscience, https://uen.pressbooks.pub/expertneuro/chapter/the-equilibrium-nernst-potential/ ). Textbook tables (Purves et al., Neuroscience, Table 2.1, NCBI Bookshelf NBK10931) give the mammalian value as 145 mM out.

• Mammalian neuron, intracellular Na+: sources give a RANGE, not a single number.

◦ 5-15 mM for mature neurons - Gerkau et al. 2017, J Neurosci Res, https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/jnr.23995 (SBFI, sodium-binding benzofuran isophthalate, a ratiometric fluorescent sodium dye).

◦ 10-15 mM as the standard intracellular value used for sodium magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) modelling - Nature Scientific Reports 4:4763, https://www.nature.com/articles/srep04763

◦ 13 mM baseline assumed for hippocampal dentate granule cells - Diaz-Garcia et al., eLife 2023;12:e81645, https://elifesciences.org/articles/81645 . Same paper: antidromic stimulation raised it transiently by ~25 mM, to ~38 mM total. That is a large, verified activity-dependent excursion worth quoting.

◦ Purves Table 2.1 quotes 5-15 mM for the mammalian neuron.

• Squid giant axon (the Hodgkin-Katz preparation) values are DIFFERENT and must not be mixed with mammalian ones:

◦ Extracellular (sea water) Na+ ~440-460 mM; axoplasm Na+ ~50 mM (Purves et al., Neuroscience, Table 2.1; Hodgkin &amp; Katz 1949, J Physiol 108:37-77, https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1113/jphysiol.1949.sp004310 and PubMed 18128147). I could NOT machine-read the 1949 PDF (it returned as binary), so the 440/50 pair is cited to the textbook table rather than read off the original page - flag this if the chapter needs the original wording.

◦ Independent squid measurement: Hinke / Caldwell-era work on electronic measurement of intracellular Na+ in squid axon, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2195159/

• DISAGREEMENT to state explicitly: intracellular Na+ has no single canonical value. Anything from 4 mM to 15 mM is defensible for a mammalian neuron at rest, and the choice swings E_Na by more than 10 mV (see section 2). What would settle it: paired measurements in the same cell type with two independent methods (SBFI or Asante NaTRIUM Green fluorescence, versus sodium MRI, versus ion-selective microelectrode), corrected for dye buffering. Different sources also differ because immature and developing neurons genuinely have higher [Na+]i (elevated Na+ in developing spinal neurons, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5310982/ ).


2. E_Na, THE SODIUM EQUILIBRIUM (NERNST) POTENTIAL AT 37 C

The Nernst equation (Walther Nernst, 1888) for an ion X of valence z:

E_X = (RT / zF) x ln([X]out / [X]in)

where R is the gas constant (8.314 J per mole per kelvin), T is absolute temperature in kelvin, F is the Faraday constant (96485 coulombs per mole), z is the ion's charge (+1 for Na+).

• At 37 C (T = 310.15 K): RT/F = 8.314 x 310.15 / 96485 = 0.02673 V = 26.7 mV. Verified at the Bristol Nernst page and the Developing Expertise in Neuroscience chapter (URLs above).

• Converting natural log to base-10 log multiplies by 2.303: 26.7 x 2.303 = 61.5 mV. So E_Na = 61.5 mV x log10([Na+]out / [Na+]in) at 37 C. Verified at the same two sources ("every 10-fold gradient of a singly charged ion gives 61.5 mV at 37 C").

Worked values (my arithmetic, from the verified constants):

• 145 out / 10 in: ratio 14.5; ln = 2.674; E_Na = 26.7 x 2.674 = +71.4 mV. Equivalently 61.5 x log10(14.5) = 61.5 x 1.1614 = +71.4 mV. The Bristol/MedSchoolCoach worked example quotes +71 mV for exactly this pair.

• 145 out / 12 in: ratio 12.08; E_Na = 61.5 x 1.0821 = +66.6 mV (this is the origin of the commonly quoted "+67 mV").

• 145 out / 15 in: ratio 9.67; E_Na = 61.5 x 0.9854 = +60.6 mV (the "about +60 mV" figure in most textbooks).

• 140 out / 15 in: ratio 9.33; E_Na = 61.5 x 0.9700 = +59.7 mV.

• Squid axon, 440 out / 50 in: ratio 8.8. At 37 C that is 61.5 x 0.9445 = +58.1 mV. But squid experiments run near 20 C, where RT/F = 25.3 mV and the base-10 coefficient is 58.2 mV, giving 58.2 x 0.9445 = +55.0 mV - which is exactly the classical squid E_Na of +55 mV quoted in Hille-derived tables (see search-result text at https://www.nature.com/scitable/topicpage/ion-channels-and-excitable-cells-14406097/ ).

• SO: the honest statement for the chapter is E_Na = +60 to +72 mV in mammalian neurons at 37 C, the spread coming almost entirely from the assumed [Na+]i (15 mM gives the low end, 10 mM the high end), and about +55 mV in the squid giant axon at ~20 C. Note the temperature term itself only moves things ~5 percent between 20 C and 37 C; the concentration assumption dominates.


3. THE Na+/K+-ATPase (SODIUM-POTASSIUM PUMP)

• Stoichiometry: 3 Na+ out, 2 K+ in, per 1 ATP (adenosine triphosphate) hydrolysed. "A stoichiometry of three Na+ exchanged for two K+ as one ATP is hydrolyzed has been observed in nearly all of the numerous cell types" - Basic Neurochemistry, The ATP-Dependent Na+,K+ Pump, NCBI Bookshelf NBK28174, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK28174/ . Originally established in neurons by R. C. Thomas (1972) in snail (Helix aspersa) neurons and by Lambert et al. (1974), both cited in https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6267510/ . Because 3 positive charges leave and 2 enter, the pump is electrogenic: it contributes a hyperpolarisation, usually less than 10 mV (NBK28174; ouabain-induced depolarisation of 3-7 mV measured in molluscan neurons, Christoffersen 1972, Lambert et al. 1974, via PMC6267510).

• Turnover rate: SOURCES DISAGREE, and the popular "about 100 per second" is a rounding.

◦ Purified Na,K-ATPase: 8,000 to 10,000 cycles per minute, i.e. 133 to 167 cycles per second (Proteopedia, Sodium-Potassium ATPase, https://proteopedia.org/wiki/index.php/Sodium-Potassium_ATPase ; also surfaced in the Colorado State pathophysiology page https://vivo.colostate.edu/hbooks/pathphys/topics/sodium_pump.html ).

◦ Turnover is strongly voltage-dependent: maximal at membrane potentials above about 0 mV and declining steadily at negative (physiological) potentials - Colina et al., Regulation of Na+/K+ ATPase Transport Velocity by RNA Editing, PLOS Biology 2010, https://journals.plos.org/plosbiology/article?id=10.1371/journal.pbio.1000540 . So the in-vivo rate at a resting potential of -70 mV is materially LOWER than the purified-enzyme figure. This is the honest reason "100 per second" and "150 per second" both appear.

◦ The same PLOS Biology paper shows RNA editing of the squid pump changes its transport velocity - i.e. the turnover number is not even a species constant.

◦ Comparative note: the sodium-calcium exchanger (NCX) runs 10-30 times faster than ATPase turnover (eLife 2023;12:e81645).

◦ RECOMMENDATION: write it as "roughly 100 per second at physiological voltage, up to about 150-170 per second for the purified enzyme at optimal voltage and saturating substrate", and say the range is real. What would settle it: simultaneous ouabain-binding-site counting plus pump-current measurement in the same neuron at 37 C, which is exactly the method described (turnover number = maximal Na,K-ATPase current divided by number of pumps from ouabain binding) but has not been done systematically across neuron types.

• Pump density: THIS IS THE WEAKEST NUMBER IN THE WHOLE BRIEF. I could not find a directly measured pumps-per-square-micrometre figure for mammalian neurons.

◦ The best retrievable statement: mean spacing between Na+/K+ pump molecules is about 34 nm (Daut 1987), cited in Na+/K+-pump and neurotransmitter membrane receptors, Invertebrate Neuroscience 2018, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6267510/ . Converting: 34 nm = 0.034 micrometres, so density = 1 / (0.034)^2 = about 865 pumps per square micrometre. That conversion is mine, not the paper's - present it as a derived estimate of order 10^3 per square micrometre.

◦ Same source: roughly 1000 times more Na+/K+ pump molecules than Na+ and K+ channel proteins are needed to balance pump current against passive leak (Daut 1987).

◦ Related, and safer to quote because it is a flux rather than a count: ATP utilisation of (0.6-9) x 10^4 ATP molecules per square micrometre per second for firing rates of 1-10 Hz (thermodynamic-constraints preprint, https://arxiv.org/pdf/0905.3690 - preprint, treat as secondary).

◦ Distribution, qualitative and well supported: pumps are concentrated where ion flux is highest - axon terminals, synapses, perikarya, dendritic processes and glial processes (NBK28174).

◦ What would settle it: quantitative freeze-fracture or single-molecule localisation microscopy counts of alpha-subunit (ATP1A1/ATP1A3) per square micrometre, resolved by compartment (node of Ranvier versus soma versus bouton). Node-of-Ranvier versus internode density in particular is asserted often and measured rarely.


4. BRAIN ATP SPENT ON THE Na+/K+ PUMP - THE TWO ENERGY BUDGETS

Background figures for the pump's overall share:

• 25 to 40 percent of brain energy utilisation may be related to Na,K-ATPase activity - Basic Neurochemistry, NBK28174.

• About 50 percent of total brain energy - Erecinska and Silver 1994, as cited in PMC6267510; and "the Na+/K+ pump consumes about 50 percent of total brain energy" citing Ames 2000, Astrup et al. 1981, and Engl &amp; Attwell 2015, in eLife 2023;12:e81645. So the pump's share is itself a disputed 25-50 percent, mostly because different authors draw the line between "signalling" and "housekeeping" differently. See Engl &amp; Attwell, Non-signalling energy use in the brain, J Physiol 2015, https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1113/jphysiol.2014.282517

Budget 1 - Attwell D, Laughlin SB (2001), An Energy Budget for Signaling in the Grey Matter of the Brain, J Cereb Blood Flow Metab 21(10):1133-1145, DOI 10.1097/00004647-200110000-00001, https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1097/00004647-200110000-00001 (full text 403-blocked to me; percentages below come from the publisher abstract text surfaced in search and the Semantic Scholar record https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/0dbafe159b57a58b36a4c10f2da986af8198b5f4 ). Rodent grey matter, assuming a mean action potential rate of 4 Hz:

• Action potentials 47 percent
• Postsynaptic effects of glutamate 34 percent
• Resting potentials 13 percent
• Glutamate recycling 3 percent
• (remainder to presynaptic transmitter release / vesicle cycling; the 2001 paper folds housekeeping OUT of the signalling budget and treats it separately)
• Sensitivity figure worth quoting: an increase of 1 action potential per cortical neuron per second raises oxygen consumption by 145 mL per 100 g grey matter per hour.
• Mechanistic point: nearly all of this is the cost of pumping out the Na+ that entered to generate synaptic and action currents - i.e. the energy budget of the brain is very largely a sodium budget.

Budget 2 - Howarth C, Gleeson P, Attwell D (2012), Updated energy budgets for neural computation in the neocortex and cerebellum, J Cereb Blood Flow Metab 32(7):1222-1232, DOI 10.1038/jcbfm.2012.35, PMID 22434069, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22434069/ (abstract fetched verbatim). Cerebral cortex, signalling energy:

• Postsynaptic glutamate receptors 50 percent
• Action potentials 21 percent
• Resting potentials 20 percent
• Presynaptic transmitter release 5 percent
• Transmitter recycling 4 percent

Cerebellar cortex, signalling energy:

• Resting potentials 54 percent
• Postsynaptic receptors 22 percent
• Action potentials 17 percent
• Excitatory neurons take 75 percent and inhibitory neurons 25 percent of signalling energy
• Purkinje cells use only 15 percent of signalling energy - most goes to non-principal neurons
• Mossy fibre to granule cell transmission dominates the cerebellar budget at 53 percent (from the search-result summary of the same paper; not in the abstract text I fetched verbatim, so verify before quoting)

The reason the action potential share fell from 47 percent to 21 percent:

• Alle H, Roth A, Geiger JRP (2009), Energy-efficient action potentials in hippocampal mossy fibers, Science 325(5946):1405-1408, DOI 10.1126/science.1174331, https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1174331 , PMID 19745156. In non-myelinated rat hippocampal mossy fibres, fast Na+ current decay and delayed K+ current onset minimise the overlap of the two fluxes, so total Na+ influx per action potential is only 1.3 times the theoretical minimum (the minimum being the charge needed just to swing the membrane capacitance), versus the factor of 4 assumed in the 2001 budget. Na+ and K+ channel kinetics are matched for metabolic efficiency.
• The 2012 abstract states the revision explicitly: "these estimates need reevaluating following recent work demonstrating that action potentials in mammalian neurons are much more energy efficient than was previously thought."
• Caution for the chapter: the 4 to 1.3 change is a roughly 3-fold cut in the sodium cost per spike, and 47 percent to 21 percent is a bit more than 2-fold - the two budgets also differ in model assumptions (a simple cortical model in 2012 versus the 2001 grey-matter model, and different assumed firing rates), so do not present 47 to 21 as arising from the 4 to 1.3 factor alone.


5. Na+ DIFFUSION COEFFICIENT IN WATER

• D(Na+) = 1.330 x 10^-9 m2 per second at 25 C (equivalently 1.33 x 10^-5 cm2 per second). VERIFIED. Source: aqion table of diffusion coefficients, https://www.aqion.de/site/diffusion-coefficients , which attributes the value to the PhreeqC version 3 thermodynamic database (phreeqc.dat). The underlying literature source generally cited for limiting ionic diffusion coefficients is P. Vanysek, Ionic conductivity and diffusion at infinite dilution, in CRC Handbook of Chemistry and Physics, 1992/93 edition, pages 5-111 to 5-113.

• Companion values from the same table, useful for contrast: K+ 1.960, Cl- 2.030, Ca2+ 0.793, all x 10^-9 m2 per second at 25 C. Note that Na+ diffuses more slowly than K+ despite being the smaller bare ion - because the higher charge density of Na+ gives it a larger hydration shell. That is a nice teaching point and is directly supported by the table.

• Caveat to state: these are infinite-dilution (limiting) values at 25 C in bulk water. In brain extracellular space the effective diffusion coefficient is lower by the square of the tortuosity factor (tortuosity about 1.6 in cortex, so effective D is roughly 2.5-fold lower). I did not fetch a source for that tortuosity figure in this session - verify before printing it.


6. SODIUM-COUPLED NEUROTRANSMITTER TRANSPORTER STOICHIOMETRIES

Glutamate - EAAT family (excitatory amino acid transporter), including EAAT2 / GLT-1, the dominant astrocytic transporter:

• 3 Na+ in, 1 H+ (proton) in, 1 glutamate in, 1 K+ out, per cycle. Net movement of 2 positive charges inward per glutamate, so the transporter is electrogenic.
• Primary source, VERIFIED via PubMed record: Zerangue N, Kavanaugh MP (1996), Flux coupling in a neuronal glutamate transporter, Nature 383(6601):634-637, 17 October 1996, DOI 10.1038/383634a0, PMID 8857541, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8857541/ . Human neuronal transporter EAAT3 (also called EAAC1), voltage clamp plus pH-sensitive fluorescent dye, reversal-potential measurements under varied external ionic conditions.
• Thermodynamic consequence stated in the paper: this stoichiometry permits glutamate concentration gradients exceeding 10^6-fold at equilibrium. That is why extracellular glutamate can be held at low micromolar or sub-micromolar levels against a millimolar intracellular pool.
• Confirmed for GLT-1 specifically (Bergles and colleagues, Comparison of Coupled and Uncoupled Currents during Glutamate Uptake by GLT-1 Transporters, J Neurosci 22(23):10153, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/22/23/10153 ). Note the separate, thermodynamically UNCOUPLED chloride conductance of EAATs: EAATs also behave as anion channels, and that Cl- flux is NOT part of the transport stoichiometry. Say this explicitly - it is a common error.
• Sodium relevance for the chapter: EAAT uptake is the single largest sodium load imposed on astrocytes by synaptic activity, and it is what links glutamate clearance to the Na+/K+ pump and hence to the functional-imaging signal.

Serotonin - SERT (also SLC6A4):

• 1 Na+ in, 1 Cl- in, 1 serotonin (5-HT) in; 1 K+ out in the same catalytic cycle. Net electroneutral, because 5-HT is a monovalent cation at physiological pH.
• Primary source: Rudnick G, Nelson PJ (1978), using platelet membrane vesicles. Summarised in Serotonin transport in the 21st century, J Gen Physiol 151(11):1248, https://rupress.org/jgp/article/151/11/1248/120419/Serotonin-transport-in-the-21st-centurySerotonin
• DISPUTE to flag: whether the counter-transported ion is strictly K+ (or whether a proton can substitute) and whether the cycle is truly electroneutral is still argued, because SERT also carries a substrate-gated uncoupled cation current. See Unifying concept of serotonin transporter-associated currents, PMID 22072712, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22072712/ , and Handling of intracellular K+ determines voltage dependence of plasmalemmal monoamine transporter function, eLife 2021, https://elifesciences.org/articles/67996 - the latter argues the intracellular K+ step is what makes monoamine transporters voltage-dependent at all.

Dopamine - DAT (SLC6A3):

• 2 Na+ in, 1 Cl- in, 1 dopamine in. Dopamine : Na+ : Cl- = 1 : 2 : 1. Net electrogenic (2 net positive charges in per dopamine).
• Source: Regulation of Glutamate, GABA and Dopamine Transporter Uptake, Surface Mobility and Expression, Front Cell Neurosci 15:670346 (2021), https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/cellular-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fncel.2021.670346/full and https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8076567/
• DISPUTE: DAT also passes an uncoupled, channel-like current far larger than the fixed stoichiometry predicts, so measured charge per dopamine exceeds 2 - the coupled stoichiometry and the measured charge movement are different quantities. Say so.

Noradrenaline / norepinephrine - NET (SLC6A2):

• 1 Na+ in, 1 Cl- in, 1 norepinephrine in - 1 : 1 : 1.
• Primary source: Gu HH, Wall S, Rudnick G (1996), Ion coupling stoichiometry for the norepinephrine transporter in membrane vesicles from stably transfected cells, J Biol Chem, https://www.researchgate.net/publication/14564679_Ion_Coupling_Stoichiometry_for_the_Norepinephrine_Transporter_in_Membrane_Vesicles_from_Stably_Transfected_Cells
• DISAGREEMENT to state: NET and DAT are close structural relatives (both SLC6, both Na+/Cl--dependent) yet the reported Na+ stoichiometry differs - 1 for NET, 2 for DAT. Several reviews note the apparent stoichiometries "seem to be different" and that values vary with method and conditions. Recent cryo-electron-microscopy structural work on human NET (Nature 2024, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-024-07638-z ) resolves two sodium sites (Na1 and Na2) in the SLC6 fold, which sits awkwardly with a 1 Na+ functional stoichiometry. What would settle it: reversal-potential or thermodynamic-equilibrium measurements on NET of the kind Zerangue and Kavanaugh did for EAAT3, plus site-occupancy measurements - the structural and flux evidence have not been reconciled.

GABA - GAT-1 (SLC6A1):

• Na+ : Cl- : GABA = 2 : 1 : 1. Net electrogenic, 1 net positive charge in per GABA.
• Source: A comparative review on the well-studied GAT1 and the understudied BGT-1 in the brain, Front Physiol 14:1145973 (2023), https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/physiology/articles/10.3389/fphys.2023.1145973/full and https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10137170/
• DISPUTE, explicitly stated in that review: some authors propose a Cl-/Cl- exchange mechanism for GAT1, which would make the NET stoichiometry 2 Na+ : 1 GABA with chloride merely exchanged rather than net-transported. Both readings are in the literature; the 2:1:1 form is the majority position.

Cross-cutting point for the chapter: every one of these transporters is powered by the inward sodium electrochemical gradient, which is itself maintained by the Na+/K+-ATPase. So the sodium gradient is not merely the substrate of the action potential - it is the cell's general-purpose secondary energy currency, and glutamate uptake alone can concentrate a substrate a millionfold on the strength of it.


CONFIDENCE SUMMARY

• High confidence, directly fetched: Howarth 2012 percentages (verbatim abstract), Zerangue &amp; Kavanaugh 1996 stoichiometry and citation details (PubMed record), Na+ diffusion coefficient 1.330 x 10^-9 m2/s, 3:2:1 pump stoichiometry, Alle/Roth/Geiger 1.3 versus 4 factor, [Na+]i of 13 mM and the 25 mM stimulation transient in dentate granule cells.
• Medium confidence, from search-result text rather than fetched full text: Attwell &amp; Laughlin 2001 percentages (47/34/13/3), the cerebellar 53 percent mossy-fibre figure, squid 440/50 mM concentrations.
• Low confidence, explicitly not settled: neuronal pump density per square micrometre (only a derived ~865 per square micrometre from a 34 nm spacing), pump turnover number at physiological voltage and 37 C, NET sodium stoichiometry, GAT-1 chloride handling.</result>

<result>CALCIUM AND MAGNESIUM AS ELEMENTS - WEB-VERIFIED FINDINGS (all sources accessed 2026-08-12)

SECTION 1 - IONIC RADII (Shannon 1976)

• Shannon, R.D. (1976), "Revised effective ionic radii and systematic studies of interatomic distances in halides and chalcogenides", Acta Crystallographica A32, 751-767. Verified values: Ca2+ effective ionic radius = 1.00 Angstrom at coordination number (CN) 6; Mg2+ = 0.72 Angstrom at CN 6. Ratio Ca/Mg = 1.39; Ca2+ occupies roughly 2.7x the volume of Mg2+.

• Caution for the book: Shannon radii are CN-dependent, so the CN must always be stated. Ca2+ radii rise with CN (roughly 1.06 A at CN 7, 1.12 A at CN 8, 1.18 A at CN 9 in the standard tables), and Mg2+ is about 0.57 A at CN 4 and 0.89 A at CN 8. I could NOT open a clean full copy of the Shannon table in this session (the two PDF mirrors returned corrupted text and WebElements returned 403/500), so ONLY the two CN 6 values above should be quoted as verified. What would settle the rest: the original Acta Cryst A32 table, or the WebElements per-CN pages.

• Source URLs: https://pxrd.ucoz.ru/Radios_ionicos_de_Shannon_5291.pdf (Shannon 1976 reprint), https://winter.group.shef.ac.uk/webelements/periodicity/ionic_radius/

SECTION 2 - COORDINATION NUMBER AND GEOMETRY

• Mg2+ is essentially always six-coordinate and octahedral. Ca2+ is flexible and irregular. Verified statement (Springer, "Magnesium Binding Sites in Proteins", Encyclopedia of Metalloproteins): "The usual coordination number for magnesium is six (octahedral coordination). Calcium demonstrates a lot more variety of coordination numbers, seven to nine being the most ordinary coordination numbers." https://link.springer.com/rwe/10.1007/978-1-4614-1533-6_257

• Metal-oxygen distances, same source: Mg-O = 2.0 to 2.1 Angstrom; Ca-O = 2.3 to 2.6 Angstrom. This is corroborated by the review "System Approach for Building of Calcium-Binding Sites in Proteins", https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7226230/

• Katz, Glusker, Beebe &amp; Bock (1996), "Calcium Ion Coordination: A Comparison with That of Beryllium, Magnesium, and Zinc", Journal of the American Chemical Society 118, 5752-5763. Surveyed the Cambridge Structural Database plus the Protein Data Bank plus ab initio calculations. Ca2+ preferred CN 6-8 in small-molecule structures, with 7-9 common in protein sites. Key contrast verified: in protein crystal structures calcium binds on average 1.5 water molecules per metal site versus 2.2 for magnesium; and calcium can chelate BOTH oxygens of a carboxylate (bidentate) whereas magnesium binds only one (monodentate). https://pubs.acs.org/doi/10.1021/ja953943i

• Dudev, T. &amp; Lim, C. (2003), "Principles governing Mg, Ca, and Zn binding and selectivity in proteins", Chemical Reviews 103, 773-788; and Dudev &amp; Lim (2014), "Competition among metal ions for protein binding sites: determinants of metal ion selectivity in proteins", Chemical Reviews 114, 538-556, https://pubs.acs.org/doi/10.1021/cr4004665 . Also Dudev &amp; Lim, "Monodentate versus Bidentate Carboxylate Binding in Magnesium and Calcium Proteins", J. Phys. Chem. B 2004, https://pubs.acs.org/doi/10.1021/jp0310347 - this is the primary source for the monodentate (Mg) versus bidentate (Ca) carboxylate rule.

• Harding, M.M. (2006), "Small revisions to predicted distances around metal sites in proteins", Acta Cryst. D62, 678-682, https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1107/S0907444906014594 . Verified scope: PDB structures at resolution &lt;= 1.25 A plus Cambridge Structural Database, covering Na, Mg, K, Ca, Mn, Fe, Co, Cu, Zn against O(water), O(Asp/Glu), O(main-chain carbonyl), N(His), S(Cys); the paper reports "a large improvement for Mg-Ocarboxylate". DISCLOSURE: the full text was paywalled (HTTP 402) in this session, so the specific Harding target distances (commonly quoted as Mg-O about 2.07 A and Ca-O about 2.36-2.42 A) are NOT verified here. Use the Springer/PMC ranges above instead, or obtain Harding 2006 Table 1 to settle it.

SECTION 3 - WATER EXCHANGE FROM THE INNER HYDRATION SHELL (the load-bearing contrast)

• Primary reference: Helm, L. &amp; Merbach, A.E. (2005), "Inorganic and Bioinorganic Solvent Exchange Mechanisms", Chemical Reviews 105, 1923-1959. https://www.researchgate.net/publication/7801575_Inorganic_and_Bioinorganic_Solvent_Exchange_Mechanisms

• Verified contrast, as reported in secondary literature citing Helm &amp; Merbach 2005: water exchange rate constant k_ex is about 10^5 per second for Mg2+ and about 10^8 to 10^9 per second for Ca2+ - a difference of three to four orders of magnitude. Equivalently, first-shell water residence time is about 10^-6 second for Mg2+ versus about 10^-9 second for Ca2+, the difference arising from the much higher charge density of Mg2+.

• Independent corroboration in log form: "the rate of water molecule exchange in the inner hydration sphere by ligands is greater for Ca ions (log K = 8.4) than for Mg ions (log K = 5.2)", i.e. k_ex about 2.5 x 10^8 per second for Ca2+ and about 1.6 x 10^5 per second for Mg2+.

• Mechanistic corroboration from molecular dynamics: Mg(H2O)6 2+ keeps a rigid octahedron and requires collective rearrangement of the whole shell to exchange a water, whereas Ca2+ has a flexible shell with several shallow free-energy minima. See "Kinetic pathways of water exchange in the first hydration shell of magnesium", J. Chem. Phys. 152, 224106 (2020) and 155, 084503 (2021), https://pubs.aip.org/aip/jcp/article/152/22/224106/1018095/ and https://opus.bibliothek.uni-augsburg.de/opus4/frontdoor/deliver/index/docId/94189/file/5.0060896.pdf ; also Allner, Nilsson &amp; Villa, "Magnesium Ion-Water Coordination and Exchange in Biomolecular Simulations", J. Chem. Theory Comput. 2012, https://pubs.acs.org/doi/10.1021/ct3000734

• HONEST CAVEAT: the exact Helm &amp; Merbach table entries (frequently given as k_ex(298 K) = 6.7 x 10^5 s^-1 for Mg2+, and about 10^8-10^9 s^-1 for Ca2+, the latter itself an estimate because Ca2+ exchange is too fast for reliable 17O NMR) could NOT be read directly - Chemical Reviews and ACS/AIP full texts returned HTTP 403 in this session. The Ca2+ number is genuinely less well established than the Mg2+ number: Mg2+ has a directly measured 17O NMR value; Ca2+ is at or beyond the NMR time-window limit and is usually quoted as an order-of-magnitude estimate or from simulation. What would settle it: Table 1 of Helm &amp; Merbach, Chem. Rev. 105 (2005) 1923, and the older Eigen (1963) ultrasonic-relaxation compilation. State it in the book as "about 10^5 s^-1 (Mg2+) versus about 10^8-10^9 s^-1 (Ca2+)" rather than as single exact figures.

SECTION 4 - WHY CALCIUM, NOT MAGNESIUM, BECAME THE INTRACELLULAR MESSENGER

• Carafoli, E. (2002), "Calcium signaling: a tale for all seasons", PNAS 99(3), 1115-1122, doi 10.1073/pnas.032427999. https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.032427999 and https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11830654/ . Verified argument: calcium's unusual coordination chemistry lets complex molecules bind it easily even in a large excess of competing cations such as magnesium, so free intracellular calcium can be held at the very low levels a signalling function demands. Carafoli also argues calcium's ubiquity reflects its early availability plus its relatively LOW concentration in the primordial sea, whereas magnesium was too common everywhere to serve as a signal.

• Carafoli cites Williams, R.J.P. (1999), "Calcium as a cellular regulator", in Carafoli &amp; Klee (eds), Oxford University Press, New York, pp. 3-27. https://www.scirp.org/reference/ReferencesPapers.aspx?ReferenceID=270383

• Carafoli, E. &amp; Krebs, J. (2016), "Why Calcium? How Calcium Became the Best Communicator", Journal of Biological Chemistry 291, 20849-20857. https://www.jbc.org/article/S0021-9258(20)35866-X/fulltext and https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27462077/ (full text returned 403 in this session; cite by title/abstract only unless re-fetched).

• Carafoli, E. (2005), "Calcium - a universal carrier of biological signals", FEBS Journal 272, 1073-1077. https://febs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1111/j.1742-4658.2005.04546.x

• Clapham, D.E. (2007), "Calcium Signaling", Cell 131(6), 1047-1058. https://www.cell.com/fulltext/S0092-8674(07)01531-0 , https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0092867407015310 , https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18083096/ . Verified numbers: cells maintain roughly a 20,000-fold gradient between extracellular Ca2+ (about 2 mM, i.e. millimolar) and free cytosolic Ca2+ (about 100 nM). NOTE: the Cell full text returned 403 in this session; the 20,000-fold / ~100 nM / ~2 mM figures were confirmed through search-indexed quotations of the review and through secondary reviews, not by direct fetch. The commonly used resting range is 50-100 nM cytosolic free Ca2+ versus 1-2 mM extracellular, giving 10,000-20,000-fold; sources differ mainly in whether they quote 50 nM or 100 nM as resting, and 1 mM (free ionised plasma) or 2 mM (total plasma) as external, which is exactly why the fold-factor is quoted as a range.

• Magnesium contrast (Romani &amp; Scarpa 1992; Wolf et al. 2003, as compiled in "Intracellular magnesium homeostasis", in Magnesium in the Central Nervous System, NCBI Bookshelf, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK507258/ ): total cellular Mg is 17-20 mM in the majority of mammalian cell types examined; free cytosolic Mg2+ is held at 0.5-1 mM, i.e. LESS THAN 5 PERCENT of total cellular magnesium content. So magnesium's free-to-total ratio is roughly 1:20-1:40, while calcium's is on the order of 1:10,000 or more - magnesium simply cannot be driven low enough, fast enough, to encode a signal.

• Independent measurement of free cytosolic Mg2+: 0.59 mM in isolated rat hepatocytes using the fluorescent indicator FURAPTRA (Kd for Mg2+ = 1.5 mM). Raju et al., "A fluorescent indicator for measuring cytosolic free magnesium", Am. J. Physiol. Cell Physiol. 256, C540 (1989), https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2923192/

SECTION 5 - Mg-ATP

• Nearly all cellular ATP is Mg-ATP. Verified figure: 84 percent (plus or minus 4 percent) of total ATP is complexed to magnesium - BioNumbers BNID 101887, from Gupta, Benovic et al. (1978), J. Biol. Chem. 253(17), 6165-6171. https://bionumbers.hms.harvard.edu/bionumber.aspx?id=101887

• A second, higher figure of about 88 percent, with cytosolic free ATP measured at 54 plus or minus 6 micromolar and MgATP at 400 plus or minus 50 micromolar (free/Mg-complexed ratio 0.135), comes from Gout, Rebeille, Douce &amp; Bligny (2014), "Interplay of Mg2+, ADP, and ATP in the cytosol and mitochondria: unravelling the role of Mg2+ in cell respiration", PNAS 111, E4560. https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1406251111 . Safe statement for the book: 84-88 percent, i.e. "the great majority".

• Binding constant - SOURCES DISAGREE, state this explicitly. Reported apparent dissociation constants for MgATP: 35 plus or minus 3 micromolar (Gout et al. 2014, PNAS, by 31P NMR); about 50 plus or minus 10 micromolar (Gupta et al., 31P NMR plus optical absorbance, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6607050/ ); about 78 micromolar (value adopted in the NCBI Bookshelf magnesium-homeostasis chapter, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK507258/ ); about 86 micromolar by a more recent fitting method at different ionic strength. The spread is real and is driven by ionic strength, pH and temperature. Association constant Ka = 1/Kd therefore falls in the range roughly 1.2 x 10^4 to 2.9 x 10^4 M^-1. BioNumbers BNID 101768 gives Ka = 12 mM^-1 = 1.2 x 10^4 M^-1 at ionic strength 0.15 and 37 degrees C (Berger et al. 1973, Eur. J. Biochem. 38, 553), https://bionumbers.hms.harvard.edu/bionumber.aspx?id=101768 . So the "about 10^4 M^-1" figure in the brief is correct as an order of magnitude; quote it as 1-3 x 10^4 M^-1 (Kd 35-90 micromolar) and name the ionic-strength dependence.

• Magnitude of the ATP magnesium pool: about 4-5 mM of cytoplasmic Mg2+ is present as Mg-ATP (NBK507258), which is the single largest metabolic magnesium pool in cytoplasm and mitochondrial matrix.

SECTION 6 - NMDA RECEPTOR Mg2+ BLOCK

• The two 1984 discovery papers, both verified: (a) Mayer, M.L., Westbrook, G.L. &amp; Guthrie, P.B. (1984), "Voltage-dependent block by Mg2+ of NMDA responses in spinal cord neurones", Nature 309, 261-263, https://www.nature.com/articles/309261a0 , https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6325946/ - mouse spinal cord neurones under voltage clamp; the voltage-sensitivity of NMDA action is greatly reduced on withdrawal of physiological Mg2+ (approximately 1 mM) from the extracellular fluid, showing the receptor is not itself voltage-sensitive but is blocked by a voltage-sensitive channel blocker. (b) Nowak, L., Bregestovski, P., Ascher, P., Herbet, A. &amp; Prochiantz, A. (1984), "Magnesium gates glutamate-activated channels in mouse central neurones", Nature 307, 462-465, https://www.nature.com/articles/307462a0 - above resting potential the current induced by a given dose of glutamate increases on depolarisation, and the NMDA response is greatly potentiated by reducing extracellular Mg2+ below the physiological level (approximately 1 mM).

• Quantitative IC50 versus voltage - verified from Wrighton, Baker, Makarewicz &amp; Mermelstein / Huang &amp; Gibb lineage paper: Clarke &amp; Johnson-style analysis in "Mg2+ block properties of triheteromeric GluN1-GluN2B-GluN2D NMDA receptors on neonatal rat substantia nigra pars compacta dopaminergic neurones", J. Physiol. (2014), https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4027860/ . GluN1-GluN2B (diheteromeric, control): IC50 = 20.9 micromolar at -90 mV, 53.3 micromolar at -70 mV, 173.4 micromolar at -50 mV, 1031 micromolar at -30 mV. GluN1-GluN2B-GluN2D (triheteromeric, ifenprodil condition): 45.9 micromolar at -90 mV, 103.7 micromolar at -70 mV, 275.7 micromolar at -50 mV, 913.3 micromolar at -30 mV. Voltage-dependence parameter: delta = 0.77 plus or minus 0.01 in control (GluN2B-containing), significantly greater than delta = 0.56 plus or minus 0.01 in the ifenprodil (GluN2D-containing) condition. The paper also estimated background Mg2+ in acute slices at 27.9 plus or minus 1.5 micromolar.

• So the brief's "delta about 0.8" is well supported for conventional GluN2A/GluN2B receptors (0.77-0.8), but state explicitly that delta is SUBUNIT-DEPENDENT: GluN2C/GluN2D-containing receptors show markedly weaker voltage dependence (delta about 0.56) and higher IC50 at every potential. Also cite Jahr &amp; Stevens (1990a,b, J. Neurosci.) for the classic fast block/unblock kinetics and Ascher &amp; Nowak (1988, J. Physiol.) for the equilibrium sequential-block treatment; and Kampa, Clements, Jonas &amp; Stuart (2004) / Vargas-Caballero &amp; Robinson (2004), "Fast and slow voltage-dependent dynamics of magnesium block in the NMDA receptor: the asymmetric trapping block model", J. Neurosci. 24, 6171, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/24/27/6171 (full text 403 in this session; cite by title).

• Extracellular and CSF magnesium - SOURCES DISAGREE, and the disagreement is mostly total versus ionised. Verified measured values: CSF ionised Mg2+ = 0.80 plus or minus 0.10 mmol/L versus serum ionised Mg2+ = 0.63 plus or minus 0.07 mmol/L; total CSF Mg = 1.25 plus or minus 0.14 mmol/L versus serum total Mg = 0.92 plus or minus 0.18 mmol/L; CSF:serum total ratio 1.37. A second study gives plasma and CSF ionised Mg2+ of 0.58 plus or minus 0.05 and 0.82 plus or minus 0.06 mmol/L. Sources: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11801809/ , https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15310301/ , https://academic.oup.com/bja/article/109/2/208/253573 , https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12129886/ . The NCBI Bookshelf chapter quotes plasma/extracellular-fluid Mg at 1.2-1.4 mM total, about one third of which is bound to extracellular protein (mainly albumin) - which reconciles with a free/ionised plasma figure near 0.6-0.9 mM. RECOMMENDED BOOK WORDING: CSF total Mg about 1.1-1.25 mM and CSF ionised Mg about 0.8 mM, both HIGHER than the corresponding plasma values (plasma total about 0.9-1.4 mM, plasma ionised about 0.6-0.9 mM); always say whether the figure is total or ionised. The frequently cited "1 mM extracellular Mg2+" used by Mayer 1984 and Nowak 1984 is a round physiological approximation, not a measured CSF ionised value.

SECTION 7 - CALMODULIN Ca2+ BINDING

• Architecture: calmodulin has four EF-hand Ca2+-binding sites, two per globular lobe, the lobes joined by a flexible central linker. Each lobe binds Ca2+ with low-micromolar affinity, with positive cooperativity WITHIN each lobe.

• Primary quantitative source: Linse, S., Helmersson, A. &amp; Forsen, S. (1991), "Calcium binding to calmodulin and its globular domains", J. Biol. Chem. 266(13), 8050-8054. https://www.jbc.org/article/S0021-9258(18)92938-8/fulltext , https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1902469/ . Verified method and conclusions: macroscopic Ca2+-binding constants of bovine calmodulin determined by titration in the presence of the chromophoric chelator 5,5'-Br2BAPTA at 0, 10, 25, 50, 100 and 150 mM KCl, with the same experiments repeated on the N-terminal and C-terminal tryptic fragments. Conclusions: the separated globular domains retain the Ca2+-binding properties they have in the intact molecule; the C-terminal domain has 6-fold higher Ca2+ affinity than the N-terminal domain; positive cooperativity of Ca2+ binding is observed within each globular domain at ALL ionic strengths.

• Macroscopic stepwise binding constants attributed to Linse 1991 and used in modelling work: {K1, K2, K3, K4} = {7.94 x 10^4, 3.98 x 10^6, 2.51 x 10^4, 3.98 x 10^5} M^-1. Inverting these gives apparent Kd values of roughly 12.6 micromolar and 0.25 micromolar for the high-affinity (C-lobe) pair and roughly 40 micromolar and 2.5 micromolar for the low-affinity (N-lobe) pair. DISCLOSURE: I could not open the JBC full text (403); these constants come from a secondary modelling paper quoting Linse 1991, so verify against the original table before printing exact digits.

• Independent modelling compilation (Stefan/Le Novere lineage): "Modulation of Calmodulin Lobes by Different Targets: An Allosteric Model with Hemiconcerted Conformational Transitions", PLOS Computational Biology 11, e1004063 (2015), https://journals.plos.org/ploscompbiol/article?id=10.1371/journal.pcbi.1004063 - verified statement: "The C-lobe has a 10-fold higher affinity, but much slower kinetics, than the N-lobe."

• Kinetics: fast Ca2+ off-rate from the N-lobe (greater than 500 per second) and slow off-rate from the C-lobe (about 10 per second); the N-lobe binds faster with lower affinity, the C-lobe slower with higher affinity. See Faas, G.C., Raghavachari, S., Lisman, J.E. &amp; Mody, I. (2011), "Calmodulin as a direct detector of Ca2+ signals", Nature Neuroscience 14, 301-304, https://www.nature.com/articles/nn.2746 (paywalled; kinetics figures above taken from open reviews citing it).

• Structural-systems analysis giving explicit domain constants: Faas-independent fitting in "Elucidating the mechanisms of cooperative calcium-calmodulin interactions: a structural systems biology approach", https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2435525/ - N-terminal domain K1 = 0.34 micromolar, C-terminal domain K2 = 0.36 micromolar, with separate cooperativity constants; and the explicit finding that raising pH raises Ca2+ affinity and eliminates cooperativity between sites. Cites Linse 1991, Minowa &amp; Yagi 1984, Iida &amp; Potter 1986, Peersen et al. 1997.

• EXPLICIT DISAGREEMENT STATEMENT for the book: published calmodulin Ca2+ Kd values span roughly 0.3 to 40 micromolar depending on ionic strength, pH, temperature, whether macroscopic or site-specific constants are reported, and above all whether a target peptide is present. Verified from eLife 2023 (SK-channel study, https://elifesciences.org/articles/81303 ): "Both proton concentration and ionic strength alter the affinity of CaM for Ca2+" (citing Linse et al. 1991; Ogawa &amp; Tanokura 1984; Steiner et al. 1983), and CaM's dynamic range of Ca2+ sensitivity varies with which target it is bound to (citing Black et al. 2005; Newman et al. 2008; Persechini et al. 2000; Westerlund &amp; Delemotte 2018). When CaM with two Ca2+ bound to one lobe associates with a target, the remaining lobe's Ca2+ affinity rises, often by at least an order of magnitude - strong positive cooperativity. Different targets move affinity in OPPOSITE directions: CaMKII, calcineurin (PP2B) and skMLCK markedly INCREASE CaM's Ca2+ affinity, while the NaV1.2 sodium-channel peptide DECREASES it (PLOS Comput. Biol. 2015).

• NOT WELL ESTABLISHED: a single canonical pair of numbers "C-lobe about 10^-6 M, N-lobe about 10^-5 M" is a defensible order-of-magnitude summary (the 6-fold to 10-fold C-over-N ratio is robust across sources), but no single Kd pair is universal. Halling, Liebeskind, Hall &amp; Aldrich (2016), "Conserved properties of individual Ca2+-binding sites in calmodulin", PNAS 113, E1216-E1225, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4780646/ , goes further and warns that "the methods used to measure parameters from standard binding curves do not have uniquely identifiable solutions" and that "Ca2+ binding should not be assumed to be equal at all four sites". What would settle it: site-specific (not macroscopic) constants measured under one stated ionic strength, pH and temperature, apo and target-bound, e.g. the ITC study Thermodynamics of Calcium binding to the Calmodulin N-terminal domain, JBIC 2015, https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00775-015-1275-1

SECTION 8 - ITEMS I COULD NOT VERIFY DIRECTLY (paywall/403), flagged so the book does not overstate

• Helm &amp; Merbach Chem. Rev. 2005 Table 1 exact k_ex entries (ACS 403). Verified only via secondary quotation.

• Clapham 2007 Cell full text (Cell/ScienceDirect 403; the PDF mirror decoded as binary). The ~100 nM / ~2 mM / 20,000-fold figures were confirmed only through indexed quotation.

• Carafoli 2002 PNAS full text (PNAS 403). Abstract-level argument verified; no in-paper numeric table extracted.

• Carafoli &amp; Krebs 2016 JBC full text (403).

• Harding 2006 Acta Cryst D full text (402 Payment Required).

• Shannon 1976 per-CN table beyond CN 6 (PDF mirrors corrupted, WebElements 403/500).

• Linse et al. 1991 JBC full text (403); the stepwise constants are second-hand.
