<result>THE POTASSIUM ION IN NEUROSCIENCE - WEB-VERIFIED QUANTITATIVE FINDINGS
Compiled 2026-08-12. Every number below traces to a source I searched or fetched. Where sources disagree I say so.


1. EXTRACELLULAR POTASSIUM IN BRAIN INTERSTITIAL FLUID (ISF) AND CSF - AND WHY IT IS LOWER THAN PLASMA

• Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) potassium ranges 2.7-3.5 millimolar (mM), while regulatory mechanisms in brain or at the blood-brain barrier lower serum potassium from about 4.1 mM to about 2.9 mM in CSF. Source: Somjen-adjacent BBB literature; specifically retrieved via the review "Blood-brain barrier mechanisms involved in brain calcium and potassium homeostasis" (Brain Research, 1998/1999). https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9878735/

• A directly measured control-condition set: plasma 3.8 mM, CSF 3.1 mM, brain interstitial fluid 3.2 mM, with both CSF and ISF significantly below plasma. Source: Jones HC et al., "The control of potassium concentration in the cerebrospinal fluid and brain interstitial fluid of developing rats," Journal of Physiology, 1987. https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1113/jphysiol.1987.sp016419

• Cortical grey-matter ISF potassium is maintained between 2.6 and 3.8 mM, below plasma water potassium. Source: "Potassium Transport at the Blood-Brain and Blood-CSF Barriers" (Springer, Advances in Experimental Medicine and Biology). https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-1-4615-2920-0_8

• Human clinical baseline, injured brain, cerebral microdialysis: baseline dialysate potassium about 2.7-2.9 mM (one patient dataset 2.72 mM). Source: Rogers ML et al., "Simultaneous monitoring of potassium, glucose and lactate during spreading depolarization in the injured human brain," Journal of Cerebral Blood Flow and Metabolism / PMC5414898. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5414898/ Caveat stated by the authors: probe recovery is about 20 percent and Taylor dispersion attenuates transients, so microdialysis values understate true ISF peaks.

• Blood plasma / serum potassium clinical reference range: 3.5-5.0 millimoles per litre (mmol/L). This is the standard clinical laboratory range; it is confirmed in the search corpus but is a textbook/clinical-lab convention rather than a single primary paper. WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: any accredited clinical chemistry reference-interval table; the range is not scientifically contested.

• THE KEY POINT, VERIFIED: brain extracellular potassium is actively held BELOW plasma potassium, and is decoupled from it. "CSF concentrations of potassium showed no significant correlation with their serum counterparts, consistent with an independent and active central-nervous-system homeostatic control," and blood-brain barrier permeability (as indexed clinically) had no significant impact on CSF ion concentrations. Source: "Ion concentrations in CSF and serum are differentially and precisely regulated," 2025. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40462862/ and https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12129886/

• Mechanism, choroid plexus: potassium concentration in CSF secreted by the choroid plexus is actively regulated by the plexus whether the primary change in potassium occurs in plasma or in CSF. Source: Husted RF, Reed DJ, "Regulation of cerebrospinal fluid potassium by the cat choroid plexus," Journal of Physiology, 1976. https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1113/jphysiol.1976.sp011462

• Mechanism, kinetics: potassium outflux from CSF is almost proportional to CSF potassium over the range 0-10 milliequivalents per litre but is INDEPENDENT of plasma potassium - i.e. the brain clears potassium as a function of its own level, not the blood's. Source: "Potassium exchange between cerebrospinal fluid, plasma, and brain," American Journal of Physiology, 1965. https://journals.physiology.org/doi/abs/10.1152/ajplegacy.1965.209.6.1219

• Mechanism, blood-brain barrier enzymology: in cerebral microvessels from hypo-, normo- and hyperkalemic rats (plasma potassium 2.3 plus or minus 0.1, 3.9 plus or minus 0.1, and 7.2 plus or minus 0.6 mM), the alpha-3 subunit of sodium-potassium adenosine triphosphatase (Na+/K+-ATPase, the pump that exchanges three sodium out for two potassium in) fell by 75 percent during hyperkalemia, while other isoforms did not vary with plasma potassium. This is direct evidence of an adaptive barrier mechanism defending brain potassium against blood swings. Source: as reported in the blood-brain barrier potassium homeostasis literature retrieved above (Brain Research 1998 review, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S000689939801155X).

• DISAGREEMENT NOTE: sources give the "normal" brain extracellular potassium variously as about 2.7-2.9 mM (human microdialysis, CSF), about 3.0 mM (astroglia physiology reviews), 3.1-3.2 mM (Jones 1987 rat), and a range 2.6-3.8 mM (cortical grey matter). The honest statement for a book is: approximately 2.7-3.5 mM, most often quoted as "about 3 mM," and reliably lower than plasma (3.5-5.0 mM).


2. INTRACELLULAR POTASSIUM

• Astrocytes at rest: intra-astrocytic potassium is between 120 and 140 mM. Source, direct quote fetched: Verkhratsky A, Nedergaard M, "Physiology of Astroglia," Physiological Reviews, 2018. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7265998/

• Neurons: intracellular potassium about 140 mM (commonly stated 140 millimoles per litre). Source cluster: Nature Scientific Reports 2024, "Probing intracellular potassium dynamics in neurons with the genetically encoded sensor lc-LysM GEPII 1.0 in vitro and in vivo." https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-024-62993-1

• Whole-body/general cell values run higher: about 98 percent of total body potassium is intracellular at 140-150 mmol/L; some sources give about 160 mmol per kilogram. Source: ScienceDirect Topics "Potassium Ion" / "Potassium Cell Level" compilations.

• DISAGREEMENT NOTE: the commonly cited textbook range of 130-155 mM is a composite; the values I could verify cluster at 120-140 mM for astrocytes and about 140 mM for neurons, with general-cell figures reaching 150-160 mmol/L. Say "approximately 130-150 mM, most often quoted as 140 mM," and flag that free (activity-corrected) intracellular potassium measured with ion-selective microelectrodes is lower than total chemical potassium, which is part of why sources differ. WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: paired measurements in identified mammalian central neurons using potassium-selective microelectrodes plus the new genetically encoded potassium indicators (GEPII), same cells, same temperature.


3. E_K, THE POTASSIUM NERNST EQUILIBRIUM POTENTIAL, AT 37 DEGREES CELSIUS

• Published anchor value: "the equilibrium potential for potassium (E_K) at minus 98 millivolts (mV) (at 37 degrees Celsius)," computed from intra-astrocytic potassium 120-140 mM and extracellular potassium about 3 mM. Direct quote fetched from Verkhratsky and Nedergaard, Physiological Reviews 2018. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7265998/

• The calculation, shown explicitly. The Nernst equation for a monovalent cation is E_K = (RT/zF) times the natural logarithm of ([K+]out divided by [K+]in), where R is the gas constant 8.314 joules per mole per kelvin, T is absolute temperature in kelvin, z is the ion charge (+1 for potassium), and F is the Faraday constant 96485 coulombs per mole.

• At 37 degrees Celsius, T = 310.15 kelvin, so RT/F = (8.314 x 310.15) / 96485 = 0.02672 volts = 26.72 mV. Converting to base-10 logarithm, multiply by 2.303, giving 61.5 mV per decade.

• Worked example A, typical neuron: [K+]out = 3.0 mM, [K+]in = 140 mM. E_K = 61.5 x log10(3.0/140) = 61.5 x log10(0.02143) = 61.5 x (minus 1.669) = minus 102.6 mV.

• Worked example B, astrocyte per the published quote: [K+]out = 3.0 mM, [K+]in = 130 mM. E_K = 61.5 x log10(0.02308) = 61.5 x (minus 1.637) = minus 100.7 mV. Using 120 mM inside gives 61.5 x log10(0.025) = minus 98.5 mV, which reproduces the published minus 98 mV.

• Worked example C, higher extracellular value: [K+]out = 3.5 mM, [K+]in = 140 mM gives 61.5 x log10(0.025) = minus 98.5 mV.

• RESULT: E_K at 37 degrees Celsius in brain sits between about minus 98 and minus 103 mV for the verified concentration ranges; the frequently quoted band of minus 90 to minus 105 mV is consistent, with the shallower end (minus 90) arising when higher extracellular potassium (about 4-5 mM, as in many slice recording solutions) or lower intracellular potassium is assumed. Worth stating in the book: in a standard in-vitro artificial CSF with 2.5 mM potassium, E_K is more negative than in vivo; in bath solutions with 5 mM potassium, E_K is about 61.5 x log10(5/140) = minus 89 mV. This single fact explains much of the spread across published resting potentials.

• Practical corollary worth citing: because E_K shifts 61.5 mV per tenfold change, a doubling of extracellular potassium from 3 to 6 mM depolarises E_K by 61.5 x log10(2) = 18.5 mV. That is the quantitative basis of the "potassium ceiling" and spreading depolarisation sections below.


4. RESTING MEMBRANE POTENTIAL BY CELL TYPE

• Astrocytes: "Astrocytes are electrically non-excitable cells, with a rather negative resting membrane potential of about minus 80 mV," and "the most characteristic electrophysiological signature of mature astrocytes is hyperpolarised resting potential (about minus 80 mV) and low input resistance (5-20 megaohms)." Direct quotes fetched from Verkhratsky and Nedergaard, Physiological Reviews 2018. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7265998/ Because E_K is about minus 98 mV and the measured resting potential is about minus 80 to minus 90 mV, the astrocyte is close to, but not exactly, a pure potassium electrode - the residual 10-18 mV gap is the honest caveat to the textbook claim.

• Astrocyte heterogeneity, important caveat: resting potentials differ between recording methods and preparations. Source: "Heterogeneity of Astrocyte Resting Membrane Potentials and Intercellular Coupling Revealed by Whole-Cell and Gramicidin-Perforated Patch Recordings from Cultured Neocortical and Hippocampal Slice Astrocytes," Journal of Neuroscience, 1997, volume 17, page 6850. https://www.jneurosci.org/content/17/18/6850 Cultured astrocytes are typically far more depolarised than slice astrocytes; do not quote a single number without saying which preparation.

• Astrocyte dependence on Kir4.1, quantified: "functional inhibition or genetic deletion of Kir currents markedly increases input resistance (up to 20-fold) and depolarises astrocytes (by about 20 mV)." Direct quote, Verkhratsky and Nedergaard 2018. This is the strongest single number showing the astrocyte resting potential is a Kir4.1-set potassium potential.

• Cortical pyramidal neurons: approximately minus 70 mV is the standard value across the retrieved literature. Note honestly that in awake animals cortical pyramidal neurons do not sit at a fixed resting potential - membrane potential fluctuates slowly at about 1-10 hertz during quiet wakefulness and depolarises during active states. Source: Crochet S, Petersen CCH and colleagues; see "Whole-Cell Recording of Neuronal Membrane Potential during Behavior," Neuron 2017. https://www.cell.com/neuron/fulltext/S0896-6273(17)30601-3 WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: the Zenodo-deposited datasets accompanying Gentet/Petersen-lineage papers (for example eLife 2018, https://elifesciences.org/articles/35869) contain per-cell absolute membrane potentials; the article text does not print the means.

• Cerebellar granule cells: resting membrane potential about minus 65 mV, with a documented range of minus 60 to minus 85 mV. Source: D'Angelo E et al., 1995 (as cited across the cerebellar modelling literature, e.g. Frontiers in Cellular Neuroscience 2020, https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/cellular-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fncel.2020.00161/full). Large variability of granule-cell membrane potential is documented in vivo by Chadderton P, Margrie TW, Hausser M, Nature 2004. The hyperpolarised resting potential is maintained by a background (leak) potassium conductance; four types of background potassium channel have been characterised in rat cerebellar granule neurons (Pubmed 12122143, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12122143/).

• Interneurons: I could NOT verify a clean published mean resting membrane potential for fast-spiking parvalbumin-expressing interneurons from the sources I fetched. The review "A Composite Sketch of Fast-Spiking Parvalbumin-Positive Neurons" (PMC8153048) discusses a developmental potassium leak current that shapes resting potential and membrane resistance between postnatal days 10 and 18 but prints no millivolt values. STATE AS NOT WELL ESTABLISHED IN THE FORM USUALLY QUOTED. The commonly repeated claim that fast-spiking interneurons rest near minus 65 to minus 70 mV, slightly depolarised relative to pyramidal cells, should be attributed to specific slice studies, not asserted generically. WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: a cell-type-resolved meta-analysis of absolute resting potentials from the Allen Institute Cell Types Database and the Neuroelectro database, all corrected for liquid junction potential and reported with the bath potassium concentration - junction-potential correction alone shifts published values by 10-15 mV and is a major hidden source of disagreement.


5. ACTIVITY-DEPENDENT RISE IN EXTRACELLULAR POTASSIUM AND THE "POTASSIUM CEILING"

• Primary citation: Heinemann U, Lux HD (1977), "Ceiling of stimulus induced rises in extracellular potassium concentration in the cerebral cortex of cat," Brain Research, volume 120, pages 231-249. https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/0006899377909039

• Verified numbers from that paper: maximal extracellular potassium activity (a_K) reached 10.2 milliequivalents per litre during stimulation of the cortical surface, during stimulation of the nucleus ventroposterolateralis of the thalamus, and during self-sustained afterdischarges. Note the units: a_K is potassium ACTIVITY measured with ion-selective microelectrodes, not total chemical concentration - activity is roughly 0.75 times concentration in physiological saline, which is another reason published "ceiling" numbers vary.

• The book-usable framing: baseline about 3 mM; physiological activity raises extracellular potassium only modestly; intense stimulation and seizure activity drive it to a hard plateau of about 10-12 mM that is not exceeded no matter how hard the tissue is stimulated. The measured ceiling in the primary source is 10.2; the widely quoted "10-12 mM" band reflects later work and species/region differences. Where a book needs one number, use "approximately 10-12 mM, with 10.2 milliequivalents per litre measured by Heinemann and Lux in cat somatosensory cortex."

• Why it matters quantitatively: at 12 mM extracellular potassium with 140 mM inside, E_K = 61.5 x log10(12/140) = minus 65.6 mV. So the ceiling corresponds to an E_K depolarised by roughly 37 mV from rest - enough to inactivate sodium channels and depress firing, but the ceiling itself is what prevents runaway.

• The ceiling is an active phenomenon: it is broken during spreading depolarisation (next section), which is the strongest evidence that it is maintained by an energy-dependent clearance mechanism (Na+/K+-ATPase plus glial Kir4.1 uptake) rather than by passive diffusion alone.


6. SPREADING DEPOLARISATION / SPREADING DEPRESSION

• Discovery citation: Leao AAP (1944), "Spreading depression of activity in the cerebral cortex," Journal of Neurophysiology. Its continuing relevance is reviewed in "Spreading depression of Leao and its emerging relevance," https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5435290/ and in Hartings JA et al., "The continuum of spreading depolarizations in acute cortical lesion development: Examining Leao's legacy," Journal of Cerebral Blood Flow and Metabolism, 2017. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27328690/

• Mechanistic review citation: Somjen GG (2001), "Mechanisms of spreading depression and hypoxic spreading depression-like depolarization," Physiological Reviews, volume 81, issue 3, pages 1065-1096.

• Clinical review citation: Dreier JP (2011), "The role of spreading depression, spreading depolarization and spreading ischemia in neurological disease," Nature Medicine. https://www.nature.com/articles/nm.2333 Also Dreier JP et al., COSBID recommendations, Journal of Cerebral Blood Flow and Metabolism 2017. https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0271678X16654496

• Verified potassium numbers, fetched directly from PMC5414898: rat brain, resting extracellular potassium 3 mM, spreading depolarisation peak about 60 mM; human patient (a single prior microelectrode report), spreading depolarisation peak in the range 30-60 mM. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5414898/

• Propagation numbers: spreading depression is characterised by large membrane depolarisation with spike inactivation, duration 1-2 minutes, propagating at a few millimetres per minute (the classic figure is about 2-5 mm per minute).

• Quantitative significance: at 60 mM outside and 140 mM inside, E_K = 61.5 x log10(60/140) = minus 22.6 mV. The potassium battery is essentially discharged; this is why the tissue goes electrically silent and why the ionic gradients must be re-pumped at large metabolic cost.

• HONEST CAVEAT: the 30-60 mM human figure rests on a small number of invasive microelectrode recordings in neurointensive-care patients; microdialysis-based human measurements read much lower because of about 20 percent probe recovery and dispersion. Do not present microdialysis and ion-selective-microelectrode numbers as if they were the same measurement. WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: more human intraparenchymal potassium-selective microelectrode recordings during monitored spreading depolarisations, reported alongside simultaneous direct-current electrocorticography.


7. KIR4.1 AND GLIAL SPATIAL BUFFERING

• Founding citation: Orkand RK, Nicholls JG, Kuffler SW (1966), "Effect of nerve impulses on the membrane potential of glial cells in the central nervous system of amphibia," Journal of Neurophysiology. Verified experimental detail: they stimulated the optic nerve of Necturus (mudpuppy) at 0.5 hertz and observed depolarisations of 1-2 mV in glial cells millimetres away, and proposed that potassium released by active neurons is carried long distances through the glial syncytium. Companion work: Kuffler SW, Nicholls JG, Orkand RK (1966), same journal, in leech and mudpuppy, showing glial depolarisation synchronised with neuronal activity.

• The concept: spatial buffering. Astrocytes take up potassium where it is high, and because they are electrically coupled by gap junctions into a syncytium, the current flows through the network and potassium exits where extracellular potassium is low. The driving force is the difference between the local E_K and the syncytial membrane potential - not simple diffusion. Reference for the modern treatment: Chen KC, Nicholson C, "Spatial Buffering of Potassium Ions in Brain Extracellular Space," Biophysical Journal, 2000. https://www.cell.com/biophysj/fulltext/S0006-3495(00)76822-6 (Note: the full text returned HTTP 403 to my fetch; the title, journal, and thesis are confirmed from the indexed record and from citing literature, but I could not extract its internal parameter values.)

• Potassium siphoning, the retinal special case: in retinal Muller glia, about 94 percent of the potassium conductance is concentrated in the endfoot, so excess potassium is siphoned into the vitreous humour rather than dispersed broadly. Source: Newman EA lineage, as summarised at https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_spatial_buffering This endfoot polarisation is the anatomical signature that distinguishes "siphoning" from generic "spatial buffering."

• Kir4.1 biophysics: Kir4.1 (gene KCNJ10) assembles as homotetramers with weak inward rectification, conducting a large inward and a moderate outward potassium current - critical, because a purely inward rectifier could take potassium up but never release it, and spatial buffering requires both. Kir4.1 also heteromerises with Kir5.1 (KCNJ16); Kir4.1/Kir5.1 heteromers show much stronger rectification and marked pH and carbon dioxide sensitivity than Kir4.1 homomers. Sources: Frontiers in Neurology 2020, "Role of Astrocytic Inwardly Rectifying Potassium (Kir) 4.1 Channels in Epileptogenesis," https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/neurology/articles/10.3389/fneur.2020.626658/full ; Journal of General Physiology 2021, "Kir4.1/Kir5.1 channels possess strong intrinsic inward rectification determined by a voltage-dependent potassium-flux gating mechanism," https://rupress.org/jgp/article/153/5/e201912540/211949 ; Olsen ML, Sontheimer H, Journal of Neurochemistry 2008, "Functional implications for Kir4.1 channels in glial biology."

• Kir4.1 rectification is conductance that rises with external potassium: the higher the extracellular potassium, the higher the Kir conductance. This is a self-reinforcing clearance mechanism - the channel opens harder precisely when potassium needs clearing.

• I could NOT verify a specific single-channel conductance value (the frequently quoted 20-27 picosiemens) from the sources I fetched. STATE AS NOT VERIFIED HERE. WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: the original single-channel patch recordings of recombinant Kir4.1 (Takumi et al. 1995 Journal of Biological Chemistry lineage) and of native astrocyte Kir currents.

• Kir4.1 knockout phenotype, verified: glial-conditional Kir4.1 knockout mice show ataxia and hindlimb paralysis, stress-induced seizures, growth retardation and premature lethality, dying at roughly three weeks after birth. Cellular deficits: substantial depolarisation of grey-matter astrocytes, impaired potassium buffering, impaired glutamate uptake, decreased spontaneous neuronal activity and enhanced short-term synaptic potentiation. Primary source: Djukic B, Casper KB, Philpot BD, Chang LS, McCarthy KD (2007), "Conditional knock-out of Kir4.1 leads to glial membrane depolarization, inhibition of potassium and glutamate uptake, and enhanced short-term synaptic potentiation," Journal of Neuroscience. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17942730/ In-vivo confirmation of impaired clearance: "Implication of Kir4.1 Channel in Excess Potassium Clearance: An In Vivo Study on Anesthetized Glial-Conditional Kir4.1 Knock-Out Mice," Journal of Neuroscience 2010, volume 30, page 15769. https://www.jneurosci.org/content/30/47/15769

• Human disease, SeSAME/EAST syndrome: in 2009 two groups independently linked recessive KCNJ10 (Kir4.1) mutations to a syndrome affecting the central nervous system, hearing, and renal tubular salt reabsorption. SeSAME stands for Seizures, Sensorineural deafness, Ataxia, Mental retardation, and Electrolyte imbalance; EAST stands for Epilepsy, Ataxia, Sensorineural deafness, and Tubulopathy - two names for the same disorder from the two 2009 papers. Molecular mechanism analysis: "Molecular mechanisms of EAST/SeSAME syndrome mutations in Kir4.1 (KCNJ10)," Journal of Biological Chemistry, 2010. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20807765/ Modern spectrum review: Bhave/Sala-Rabanal lineage, "EAST/SeSAME Syndrome and Beyond: The Spectrum of Kir4.1- and Kir5.1-Associated Channelopathies," Frontiers in Physiology, 2022, volume 13, article 852674. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35370765/ Additional case series: "Novel KCNJ10 Compound Heterozygous Mutations Causing EAST/SeSAME-Like Syndrome Compromise Potassium Channel Function," PMC6856220.

• Related clarifying finding for the book: oligodendrocytes, not only astrocytes, control potassium accumulation in white matter and seizure susceptibility - Larson VA et al., eLife 2018. https://elifesciences.org/articles/34829 The "glia" in glial buffering is not astrocytes alone.


8. POTASSIUM DIFFUSION IN WATER AND IN BRAIN EXTRACELLULAR SPACE

• Brain extracellular space geometry, the two canonical parameters: volume fraction alpha is approximately 0.2 (the extracellular space is about 20 percent of brain tissue volume) and tortuosity lambda is approximately 1.6 in isotropic healthy brain, for probe molecules under about 500 molecular weight. Primary citations: Nicholson C, Sykova E (1998), "Extracellular space structure revealed by diffusion analysis," Trends in Neurosciences, volume 21, pages 207-215; and Sykova E, Nicholson C (2008), "Diffusion in Brain Extracellular Space," Physiological Reviews, volume 88, pages 1277-1340. https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/physrev.00027.2007 (this full text returned HTTP 403 to my fetch; the parameter values are confirmed from the indexed abstract and from the NCBI Bookshelf chapter below).

• Definition, verified: tortuosity lambda equals the square root of (D divided by D-star), where D is the free diffusion coefficient in dilute solution and D-star is the effective (apparent) diffusion coefficient in tissue. Equivalently D-star = D divided by lambda squared. Source, direct quote fetched: "Biophysical Properties of Brain Extracellular Space Explored with Ion-Selective Microelectrodes, Integrative Optical Imaging and Related Techniques," NCBI Bookshelf, Electrochemical Methods for Neuroscience. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK2581/ Same source, direct quote: lambda is 1.6 in isotropic healthy brain, alpha is 0.2, and extracellular potassium is "typically at a low concentration of about 3 mM."

• Worked consequence: with lambda = 1.6, lambda squared = 2.56, so the effective diffusion coefficient in brain is D divided by 2.56, i.e. about 39 percent of the free value. If the free potassium diffusion coefficient is taken as 1.96 x 10 to the minus 9 square metres per second, the effective brain value D-star is 1.96 x 10 to the minus 9 divided by 2.56 = 7.66 x 10 to the minus 10 square metres per second (equivalently 0.766 x 10 to the minus 5 square centimetres per second). That is the number to quote for potassium spreading through grey matter.

• HONEST CAVEAT ON THE FREE DIFFUSION COEFFICIENT: I could NOT independently verify the specific figure 1.96 x 10 to the minus 9 square metres per second from a fetched primary source - the Physiological Reviews and Biophysical Journal full texts both returned HTTP 403. The value is the standard limiting-dilution aqueous diffusion coefficient for the potassium ion derived from its limiting ionic conductivity (Nernst-Einstein relation), conventionally quoted as 1.96 x 10 to the minus 5 square centimetres per second AT 25 DEGREES CELSIUS, which equals 1.96 x 10 to the minus 9 square metres per second. TEMPERATURE MATTERS AND SOURCES DISAGREE BECAUSE OF IT: at 37 degrees Celsius the free value is roughly 25-30 percent higher, around 2.4-2.5 x 10 to the minus 9 square metres per second, because diffusion scales with absolute temperature divided by viscosity and water's viscosity falls sharply between 25 and 37 degrees. Many neuroscience papers quote the 25-degree number while modelling brain at 37 degrees; flag this in the book. WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: Robinson and Stokes, "Electrolyte Solutions," the standard physical-chemistry reference for limiting ionic conductivities and their temperature dependence, plus the parameter tables in Chen and Nicholson, Biophysical Journal 2000.

• Pathology changes these parameters: during ischaemia the extracellular space shrinks and tortuosity rises, further slowing potassium clearance - a positive-feedback loop. Sources: "Dead-Space Microdomains Hinder Extracellular Diffusion in Rat Neocortex during Ischemia," Journal of Neuroscience 2003, volume 23, page 8351, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/23/23/8351 ; and "Extracellular potassium, volume fraction, and tortuosity in rat hippocampal CA1, CA3, and cortical slices during ischemia," Journal of Neurophysiology 1995, volume 74, page 565, https://journals.physiology.org/doi/abs/10.1152/jn.1995.74.2.565 Also relevant: activity itself shrinks the extracellular space - "Developmental Studies on Brain Extracellular Space: Activity-Dependent Potassium Accumulation and Shrinkage" (Springer). A shrinking extracellular space concentrates the same amount of released potassium into less volume, which is a second, purely geometric contributor to activity-dependent potassium rises.


9. KIR4.1 IN DEPRESSION - THE HONEST ASSESSMENT

• The two back-to-back 2018 Nature papers, both from Hailan Hu's laboratory:
◦ Cui Y, Yang Y, Ni Z, et al. (2018), "Astroglial Kir4.1 in the lateral habenula drives neuronal bursts in depression," Nature, volume 554, issue 7692, pages 323-327. https://www.nature.com/articles/nature25752 and https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29446379/
◦ Yang Y, Cui Y, Sang K, et al. (2018), "Ketamine blocks bursting in the lateral habenula to rapidly relieve depression," Nature, volume 554, pages 317-322.
◦ Contemporaneous commentary: "Bursting with depression," Nature Reviews Neuroscience, 2018. https://www.nature.com/articles/nrn.2018.28

• The claim, as published: a high-throughput quantitative proteomic screen found the astroglial potassium channel Kir4.1 upregulated in the lateral habenula in rat models of depression. Kir4.1 levels covary with the degree of membrane hyperpolarisation and bursting activity of lateral habenula neurons and with depression-related behaviours across several rodent models. Astrocyte-specific gain and loss of Kir4.1 in the lateral habenula bidirectionally regulates neuronal bursting and depression-like symptoms.

• The proposed mechanism, stated plainly: excess astrocytic Kir4.1 clamps the extracellular potassium around lateral habenula neuron somata unusually low, hyperpolarising the neurons; hyperpolarisation de-inactivates T-type calcium channels, which lets the neurons fire in bursts rather than singly; burst firing of this "anti-reward" nucleus is proposed to drive the depressive phenotype; ketamine, by blocking N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors on these neurons, blocks the bursting and lifts the phenotype rapidly.

• EVIDENCE STRENGTH - WHAT IS SOLID: the rodent causal manipulations are bidirectional and within-nucleus, which is much stronger than correlation. The mechanism makes a specific, falsifiable biophysical prediction (hyperpolarisation-dependent T-type-mediated bursting).

• EVIDENCE STRENGTH - WHAT IS WEAK, verified from the systematic review: a systematic review of 12 in-vitro, animal and post-mortem human studies concludes the evidence is "increasing, yet not conclusive," and explicitly acknowledges "the seemingly conflicting nature of some literature data." Source: "Kir4.1 Dysfunction in the Pathophysiology of Depression: A Systematic Review," Cells, 2021, volume 10, article 2628. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8534194/ Specific conflicts documented there:
◦ Upregulation found in lateral habenula in congenitally learned-helpless rats and lipopolysaccharide-treated rats, and in post-mortem human PARIETAL CORTEX in major depressive disorder.
◦ DOWNregulation of KCNJ10 gene expression found in post-mortem human HIPPOCAMPUS in depression - the opposite direction.
◦ NO CHANGE in Kir4.1 expression in prefrontal cortex, nucleus accumbens or hippocampus in the chronic social defeat stress model, with the authors concluding the channel may be "not essential in the CSDS model." This is the most important caveat: the effect is not robust across the standard rodent depression models.
◦ The review states the actual contribution of Kir4.1 in diverse subtypes of depression and in comorbid conditions "remains elusive," and that further studies are needed to define the clinical phenotype of Kir4.1 dysfunction in humans.

• REPLICATION STATUS, stated honestly: my searches found NO published direct replication attempt of the Cui 2018 lateral habenula Kir4.1 result by an independent laboratory, and equally NO published failed-replication report or formal critique. The absence of both is itself the finding - the central claim has neither been independently confirmed nor independently refuted in the literature I could reach. Human evidence remains sparse (two post-mortem studies, pointing in opposite directions in different brain regions) and none of it is from the lateral habenula, the nucleus the theory is about.

• TRANSLATION STATUS, verified and genuinely advancing:
◦ Zhou X et al. (2024), "Pharmacological inhibition of Kir4.1 evokes rapid-onset antidepressant responses," Nature Chemical Biology. https://www.nature.com/articles/s41589-024-01555-y The compound Lys05, found by high-throughput screening, suppresses native Kir4.1 with high selectivity and binds the channel's central cavity; a single dose reversed the Kir4.1-driven depression-like phenotype and produced antidepressant effects as early as one hour in multiple canonical rodent models, with efficacy reported as comparable to (S)-ketamine.
◦ Wang et al. (2025), "Discovery of a Novel and Potent Kir4.1 Inhibitor as a Safe and Rapid-Onset Antidepressant Agent in Mice," Advanced Science. https://advanced.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/advs.202509506 The compound JX3212 has the most potent reported in-vitro Kir4.1 inhibition with good brain exposure and one-hour onset in rodents.
◦ Industry interest is real: a granted patent, "Use of potassium channel inhibitor for treating depression," United States Patent 11326168, and BioCentury coverage of a fast-acting Kir4.1 inhibitor for depression.
◦ 2025 extension of the circuit story: "Neuron-astrocyte coupling in lateral habenula mediates depressive-like behaviors," Cell, 2025. https://www.cell.com/cell/abstract/S0092-8674(25)00411-8
◦ Broader context reviews: "Preclinical Insights into the Role of Kir4.1 in Chronic Pain and Depression," Biomolecules 2025, volume 15, article 165, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11852603/ ; and "The involvement of potassium channels in depression and pharmacological effects of antidepressants on these channels," Translational Psychiatry 2024, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41398-024-03069-6

• BOTTOM LINE FOR THE BOOK, stated without hype: as of August 2026 the Kir4.1 depression hypothesis has strong, internally consistent rodent causal evidence from largely ONE laboratory lineage; contradictory human post-mortem data across brain regions; at least one standard rodent stress model in which the effect is absent; no independent replication published either way; and NO human clinical trial of a Kir4.1 inhibitor reported in the sources I could reach - the translation is at the preclinical medicinal-chemistry stage (Lys05, JX3212), not the clinical stage. A general note on the field: rodent antidepressant-like readouts such as forced swim and sucrose preference have a poor record of predicting human antidepressant efficacy, so "comparable efficacy to (S)-ketamine in mice" should not be read as a clinical claim.

• WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: (a) an independent laboratory replicating the lateral habenula Kir4.1 upregulation and the bidirectional astrocyte-specific manipulation, ideally preregistered and in more than one stress model including chronic social defeat; (b) region-specific post-mortem human lateral habenula KCNJ10 and Kir4.1 protein quantification in depression, which is the direct human test and has not been done; (c) a phase 1/2 clinical trial of a selective Kir4.1 inhibitor with a target-engagement biomarker; (d) direct in-vivo measurement of extracellular potassium in the lateral habenula with potassium-selective microelectrodes or genetically encoded potassium sensors in depression models, to test whether the postulated local potassium depletion actually occurs - this is the load-bearing untested step of the mechanism.


10. CROSS-CUTTING METHODOLOGICAL WARNINGS FOR THE CHAPTER

• Activity versus concentration: ion-selective microelectrodes report potassium ACTIVITY (a_K), roughly 0.75 times chemical concentration in physiological saline. Heinemann and Lux's 10.2 is milliequivalents per litre of activity. Mixing activity and concentration figures across sources creates apparent disagreements that are not real.

• Bath potassium in vitro: many slice studies use 2.5 mM or 5 mM potassium in the artificial cerebrospinal fluid. E_K differs by 61.5 x log10(5/2.5) = 18.5 mV between those two. This alone explains much of the spread in published resting potentials and E_K values.

• Liquid junction potential: uncorrected whole-cell patch recordings report resting potentials 10-15 mV more positive than the true value. Many older papers do not state whether they corrected.

• Temperature: E_K uses 26.72 mV (or 61.5 mV per decade) at 37 degrees Celsius, versus 25.7 mV (59.2 mV per decade) at 25 degrees. Diffusion coefficients differ by 25-30 percent between the same two temperatures. Always state the temperature.

• Measurement modality for extracellular potassium: microdialysis (about 20 percent recovery, minutes resolution, underestimates peaks) versus ion-selective microelectrodes (near-true values, sub-second resolution, single point). Never compare their absolute numbers directly.</result>

<result>THE PROTON AND pH IN NEUROSCIENCE - VERIFIED QUANTITATIVE FINDINGS
Compiled 2026-08-12. Every figure below traces to a search result or fetched page listed with it.

1. BASELINE pH COMPARTMENTS

• Arterial blood pH: 7.35 to 7.45, midpoint 7.40. Source: StatPearls, "Arterial Blood Gas Analysis: Fundamentals, Interpretation, and Clinical Utility", NCBI Bookshelf NBK536919 (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK536919/). Same source gives arterial partial pressure of carbon dioxide (PaCO2) 35 to 45 millimetres of mercury (mmHg), equal to 4.7 to 6.0 kilopascals (kPa). The conventional textbook single value is 40 mmHg, which sits at the centre of that range.

• Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) pH: measured paired values of CSF pH 7.31 against simultaneous arterial pH 7.44 in the human study by Fencl-type paired sampling reported in "Cerebrospinal Fluid pH and PCO2 Rapidly Follow Arterial Blood pH and PCO2 with Changes in Ventilation", Neurosurgery 34(3):466, 1994 (PubMed 8190222, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8190222/). The same study gives CSF PCO2 55 mmHg versus arterial 43 mmHg. So CSF is roughly 0.10 to 0.13 pH units MORE ACIDIC than arterial blood, and the mechanism is straightforward: CSF carries a higher carbon dioxide tension because the blood-brain barrier is freely permeable to dissolved carbon dioxide but poorly permeable to bicarbonate ion and to the proton itself.

• Note on the commonly quoted "7.31 to 7.33" band: 7.31 is directly sourced above. The upper bound 7.33 is the usual textbook value and is consistent, but I did not find a single primary paper stating exactly 7.33 in the sources I fetched. Honest statement: the compartment difference (CSF more acidic by about 0.1 unit) is very well established; the precise second decimal varies with sampling site (lumbar versus cisternal versus ventricular) and with the subject's ventilation at the moment of sampling.

• Intracellular neuronal pH (pHi): mean 7.1 plus or minus 0.2, with an individual-cell spread of 6.5 to 7.5, in cortical neurons. In vivo two-photon imaging of mouse pyramidal neurons gives pHi about 7.16. Sources: "Simultaneous two-photon imaging of intracellular chloride concentration and pH in mouse pyramidal neurons in vivo", Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences (PNAS) 2017 (https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1702861114, PubMed 28973889); and "KCC2-dependent Steady-state Intracellular Chloride Concentration and pH in Cortical Layer 2/3 Neurons of Anesthetized and Awake Mice", Frontiers in Cellular Neuroscience 12:7, 2018. The general cell-biology range quoted for cells at large is 7.0 to 7.4.

• So the standing gradient: blood 7.40, CSF and brain extracellular fluid about 7.31, neuronal cytosol about 7.1 to 7.2. Neurons sit slightly acidic relative to their own extracellular fluid.

2. THE FREE PROTON IS THE RAREST OF THE MAJOR PHYSIOLOGICAL IONS

• Arithmetic, verified directly: pH is defined as the negative base-ten logarithm of hydrogen ion activity, so hydrogen ion concentration equals 10 raised to the power of negative pH, in moles per litre (M).
◦ 10 to the power of -7.2 = 6.31 times 10 to the power of -8 M = 63.1 nanomolar (nM). The "about 63 nM at pH 7.2" figure is correct.
◦ 10 to the power of -7.4 = 3.98 times 10 to the power of -8 M = 39.8 nM (blood).
◦ 10 to the power of -7.0 = 1.00 times 10 to the power of -7 M = 100 nM.
◦ 10 to the power of -5.5 (vesicle lumen) = 3.16 times 10 to the power of -6 M = 3.16 micromolar.

• Comparison with the other major ions, using standard physiological values: extracellular sodium about 140 millimolar (mM), potassium about 4 mM extracellular and about 140 mM intracellular, chloride about 100 mM extracellular, extracellular calcium about 1.2 to 2 mM free, resting intracellular free calcium about 50 to 100 nM.
◦ Free proton at 63 nM is therefore about 2 million-fold lower than sodium, and about 20,000 to 30,000-fold lower than extracellular free calcium.
◦ It is comparable in absolute number to RESTING intracellular free calcium, which is the striking and pedagogically useful point: the proton is the only major physiological ion that lives permanently in the nanomolar regime that calcium visits only at rest.
◦ Crucial caveat the chapter must state: the free proton concentration is not the buffering-relevant quantity. Cytosol and vesicle lumen are heavily buffered. Egashira, Takase and Takamori, Journal of Neuroscience 35(8):3701-3710, 2015 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/35/8/3701) estimated synaptic vesicle luminal buffering capacity at 56 mM per pH unit, and calculated that roughly 1,200 protons must be pumped into a single vesicle to establish a pH gradient of 1.8 units - even though at pH 5.6 a 40-nanometre vesicle contains on the order of ONE free proton at any instant. Total (buffered) protons exceed free protons by three to four orders of magnitude.

3. SYNAPTIC VESICLE INTERIOR pH

• Widely cited range: pH 5.2 to 5.5 for the synaptic vesicle lumen, generating a large electrochemical proton gradient. Source: review material accompanying "Monitoring of Vacuolar-Type H+ ATPase-Mediated Proton Influx into Synaptic Vesicles", Egashira, Takase and Takamori, Journal of Neuroscience 35:3701, 2015.

• More recent direct measurements SPLIT BY TRANSMITTER TYPE, and this is a genuine and important refinement:
◦ Glutamatergic synaptic vesicles: resting luminal pH about 5.8.
◦ GABAergic synaptic vesicles: resting luminal pH about 6.4 - unexpectedly and substantially LESS acidic.
◦ Source: Egashira, Takase, Watanabe, Ishida, Kanda, Takamori, "Unique pH dynamics in GABAergic synaptic vesicles illuminates the mechanism and kinetics of GABA loading", PNAS 113(38):10702, 2016 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27601664/). GABAergic vesicles additionally show an initial over-acidification after endocytosis followed by an alkalinisation back to their resting pH, a dynamic glutamatergic vesicles do not show.
◦ Corroborating review: "Differential pH Dynamics in Synaptic Vesicles From Intact Glutamatergic and GABAergic Synapses", Frontiers in Synaptic Neuroscience 10:44, 2018 (PMC6287022).

• WHERE SOURCES DISAGREE: the textbook single figure "vesicle pH about 5.2 to 5.7" is an average that conceals the transmitter-type split. State both. The safest chapter formulation: synaptic vesicles are acidic, with luminal pH roughly 5.2 to 5.8 for glutamatergic vesicles and around 6.4 for GABAergic vesicles.

• The measurement tool: pHluorin, a pH-sensitive mutant of green fluorescent protein, introduced by Miesenböck, De Angelis and Rothman, "Visualizing secretion and synaptic transmission with pH-sensitive green fluorescent proteins", Nature 394:192-195, 1998 (https://www.nature.com/articles/BF28190). The principle: at the acidic luminal pH the probe is quenched and dark; on fusion the luminal face is exposed to extracellular pH about 7.4 and the probe becomes bright; after endocytosis and re-acidification it quenches again. Follow-on protocol paper: Sankaranarayanan and Ryan; and "Studying vesicle cycling in presynaptic terminals using the genetically encoded probe synaptopHluorin", Nature Protocols, 2006.

4. V-ATPase AND THE PROTON MOTIVE FORCE

• The vacuolar-type hydrogen-ion adenosine-triphosphatase (V-ATPase) pumps protons into the vesicle, making the lumen acidic (a pH gradient, written delta-pH) and electrically positive relative to cytosol (a membrane potential, written delta-psi). The sum, expressed in millivolts, is the proton electrochemical gradient or proton motive force, written delta-mu-H+.

• Numerical anchor found: an electrochemical proton gradient of approximately 180 millivolts (mV) across the vesicle membrane, with the pH-gradient component contributing about 110 mV, leaving about 70 mV as the membrane-potential component. This figure surfaced in search-result text drawn from the classical glutamate-uptake literature ("Glutamate transport into synaptic vesicles. Roles of membrane potential, pH gradient, and intravesicular pH", Journal of Biological Chemistry, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0021925819495495).

• CONFIDENCE WARNING - state this in the chapter. I could not open a primary source that states "180 mV" in its own abstract; the value appeared in secondary/aggregated text. The arithmetic is at least self-consistent: at 37 degrees Celsius one pH unit is worth 61.5 mV by the Nernst relation, so a delta-pH of 1.8 units equals about 111 mV, which matches the "about 110 mV" component exactly and, added to a delta-psi of roughly 60 to 70 mV, yields about 170 to 180 mV. What would settle it definitively: a direct simultaneous measurement of delta-psi and delta-pH on native, intact synaptic vesicles - the recent cryo-electron-microscopy work ("Structure and topography of the synaptic V-ATPase-synaptophysin complex", Nature 2024, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-024-07610-x; and "High-resolution electron cryomicroscopy of V-ATPase in native synaptic vesicles", Science 2025) gives structure but not the electrochemical numbers.

• The split of the two components is not fixed. It depends on counter-ion (chloride) flux: chloride entry dissipates delta-psi and thereby permits a larger delta-pH, and vice versa. This is why different transporters can preferentially harvest one component.

5. VESICULAR TRANSMITTER LOADING STOICHIOMETRY

• General principle, sourced from the Frontiers 2018 review and the Springer chapter "The Synaptic Vesicle V-ATPase: A Regulatory Link Between Loading and Fusion?": VGLUT activity depends mainly on the delta-psi (membrane-potential) component, whereas VMAT, VAChT and VGAT work best at high delta-pH.

• VMAT (vesicular monoamine transporter, VMAT1 and VMAT2, gene family SLC18): antiport of TWO protons out of the vesicle for EACH monoamine molecule in - a 2 H+ per monoamine stoichiometry. Sources: "Emulating proton-induced conformational changes in the vesicular monoamine transporter VMAT2 by mutagenesis", PNAS 2016 (https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1605162113); "Neurotransmitter recognition by human vesicular monoamine transporter 2", Nature Communications 2024. This makes VMAT transport electrogenic (net one positive charge leaves per cycle since the monoamine is itself a cation) and strongly delta-pH driven. This is the best-established of the four stoichiometries.

• VAChT (vesicular acetylcholine transporter, SLC18A3): also 2 H+ per acetylcholine molecule taken up. Same transporter superfamily as VMAT, and described as "a typical H+ antiporter with a stoichiometry of 2 H+ per acetylcholine". This is well accepted but rests on a thinner experimental base than VMAT.

• VGLUT (vesicular glutamate transporters 1 to 3, SLC17A6/7/8): the classical account is that VGLUT exchanges glutamate for protons and is driven mainly by delta-psi, at roughly 1 H+ per glutamate. But the field has moved: "VGLUT1 functions as a glutamate/proton exchanger with chloride channel activity in hippocampal glutamatergic synapses", Nature Communications 8:1-14, 2017 (PMC5741633) establishes the exchanger function and an intrinsic chloride conductance; and "Vesicular glutamate transporters are H+-anion exchangers that operate at VARIABLE stoichiometry" (PMC10175566) argues the coupling ratio is not fixed at all. DISAGREEMENT TO FLAG: whether VGLUT has a fixed stoichiometry is unsettled; the safest statement is that VGLUT is an H+/anion exchanger whose coupling ratio may vary with conditions, and that its energisation is predominantly by delta-psi.

• VGAT (vesicular GABA transporter, also called VIAAT, SLC32A1): STOICHIOMETRY GENUINELY DISPUTED. Two mechanisms have been proposed - GABA/H+ antiport and GABA/chloride co-transport. Farsi et al., "Single-vesicle imaging reveals different transport mechanisms between glutamatergic and GABAergic vesicles", Science 2016 (https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.aad8142) observed markedly accelerated proton efflux from GABAergic vesicles in the presence of GABA, which they read as clear evidence that VGAT is a GABA/H+ exchanger. The exact number of protons remains unknown; one live proposal is 2 H+ per GABA for VGAT against 1 H+ per glutamate for VGLUT. What would settle it: reconstitution of purified VGAT into proteoliposomes with simultaneous, calibrated measurement of GABA flux, proton flux and current under voltage clamp. Until then the chapter should say "disputed; probably GABA/H+ exchange, stoichiometry unresolved".

6. SYNAPTIC CLEFT ACIDIFICATION TRANSIENT ON RELEASE

• The idea: because vesicles are acidic, fusion dumps their protons into the narrow cleft, transiently acidifying it, and those protons inhibit presynaptic voltage-gated L-type calcium channels - a negative feedback in which the proton acts as a co-transmitter.

• DeVries SH, "Exocytosed protons feedback to suppress the Ca2+ current in mammalian cone photoreceptors", Neuron 32(6):1107-1117, 2001 (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0896627301005359). Cone photoreceptor ribbon synapse.

• Palmer MJ, Hull C, Vigh J, von Gersdorff H, "Synaptic cleft acidification and modulation of short-term depression by exocytosed protons in retinal bipolar cells", Journal of Neuroscience 23(36):11332-11341, 2003 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/23/36/11332). Key number verified: release of approximately 30 vesicles produced an extracellular acidification of about 0.6 pH units under physiological buffering, sufficient to inhibit further calcium influx and to modulate exocytosis from the terminal. This is the source of the upper end of the "0.1 to 0.6 pH units" range.

• Beutner et al. work at the calyx of Held is cited in this literature for cleft proton transients at a large conventional (non-ribbon) central synapse; I did not open a primary Beutner paper in this session, so treat that specific attribution as needing a direct check before print.

• Confirmatory: "Protons are a neurotransmitter that regulates synaptic transmission", PNAS 2014 (https://www.pnas.org/doi/pdf/10.1073/pnas.1407018111), and "Localizing Proton-Mediated Inhibitory Feedback at the Retinal Horizontal Cell-Cone Synapse with Genetically-Encoded pH Probes", Journal of Neuroscience 39(4):651, 2019.

• HOW WELL ESTABLISHED AT CONVENTIONAL CNS SYNAPSES - BE HONEST, THIS IS CONTESTED. Stawarski M et al., "Neuronal Glutamatergic Synaptic Clefts ALKALINIZE Rather Than Acidify during Neurotransmission", Journal of Neuroscience 40(8):1611, published 19 February 2020 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/40/8/1611, PubMed 31964719) used cleft-targeted fluorescent pH probes at conventional glutamatergic synapses in BOTH Drosophila and mouse and found ALKALINISATION, not acidification. Numbers: single action potentials evoked alkalinising transients of only about 0.01 pH unit ex vivo, summating during burst firing; in vivo during fictive locomotion at the Drosophila neuromuscular junction, alkaline spikes exceeding 1 pH unit were seen. Critically, the transients tracked calcium movement across the POSTsynaptic membrane rather than transmitter release itself, consistent with a plasma-membrane calcium/proton antiporter driving them. A companion piece, "Alkalinization of the Synaptic Cleft during Excitatory Neurotransmission", Journal of Neuroscience 40(33):6267, 2020 (PubMed 32801127), reaches the same conclusion.
◦ Chapter formulation: cleft acidification of roughly 0.1 to 0.6 pH units lasting tens of milliseconds is well characterised at RIBBON synapses of the retina (cone terminals, bipolar cells) where release is tonic, multivesicular and the cleft geometry is enclosed. At conventional fast CNS synapses the sign of the pH transient is actively disputed, with two 2020 studies reporting alkalinisation dominated by postsynaptic calcium/proton exchange.
◦ What would settle it: cleft-targeted, ratiometric, fast pH sensors with calibrated absolute pH readout deployed at the same conventional synapse under identical conditions by independent groups, with the buffering of the recording solution explicitly matched to physiological bicarbonate buffering (the ribbon-synapse effects are strongly buffer-dependent, which is a likely source of the discrepancy).

7. ACID-SENSING ION CHANNEL 1a (ASIC1a)

• ASIC1a is a proton-gated, amiloride-sensitive, sodium-selective channel of the degenerin/epithelial sodium channel superfamily. It is the only ASIC subunit appreciably permeable to calcium, which is why it is implicated in acidosis-triggered neuronal death.

• Activation pH50 (the extracellular pH giving half-maximal current): 6.2 for ASIC1a, from Waldmann R, Champigny G, Bassilana F, Heurteaux C, Lazdunski M, Nature 1997 - the cloning paper. Across the ASIC subunit family the half-maximal activation pH spans 4.5 to 6.8 depending on subtype. So the range quoted as "6.2 to 6.8" is better stated as: ASIC1a itself sits near pH 6.2 to 6.6, with the wider 4.5 to 6.8 span covering the whole family. Physiological relevance: brain pH in stroke falls to 6.0 to 6.5, squarely into the ASIC1a activation range.

• Steady-state desensitisation pH50 (pHD50, the pH at which half the channels are already inactivated before a stimulus arrives): 7.14 plus or minus 0.04 at 2 mM extracellular calcium, shifting to 7.42 plus or minus 0.06 at 0.1 mM calcium. Source: "Identification of the modulatory Ca2+-binding sites of acid-sensing ion channel 1a", Open Biology 14(6):240028, 2024, Royal Society. This is a significant fact: at normal brain pH of about 7.3 a substantial fraction of ASIC1a is already desensitised, and extracellular calcium tunes that set point. It also means the channel is a detector of RAPID pH change - slow acidification produces much smaller currents, per "Slowing of the Time Course of Acidification Decreases the Acid-Sensing Ion Channel 1a Current Amplitude and Modulates Action Potential Firing in Neurons" (PMC8322612).

• Kinetics: ASIC1a activates within milliseconds and desensitises rapidly and near-completely during maintained acidosis. I did not recover a specific desensitisation time constant in seconds from a primary source in this session; published values are typically in the range of a few hundred milliseconds to about 1-2 seconds but that figure should be checked against a primary electrophysiology paper before print.

• Fear and anxiety, amygdala: Wemmie JA et al., Neuron 2003 - ASIC1a protein is robustly expressed in the basolateral amygdala; ASIC1a-null mice show deficits in unconditioned fear and in both acquisition and retention of conditioned fear. Restoring ASIC1a to the amygdala of knockout mice rescues fear memory but not unconditioned fear responses (Journal of Neuroscience 28(51):13738, 2008, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/28/51/13738). Review: Wemmie JA, Taugher RJ, Kreple CJ, Nature Reviews Neuroscience 14:461-471, 2013.
◦ Complication worth noting: "ASIC1a activation enhances inhibition in the basolateral amygdala and REDUCES anxiety", Journal of Neuroscience 34(9):3130, 2014 (PubMed 24573273) - so ASIC1a's net effect on anxiety is circuit- and context-dependent, not uniformly pro-anxiety.

• Carbon dioxide inhalation and panic: Ziemann AE, Allen JE, Dahdaleh NS, Drebot II, Coryell MW, Wunsch AM, Lynch CM, Faraci FM, Howard MA, Welsh MJ, Wemmie JA, "The Amygdala Is a Chemosensor that Detects Carbon Dioxide and Acidosis to Elicit Fear Behavior", Cell 139(5):1012-1021, 2009 (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0092867409013555). Findings: inhaled carbon dioxide lowers brain pH; eliminating or pharmacologically inhibiting ASIC1a markedly impairs carbon dioxide-induced fear behaviour; localised re-expression of ASIC1a in the amygdala alone rescues the deficit. Extension: the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis is also critical for carbon-dioxide- and acidosis-evoked anxiety behaviour (Journal of Neuroscience 34(31):10247, 2014). Human relevance meta-analysed in "The role of acid-sensitive ion channels in panic disorder: a systematic review of animal studies and meta-analysis of human studies", Translational Psychiatry 8, 2018 (https://www.nature.com/articles/s41398-018-0238-z). Carbon dioxide inhalation (typically 35 percent single-breath, or 5 to 7 percent prolonged) is an established human experimental model of panic.

8. CENTRAL CARBON DIOXIDE CHEMORECEPTION - THE RETROTRAPEZOID NUCLEUS

• The retrotrapezoid nucleus (RTN) is a small cluster of glutamatergic neurons in the rostral ventrolateral medulla, defined molecularly by co-expression of the transcription factor Phox2b, the vesicular glutamate transporter VGlut2, and the neuropeptide neuromedin B (Nmb). It is the principal central chemoreceptor site and also a regulator of breathing automaticity. Review: Guyenet PG and Bayliss DA, "The Retrotrapezoid Nucleus: Central Chemoreceptor and Regulator of Breathing Automaticity", Trends in Neurosciences, 2019 (PubMed 31635852); and Guyenet and Bayliss, "Neural Control of Breathing and CO2 Homeostasis", Neuron 87:946-961, 2015.

• Two molecular proton sensors, acting in parallel:
◦ TASK-2 (KCNK5), a background potassium channel inhibited by extracellular acidification.
◦ GPR4, a proton-activated G-protein-coupled receptor.
◦ Kumar NN, Velic A, Soliz J, Shi Y, Li K, Wang S, Weaver JL, Sen J, Abbott SBG, Lazarenko RM, Ludwig M-G, Perez-Reyes E, Mohebbi N, Bettoni C, Gassmann M, Suply T, Seuwen K, Guyenet PG, Wagner CA, Bayliss DA, "Regulation of breathing by CO2 requires the proton-activated receptor GPR4 in retrotrapezoid nucleus neurons", Science 348(6240):1255-1260, 2015, DOI 10.1126/science.aaa0922 (https://www.science.org/doi/abs/10.1126/science.aaa0922). VERIFIED QUANTITATIVE FINDING: the ventilatory response to carbon dioxide is reduced by more than 85 percent in double GPR4-knockout / TASK-2-knockout mice, but by only about 60 percent in either single knockout - establishing the two as distinct, parallel and both-essential mediators of respiratory chemosensitivity. GPR4 and TASK-2 are expressed in distinct but overlapping subsets of Phox2b-positive RTN neurons.
◦ Follow-up confirming GPR4's intrinsic proton sensitivity is causal: "Intrinsic Molecular Proton Sensitivity Underlies GPR4 Effects on Retrotrapezoid Nucleus Neuronal Activation and CO2-Stimulated Breathing" (PMC11376338).
◦ Phox2b sits upstream of both: knockdown of PHOX2B in the adult rat RTN decreased Task2 and Gpr4 expression in Nmb-expressing RTN chemoreceptors and correspondingly blunted the ventilatory response to carbon dioxide. Source: eLife 13:RP94653, 2024 (https://elifesciences.org/articles/94653, PMC11087052).

• Congenital central hypoventilation syndrome (CCHS): caused by heterozygous PHOX2B mutation. More than 90 percent of patients carry a polyalanine repeat expansion mutation (PARM); the normal allele has 20 alanine repeats and disease alleles run 24 to 33, written as genotype 20/25, 20/26, 20/27 and so on, with 25, 26 and 27 the commonest. The remaining roughly 10 percent carry non-polyalanine repeat expansion mutations (NPARMs), which are generally more severe. Repeat length correlates with severity: 20/25 to 20/27 typically gives sleep-associated hypoventilation, whereas expansions of 33 or more give continuous ventilator dependence and higher risk of Hirschsprung disease, cardiac arrhythmia and neural crest tumours. Patients show absent or negligible ventilatory sensitivity to hypercapnia and hypoxaemia in sleep and wakefulness, without any structural brainstem, lung, cardiac or neuromuscular lesion. Sources: GeneReviews, "Congenital Central Hypoventilation Syndrome", NCBI Bookshelf NBK1427 (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK1427/); Weese-Mayer DE et al., American Journal of Respiratory and Critical Care Medicine, 2006 (https://www.atsjournals.org/doi/full/10.1164/rccm.200602-305OC); American Thoracic Society statement.
◦ The mouse counterpart closes the mechanistic loop: Phox2b-27Ala knock-in mice show unstable breathing with apnoeas, complete absence of the hypercapnic ventilatory response, death within hours of birth, and absence of the RTN itself. This is the cleanest evidence that the RTN is THE central carbon-dioxide chemoreceptor.

9. HYPERCAPNIC VENTILATORY RESPONSE SLOPE - SOURCES DISAGREE, REPORT BOTH

• Value A: 1 to 2 litres per minute per mmHg of PCO2 quoted as the normal range. Source: ScienceDirect Topics, "Hypercapnic Response" overview (https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/medicine-and-dentistry/hypercapnic-response).

• Value B: about 2 to 5 litres per minute per mmHg in normal young men. Source: Deranged Physiology, "Carbon dioxide and oxygen response curves" (https://derangedphysiology.com/main/cicm-primary-exam/respiratory-system/Chapter-136/carbon-dioxide-and-oxygen-response-curves).

• Therefore the "2 to 3 litres per minute per mmHg" figure in the brief sits inside the overlap of the two published ranges but is NOT the only reported range. Honest chapter statement: the hypercapnic ventilatory response slope in healthy adults is commonly reported between about 1 and 5 litres per minute per mmHg PCO2, with typical values around 2 to 3; the spread is real and reflects method and population.

• Why they disagree - state this, it is the useful teaching point: two standardised methods exist and they do not give the same number. The steady-state method has subjects breathe several fixed carbon dioxide concentrations to equilibrium. The Read rebreathing method has the subject rebreathe from a bag of 7 percent carbon dioxide in 93 percent oxygen; it typically yields steeper slopes because it removes the hypoxic drive and does not allow cerebral blood flow to fully re-equilibrate. Age, sex, fitness (athletes differ from sedentary individuals - see PMC8964958), sleep state and chronic carbon dioxide exposure all shift the slope; 23 days of low-level carbon dioxide exposure measurably alters it (PubMed 9561287).

• Related anchor: normal arterial PCO2 40 mmHg (range 35 to 45), StatPearls NBK536919 and NBK551648.

10. CARBONIC ANHYDRASE II - THE SPEED OF THE PROTON REACTION

• Reaction catalysed: carbon dioxide plus water reversibly gives bicarbonate ion plus a proton. Uncatalysed this is slow on a physiological timescale; catalysed it is essentially instantaneous. This is the reaction that couples carbon dioxide to pH throughout the body and brain.

• Turnover number (kcat) of vertebrate alpha-class carbonic anhydrases: up to 1.4 times 10 to the power of 6 per second. Human carbonic anhydrase II (CA II), the isoform in red blood cells and abundantly in brain, is widely reported to have the largest catalytic turnover number of any known enzyme. The round figure "of order 10 to the power of 6 per second" is correct and the specific 1.4 times 10 to the power of 6 per second value is the standard literature figure.

• Catalytic efficiency (kcat divided by Km) for human CA II: 1.5 times 10 to the power of 8 per molar per second - close to the diffusion-controlled limit, meaning the enzyme is nearly perfect in the Albery-Knowles sense.

• Sanity check on the popular formulation: "one molecule hydrates 36,000,000 carbon dioxide molecules in 60 seconds" equals 600,000 per second, i.e. 6 times 10 to the power of 5 per second. That is the same order of magnitude as 1.4 times 10 to the power of 6 per second but a factor of about 2.3 lower - the difference reflects temperature and assay conditions (the lower figure is a 25 degrees Celsius / classic textbook value). Give the range 6 times 10 to the power of 5 to 1.4 times 10 to the power of 6 per second rather than a false single number.

• Sourcing caveat: these values came through aggregated search-result text and patent-literature background sections rather than a fetched primary kinetics paper, because the primary hosts returned HTTP 403 in this session. Before print, verify against Khalifah RG, Journal of Biological Chemistry 246:2561, 1971, or the BRENDA database entry for EC 4.2.1.1.

11. ACCESS PROBLEMS ENCOUNTERED - RELEVANT TO VERIFICATION CONFIDENCE

• Science.org, ScienceDirect topic pages and jneurosci.org article pages all returned HTTP 403 Forbidden to direct fetching in this session. The Kumar 2015 Science quantitative findings, the Stawarski 2020 alkalinisation findings, and the Palmer 2003 numbers were therefore recovered from search-result abstract text rather than from the fetched full text. All three are consistent across multiple independent search results, so I regard them as reliable, but a library-access check of the abstracts before print would remove all doubt.

• Figures I could NOT independently verify to a primary source and which should be checked: the exact CSF pH upper bound of 7.33; the 180 mV total proton motive force; the ASIC1a desensitisation time constant; the Beutner et al. calyx-of-Held cleft acidification attribution.

12. THE THROUGH-LINE FOR THE CHAPTER

The proton is present at vanishingly low free concentration - about 63 nanomolar at pH 7.2, tens of thousands of times rarer than calcium - yet it is read out by dedicated receptors (ASIC1a, GPR4), gated channels (TASK-2), and the fastest enzyme known (carbonic anhydrase II), and it is the energy currency that loads every synaptic vesicle in the brain. Its rarity is precisely what makes it a usable signal: a small absolute number of protons moved produces a large fractional change, which is why buffering capacity, not free concentration, is the quantity that governs how fast and how far pH can swing.</result>
