TOTAL BODY AND BRAIN ZINC

• Total body zinc in an adult human: approximately 2-3 g. Zinc is the second most abundant trace metal in the body after iron; roughly 85 percent of whole-body zinc is in muscle and bone, about 11 percent in skin and liver. Sources: Linus Pauling Institute Micronutrient Information Center, Zinc (https://lpi.oregonstate.edu/mic/minerals/zinc); review summaries in Biomolecules 2022, 12:1000, "Zinc in Cognitive Impairment and Aging" (https://www.mdpi.com/2218-273X/12/7/1000). Note: the 2-3 g figure is a long-standing textbook consensus rather than a single primary measurement; I could not fetch a primary balance study.

• Adult human brain contains roughly 0.04 g zinc, about 1.5 percent of total body zinc (implies total body ~2.7 g, consistent with the 2-3 g figure). Source: MDPI Biomolecules review above.

• Total tissue zinc in gray matter: 60-80 ppm (i.e. 60-80 micrograms/g wet weight) across hippocampal regions; a whole-brain figure of approximately 150 micromolar total intracellular zinc (overwhelmingly protein-bound) is commonly quoted. Primary source: Frederickson et al. 1983 (stable-isotope dilution mass spectrometry, hippocampal zinc 77.2-92.7 ppm). Secondary/summary source: Nakashima &amp; Dyck-type review, "Modulation of Neuronal Signal Transduction and Memory Formation by Synaptic Zinc," Front Behav Neurosci 2011, 5:68 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3211062/).

• Hippocampus: approximately 12.59 +/- 0.85 micrograms zinc per g wet weight reported by Frederickson et al. 1984, "The distribution and binding of zinc in the hippocampus," J Neurosci 4:1662 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/4/6/1662). CAUTION - this number is roughly six-fold lower than the 60-92 ppm figures above; the discrepancy reflects different fractions measured (histochemically reactive/chelatable zinc versus total zinc) and different tissue preparations. I could not fetch the 1984 full text (HTTP 403), so treat the 12.59 figure as the chelatable/vesicular-associated pool, not total zinc.

• Regional ranking: highest in hippocampus (especially hilus and stratum lucidum of CA3, where mossy fibre terminals are), with cortical gray matter nearly as rich; also high in amygdala, striatum and neocortex; low in cerebellum and white matter. Amygdala zinc has been reported as more than 200 micromolar in adult rat. Sources: MDPI Biomolecules 2022 12:1000; Frontiers in Aging Neuroscience 2013, 5:33, "Zinc homeostasis and neurodegenerative disorders" (https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/aging-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnagi.2013.00033/full).

• Free (unbound) zinc pools are vastly lower than total zinc: cytosolic free zinc approximately 0.4-0.6 nanomolar (subnanomolar); resting extracellular/CSF labile zinc approximately 5-25 nanomolar. Primary source: Frederickson CJ, Giblin LJ, Krezel A, et al. 2006, "Concentrations of extracellular free zinc (pZn)e in the central nervous system during simple anesthetization, ischemia and reperfusion," Exp Neurol 198(2):285-293 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16443223/).

ZINC PROTEOME AND ZINC FINGER TRANSCRIPTION FACTORS

• IMPORTANT CITATION CORRECTION. The "approximately 10 percent of the human proteome is zinc-binding" figure does NOT come from Andreini, Banci, Bertini &amp; Rosato 2006 "Zinc through the three domains of life." It comes from the companion paper by the same group: Andreini C, Banci L, Bertini I, Rosato A (2006), "Counting the Zinc-Proteins Encoded in the Human Genome," J Proteome Res 5(1):196-201 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16396512/ ; https://pubs.acs.org/doi/10.1021/pr050361j). Their exact wording: "ca. 2800 human proteins are potentially zinc-binding in vivo, corresponding to 10% of the human proteome, with an uncertainty of 400 sequences." Method breakdown: 1684 proteins identified as zinc proteins by all three computational approaches, 746 by two, 777 by only one.

• Andreini, Banci, Bertini &amp; Rosato (2006), "Zinc through the Three Domains of Life," J Proteome Res 5(11):3173-3178 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17081069/ ; https://pubs.acs.org/doi/10.1021/pr0603699) - exact figures: across 57 organisms, "zinc-binding proteins are an abundant fraction of these proteomes, ranging between 4% and 10%"; the proportionality constant is 8.8 percent for Eukaryota versus 5-6 percent for Bacteria and Archaea; the eukaryotic enrichment is attributed mostly to a larger portfolio of regulatory (DNA-binding) proteins.

• So the two defensible statements are: eukaryotic proteomes are approximately 8.8 percent zinc-binding (three-domains paper), and the human proteome specifically is approximately 10 percent, 2800 +/- 400 proteins (human-genome-counting paper).

• Human C2H2 zinc finger proteins: over 700 of the approximately 1600 putative human transcription factors are C2H2 zinc finger proteins - the largest single transcription-factor class. Of these, approximately 350 also carry a KRAB (Kruppel-associated box) domain, ~52 a BTB domain, ~50 a SCAN domain, 12 a SET domain, and over 200 have no conserved domain beyond the zinc finger array. Source: Najafabadi/Schmitges et al., "Multiparameter functional diversity of human C2H2 zinc finger proteins," Genome Research 2016, 26:1742 (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5131825/). The ~1600 human transcription factor total is from Lambert et al. 2018, "The Human Transcription Factors," Cell 172(4):650-665.

• So the "~700-1000 human zinc finger transcription factors" claim is VERIFIED at the lower end: &gt;700 C2H2-ZF transcription factors. Counts above 1000 typically come from counting all zinc-finger-domain-containing proteins (including non-transcription-factor RING, PHD, LIM fingers), not zinc finger transcription factors. An older and looser claim - "up to 1% of the human genome codes for zinc finger proteins" - appears in Frederickson et al. 2000 (see below).

VESICULAR AND SYNAPTIC ZINC - THE GENUINE DISAGREEMENT

This is the least settled area in the whole topic. Report it as an open controversy.

HIGH CAMP (Frederickson and colleagues)

• Frederickson CJ, Suh SW, Silva D, Frederickson CJ, Thompson RB (2000), "Importance of zinc in the central nervous system: the zinc-containing neuron," J Nutr 130(5S Suppl):1471S-1483S (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10801962/). Exact wording from the abstract: "The zinc in the vesicles probably exceeds 1 mmol/L in concentration and is only weakly coordinated with any endogenous ligand." Also: "It is estimated that up to 1% of the human genome codes for zinc finger proteins."

• Vesicular zinc in mossy fibre boutons of approximately 1 mM was inferred by extrapolating from total zinc in the hippocampal hilus (Frederickson et al. 1983); the minimum concentration detectable by Timm's/histochemical staining independently implies at least 150 micromolar vesicular zinc (Danscher &amp; Stoltenberg 2006). Source summary: Front Behav Neurosci 2011, 5:68 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3211062/). Claims up to 5 mM appear in the secondary literature but I could not trace a primary measurement supporting 5 mM - treat that upper figure as unverified.

• Frederickson's camp predicted synaptic cleft transients of 100-300 micromolar following release (this prediction is quoted and attacked explicitly by Kay 2003, see below).

INTERMEDIATE / FUNCTIONAL-INFERENCE CAMP

• Vogt K, Mellor J, Tong G, Nicoll R (2000), "The actions of synaptically released zinc at hippocampal mossy fiber synapses," Neuron 26(1):187-196 (https://www.cell.com/fulltext/S0896-6273(00)81149-6 ; PMID 10798403). They showed zinc is co-released with glutamate at mossy fibre synapses (spontaneously and on electrical stimulation) and inhibits NMDA receptors there but not at neighbouring associational/commissural synapses. Their inference: because the LOW-affinity zinc site is occupied, cleft zinc must exceed 10-20 micromolar at mossy fibre synapses. Note this is a pharmacological inference from receptor occupancy, not a direct measurement.

• Glycinergic synapse work puts free cleft zinc at "at least 1 micromolar" (Front Mol Neurosci 2016, 9:88, https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/molecular-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnmol.2016.00088/full).

LOW CAMP (Kay and Toth)

• Kay AR (2003), "Evidence for chelatable zinc in the extracellular space of the hippocampus, but little evidence for synaptic release of Zn," J Neurosci 23(17):6847-6855 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/23/17/6847 ; open access at https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6740710/). Method: FluoZin-3 plus the slow chelator Ca-EDTA to suppress background. Numbers: baseline extracellular zinc 2.0 +/- 0.3 nM with Ca-EDTA present; electrical stimulation raised it to a peak of 4.2 +/- 0.4 nM (an increase of only ~2 nM); 50 mM KCl plus raised osmolarity gave a peak of 7.5 +/- 1.1 nM (~6 nM increase). Correcting for the ~20 percent extracellular volume fraction he estimated an extracellular zinc "veneer" of about 3.2 micromolar in the extracellular space. Kay's explicit critique: the transients "seemed inconsistent with the 100-300 microM Zn elevations predicted by others," and even under extreme depolarization "very little makes its way into the synaptic cleft, perhaps being detained through an association with vesicular proteins."

• Kay AR, Toth K (2008), "Is zinc a neuromodulator?" Science Signaling 1(19):re3 (https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/stke.119re3 ; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18480018/). Argues that whether synaptic zinc actually transits the cleft in amounts sufficient to alter channel responses has never been firmly established; notes vesicular zinc content is far below vesicular glutamate (20-100 mM), implying any zinc action must be tightly restricted in time and space. Nydegger et al. 2010 is a further paper in the same low-estimate line.

HOW THE FIELD DESCRIBES THE SPREAD

• Reviews describe positions ranging from "no zinc release" (Kay 2003; Kay &amp; Toth 2008; Nydegger et al. 2010) to "massive zinc release." See Modelling zinc changes at the hippocampal mossy fiber synaptic cleft, J Comput Neurosci 2016 (https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10827-016-0620-x) and Vergnano et al., "Zinc dynamics and action at excitatory synapses," Neuron 2014, 82(5):1101-1114 (https://www.cell.com/neuron/fulltext/S0896-6273(14)00349-3 - paywalled to fetch, cited from search results).

• Bottom line to state honestly in the book: total vesicular zinc in mossy fibre boutons is probably of order 1 mM (well-supported lower bound approximately 150 micromolar from histochemistry); the peak FREE zinc concentration actually reached in the cleft after release is NOT established, with credible published estimates spanning more than four orders of magnitude - from a few nanomolar above baseline (Kay 2003, direct fluorimetry) through approximately 1 micromolar (glycinergic synapse sensors) and &gt;10-20 micromolar (Vogt 2000, receptor-occupancy inference) up to a predicted 100-300 micromolar (Frederickson camp).

• What would settle it: a genetically encoded or synthetic zinc sensor targeted to the synaptic cleft itself, with an in-situ absolute calibration (known-zinc buffered solutions applied to the same preparation), fast enough to resolve millisecond transients, and validated against ZnT3 knockout tissue as a zero-zinc negative control. Progress in this direction: two-photon imaging of vesicular Zn2+ in individual mossy fibre boutons with the membrane-impermeant probe 6-CO2H-ZAP4 (Khan et al., PNAS 2014, https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1405154111), which resolves release at single presynaptic sites but still does not give an absolute calibrated cleft concentration. Until such a calibration exists, quote a range and name both camps.

ZnT3 (SLC30A3) KNOCKOUT MOUSE

• Cole TB, Wenzel HJ, Kafer KE, Schwartzkroin PA, Palmiter RD (1999), "Elimination of zinc from synaptic vesicles in the intact mouse brain by disruption of the ZnT3 gene," PNAS 96(4):1716-1721 (https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.96.4.1716). Findings: histochemically reactive (vesicular) zinc is UNDETECTABLE throughout the brain; TOTAL zinc in hippocampus and cortex is reduced by about 20 percent (i.e. the vesicular pool is roughly a fifth of tissue zinc in these regions); mossy fibre bouton ultrastructure is unaffected; heterozygotes have reduced ZnT3 protein on vesicle membranes and intermediate vesicular zinc, indicating vesicular zinc loading is set by ZnT3 abundance. Note the fifth author is Schwartzkroin, not "Kafer/Schweizer" as in the brief.

• Background: ZnT3 was identified as the vesicular zinc transporter by Palmiter et al. 1996, PNAS 93:14934 (https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.93.25.14934), and localized to synaptic vesicle membranes in mossy fibre boutons of mouse and monkey by Wenzel et al. 1997, PNAS 94:12676 (https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.94.23.12676).

• Adlard PA, Parncutt JM, Finkelstein DI, Bush AI (2010), "Cognitive loss in zinc transporter-3 knock-out mice: a phenocopy for the synaptic and memory deficits of Alzheimer's disease?" J Neurosci 30(5):1631-1636 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/30/5/1631). Findings: learning and memory deficits are AGE-DEPENDENT - present at 6 months but absent at 3 months. Associated with reduced hippocampal levels of the presynaptic protein SNAP25, the postsynaptic scaffold PSD95, and AMPA and NMDA receptors.

• Later confirmation and mechanism: Frontiers in Molecular Neuroscience 2024, "Genetic deletion of zinc transporter ZnT3 induces progressive cognitive deficits in mice by impairing dendritic spine plasticity and glucose metabolism" (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11130425/); and Int J Mol Sci 2020, 21:839, "Zinc transporter-3 knockout mice demonstrate age-dependent alterations in the metalloproteome" (https://doi.org/10.3390/ijms21030839). Caveat worth stating: baseline ZnT3-KO mice are grossly normal and show no overt seizure or gross behavioural phenotype at young ages, which is itself an argument used by the low-estimate camp against a large tonic role for vesicular zinc.

ZINC AND NMDA RECEPTORS

• Paoletti P, Ascher P, Neyton J (1997), "High-affinity zinc inhibition of NMDA NR1-NR2A receptors," J Neurosci 17(15):5711-5725 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/17/15/5711 ; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9221770/). The abstract itself states only "voltage-independent inhibition occurs at much lower Zn2+ concentrations in NR1a-NR2A receptors (IC50 in the nanomolar range) than in NR1a-NR2B receptors (IC50 in the micromolar range)"; no single numeric IC50 appears in the abstract. The widely cited figure derived from this paper is an IC50 of approximately 5 nM for GluN2A (NR2A). The paper attributes the high apparent affinity mostly to SLOW DISSOCIATION of zinc from its site. It also shows that adding low concentrations of heavy-metal chelators markedly potentiates NR1a-NR2A but not NR1a-NR2B responses - i.e. trace contaminating zinc tonically inhibits GluN2A receptors in ordinary saline. Commonly quoted range in reviews: approximately 5-20 nM for GluN2A. Because the exact value is sensitive to zinc buffering and contamination control, quote it as "low nanomolar, approximately 5-20 nM depending on preparation and buffering" rather than a single number.

• Rachline J, Perin-Dureau F, Le Goff A, Neyton J, Paoletti P (2005), "The micromolar zinc-binding domain on the NMDA receptor subunit NR2B," J Neurosci 25(2):308-317 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/25/2/308 ; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15647474/). Verified numbers: the GluN2B (NR2B) N-terminal domain also binds zinc but with lower affinity; the reported zinc IC50 for NR1/NR2B is 760 nM (approximately 0.76 micromolar) - the abstract calls it "submicromolar," so "approximately 1 micromolar" is a fair rounding. Zinc and the neuroprotectant ifenprodil compete for an overlapping site on the NR2B N-terminal domain. NR2C and NR2D do not bind zinc at this site. The authors' conclusion: NMDA receptors carry subunit-specific zinc sensors of high (nanomolar, GluN2A) and low (micromolar, GluN2B) affinity, allowing zinc to exert both TONIC and PHASIC control of excitability.

• Review consolidating this: Paoletti P, Bellone C, Zhou Q (2013), "NMDA receptor subunit diversity: impact on receptor properties, synaptic plasticity and disease," Nat Rev Neurosci 14(6):383-400 (https://www.nature.com/articles/nrn3504 ; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23686171/). Paywalled - I could not fetch the full text to extract its exact tabulated IC50s, so the numbers above are cited to the two primary papers instead.

• Functional consequence in vivo: Nozaki et al. 2011, "Zinc alleviates pain through high-affinity binding to the NMDA receptor NR2A subunit," Nat Neurosci (https://www.nature.com/articles/nn.2844) - uses a knock-in mouse with the high-affinity zinc site abolished, which is the cleanest genetic test of the nanomolar GluN2A site.

• Related: zinc also inhibits AMPA receptors at synapses where it is co-released - Kalappa BI, Anderson CT, Goldberg JM, Lippard SJ, Tzounopoulos T (2015), "AMPA receptor inhibition by synaptically released zinc," PNAS 112(51):15749-15754 (https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1512296112). Also "Synaptic zinc inhibition of NMDA receptors depends on the association of GluN2A with the zinc transporter ZnT1," Sci Adv 2020 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7458442/).

ZINC AND GABA-A RECEPTORS

• Smart TG, Moss SJ, Xie X, Huganir RL (1991), "GABAA receptors are differentially sensitive to zinc: dependence on subunit composition," Br J Pharmacol 103:1837-1839 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1655141/ ; PDF at https://bpspubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/pdf/10.1111/j.1476-5381.1991.tb12337.x). Established the key rule: receptors LACKING a gamma subunit are inhibited by zinc; incorporating gamma2 confers relative zinc insensitivity. I could not retrieve the paper's per-combination IC50 table (publisher returned HTTP 402), so do not quote specific 1991 IC50 numbers without checking the PDF directly.

• Hosie AM, Dunne EL, Harvey RJ, Smart TG (2003), "Zinc-mediated inhibition of GABA-A receptors: discrete binding sites underlie subtype specificity," Nat Neurosci 6(4):362-369 (https://www.nature.com/articles/nn1030 ; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12640458/). Verified mechanism: zinc inhibits allosterically and the potency is critically subunit-dependent - "alphabeta subunit combinations show the highest sensitivity, and alphabetagamma isoforms are the least sensitive." Three discrete zinc sites: ONE inside the ion channel pore and TWO on the external N-terminal face at alpha-beta subunit interfaces. The low zinc sensitivity of gamma2-containing receptors arises because gamma2 co-assembly DISRUPTS TWO OF THE THREE sites (the two N-terminal interface sites), leaving only the intra-channel site. The published abstract carries no numeric IC50s.

• Order-of-magnitude values commonly quoted from this literature (state as approximate, and flag that they are preparation-dependent): alpha-beta binary receptors are inhibited with sub-micromolar to low-micromolar IC50 (often quoted around 0.1-1 micromolar); alpha-beta-gamma2 ternary (typical synaptic) receptors require tens to hundreds of micromolar, a shift of roughly two to three orders of magnitude. I was NOT able to verify these specific numbers against a fetched primary source - both Smart 1991 and Hosie 2003 full texts were paywalled - so either fetch those PDFs before printing numbers, or print only the qualitative rule plus the "two to three orders of magnitude" shift, which is well supported by the fetched abstracts.

• Delta-subunit extrasynaptic receptors are also zinc-sensitive: Carver, Wu, Gangisetty, Reddy (2016), "Zinc selectively blocks neurosteroid-sensitive extrasynaptic delta-GABA-A receptors in the hippocampus," J Neurosci 36(31):8070 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/36/31/8070). Structural work on alpha-beta binary receptors: Nature 2022, "Mechanisms of inhibition and activation of extrasynaptic alpha-beta GABA-A receptors" (https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-022-04402-z).

• Endogenous relevance in tissue: "Endogenous zinc inhibits GABA-A receptors in a hippocampal pathway," J Neurophysiol 2004 (https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/jn.00755.2003), and Buhl-type work on dentate basket cells, J Neurosci 18:2437 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/18/7/2437) showing zinc block via altered desensitization kinetics.

VALUES I COULD NOT VERIFY - DO NOT PRINT WITHOUT FURTHER SOURCING

• The 5 mM upper bound for vesicular zinc - no primary source traced; only 1 mM (Frederickson 2000, "probably exceeds 1 mmol/L") and the &gt;=150 micromolar histochemical floor are supported.
• Specific numeric IC50s for zinc at GABA-A subunit combinations - both key primary papers were paywalled.
• A single canonical numeric IC50 for GluN2A - the 1997 abstract gives only "nanomolar range"; the 5 nM figure comes from secondary citation of the paper's figures.
• Region-by-region human brain zinc in micrograms/g for amygdala and neocortex separately - only rank ordering and the hippocampal figures could be verified.</result>

<result>FINDINGS: THE CALCIUM ION IN NEUROSCIENCE - WEB-VERIFIED QUANTITATIVE DUMP

Compiled 2026-08-12. Every number below was seen in a source I fetched or in search-result text from a named source. Disagreements are flagged explicitly.


SECTION 1 - EXTRACELLULAR FREE CALCIUM (CSF AND BRAIN INTERSTITIUM)

• Human cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), healthy: ionized (free) Ca2+ = 1.0 mM; total Ca = 1.2 mM; ionized fraction 86 percent. Measured with an ion-selective electrode.

• Citation: Forsberg M, Seth H, Bjorefeldt A, Lyckenvik T, Andersson M, Wasling P, Zetterberg H, Hanse E (2019). "Ionized calcium in human cerebrospinal fluid and its influence on intrinsic and synaptic excitability of hippocampal pyramidal neurons in the rat." Journal of Neurochemistry 149(4):452-470. URL: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30851210/

• This is an important correction to the textbook habit of quoting 2 mM. The commonly cited "extracellular Ca2+ = 1.2 mM" used in the neuronal-gradient literature agrees well with the Forsberg 2019 CSF measurement, whereas artificial CSF used in most slice experiments is typically 2.0-2.5 mM - i.e. roughly double the true in-vivo free level. Forsberg and colleagues made exactly that point (their paper is about how the lower physiological value changes excitability).

• Plasma total calcium in humans: approximately 2.2-2.6 mM, of which roughly half is protein-bound (chiefly to albumin); the free ionized fraction of plasma is approximately 1.1-1.3 mM.

• Reason CSF total Ca is lower than plasma total Ca: very little protein crosses from plasma into CSF, and about half of plasma calcium is protein-bound; protein-bound calcium cannot cross the blood-brain barrier, so CSF carries much less total calcium while its FREE calcium is close to the free plasma value. Source text confirming this reasoning: search-result summary of the CSF-homeostasis literature, and Nattie/Chester-type CSF ionic homeostasis reviews (Homeostasis of the ionic composition of the cerebrospinal fluid, ScienceDirect, URL: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0014483577800225).

• Developmental and species data: in adult rats CSF ionized Ca2+ is significantly LOWER than plasma ionized Ca2+; in younger animals plasma, CSF, and brain interstitial fluid ionized Ca2+ did not differ significantly. Citation: Jones HC and colleagues, "Brain fluid calcium concentration and response to acute hypercalcaemia during development in the rat," Journal of Physiology (1988). URLs: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1191910/ and https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3236250/

• Regulation: potassium, calcium, and magnesium concentrations in mammalian CSF are held remarkably stable despite severe and prolonged swings in plasma concentrations - CSF calcium is actively regulated, not a passive plasma filtrate.

• WHERE SOURCES DISAGREE: the working range you should quote for brain extracellular free Ca2+ is 1.0-1.5 mM, not 1.8-2.0 mM. 1.0 mM is the directly measured human CSF value (Forsberg 2019); 1.2 mM is the value used in most quantitative neuronal-calcium reviews; 1.8-2.0 mM values in the literature almost always come from artificial CSF or from serum ionized calcium, not from brain interstitium. Say so explicitly.

• NOT WELL ESTABLISHED: the free Ca2+ in the brain INTERSTITIAL space during intense neural activity. Ca2+ is depleted from the synaptic cleft by influx during activity - measured directly at the calyx of Held by Borst and Sakmann (1999), "Depletion of calcium in the synaptic cleft of a calyx-type synapse in the rat brainstem," Journal of Physiology, URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2269650/ - so the effective local extracellular concentration during a train is lower than the bulk 1.0-1.2 mM by an amount that is still debated. What would settle it: direct nanoscale Ca2+-selective measurement in the cleft in vivo during physiological firing.


SECTION 2 - RESTING CYTOSOLIC FREE CALCIUM AND THE GRADIENT

• Resting cytosolic free Ca2+ in most neurons: 50-100 nM. Some sources give approximately 100 nM as the canonical single value; some cell types and some measurements give up to approximately 150 nM.

• Simon and Llinas (1985) explicitly used a basal free intracellular Ca2+ of approximately 50 nM in their presynaptic terminal model.

• Allbritton, Meyer and Stryer (1992) measured Ca2+ diffusion at a resting free Ca2+ of "about 90 nM," which independently anchors the approximately 100 nM figure.

• GRADIENT: with extracellular free Ca2+ = 1.2 mM = 1,200,000 nM and cytosolic free Ca2+ = 100 nM, the ratio is 12,000-fold. Using the range 50-100 nM cytosolic and 1.0-1.5 mM extracellular, the gradient spans roughly 10,000-fold to 30,000-fold. The standard textbook statement is "approximately 10,000- to 20,000-fold."

• Why this matters (verified framing from the reviews): the extracellular-to-cytosolic ratio for Ca2+ is roughly 10,000:1, whereas for sodium it is about 10:1 and for potassium about 1:30. Consequently, sodium influx and potassium efflux move the membrane potential a lot but barely change the intracellular concentration of those ions, while calcium influx barely changes the membrane potential but changes intracellular calcium concentration enormously. That asymmetry is the entire reason calcium works as a second messenger.

• During intense electrical activity, bulk cytosolic Ca2+ can transiently reach approximately 1-10 micromolar (i.e. a 10- to 100-fold rise over rest), with far higher values locally (see Section 4).


SECTION 3 - THE CALCIUM EQUILIBRIUM POTENTIAL (E_Ca), WITH THE NERNST CALCULATION SHOWN

• The Nernst equation: E_ion = (RT / zF) times the natural logarithm of ([ion] outside divided by [ion] inside).

• At 37 degrees Celsius (310.15 kelvin), with R = 8.314 joules per mole per kelvin and F = 96,485 coulombs per mole, the factor RT/F = 0.02672 volts = 26.72 millivolts. Converting to base-10 logarithms multiply by 2.303, giving 61.5 millivolts per decade for a monovalent ion.

• For calcium, z = +2, so the prefactor is halved: 26.72 / 2 = 13.36 millivolts (natural log form), or 61.5 / 2 = 30.8 millivolts per tenfold concentration ratio (base-10 form).

• WORKED CALCULATION A - the physiologically best-supported numbers. Outside = 1.2 mM (1.2 times 10 to the minus 3 molar); inside = 100 nM (1.0 times 10 to the minus 7 molar). The ratio is 12,000. Log base 10 of 12,000 = 4.079. E_Ca = 30.8 millivolts times 4.079 = +125.6 millivolts. Round to approximately +126 mV.

• WORKED CALCULATION B - the classic textbook version. Outside = 2 mM; inside = 100 nM (0.0001 mM). Ratio = 20,000. Log base 10 = 4.301. E_Ca = 30.8 times 4.301 = +132.5 millivolts. A widely reproduced textbook worked example of exactly this form gives approximately +123 millivolts at 20 degrees Celsius and approximately +135 millivolts at 37 degrees Celsius - both values appeared in the sources I checked.

• WORKED CALCULATION C - low resting calcium. Outside = 1.2 mM; inside = 50 nM. Ratio = 24,000. Log base 10 = 4.380. E_Ca = 30.8 times 4.380 = +134.9 millivolts, i.e. approximately +135 mV.

• CONCLUSION AND RANGE: E_Ca in a mammalian neuron at body temperature is approximately +120 to +140 millivolts, with +125 to +135 millivolts the best-supported central band. The spread across published values comes almost entirely from two choices - whether the author uses 1.2 mM or 2.0-2.5 mM external calcium, and whether resting cytosolic calcium is taken as 50 or 100 nanomolar - not from any experimental disagreement.

• Sources for the Nernst treatment of calcium: Purves and colleagues, Neuroscience (NCBI Bookshelf, "The Forces that Create Membrane Potentials," URL: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK11102/); "The Equilibrium (Nernst) Potential," Developing Expertise in Neuroscience, URL: https://uen.pressbooks.pub/expertneuro/chapter/the-equilibrium-nernst-potential/; and "Membrane Potential: Its components and maintenance," Neuroscience Canadian 3rd Edition, URL: https://ecampusontario.pressbooks.pub/neurosciencecdn3/chapter/membrane-potential-its-components-and-maintenance/

• IMPORTANT CAVEAT worth stating in the chapter: E_Ca is a formal Nernst number, not a potential the neuron ever approaches. Because calcium is continuously pumped out, the cell sits 200 millivolts or more away from E_Ca; the driving force on calcium is therefore always large and always inward, at every membrane potential a neuron ever reaches. Every open calcium channel is, in effect, an inward calcium current with essentially no reversal within the physiological voltage range.


SECTION 4 - MICRODOMAINS AND NANODOMAINS AT THE MOUTH OF AN OPEN CHANNEL

• SIMON AND LLINAS (1985) - the founding calculation. Simon SM, Llinas RR, "Compartmentalization of the submembrane calcium activity during calcium influx and its significance in transmitter release," Biophysical Journal 48:485-498 (1985). Full text PDF: https://lab.rockefeller.edu/simon/assets/file/Lab%20Publications/1985_-_Biophysical_journal_-_Compartmentalization_of_the_submembrane_calcium.pdf
◦ They modelled the presynaptic terminal and showed that the intracellular calcium concentration profile during an inward calcium current consists of discrete peaks immediately adjacent to individual calcium channels rather than a uniform submembrane rise.
◦ Quantitatively: against a basal free intracellular Ca2+ of approximately 50 nanomolar, local domains around open channels rise about a THOUSANDFOLD (i.e. into the tens of micromolar), and both build and decay very fast (sub-millisecond, on channel-gating timescales).

• LLINAS, SUGIMORI AND SILVER (1992) - the direct measurement. "Microdomains of high calcium concentration in a presynaptic terminal," Science 256:677-679. URL: https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1350109 . This is the paper that imaged the predicted microdomains in the squid giant presynaptic terminal with n-aequorin-J and gave the name "microdomain" its currency. Reported peak microdomain concentrations were in the range of 100-250 micromolar, greatly exceeding the bulk terminal rise.

• NEHER (1998) - the microdomain/nanodomain distinction with explicit numbers. Neher E, "Vesicle pools and Ca2+ microdomains: new tools for understanding their roles in neurotransmitter release," Neuron 20:389-399 (1998). URLs: https://www.cell.com/neuron/fulltext/S0896-6273(00)80983-6 and https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0896627300809836
◦ NANODOMAIN: source-to-sensor separation of approximately 20 nanometres gives a local Ca2+ signal of approximately 100 micromolar. This is the signal from a SINGLE open channel very close to the sensor.
◦ MICRODOMAIN: source-to-sensor separation of approximately 200 nanometres gives approximately 5-10 micromolar. This is the overlapping signal from many channels summing at a more distant sensor.
◦ The 10-fold difference in distance produces roughly a 10- to 20-fold difference in local concentration, which is the essential point: local Ca2+ falls off approximately as 1/r (inverse of distance) around a point source at steady state.

• AUGUSTINE GJ, SANTAMARIA F, TANAKA K (2003), "Local calcium signaling in neurons," Neuron 40(2):331-346. URL: https://www.cell.com/neuron/fulltext/S0896-6273(03)00639-1 (note: this URL returned HTTP 403 to automated fetch; the citation and its role as the standard review of local domains were confirmed via search-result indexing of the article). This review is the standard reference for the composite statement that Ca2+ reaches 10-100 micromolar within 10-100 nanometres of an open channel mouth, decaying to bulk levels within a fraction of a micrometre.

• HEADLINE NUMBER FOR THE CHAPTER, with the sources it rests on: at the mouth of a single open voltage-gated calcium channel, free Ca2+ reaches approximately 10-100 micromolar within approximately 10-100 nanometres of the pore, i.e. 100- to 1000-fold higher than the bulk cytosolic rise, and it collapses within microseconds of channel closure. Supported jointly by Simon and Llinas 1985 (theory), Llinas et al. 1992 (measurement, 100-250 micromolar), Neher 1998 (20 nm gives approximately 100 micromolar; 200 nm gives 5-10 micromolar), and Augustine et al. 2003 (review).

• Supporting analytic treatment: Naraghi M and Neher E (1997), "Linearized buffered Ca2+ diffusion in microdomains and its implications for calculation of [Ca2+] at the mouth of a calcium channel," Journal of Neuroscience 17(18):6961-6973. URL: https://www.jneurosci.org/content/17/18/6961 . This is the paper that gives the closed-form expression usually used to compute local concentration as a function of distance, single-channel current, and buffer parameters - worth citing if the chapter shows the 1/r falloff.

• WHERE SOURCES DISAGREE: the numbers depend strongly on assumed single-channel calcium current and on buffer concentration and on-rate, and different laboratories assume different values. There is genuine ongoing disagreement about whether a given synapse uses nanodomain coupling (one channel per vesicle, tight, roughly 10-20 nm) or microdomain coupling (many channels, loose, roughly 100-300 nm), and this differs by synapse type and changes during development. What would settle it for any given synapse: comparison of the release-blocking potency of the fast buffer BAPTA against the slow buffer EGTA - strong EGTA sensitivity indicates loose microdomain coupling, EGTA insensitivity with BAPTA sensitivity indicates tight nanodomain coupling. This is the standard experimental discriminator and should be described in the chapter.


SECTION 5 - THE CALCIUM SENSOR: SYNAPTOTAGMIN-1 COOPERATIVITY AND AFFINITY

• DODGE FA and RAHAMIMOFF R (1967), "Co-operative action of calcium ions in transmitter release at the neuromuscular junction," Journal of Physiology 193:419-432. URLs: https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1113/jphysiol.1967.sp008367 and https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6065887/
◦ At the frog neuromuscular junction, with intracellular recording and endplate potential amplitude as the index of quantal release, a double-logarithmic plot of release against external calcium concentration gives an initial straight line of slope nearly four.
◦ THE EXACT MEASURED NUMBER: mean slope 3.78, standard deviation 0.2, across 28 experiments. This is the primary source for "the fourth-power law" and the chapter should quote 3.78 plus or minus 0.2 rather than a bare "4."
◦ Interpretation offered in the paper: the cooperative involvement of about four calcium ions in the release of each transmitter quantum.
◦ CAVEAT the chapter must state: Dodge and Rahamimoff varied EXTERNAL calcium, so their exponent of 3.78 measures the cooperativity of the whole chain from external concentration through channel influx to release. It is not, strictly, the Hill coefficient of the intracellular sensor. The two turn out to be similar, but they are conceptually different measurements.

• SCHNEGGENBURGER R and NEHER E (2000), "Intracellular calcium dependence of transmitter release rates at a fast central synapse," Nature 406:889-893. URL: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10972290/ . Calyx of Held, caged-calcium flash photolysis, which controls intracellular calcium directly and so measures the sensor itself.
◦ Step-like elevations to only 10 micromolar intracellular Ca2+ induce fast transmitter release.
◦ Transient local elevations of approximately 0.5 millisecond duration to peak values AS LOW AS 25 micromolar can account for transmitter release during a single presynaptic action potential.
◦ Approximately 80 percent of the pool of available vesicles is depleted in less than 3 milliseconds at these concentrations.

• BOLLMANN JH, SAKMANN B, BORST JGG (2000), "Calcium sensitivity of glutamate release in a calyx-type terminal," Science 289:953-957. The companion paper, same year, same preparation, same caged-calcium approach, reaching the same conclusion: low micromolar internal calcium suffices to trigger fast release. Peak release rates of approximately 1000 vesicles per second per readily-releasable vesicle were reached at approximately 10 micromolar intracellular calcium.

• SYNTHESIS FOR THE CHAPTER: the presynaptic calcium sensor - synaptotagmin-1 at most fast central and peripheral synapses - operates with a Hill coefficient of approximately 4 to 5 and requires local calcium in the range of approximately 10 to 25 micromolar for half-maximal to near-maximal release rates. That local requirement is met only inside a nanodomain (Section 4), which is precisely why release is fast and tightly localized.

• WHERE SOURCES DISAGREE - THIS IS A REAL AND IMPORTANT DISAGREEMENT, DO NOT SMOOTH IT OVER:
◦ Calyx of Held (Schneggenburger and Neher 2000; Bollmann et al. 2000): HIGH apparent affinity, with significant release already below 5 micromolar intracellular calcium and a steep dose-response curve.
◦ Squid giant synapse and goldfish retinal bipolar cells: a fourth-to-fifth power relation but with HALF-SATURATION AT APPROXIMATELY 100 MICROMOLAR - roughly ten times less sensitive than the calyx.
◦ Cerebellar mossy fibre boutons: Hill coefficient of only 1.2 to 1.6 and a half-maximal calcium concentration GREATER THAN 50 micromolar, a shallow dose-response that does not saturate up to 50 micromolar; these authors argue for co-existing high-affinity and low-affinity calcium sensors rather than a single uniform sensor. Source: "Calcium dependence of neurotransmitter release at a high fidelity synapse," URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8494478/
◦ A 2024/2025 Science paper measured the intracellular calcium sensitivity of transmitter release at glutamatergic NEOCORTICAL boutons directly, which is the closest thing yet to a cortical answer. URL: https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.adp0870
◦ BOTTOM LINE: "Hill coefficient approximately 4-5, half-maximal local Ca approximately 10-25 micromolar" is correct for the calyx of Held and is the standard textbook value, but it is NOT universal. Synapse-to-synapse variation in both cooperativity (1.2 to 5) and half-maximal concentration (roughly 10 to more than 100 micromolar) is large and real. What would settle the general case: direct caged-calcium sensor calibration at many more synapse types, which is exactly what the neocortical-bouton work has begun.

• Also relevant: allosteric models supersede the simple Hill description. Lou X, Scheuss V, Schneggenburger R (2005), "Allosteric modulation of the presynaptic Ca2+ sensor for vesicle fusion," Nature 435:497-501. This showed that release is not a pure fifth-power switch - the sensor has an allosteric component that permits slow spontaneous fusion at resting calcium.


SECTION 6 - ENDOGENOUS CALCIUM BUFFERING: THE BINDING RATIO KAPPA

• DEFINITION: the endogenous calcium binding ratio, written kappa or kappa-sub-S (K_S), is the ratio of BOUND calcium to FREE calcium for a small increment of total calcium. A kappa of 100 means that of every 101 calcium ions entering, 100 are captured by buffer and only 1 remains free. It is dimensionless. Practically, kappa scales both the amplitude of the free-calcium transient (divided by kappa+1) and its decay time constant (multiplied by kappa+1).

• FOUNDING PAPER: Neher E, Augustine GJ (1992), "Calcium gradients and buffers in bovine chromaffin cells," Journal of Physiology 450:273-301. URL to citation record: https://modeldb.science/citations/15868 . This paper introduced the "added buffer" method of measuring kappa by loading known amounts of exogenous indicator and extrapolating back to zero added buffer. Value obtained for bovine chromaffin cells: kappa_S = 40 to 75. Companion paper: Zhou Z, Neher E (1993), "Mobile and immobile calcium buffers in bovine adrenal chromaffin cells," Journal of Physiology, URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1143870/

• TABLE OF PUBLISHED KAPPA_S VALUES BY CELL TYPE. The following compilation was fetched from Foehring and colleagues, "Endogenous calcium buffering capacity of substantia nigral dopamine neurons," Journal of Neurophysiology, URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2775382/

◦ Calyx of Held presynaptic terminal: kappa_S approximately 40. Source: Helmchen F, Borst JGG, Sakmann B (1997), Biophysical Journal. Described as a LOW endogenous buffer capacity, probably reflecting mostly immobile endogenous buffers. Later work identified parvalbumin as a mobile presynaptic buffer at this terminal that accelerates calcium decay and shortens facilitation - Muller M et al. (2007), Journal of Neuroscience 27(9):2261, URL: https://www.jneurosci.org/content/27/9/2261

◦ Bovine adrenal chromaffin cells: kappa_S = 40 to 75 (Neher and Augustine 1992).

◦ Spinal motoneurons: kappa_S = 30 to 60.

◦ CA1 hippocampal pyramidal neurons: kappa_S = 30 to 60. NOTE - this is LOWER than the "approximately 100" figure often quoted for hippocampal pyramidal cells; see disagreement note below.

◦ Neocortical pyramidal cells: kappa_S = 100 to 200.

◦ Cortical GABAergic interneurons: kappa_S = 200 to 300. Interneurons buffer harder than the pyramidal cells they inhibit - consistent with their high expression of parvalbumin and calretinin.

◦ Substantia nigra dopamine neurons: kappa_S approximately 110 at postnatal days 13-17 (n = 20), rising to approximately 179 at postnatal days 25-32 (n = 10). Buffer capacity is developmentally regulated. The authors argue this relatively modest buffering may contribute to the selective vulnerability of these neurons in Parkinson's disease.

◦ Snail neurons, crayfish neuromuscular junction, mammalian sympathetic neurons: kappa_S approximately 500 to 600.

◦ CEREBELLAR PURKINJE CELLS: kappa_S approximately 2000 - the highest value measured in any neuron, an order of magnitude larger than any other neuron or neuroendocrine cell studied. Citation: Fierro L, Llano I (1996), "High endogenous calcium buffering in Purkinje cells from rat cerebellar slices," Journal of Physiology. URL: https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1113/jphysiol.1996.sp021713 . Measured in 15-day-old rats. This extraordinary value is attributable largely to the very high concentration of calbindin-D28k and parvalbumin in Purkinje cells.

• RANGE STATEMENT FOR THE CHAPTER: kappa_S spans roughly 30 to 2000 across neuronal types, i.e. a nearly 70-fold range; Neher and Augustine's own framing was that estimates "vary nearly 50-fold across cell types tested to date." The commonly quoted "kappa approximately 20 to 200" covers most ordinary neurons but explicitly EXCLUDES Purkinje cells (approximately 2000) and the invertebrate and sympathetic preparations (500-600). State the exclusion.

• WHERE SOURCES DISAGREE: hippocampal CA1 pyramidal neurons are given as kappa_S = 30-60 in the Foehring compilation, but values near 100 and above appear elsewhere in the literature, and neocortical pyramidal cells are given as 100-200 in the same table. The discrepancy is largely methodological - the added-buffer extrapolation depends on the assumed indicator dissociation constant (Kd) inside the cell, on whether the measurement is somatic or dendritic (dendrites and spines differ from somata), on temperature, and on developmental age. What would settle it: standardized measurements at physiological temperature with in-situ-calibrated indicator Kd, in the same compartment, across ages. Until then, quote kappa for hippocampal pyramidal neurons as "roughly 30 to 100, method-dependent."

• Useful conceptual review on mobile versus immobile buffers and how buffer mobility, not just capacity, shapes domains: "Buffer mobility and the regulation of neuronal calcium domains," Frontiers in Cellular Neuroscience 9:48 (2015). URLs: https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/cellular-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fncel.2015.00048/full and https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4335178/ . Also Schwaller B, "Cytosolic Ca2+ buffers," Cold Spring Harbor Perspectives in Biology 2(11):a004051, URL: https://cshperspectives.cshlp.org/content/2/11/a004051.full.pdf


SECTION 7 - EXTRUSION ACROSS THE PLASMA MEMBRANE: PMCA AND NCX

Primary source for this section: Brini M, Carafoli E (2011), "The plasma membrane Ca2+ ATPase and the plasma membrane sodium calcium exchanger cooperate in the regulation of cell calcium," Cold Spring Harbor Perspectives in Biology 3(2):a004168. Fetched full text: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3039526/ . PubMed: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21421919/

• PMCA - PLASMA MEMBRANE CALCIUM ATPase
◦ Function: ATP-driven primary active transport of Ca2+ out of the cell; the housekeeping pump that sets and holds the resting cytosolic level near 100 nanomolar.
◦ Stoichiometry: Brini and Carafoli state PMCA operates with a 1:1 Ca2+ per ATP stoichiometry, acting as a Ca2+/H+ exchanger; the exact proton stoichiometry remains debated. NOTE THE CONTRAST WITH SERCA, which moves 2 Ca2+ per ATP - PMCA is the less thermodynamically efficient of the two.
◦ Affinity - SOURCES DISAGREE, AND THE DISAGREEMENT IS RESOLVABLE AND WORTH EXPLAINING:
 - Brini and Carafoli 2011 (fetched): Km for Ca2+ is 10 to 20 micromolar in the RESTING, autoinhibited state, falling to LESS THAN 1 micromolar after calmodulin binds.
 - Other widely cited sources (including the Plasma membrane Ca2+ ATPase Wikipedia entry, URL: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plasma_membrane_Ca2%2B_ATPase, and the Physiological Reviews treatment) give a Km of 100 to 200 nanomolar, describing PMCA isoforms and splice variants as having evolved to cover basal Ca2+ regulation in the 100 nanomolar range.
 - RESOLUTION: these are not contradictory measurements but measurements of DIFFERENT STATES of the same pump. PMCA is autoinhibited by its own carboxy-terminal tail; in that state it is low-affinity (10-20 micromolar). Rising calcium loads calmodulin, calmodulin displaces the autoinhibitory tail, and the activated pump becomes high-affinity (100-200 nanomolar, i.e. well under 1 micromolar). The pump is therefore self-activating: it is switched on by the very signal it exists to terminate. State BOTH numbers and this mechanism in the chapter.
◦ Turnover rate: slow. Approximately 30 per second overall; directly measured at 33 hertz for the erythrocyte PMCA; isoforms 1 and 4 operate in the range 10 to 150 per second. Compare NCX below - PMCA is roughly 100 to 500 times slower per molecule.
◦ Summary descriptor: HIGH AFFINITY, LOW CAPACITY. Suited to holding the resting baseline and to clearing the last, smallest residue of a calcium load. Reference for disease relevance in cerebellum: "Plasma membrane calcium ATPases and cerebellar pathology," Biology Direct (2025), URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12590814/ . Broader review: "The plasma membrane calcium ATPases and their role as major new players in human disease," Physiological Reviews (2017), URL: https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/physrev.00028.2016

• NCX - SODIUM/CALCIUM EXCHANGER
◦ Stoichiometry: 3 Na+ in exchange for 1 Ca2+ out. Confirmed in Brini and Carafoli 2011 ("three Na+ ions enter the cell and one Ca2+ ion is extruded against its gradient"), and confirmed structurally and functionally for the prokaryotic archetype NCX_Mj: "The prokaryotic Na+/Ca2+ exchanger NCX_Mj transports Na+ and Ca2+ in a 3:1 stoichiometry," URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5749117/ . Structural mechanism: Liao J et al. (2012), "Structural insight into the ion-exchange mechanism of the sodium/calcium exchanger," Science, URL: https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1215759
◦ ELECTROGENIC: 3 positive charges in for 2 positive charges out means each cycle carries one net positive charge INTO the cell. NCX therefore generates an inward (depolarizing) current when extruding calcium. This is a genuinely important point for neurons - calcium extrusion is not electrically silent, and NCX current contributes to afterdepolarizations and to plateau potentials.
◦ Energy source: it is SECONDARY active transport - it spends the sodium gradient, which the sodium/potassium ATPase maintains at ATP cost. So calcium extrusion by NCX is still ultimately ATP-powered, just indirectly.
◦ REVERSAL: NCX can run in reverse (calcium IN, sodium OUT) when intracellular sodium is high or the membrane is strongly depolarized. This is a major mechanism of pathological calcium loading in ischaemia and excitotoxicity, and should be flagged in any clinical section. Confirmed in the Pflugers Archiv review, "Sodium-calcium exchangers (NCX): molecular hallmarks underlying the tissue-specific and systemic functions," URL: https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00424-013-1405-y
◦ Turnover rate: FAST. A maximum turnover of approximately 5000 per second is estimated for the cardiac exchanger; turnover rates of approximately 5000 per second for both sodium and calcium transport have been detected at non-saturating ion concentrations. Source: Sodium/Calcium Exchanger review, Physiological Reviews (2006), URL: https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/physrev.00018.2005
◦ Affinity for cytosolic Ca2+: LOW. Brini and Carafoli state qualitatively "low Ca2+ affinity" but give NO numerical Km. The frequently quoted figure of approximately 1 to 10 micromolar (sometimes narrowed to 0.5-2 micromolar) is widespread in textbooks but I COULD NOT VERIFY A SPECIFIC PRIMARY NUMBER for the neuronal isoforms in the sources fetched. TREAT THIS AS NOT WELL ESTABLISHED in a single canonical citation. What would settle it: isoform-specific (NCX1, NCX2, NCX3) Km determinations in neurons under matched ionic and voltage conditions - the value is also complicated by the fact that NCX has a separate ALLOSTERIC regulatory calcium site, distinct from the transport site, whose occupancy switches the exchanger on (the "absolute requirement for internal Ca2+" noted in Blaustein and Lederer 1999), so "the Km" is not a single well-posed quantity.
◦ Summary descriptor: LOW AFFINITY, HIGH CAPACITY. Suited to clearing large calcium loads quickly - exactly the job in an excitable cell after a burst.
◦ Canonical review: Blaustein MP, Lederer WJ (1999), "Sodium/calcium exchange: its physiological implications," Physiological Reviews 79(3):763-854. URL: https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/physrev.1999.79.3.763 . Neuron-specific: "Na+/Ca2+ exchangers and Ca2+ transport in neurons," URL: https://link.springer.com/rwe/10.1007/978-0-387-30380-2_11
◦ Astrocytes: NCX can support regenerative calcium entry in thin astrocyte processes - Frontiers in Cellular Neuroscience (2018), URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6102320/ . Worth a sentence if the chapter covers glia.

• THE DIVISION OF LABOUR, in one sentence usable in the chapter: PMCA is the high-affinity, low-capacity, ATP-burning trimmer that holds the resting level at 100 nanomolar; NCX is the low-affinity, high-capacity, sodium-gradient-burning bulk exporter that dumps the big loads fast and, uniquely, carries a depolarizing current while doing so. Brini and Carafoli frame this as PMCA having "a housekeeping role in maintaining cytosolic Ca2+" and NCX "the dynamic role of counteracting large cytosolic Ca2+ variations, especially in excitable cells."

• A THIRD ROUTE not requested but needed for completeness: mitochondria take up calcium via the mitochondrial calcium uniporter (MCU) and release it via the mitochondrial sodium/calcium exchanger NCLX. Review: "NCLX: the mitochondrial sodium calcium exchanger," URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3951392/ . Mitochondria are low-affinity, very-high-capacity buffers that engage only near microdomains where local calcium is high - they matter for large loads and for excitotoxicity.


SECTION 8 - SERCA AND THE ENDOPLASMIC RETICULUM STORE

• ER FREE CALCIUM CONCENTRATION - SOURCES DISAGREE MODERATELY, both values are defensible:
◦ Approximately 100 to 400 micromolar (one commonly cited range).
◦ Approximately 200 to 700 micromolar with a mean of approximately 500 micromolar, measured directly in HEK293 cells with targeted low-affinity ER calcium sensors.
◦ RECOMMENDED CHAPTER STATEMENT: ER luminal free Ca2+ is approximately 100 to 500 micromolar (some cell types and some sensors give up to approximately 700 micromolar). Sources: "Regulation of calcium homeostasis and flux between the endoplasmic reticulum and the cytosol," Journal of Biological Chemistry, URL: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0021925822005014 ; "Endoplasmic reticulum calcium pumps and tumor cell differentiation," URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7247589/ ; "Endoplasmic reticulum calcium signaling in hippocampal neurons," Biomolecules 14(12):1617 (2024), URL: https://www.mdpi.com/2218-273X/14/12/1617
◦ CONSEQUENCE WORTH STATING: with cytosol at approximately 100 nanomolar and ER lumen at approximately 100-500 micromolar, the ER-to-cytosol gradient is 1000- to 5000-fold - the SERCA reviews describe SERCA as generating "a several thousand-fold calcium ion concentration gradient between the cytosol (low nanomolar) and the ER lumen (high micromolar)." The ER is therefore a second, INTERNAL calcium battery, chemically similar in magnitude to the external one, and it can be discharged without any channel in the plasma membrane opening at all. This is the conceptual key to intracellular calcium signalling.
◦ NOT WELL ESTABLISHED: ER free calcium specifically in NEURONAL dendrites and spines in vivo. Most direct measurements come from non-neuronal cell lines. What would settle it: quantitative ratiometric ER-targeted sensors calibrated in situ in identified neurons in slice or in vivo.

• SERCA - SARCO/ENDOPLASMIC RETICULUM CALCIUM ATPase
◦ STOICHIOMETRY: 2 Ca2+ transported from cytosol into the ER/SR lumen per 1 ATP hydrolysed, with 2 or 3 H+ countertransported in the opposite direction (protons balance the charge of the empty binding site). Confirmed across the SERCA literature. URL: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SERCA and the SERCA mechanism papers, e.g. "Structural basis for the function of the C-terminal proton release pathway in the calcium pump," URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8037123/
◦ CONTRAST WITH PMCA: SERCA gets 2 calcium ions per ATP; PMCA gets 1. Filling the internal store is energetically cheaper per ion than exporting to the outside.
◦ AFFINITY: apparent Ca2+ affinity K-half values measured at approximately 0.12 to 0.17 micromolar (specifically 0.17 plus or minus 0.02 and 0.12 plus or minus 0.01 micromolar in the cited work), consistent with the commonly quoted range of 0.1 to 0.4 micromolar. SERCA maintains cytosolic calcium around 0.1 micromolar. High-affinity ubiquitous isoform structural basis: Inoue and colleagues, PNAS (2009), "Structural basis for the high Ca2+ affinity of the ubiquitous SERCA2b Ca2+ pump," URL: https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.0906797106
◦ Isoforms: SERCA1 (fast skeletal muscle), SERCA2a (cardiac and slow muscle), SERCA2b (ubiquitous, including neurons - the high-affinity isoform), SERCA3 (various). Neurons rely chiefly on SERCA2b.
◦ Pharmacology to mention: thapsigargin and cyclopiazonic acid are the standard SERCA blockers used to empty the store experimentally. Reference on SERCA drug interactions: URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5904271/
◦ SERCA is regulated by small transmembrane micropeptides - phospholamban (inhibitory, cardiac), sarcolipin (inhibitory), and stimulatory micropeptides. Mechanism: phospholamban inhibits by reversing the allosteric enhancement of calcium affinity by ATP. URL: https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.1101/2023.07.20.549928.full.pdf


SECTION 9 - THE RELEASE CHANNELS: IP3 RECEPTOR AND RYANODINE RECEPTOR

Master modern reference for this whole section: Woll KA, Van Petegem F (2022), "Calcium-release channels: structure and function of IP3 receptors and ryanodine receptors," Physiological Reviews 102(1):209-268. URLs: https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/physrev.00033.2020 and https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34280054/ . (The full-text URL returned HTTP 403 to automated fetch; content below was confirmed from search-result extracts of this review and from independent primary sources.)

• SHARED PROPERTIES
◦ Both are giant tetrameric channels in the ER/SR membrane and are evolutionarily related. RyR is the largest known ion channel, at roughly 2.2 megadaltons as a tetramer.
◦ Both are POORLY SELECTIVE for calcium. The Woll and Van Petegem review gives a permeability ratio of Ca2+ to K+ of approximately 7 (an independent RyR source gives selectivity Ca2+/K+ of approximately 6). They conduct monovalent cations (Na+, K+) and divalent cations (Ca2+, Mg2+). This is a crucial contrast with plasma-membrane voltage-gated calcium channels, whose Ca2+ over Na+ selectivity is in the thousands. The release channels do not need high selectivity because the ER lumen is a calcium-rich, high-calcium compartment; they need THROUGHPUT.
◦ Both are activated by cytosolic Ca2+ - this is calcium-induced calcium release (CICR), the positive-feedback mechanism that lets a small trigger calcium signal be amplified and propagated as a regenerative wave.
◦ Both display a BELL-SHAPED dependence of open probability on cytosolic Ca2+: activated by modest calcium, INHIBITED by high calcium. This built-in self-termination is what stops CICR from being an all-or-none catastrophe and is what makes discrete, self-limiting events (puffs, sparks) possible.

• RYANODINE RECEPTOR (RyR) QUANTITATIVE PROPERTIES
◦ SINGLE-CHANNEL CONDUCTANCE: approximately 100 picosiemens when Ca2+ is the charge carrier; approximately 700 to 750 picosiemens when K+ is the charge carrier. These are enormous - for comparison a typical voltage-gated sodium channel is around 15-20 picosiemens and an AMPA receptor around 10 picosiemens. RyR is among the highest-conductance channels known. Sources: Woll and Van Petegem (2022) Physiological Reviews; Fill M, Copello JA (2002), "Ryanodine receptor calcium release channels," Physiological Reviews 82:893-922, URL: https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/physrev.00013.2002 ; Lindsay ARG et al. (1991), "Monovalent cation conductance in the ryanodine receptor-channel of sheep cardiac muscle sarcoplasmic reticulum," Journal of Physiology, URL: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1716676/
◦ The rate of cation translocation through the RyR pore is higher than that of other ion channels, attributable to a short, wide selectivity filter (Pancreapedia RyR entry, URL: https://pancreapedia.org/molecules/ryanodine-receptor).
◦ Bell-shaped calcium dependence: cytosolic Ca2+ is the most potent activator; feedback inhibition occurs through binding of Ca2+ to LOW-affinity calcium binding sites at higher concentrations. The founding work on calcium-induced calcium release and its calcium-dependent inactivation is Fabiato A (1983 and subsequent). Structural confirmation of the calcium INACTIVATION site: "Ca2+ inactivation of the mammalian ryanodine receptor type 1 in a lipidic environment revealed by cryo-EM," eLife (2022), URL: https://elifesciences.org/articles/75568
◦ Magnesium is a physiologically important competitive brake, binding at both the activation and the inhibition sites: URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8821920/
◦ Isoforms: RyR1 (skeletal muscle), RyR2 (cardiac; also the dominant neuronal isoform in many regions), RyR3 (brain-enriched). Neuronal role example: CICR through RyR3 modulates the slow afterhyperpolarizing current (sIAHP) in hippocampal pyramidal neurons - URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7304577/ . This is a concrete, citable example of ER calcium release shaping neuronal firing.
◦ Countercurrent point worth a footnote: because RyR passes monovalents so well, it carries its own charge-compensating countercurrent, so SR membrane potential does not collapse during release. Gillespie and Fill, Biophysical Journal (2008), URL: https://www.cell.com/biophysj/fulltext/S0006-3495(08)00671-1

• IP3 RECEPTOR (IP3R) QUANTITATIVE PROPERTIES
◦ THE FOUNDING BELL-SHAPE PAPER: Bezprozvanny I, Watras J, Ehrlich BE (1991), "Bell-shaped calcium-response curves of Ins(1,4,5)P3- and calcium-gated channels from endoplasmic reticulum of cerebellum," Nature 351(6329):751-754. URL: https://www.nature.com/articles/351751a0
 - The open probability of BOTH channel types (the InsP3-gated channel and the calcium-gated / ryanodine channel), reconstituted from cerebellar ER into planar lipid bilayers, showed a bell-shaped dependence on free calcium.
 - For the InsP3-gated channel the MAXIMUM open probability occurred at 0.2 MICROMOLAR free calcium, with sharp decreases on either side of the maximum.
 - Interpretation: cytosolic calcium is simultaneously a CO-AGONIST (with IP3) and an INHIBITOR of the type-1 IP3 receptor. Note that the peak sits at 0.2 micromolar, only about twice the resting cytosolic level - so the IP3R is poised right at the foot of its activation curve at rest, which is why small calcium rises are so effective at recruiting it.
◦ SINGLE-CHANNEL CONDUCTANCE: IP3R, like RyR, is a large-conductance, poorly selective cation channel. The frequently quoted figure is approximately 50 to 80 picosiemens in physiological divalent conditions (commonly rounded to approximately 70 picosiemens), with much larger values in monovalent-only conditions. I COULD NOT VERIFY A SINGLE PRIMARY CITATION FOR THE 70 PICOSIEMEN NUMBER in the sources fetched - treat it as approximate and cite Woll and Van Petegem (2022) as the review rather than asserting a precise primary value.
◦ FLUX RATE - a well-sourced and vivid number: an open IP3R passes approximately 500,000 Ca2+ ions per second. That is roughly half a million ions from a single channel in one second, which is why a single channel opening produces a detectable local "puff."
◦ Modern structural basis of gating by IP3 and Ca2+: "Structural basis for the regulation of inositol trisphosphate receptors by Ca2+ and IP3," Nature Structural and Molecular Biology (2018), URL: https://www.nature.com/articles/s41594-018-0089-6 ; and "Structural basis for activation and gating of IP3 receptors," Nature Communications (2022), URL: https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-022-29073-2
◦ Single-channel kinetic modelling of type-1 IP3R by IP3 and calcium: URL: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10352034/
◦ Signalling context: Berridge MJ (1993), "Inositol trisphosphate and calcium signalling," Nature 361:315-325, URL: https://www.nature.com/articles/361315a0 - the canonical framing reference for the IP3 pathway.
◦ Luminal calcium also controls IP3R gating, so the store senses its own fill level: "Ca2+ release induced by inositol 1,4,5-trisphosphate is a steady-state phenomenon controlled by luminal Ca2+ in permeabilized cells," Nature 357:599 (1992), URL: https://www.nature.com/articles/357599a0
◦ Neuronal relevance: quantitative interactions between the A-type potassium current and IP3 receptors regulate intraneuronal calcium waves and synaptic plasticity - URL: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23283761/

• WHERE SOURCES DISAGREE ON RELEASE CHANNELS: single-channel conductance values vary substantially with the charge carrier used (K+ versus Cs+ versus Ca2+), its concentration, and the lipid environment, so quoted picosiemen values are not directly comparable across papers unless the ionic conditions are matched. The bell-shape peak position for IP3R also shifts with IP3 concentration - higher IP3 broadens and right-shifts the curve - so "peak at 0.2 micromolar" is specific to the conditions of Bezprozvanny 1991 (cerebellar type-1 receptor in bilayers), not a universal constant. What would settle it: standardized bilayer and on-nucleus patch measurements under matched physiological ionic conditions across isoforms. On-nucleus patch clamp is the emerging method - see "Characterization of ryanodine receptor type 1 single channel activity using on-nucleus patch clamp," URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4120733/ and "IP3 receptors - lessons from analyses ex cellula," Journal of Cell Science (2019), URL: https://journals.biologists.com/jcs/article/132/4/jcs222463/57535/


SECTION 10 - CALCIUM DIFFUSION AND THE RANGE OF THE SIGNAL

• THE KEY PAPER: Allbritton NL, Meyer T, Stryer L (1992), "Range of messenger action of calcium ion and inositol 1,4,5-trisphosphate," Science 258(5089):1812-1815. URL: https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1465619 . Measurements were made in a cytosolic extract from Xenopus laevis oocytes.
◦ MEASURED DIFFUSION COEFFICIENT OF Ca2+ IN CYTOSOLIC EXTRACT: D increased from 13 to 65 square micrometres per second as free calcium was raised from about 90 nanomolar to 1 micromolar. The reason D rises with calcium is buffer SATURATION - at higher calcium the buffers are increasingly occupied, so a larger fraction of the calcium is free to move, and apparent mobility goes up. Calcium's effective diffusion coefficient is therefore NOT A CONSTANT; it depends on the calcium concentration itself. This is a genuinely important and often-omitted point.
◦ COMPUTED RANGES OF MESSENGER ACTION (from diffusion coefficient combined with lifetime): free Ca2+ before it is buffered, approximately 0.1 micrometre; buffered Ca2+, approximately 5 micrometres; IP3, approximately 24 micrometres.
◦ CONCLUSION OF THE PAPER, in the authors' framing: for a transient point source of messenger in cells smaller than 20 micrometres, IP3 is a GLOBAL messenger whereas Ca2+ acts in RESTRICTED DOMAINS. This single sentence is the best available one-line justification for the entire microdomain concept and is worth quoting.

• DIFFUSION COEFFICIENT OF Ca2+ IN FREE AQUEOUS SOLUTION: 790 square micrometres per second at 25 degrees Celsius (equivalently 7.9 times 10 to the minus 6 square centimetres per second). The value approximately 780 square micrometres per second is also widely quoted; the two differ only by the temperature and ionic-strength assumptions. Quote it as "approximately 780 to 790 square micrometres per second."

• THE HEADLINE COMPARISON FOR THE CHAPTER: calcium diffuses at roughly 780-790 square micrometres per second in free water but at only about 13 to 65 square micrometres per second in cytosol - a slowing of roughly 12-fold to 60-fold, caused almost entirely by binding to endogenous buffers rather than by cytoplasmic viscosity or crowding. In muscle the reported reduction is about fiftyfold. Because a diffusion distance scales as the SQUARE ROOT of D times time, a 50-fold reduction in D shortens the distance reached in a given time by about sevenfold. That is what confines the calcium signal to its point of entry.

• WHERE SOURCES DISAGREE - AN IMPORTANT AND UNDER-APPRECIATED CONFLICT:
◦ Donahue BS, Abercrombie RF (1987), "Free diffusion coefficient of ionic calcium in cytoplasm," Cell Calcium 8(6):437-448. URLs: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pubmed/3435913 and https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/0143416087900273 . When precautions were taken to MINIMIZE sequestration and chelation of the radiotracer calcium-45, the measured cytoplasmic diffusion coefficient was 5.3 times 10 to the minus 6 square centimetres per second - THAT IS 530 SQUARE MICROMETRES PER SECOND, close to the free-solution value and roughly 10 to 40 times higher than the Allbritton 1992 numbers.
◦ These are not in conflict once the distinction is made explicit: 530 square micrometres per second is the diffusion coefficient of the FREE calcium ION itself once buffering and uptake are suppressed; 13-65 square micrometres per second is the EFFECTIVE or APPARENT diffusion coefficient of calcium in a normal buffered cytosol, which is what actually governs signal spread. Donahue and Abercrombie also reported that in untreated cytoplasm calcium migration is complex and CANNOT be described by a single diffusion coefficient at all, and that bulk movement of calcium buffers may occur.
◦ A more recent challenge: "High rates of calcium-free diffusion in the cytosol of living cells," Biophysical Journal (2021). URLs: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0006349521006858 and https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8510970/ . This work argues that free calcium diffuses faster in living cytosol than the classic effective-D values imply.
◦ RECOMMENDED CHAPTER TREATMENT: give BOTH numbers and label them properly. "The free calcium ion, with buffering suppressed, diffuses at roughly 500-800 square micrometres per second, essentially as it does in water. In real cytosol, buffering reduces its EFFECTIVE diffusion coefficient to roughly 13-65 square micrometres per second, and that effective value rises with calcium concentration as buffers saturate. A recent line of work (Biophysical Journal 2021) argues the true free-ion mobility in living cytosol is higher than classical effective estimates, so the exact figure remains under active revision." That is an honest statement of the state of the field.
◦ WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: single-molecule or fluorescence-correlation measurement of calcium mobility in intact neuronal cytoplasm with buffering characterized independently, at physiological temperature, resolving free-ion mobility and buffer-bound mobility separately.

• Applied to neurons - useful supporting reference: "Barriers to diffusion in dendrites and estimation of calcium spread following synaptic inputs," PLOS Computational Biology (2011). URL: https://journals.plos.org/ploscompbiol/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pcbi.1002182 . Relevant to whether a spine is a diffusionally isolated calcium compartment - the spine neck is the barrier, and this is the mechanistic basis for the claim that each dendritic spine is an independent calcium compartment and therefore an independent unit of plasticity.


SECTION 11 - SUMMARY TABLE OF HEADLINE NUMBERS

• Extracellular free Ca2+, human CSF: 1.0 mM free, 1.2 mM total, 86 percent ionized (Forsberg et al. 2019, Journal of Neurochemistry)
• Plasma total Ca: 2.2-2.6 mM, about half protein-bound; plasma free Ca approximately 1.1-1.3 mM
• Resting cytosolic free Ca2+: 50-100 nM (Simon and Llinas 1985 used 50 nM; Allbritton et al. 1992 measured at about 90 nM)
• Transmembrane gradient: approximately 10,000- to 20,000-fold
• E_Ca at 37 degrees Celsius: approximately +125 to +135 mV (computed here; z = 2, 30.8 mV per decade)
• Nanodomain Ca2+ at 20 nm from an open channel: approximately 100 micromolar (Neher 1998)
• Microdomain Ca2+ at 200 nm: approximately 5-10 micromolar (Neher 1998)
• Measured presynaptic microdomains, squid: approximately 100-250 micromolar (Llinas et al. 1992, Science)
• Ca2+ cooperativity of release, external-Ca measurement: slope 3.78 plus or minus 0.2, n = 28 (Dodge and Rahamimoff 1967)
• Intracellular Ca2+ for fast release, calyx of Held: 10 micromolar step; up to 25 micromolar for approximately 0.5 ms during an action potential (Schneggenburger and Neher 2000, Nature)
• Release rate at 10 micromolar: approximately 1000 vesicles per second per releasable vesicle (Bollmann et al. 2000, Science)
• Buffer binding ratio kappa_S: calyx of Held approximately 40 (Helmchen et al. 1997); chromaffin cells 40-75 (Neher and Augustine 1992); CA1 pyramidal 30-60; neocortical pyramidal 100-200; cortical interneurons 200-300; substantia nigra dopamine neurons 110 to 179 with age; Purkinje cells approximately 2000 (Fierro and Llano 1996)
• PMCA: Km 10-20 micromolar autoinhibited, less than 1 micromolar (100-200 nM) calmodulin-activated; turnover approximately 30 per second (33 Hz erythrocyte); 1 Ca2+ per ATP; high affinity low capacity
• NCX: 3 Na+ per 1 Ca2+, electrogenic (net one positive charge inward per cycle); turnover approximately 5000 per second; low affinity, high capacity; reverses under high internal Na+ or depolarization
• SERCA: 2 Ca2+ per ATP, 2-3 H+ countertransported; K-half approximately 0.12-0.17 micromolar
• ER luminal free Ca2+: approximately 100-500 micromolar (up to approximately 700 micromolar in some measurements); ER-to-cytosol gradient approximately 1000- to 5000-fold
• RyR single-channel conductance: approximately 100 pS with Ca2+, approximately 700-750 pS with K+; Ca2+/K+ selectivity approximately 6-7
• IP3R: bell-shaped open probability peaking at 0.2 micromolar free Ca2+ (Bezprozvanny et al. 1991, Nature 351:751); approximately 500,000 Ca2+ ions per second when open
• D of Ca2+ in free water: approximately 780-790 square micrometres per second at 25 degrees Celsius
• Effective D of Ca2+ in cytosol: 13 square micrometres per second at 90 nM rising to 65 square micrometres per second at 1 micromolar (Allbritton, Meyer and Stryer 1992, Science 258:1812)
• Free-ion D in cytoplasm with buffering suppressed: 530 square micrometres per second (Donahue and Abercrombie 1987) - CONFLICTS with the effective value; see Section 10
• Range of action: free Ca2+ approximately 0.1 micrometre; buffered Ca2+ approximately 5 micrometres; IP3 approximately 24 micrometres (Allbritton et al. 1992)


SECTION 12 - ITEMS I COULD NOT FULLY VERIFY, LISTED HONESTLY

• The specific Km of neuronal NCX for cytosolic calcium. The qualitative "low affinity, high capacity" is very well sourced (Brini and Carafoli 2011); a specific micromolar Km for NCX1/2/3 in neurons was not obtainable from the sources fetched. The quantity is also arguably ill-posed because NCX has a separate allosteric regulatory calcium site. Do not state a precise number without a primary citation.

• The precise IP3 receptor single-channel conductance in picosiemens under physiological divalent conditions. The approximately 70 picosiemen figure is widely repeated but I did not reach a primary measurement. Cite the Woll and Van Petegem 2022 Physiological Reviews review rather than a specific value.

• Whether the standard "kappa approximately 100" for hippocampal pyramidal neurons or the "30-60" from the Foehring compilation is correct. Method-dependent; give a range.

• ER luminal free calcium specifically in neuronal dendrites and spines in vivo - mostly extrapolated from cell lines.

• Several key full texts returned HTTP 403 to automated fetching and were confirmed only through search-result extracts rather than direct reading: Augustine, Santamaria and Tanaka 2003 (Neuron, Cell Press); Neher 1998 (Neuron, ScienceDirect); Woll and Van Petegem 2022 (Physiological Reviews); Brini and Carafoli 2011 at the Cold Spring Harbor Perspectives host (though the PubMed Central mirror at https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3039526/ was fetched successfully and is the source of the PMCA and NCX quantitative claims). The bibliographic details and the specific numbers attributed to these papers above came from indexed extracts of the papers themselves; anyone finalizing the chapter should read the three paywalled papers directly to confirm the exact figures before print.</result>
