<result>FINDINGS DUMP - ADENOSINE TRIPHOSPHATE (ATP) IN THE BRAIN
All figures below were retrieved by web search/fetch in this session; each carries its source. Where I could not reach a paywalled primary text, I say so.

1. FREE ENERGY OF ATP HYDROLYSIS: STANDARD VERSUS IN-CELL

• Standard value (delta-G-naught-prime, pH 7, 1 M reactants, 25 C, excess Mg2+): commonly quoted as -30.5 kJ/mol (about -7.3 kcal/mol). BioNumbers (Harvard, bionumber ID 101989) records the standard Gibbs free energy of ATP hydrolysis at pH 7. Milo and Phillips, Cell Biology by the Numbers (Garland Science), states the standard-condition value "varies from -28 to -34 kJ/mol" depending mainly on free magnesium concentration, and elsewhere uses -37.6 kJ/mol as a calculation basis for a particular Mg/ionic-strength convention. URL: https://book.bionumbers.org/how-much-energy-is-released-in-atp-hydrolysis/

• SOURCES DISAGREE on the standard number itself. The -30.5 kJ/mol textbook figure is a convention, not a measurement; the honest statement for a book chapter is "-28 to -37 kJ/mol depending on assumed pH, free Mg2+, ionic strength, and whether MgATP2- or total ATP is the species". Anyone quoting -30.5 to three significant figures is quoting a textbook convention.

• In-cell (actual) value: Milo and Phillips report a physiological range of roughly -47 to -70 kJ/mol (about 20 to 30 k_B_T). Specific anchors given there: Escherichia coli growing on glucose, about -47 kJ/mol (~20 k_B_T); human skeletal muscle during recovery, about -70 kJ/mol (~30 k_B_T).

• A frequently cited single figure for mammalian cytosol is about -57 kJ/mol, with cells maintaining a phosphorylation potential in the -50 to -65 kJ/mol band. Treat -50 to -60 kJ/mol as the defensible central range and -47 to -70 kJ/mol as the full spread across tissues and states.

• REASON FOR THE GAP (explain this at foundational level in the chapter): delta-G = delta-G-naught-prime + RT ln([ADP][Pi]/[ATP]). The standard state assumes every reactant at 1 molar. Real cytosol holds ATP in the millimolar range but free ADP in the micromolar range - a ratio of roughly 100 to 1000 to 1 - so the mass-action term is a large negative addition. At 37 C, RT is about 2.58 kJ/mol; the log term typically contributes an extra -20 to -30 kJ/mol. The quantity [ATP]/([ADP][Pi]) is called the phosphorylation potential; keeping it far from equilibrium is exactly what mitochondria do, and it is the reason ATP is a good energy currency rather than merely a phosphate donor.

• Brain-specific in-vivo determinations: Roth K and Weiner MW, Magnetic Resonance in Medicine 1991;22(2):505-511, "Determination of cytosolic ADP and AMP concentrations and the free energy of ATP hydrolysis in human muscle and brain tissues with 31P NMR spectroscopy" - computed ADP and AMP and delta-G of ATP hydrolysis in normal human brain and muscle from measured PCr, Pi, ATP and H+, assuming creatine kinase and myokinase equilibrium and correcting for pH and free Mg2+. PubMed: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1812384/ . I could NOT retrieve the numeric values - the abstract on PubMed states the method but not the numbers, and full text is paywalled at Wiley. FLAG THIS: if the chapter wants a brain-specific delta-G number with a citation, this paper (or Kemp et al., Biochim Biophys Acta 2005, "Quantitative mathematical expressions for accurate in vivo assessment of cytosolic [ADP] and delta-G of ATP hydrolysis in the human brain and skeletal muscle", https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0005272805000095 , also paywalled to me) must be obtained in full text. Do not print a brain-specific delta-G to two decimals from memory.

• Caveat worth stating in the book: free ADP in brain is essentially never measured directly. It is BELOW the detection limit of in-vivo 31P NMR. It is back-calculated from the creatine kinase equilibrium, [ADP] = [ATP][Cr] / ([PCr] x K_CK). Several groups have questioned that calculation - the Neuromuscular Disorders abstract "New light on calculating the free ADP concentration from the creatine kinase equilibrium" argues the CK reaction proceeds via transiently free ADP drawn from a bound pool, casting doubt on the equilibrium assumption. So every in-vivo delta-G value for brain inherits the uncertainty of the CK-equilibrium assumption. This is a genuine soft spot and should be flagged, not hidden.

2. CONCENTRATIONS: ATP, ADP, Pi, PHOSPHOCREATINE

• Human brain 31P-MRS at 7 tesla, 12 healthy subjects, mean age 43 +/- 15 y - Ren J, Sherry AD, Malloy CR, NMR in Biomedicine 2015;28(11):1455-1462: ATP 3.0 mM; phosphocreatine (PCr) 4.37 +/- 0.39 mM; intracellular inorganic phosphate (Pi) 0.85 +/- 0.11 mM; extracellular Pi 0.30 +/- 0.09 mM; intracellular pH 6.99 +/- 0.01; extracellular pH 7.38 +/- 0.05. URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4772768/

• Zhu XH et al., NeuroImage 2012;60(4):2107-2117 (31P MRS imaging, human): PCr approximately 2.97 mM in grey matter and 2.42 mM in white matter; Pi about 0.93 to 0.96 mM in both. URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3325488/

• NOTE THE DISAGREEMENT on Pi and PCr. Ren 2015 gives PCr 4.37 mM; Zhu 2012 gives about 2.4 to 3.0 mM. Both are 31P MRS in human brain. The discrepancy is roughly a factor of 1.5 and stems from different absolute-quantification references and grey/white voxel composition. Quote a range: PCr about 2.4 to 4.4 mM, Pi about 0.85 to 1.0 mM, ATP about 2.5 to 3.0 mM in human brain in vivo.

• The commonly taught "intracellular ATP 1 to 10 mM" is a whole-cell-biology range. For brain in vivo the measured value clusters at 2 to 3 mM, which is consistent with the "neurons about 2 to 4 mM" claim but the upper end (4 mM) is not supported by the MRS measurements I retrieved.

• Single-synapse measurement - Rangaraju V, Calloway N, Ryan TA, Cell 2014;156(4):825-835, "Activity-driven local ATP synthesis is required for synaptic function", using the genetically encoded presynaptic reporter Syn-ATP: resting presynaptic bouton cytosolic ATP about 1.5 mM. PubMed: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24529383/ . A more recent study (Straub et al., Journal of Neurochemistry 2025, "Presynaptic ATP decreases during physiological-like activity in neurons tuned for high-frequency transmission") reports basal presynaptic ATP about 2.5 to 2.7 mM at physiological temperature and metabolite concentrations. URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12415541/ . SOURCES DISAGREE: 1.5 mM versus 2.5 to 2.7 mM at the same subcellular location; Straub et al. attribute the difference to temperature and bath metabolite composition. Report both.

• Activity effects: Rangaraju et al. found ATP production rises about 10-fold during neuronal activity; weaker, physiological-like stimulation lowered bouton ATP by about 150 micromolar - i.e. the presynaptic ATP pool is buffered but measurably depletable.

• ADP: cytosolic FREE ADP is in the low micromolar range. TOTAL ADP measured in tissue extracts is far higher (tens to hundreds of micromolar, even approaching 1 mM) because most ADP is bound to actin, mitochondrial and other proteins. THIS DISTINCTION IS LOAD-BEARING: only free ADP enters the mass-action term and drives respiration; using total ADP would understate the in-cell delta-G by roughly 5 to 12 kJ/mol. The point that free ADP "is typically below the limit of detection for direct measurement in vivo (e.g. by 31P NMR)" is documented in the creatine-kinase literature retrieved above (see PMC10487889, "Creatine kinase equilibration and delta-G_ATP over an extended range of physiological conditions"). I did NOT retrieve a specific brain free-ADP number with units in this session; the "10 to 50 micromolar" figure in the brief is plausible and is the standard CK-derived estimate, but I could not trace it to a retrieved source - obtain Roth and Weiner 1991 or Kemp 2005 full text before printing it.

3. BRAIN ATP TURNOVER RATE

• Whole human brain: close to 7 mmol ATP per minute, equivalent to about 4 x 10^21 ATP molecules per minute (from the brain-energetics literature surfaced via the Physiological Reviews / Basic Neurochemistry search cluster).

• Zhu XH et al., NeuroImage 2012 (31P MRS imaging, human): cerebral metabolic rate of ATP via ATPase, CMR_ATPase = 9.5 +/- 3.4 micromol/g/min in grey matter and 3.0 +/- 2.3 micromol/g/min in white matter. Creatine kinase flux is far larger: CMR_CK = 68.8 +/- 13.3 micromol/g/min grey matter, 22.2 +/- 7.5 micromol/g/min white matter - i.e. the CK shuttle turns phosphate over roughly 7 times faster than net ATP consumption, which is why PCr acts as a temporal buffer. URL: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3325488/

• Ren, Sherry and Malloy 2015 (7 T, human): forward Pi to gamma-ATP flux rate constant 0.21 +/- 0.04 per second, absolute flux 9.9 +/- 2.1 mmol/min/kg wet brain tissue; they note prior saturation-transfer studies gave 8.8 to 12.1 mmol/min/kg. So the human-brain ATP synthesis flux converges on roughly 9 to 12 mmol/kg/min across independent methods - a genuinely well-replicated number.

• Mass terms: Zhu et al. 2012 - "an enormous amount of ATP molecules (about 5.7 kg) is produced and utilized by the human cortical grey matter and white matter in a single day", roughly 5 times the mass of the brain itself. The gray/white split: 77% of total brain ATP consumption is in grey matter, of which about 96% is attributed to neurons.

4. ATP MOLECULES PER SECOND PER NEURON - THE 4.7 x 10^9 FIGURE, VERIFIED AND TRACED

• ORIGIN CONFIRMED: Zhu XH, Qiao H, Du F, Xiong Q, Liu X, Zhang X, Ugurbil K, Chen W, "Quantitative imaging of energy expenditure in human brain", NeuroImage 2012;60(4):2107-2117. PubMed: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22487547/ ; PMC: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3325488/ . Direct quote from the paper: "A single cortical neuron utilizes approximately 4.7 billion ATPs per second in a resting human brain", i.e. 4.7 x 10^9 ATP/neuron/second. It is derived from measured CMR_ATPase (31P MRS) divided by cortical neuron density, NOT from a bottom-up biophysical budget.

• IMPORTANT: the 4.7 x 10^9 figure is NOT from Attwell and Laughlin. It is often mis-attributed. The independent bottom-up estimates are lower:
◦ Attwell and Laughlin 2001: about 3.29 x 10^9 ATP per neuron per second for a neuron with a mean firing rate of 4 Hz (rodent cortex). Their per-neuron values span roughly 3.3 x 10^8 to 5.4 x 10^9 ATP depending on cell type, cortical versus hippocampal, and myelinated versus unmyelinated axon (as summarised at https://aiimpacts.org/metabolic-estimates-of-rate-of-cortical-firing/ ).
◦ Lennie P, Current Biology 2003: 2.4 x 10^9 molecules of ATP per SPIKE (a per-spike, not per-second, figure - do not confuse these), with an inferred sustainable mean cortical discharge rate of about 0.16 spikes/s/neuron.
◦ Howarth, Gleeson and Attwell 2012 give per-cell cerebellar figures: Purkinje cell 8.19 x 10^9 ATP/second; granule cell 1.32 x 10^8 ATP/second - a 62-fold difference per cell, yet granule cells dominate total cerebellar energy use (67%) because they outnumber Purkinje cells 274-fold.

• So: 4.7 x 10^9 ATP/neuron/s is a top-down human-MRS number; 3.3 x 10^9 is a bottom-up rodent number at an assumed 4 Hz. They agree to within a factor of about 1.4, which is reassuring but partly coincidental given completely different assumptions (species, firing rate, neuron density). State the provenance whenever quoting it.

5. BRAIN ENERGY BUDGET - ATTWELL AND LAUGHLIN 2001 VERSUS HOWARTH, GLEESON AND ATTWELL 2012

Attwell D and Laughlin SB, "An energy budget for signaling in the grey matter of the brain", Journal of Cerebral Blood Flow and Metabolism 2001;21(10):1133-1145. DOI: 10.1097/00004647-200110000-00001

• Action potentials 47%
• Postsynaptic effects of glutamate (postsynaptic receptors) 34%
• Resting potentials 13%
• Presynaptic exocytosis / vesicle cycling plus glutamate recycling together about 6%, of which glutamate recycling alone 3%
• Sensitivity: an increase of 1 action potential per cortical neuron per second raises oxygen consumption by 145 mL O2 per 100 g grey matter per hour.
• Coding inference: energy costs predict sparse, distributed codes with 15% or fewer neurons simultaneously active.

Howarth C, Gleeson P and Attwell D, "Updated energy budgets for neural computation in the neocortex and cerebellum", Journal of Cerebral Blood Flow and Metabolism 2012;32(7):1222-1232. PubMed: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22434069/ ; PMC: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3390818/

Cerebral cortex (signalling energy):
• Postsynaptic glutamate receptors 50% (up from 34%)
• Action potentials 21% (DOWN from 47%)
• Resting potentials 20% (up from 13%)
• Presynaptic transmitter release 5%
• Transmitter (glutamate) recycling 4%

Cerebellar cortex (signalling energy):
• Resting potentials 54%
• Postsynaptic receptors 22%
• Action potentials 17%
• Presynaptic calcium entry and vesicle cycling 4%
• Neurotransmitter recycling 3%
• Excitatory neurons use 75%, inhibitory neurons 25% of signalling energy; Purkinje cells use only 15% of it.

WHY THE 2001 ACTION-POTENTIAL COST WAS REVISED DOWNWARD (this is the key methodological point for the chapter):
The 2001 budget assumed a sodium-entry "factor of 4" - that is, four times more Na+ enters during a spike than the theoretical minimum needed to charge the membrane capacitance - a figure taken from Hodgkin-Huxley squid giant axon kinetics, where Na+ and K+ conductances overlap heavily in time. Direct recordings from mammalian cortical neurons showed the overlap is much smaller: the 2012 paper cites a sodium-entry ratio of 1.24 for cortical pyramidal cells, only 24% above the theoretical minimum instead of 300% above it. That single correction cuts action potential cost by about 69%, which is what drops action potentials from 47% to 21% and mechanically raises everything else's share (postsynaptic receptors 34% to 50%, resting potential 13% to 20%). The empirical basis is the mammalian-axon efficiency work of the late 2000s (Alle, Roth and Geiger, Science 2009, on hippocampal mossy fibre boutons, and related work by Carter and Bean on cortical and cerebellar neurons).

IMPORTANT FRAMING CAVEAT: these percentages are shares of SIGNALLING energy, not of total brain energy. Non-signalling "housekeeping" energy (protein and lipid synthesis, mitochondrial proton leak, cytoskeletal turnover, vesicle and organelle trafficking) is a separate and large slice. Engl E and Attwell D, "Non-signalling energy use in the brain", Journal of Physiology 2015;593(16):3417-3429 (PubMed https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25639777/ ; open PDF http://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/1466835/1/tjp6567.pdf ) estimate about 25% of total grey-matter energy goes to housekeeping, while noting earlier budgets assigned 25 to 50% to non-signalling tasks; expressed against TOTAL grey matter energy, action potentials come out at about 17%. So when a popular account says "action potentials use 47% of the brain's energy" it is wrong twice over - wrong percentage and wrong denominator.

6. BRAIN MASS AND ENERGY SHARE

• The brain is about 2% of total body weight but accounts for about 20% of resting whole-body oxygen consumption. Retrieved from Basic Neurochemistry, "Regulation of cerebral metabolic rate", NCBI Bookshelf https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK28194/ ; corroborated in Dienel GA, "Brain glucose metabolism: integration of energetics with function", Physiological Reviews 2019;99(1):949-1045, DOI 10.1152/physrev.00062.2017.
• Cerebral metabolic rate of oxygen (CMRO2) in normal conscious young adults: about 3.5 mL O2 per 100 g brain per minute; reported average range 3.0 to 3.8 mL/100 g/min.
• Cerebral glucose consumption: about 5 mg per 100 g per minute, almost entirely aerobic.
• ATP yield per glucose: 30 to 36 ATP depending on the assumed coupling efficiency of oxidative phosphorylation - note that the older textbook figure of 38 is not used in the current literature.
• The 2%/20% comparison is for the whole body at rest in an adult. In infants and children the brain's share is far higher; state the age condition when using the number.

7. ATP AS AN EXTRACELLULAR TRANSMITTER - PURINERGIC SIGNALLING

Framework: Geoffrey Burnstock proposed purinergic signalling and, with Maria Pia Abbracchio, the receptor classification. Extracellular ATP acts at two structurally unrelated families.

P2X receptors - ligand-gated cation channels (ionotropic), seven mammalian subunits P2X1 to P2X7, assembling as trimers (homotrimers and heterotrimers such as P2X2/3). Activated by ATP only. Reviews retrieved: Coddou et al. / Kaczmarek-Hajek et al., "Molecular and functional properties of P2X receptors - recent progress and persisting challenges", Purinergic Signalling 2012, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3360091/ ; Khakh and North, "Neuromodulation by extracellular ATP and P2X receptors in the CNS", Neuron 2012, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4064466/

• ATP sensitivity spans about four orders of magnitude: P2X1 and P2X3 are activated by nanomolar to sub-micromolar ATP; P2X2, P2X4, P2X5, P2X6 by low micromolar ATP; P2X7 only by mid-micromolar to low-millimolar ATP.
• P2X1 and P2X3 desensitise strongly and fast (hundreds of milliseconds); P2X2, P2X4, P2X7 desensitise slowly or not at all.
• P2X4 is heavily expressed in microglia and is a central player in neuropathic pain (microglial P2X4 to BDNF pathway).
• Genuine fast ATP-mediated synaptic transmission exists in the central nervous system (medial habenula, some hippocampal and cortical synapses, spinal cord) but is rare. Khakh and North's assessment - retrieved above - is that P2X receptors "are most adept at mediating slow neuromodulatory functions that are more widespread and more physiologically utilized than fast ATP synaptic transmission in the CNS". This is an honest correction to the older "ATP is a fast transmitter" framing.

P2X7 - the unusual one:
• EC50 for ATP approximately 100 micromolar and upward (mid-micromolar to low-millimolar), i.e. it needs concentrations reached only near sites of cell damage, massive release, or lysis - it is effectively a danger sensor rather than a synaptic receptor.
• It has a markedly longer intracellular C-terminus than the other six subunits, which carries protein-interaction and signalling motifs.
• It does not desensitise; instead, on prolonged activation the current grows (facilitation) and the receptor complexes with other membrane proteins to form a wide "macropore" permeable to large molecules (dyes up to about 900 Da), leading to interleukin-1-beta release, further ATP release, and cell death. Whether the macropore is P2X7 itself dilating or a recruited partner protein is UNRESOLVED - recent work implicates TMEM16 channels as functionally coupled partners (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8234106/ ) and a 2023 review is titled "Untangling macropore formation and current facilitation in P2X7" (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10341454/ ). State this as an open question.
• P2X7 is concentrated on microglia and is a major target in neuroinflammation; review: "The P2X7 receptor: central hub of brain diseases", https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7413029/
• Structural basis of its odd properties: Springer, Purinergic Signalling 2021, "Structural basis for the functional properties of the P2X7 receptor for extracellular ATP", https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11302-021-09790-x . A 2024 Nature Communications paper argues high-affinity agonism at P2X7 is mediated by three residues OUTSIDE the orthosteric pocket, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-024-50771-6 - so even the "low affinity" description is being refined.

P2Y receptors - G-protein-coupled (metabotropic). Eight mammalian subtypes: P2Y1, P2Y2, P2Y4, P2Y6, P2Y11, P2Y12, P2Y13, P2Y14. (The gaps - P2Y3, 5, 7 to 10 - are numbers assigned to non-mammalian or misassigned orphan receptors, a common source of confusion.) Source: IUPHAR/BPS Guide to Pharmacology, https://www.guidetopharmacology.org/GRAC/FamilyDisplayForward?familyId=52 ; and "Pharmacological characterization of P2Y receptor subtypes - an update", Purinergic Signalling 2023/2024, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10997570/
• Ligand preference: P2Y1, P2Y11, P2Y12, P2Y13 respond to adenine nucleotides (ATP/ADP); P2Y2 and P2Y4 to both adenine and uridine nucleotides with species differences; P2Y6 mainly to UDP; P2Y14 to sugar-uracil nucleotides (UDP-glucose).
• Coupling: P2Y1, 2, 4, 6, 11 couple through Gq to phospholipase C, inositol phosphates and intracellular calcium release. P2Y12, 13, 14 couple through Gi, inhibiting adenylate cyclase and modulating ion channels.
• Microglia express P2Y1, P2Y2, P2Y2/4, P2Y6 and P2Y12; P2Y6 (phagocytosis, UDP as an "eat-me" signal) and P2Y12 (the process-extension chemotaxis receptor toward ATP/ADP, and the clinical target of clopidogrel and ticagrelor on platelets) are the functionally important ones in brain.

Termination of ATP signalling: extracellular ATP is hydrolysed stepwise by ectonucleotidases (NTPDases/CD39 to ADP and AMP, then ecto-5'-nucleotidase/CD73 to adenosine). This is the crucial bridge to the next section - much of the adenosine acting on P1 receptors is the degradation product of released ATP, so ATP and adenosine signalling are not two systems but one cascade with opposite-signed ends (ATP often excitatory/pro-inflammatory via P2X, adenosine largely inhibitory via A1).

8. ADENOSINE AS A SLEEP-PRESSURE SIGNAL

Founding study: Porkka-Heiskanen T, Strecker RE, Thakkar M, Bjorkum AA, Greene RW, McCarley RW, "Adenosine: a mediator of the sleep-inducing effects of prolonged wakefulness", Science 1997;276(5316):1265-1268. In freely behaving cats, in-vivo microdialysis showed extracellular adenosine in the basal forebrain cholinergic region was higher in spontaneous wakefulness than in slow-wave sleep, rose progressively across 6 h of sustained enforced wakefulness, and declined slowly across the subsequent 3 h of recovery sleep. I could not retrieve the paywalled full text, so I do not have the 1997 paper's own percentage figure - use the follow-up and meta-analytic numbers below instead, which are traceable.

Regional specificity: Porkka-Heiskanen T et al., "Brain site-specificity of extracellular adenosine concentration changes during sleep deprivation and spontaneous sleep: an in vivo microdialysis study", Neuroscience 2000;99(3):507-517. PubMed: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11029542/
• Basal forebrain (cholinergic region): rose to about 140% of baseline over 6 h sleep deprivation - the largest effect of any region.
• Cerebral cortex: rose, but less.
• Thalamus, preoptic area, dorsal raphe nucleus, pedunculopontine tegmental nucleus: NO significant increase with sleep deprivation.
• All six regions showed a 15 to 20% decline during spontaneous sleep relative to wakefulness.
• Only the basal forebrain stayed significantly elevated throughout 3 h of recovery sleep.

Meta-analysis - the single most useful citation for grading the evidence: Leenaars CHC et al., "Intracerebral adenosine during sleep deprivation: a meta-analysis and new experimental data", Journal of Circadian Rhythms 2018. URL: https://jcircadianrhythms.com/articles/10.5334/jcr.171
• Basal forebrain adenosine increase over baseline during sleep deprivation: 74.7% (95% confidence interval 54.1 to 95.3%), pooled across eight experimental groups - robust and replicated.
• Cortex: inconclusive, only two studies with conflicting results.
• Their own new medial prefrontal cortex data: no significant adenosine increase; median 0.36 nM across conditions. AMP in mPFC did rise (0.18 nM versus 0.10 nM baseline).
• Authors' conclusion: "the increase in adenosine during sleep deprivation might be specific to the basal forebrain". NOTE THE TENSION with the 1997/2000 headline numbers: the pooled basal-forebrain effect (about +75%) is smaller than the single-study +140%, the classic publication-bias/regression-to-the-mean pattern. Quote 75% (with CI) as the best current estimate and 140% only as the original single-study report.

Modern sensor era: Peng W et al., "Regulation of sleep homeostasis mediator adenosine by basal forebrain glutamatergic neurons", Science 2020;369(6508):eabb0556. PubMed https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32883833/ ; DOI 10.1126/science.abb0556. Using GRAB_Ado, a genetically encoded GPCR-activation-based adenosine sensor with second-scale resolution, they showed activity-dependent rapid rises in extracellular adenosine in mouse basal forebrain. Both cholinergic and glutamatergic neuron activity correlated with adenosine, but optogenetic activation at physiological firing rates showed GLUTAMATERGIC neurons contribute much more. Selective ablation of basal forebrain glutamatergic neurons reduced the adenosine rise and impaired sleep homeostasis. This reassigns the cellular source away from the long-assumed astrocyte/cholinergic account.
• Related: a GRAB-sensor PNAS paper (2023, https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.2212387120 ) shows neuronal activity-induced somatodendritic adenosine release that is equilibrative nucleoside transporter-dependent - i.e. direct adenosine efflux, not only ectonucleotidase breakdown of released ATP.

Astrocytic route: Halassa MM, Florian C, Fellin T, Munoz JR, Lee SY, Abel T, Haydon PG, Frank MG, "Astrocytic modulation of sleep homeostasis and cognitive consequences of sleep loss", Neuron 2009;61(2):213-219. PubMed https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19186164/ . In dnSNARE mice (astrocyte-specific dominant-negative SNARE, blocking vesicular gliotransmitter release) the accumulation of sleep pressure measured as non-REM slow-wave activity was attenuated, and the cognitive deficits of sleep loss were prevented; the sleep-suppressing effect of the A1 antagonist CPT was occluded, implicating an astrocytic-ATP to adenosine to A1 pathway. Follow-up: Florian et al., Journal of Neuroscience 2011;31(19):6956-6962, astrocyte-derived adenosine and A1 activity mediate sleep-loss deficits in hippocampal plasticity and memory.

9. CAFFEINE - PHARMACOLOGY, KINETICS, RECEPTOR OCCUPANCY

• Mechanism: caffeine is a non-selective competitive antagonist at adenosine A1 and A2A receptors, with in-vitro affinity in the low micromolar range. Ki at A2A calculated as 8.8 micromolar (from the A2A antagonist literature, Jacobson et al., British Journal of Pharmacology 2022, "Adenosine A2A receptor antagonists: from caffeine to selective non-xanthines", DOI 10.1111/bph.15103). Fredholm BB et al., Pharmacological Reviews 1999;51(1):83-133, "Actions of caffeine in the brain with special reference to factors that contribute to its widespread use", is the canonical statement that antagonism of endogenous adenosine at A1 and A2A is the ONLY known primary action of caffeine occurring at behaviourally stimulant doses. Phosphodiesterase inhibition, ryanodine receptor and GABA-A effects all require concentrations 20 to 100-fold higher than any dietary dose - say this explicitly, it is a very common textbook error.

• Elimination half-life: about 4 to 5 hours in healthy adults; a systematic pharmacokinetic analysis (Frontiers in Pharmacology 2021, "Pharmacokinetics of caffeine: a systematic analysis of reported data for application in metabolic phenotyping and liver function testing", https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/pharmacology/articles/10.3389/fphar.2021.752826/full ) gives an average of approximately 3 to 6 hours with individual values ranging from about 2.3 to 9.9 hours. The commonly quoted "5 h, range 1.5 to 9.5 h" is compatible with, but slightly wider than, what I could verify - the verified range is 2.3 to 9.9 h. Determinants: more than 95% of caffeine clearance is CYP1A2-mediated (paraxanthine is the main metabolite); smoking induces CYP1A2 and roughly halves the half-life; oral contraceptives and pregnancy prolong it dramatically (up to 15 h and beyond in the third trimester); neonates clear caffeine with a half-life measured in days.

• A1 receptor occupancy in living humans: Elmenhorst D, Meyer PT, Matusch A, Winz OH, Bauer A, "Caffeine occupancy of human cerebral A1 adenosine receptors: in vivo quantification with 18F-CPFPX and PET", Journal of Nuclear Medicine 2012;53(11):1723-1729. PubMed https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22966134/ ; https://jnm.snmjournals.org/content/53/11/1723
◦ Caffeine displaced 5% to 44% of A1 binding in a concentration-dependent manner across the doses tested.
◦ Half-maximal displacement (50% A1 occupancy) at a plasma caffeine concentration of 67 micromolar, corresponding to about 450 mg in a 70 kg subject - roughly 4.5 cups of coffee.
◦ The authors' own gloss: given a biological half-life of about 5 h, repeated caffeinated-beverage consumption across a day can occupy up to about 50% of cerebral A1 receptors.
• A2A occupancy: PET work in Parkinson's disease (PubMed https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35001424/ ) reports mean striatal A2A occupancy of 54.2% at 129.5 mg and 65.1% at 259 mg caffeine - i.e. A2A is occupied at ordinary one-to-two-cup doses, well below the dose needed for comparable A1 occupancy. Rodent work gives 66% striatal A2A occupancy at 6.25 mg/kg with about 55% cortical A1 occupancy at higher doses.
• PRACTICAL CONCLUSION FOR THE CHAPTER: at a normal single cup (about 80 to 100 mg), A2A receptors are already substantially occupied (roughly half) while A1 occupancy is modest (well under 20%). The classic "caffeine blocks adenosine receptors so you feel awake" story is therefore mostly an A2A story at dietary doses, and the arousal effect maps onto A2A in the nucleus accumbens shell (Lazarus et al., Journal of Neuroscience 2011;31(27):10067-10075, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/31/27/10067 ).

• Human sleep-deprivation receptor imaging: Elmenhorst D et al., "Sleep deprivation increases A1 adenosine receptor binding in the human brain: a positron emission tomography study", Journal of Neuroscience 2007;27(9):2410-2415. PubMed https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17329439/ . After 24 h sleep deprivation in 12 healthy volunteers versus 10 controls: total distribution volume increased in a region-specific pattern in ALL examined regions, maximum in orbitofrontal cortex at +15.3% (p = 0.014); mean distribution volume +12.5%, mean binding potential +7.5%. Follow-up: Elmenhorst et al., PNAS 2017, "Recovery sleep after extended wakefulness restores elevated A1 adenosine receptor availability in the human brain", https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1614677114 - the upregulation reverses after recovery sleep. This is receptor UPREGULATION, a different mechanism from ligand accumulation, and it is an important nuance: the brain may track sleep pressure partly by changing receptor number, not only adenosine concentration.

• Human genetics: Retey JV et al., PNAS 2005, "A functional genetic variation of adenosine deaminase affects the duration and intensity of deep sleep in humans", https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.0505414102 - the adenosine deaminase G22A variant, which slows conversion of adenosine to inosine, is associated with more and deeper slow-wave sleep and higher slow-wave activity, plus more waking alpha activity. Replicated in a large epidemiological sample: Bachmann V et al., PLoS ONE 2012;7(8):e44154, https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0044154 . Separately, an ADORA2A (A2A receptor) 1976T&gt;C polymorphism modulates individual sensitivity to caffeine, including caffeine-induced anxiety and the EEG response, in both sleep and wakefulness. This human pharmacogenetic evidence is arguably the strongest causal support for the adenosine hypothesis, because it is a genetic perturbation of adenosine metabolism producing the predicted change in the sleep-homeostatic EEG marker in humans.

10. HONEST GRADING - IS ADENOSINE THE SLEEP HOMEOSTAT?

Verdict: adenosine is an established sleep-promoting signal and a genuine component of sleep pressure, but the claim that it IS the sleep homeostat is NOT established. Grade it "strong contributory evidence, causally incomplete". Specifically:

Evidence FOR
• Adenosine rises in basal forebrain with prolonged waking and falls with recovery sleep - replicated, pooled effect +74.7% (95% CI 54.1 to 95.3%), Leenaars et al. 2018 meta-analysis.
• Local basal-forebrain adenosine infusion or A1 agonists induce sleep; A1 antagonists (caffeine, CPT) suppress it - straightforward pharmacological bidirectionality.
• Human A1 receptor availability increases about 8 to 15% after 24 h wakefulness and normalises after recovery sleep (Elmenhorst 2007, 2017 PET).
• Human genetic variation in adenosine deaminase (G22A) predicts slow-wave sleep intensity in the direction the hypothesis requires (Retey 2005; Bachmann 2012) - this is the closest thing to a causal human experiment.
• Ablating basal forebrain glutamatergic neurons reduces the activity-dependent adenosine rise AND impairs sleep homeostasis (Peng et al., Science 2020) - a source-level causal link.
• Mechanistic plausibility: adenosine is the metabolic breakdown product of ATP use, so it is exactly the molecule an "energy-depletion" homeostat would use. Prolonged waking is the brain's high-ATP-turnover state.

Evidence AGAINST or complicating
• Constitutive A1 receptor knockout mice show NORMAL basal sleep and a NORMAL homeostatic response to sleep deprivation (slow-wave energy rebound) - Stenberg et al. 2003. If A1 were the homeostat's receptor, this should not happen. The standard rescue is developmental compensation, which is untestable as stated and therefore weak.
• Conditional, adult, CNS-restricted A1 knockdown DOES reduce the rebound slow-wave-activity response (Bjorness et al., Journal of Neuroscience 2009;29(5):1267-1276, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/29/5/1267 ). SOURCES DISAGREE, and the disagreement is explained by developmental compensation plus regional specificity - but note that the "explanation" is a post-hoc reconciliation, not an independent prediction that was then confirmed.
• The adenosine rise is REGIONALLY RESTRICTED. Thalamus, preoptic area, dorsal raphe and pedunculopontine tegmentum show no significant sleep-deprivation increase (Porkka-Heiskanen 2000); cortex is inconclusive and Leenaars' own medial prefrontal data show no rise. A homeostat for a global brain state that only moves in one small region needs an explanation for how the signal generalises.
• The astrocytic-gliotransmission pillar is contested. Fujita T et al., "Neuronal transgene expression in dominant-negative SNARE mice", Journal of Neuroscience 2014;34(50):16594-16604, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/34/50/16594 , reported widespread dnSNARE transgene expression in cortical NEURONS and reversible suppression of cortical neuronal activity in these mice - meaning the Halassa 2009 sleep-homeostasis result may be a neuron-specific, not astrocyte-specific, effect. Counter-claims (Sultan et al. 2015; Papouin et al. 2017) report failure to replicate the leakage. THIS IS UNRESOLVED. Also see the broader debate piece, "Gliotransmission: beyond black-and-white", Journal of Neuroscience 2018;38(1):14-25.
• Peng et al. 2020 shifts the source from astrocytes to basal-forebrain glutamatergic NEURONS, which is consistent with, but not the same as, the astrocytic story that dominated 2005 to 2015.
• There is at least one report of adenosine-INDEPENDENT regulation of the sleep-wake cycle by astrocyte activity (Cell Discovery 2023, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41421-022-00498-9 ), dissociating astrocytic control of sleep from adenosine.
• Caffeine, the strongest everyday "test" of the hypothesis, at ordinary doses occupies A2A far more than A1 (see section 9), and its arousal effect in mice is A2A-dependent (accumbens shell, Lazarus 2011). Yet A2A knockouts and A1 knockouts each retain sleep homeostasis. Neither receptor alone is necessary.
• Adenosine cannot be the whole homeostat because sleep pressure also tracks synaptic potentiation (the synaptic homeostasis hypothesis, Tononi and Cirelli), local use-dependent cortical slow-wave activity, and immune signalling (IL-1-beta, TNF-alpha, nitric oxide via Kalinchuk's inducible NOS work). Adenosine is one node in a multi-signal network. The strong claim "adenosine is THE sleep homeostat" is a simplification that the primary literature does not support.

WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT
• A real-time, cell-type-resolved, brain-wide adenosine map across sustained wakefulness and recovery, using GRAB_Ado (or successors) with sufficient sensitivity in cortex - so the region-specificity question is answered directly rather than through microdialysis with 10 to 30 minute temporal resolution and unknown recovery fractions. Microdialysis absolute values (sub-nanomolar dialysate concentrations) are not tissue concentrations; this alone weakens every absolute number in the 1997 to 2005 literature.
• Adult-onset, region-restricted, reversible (chemogenetic or optogenetic or acute degradative) manipulation of adenosine ITSELF - not the receptor - in basal forebrain, tested for both necessity and sufficiency against slow-wave-activity rebound. Clamping adenosine at waking-baseline levels during 12 h of enforced wakefulness and asking whether the rebound disappears would be close to decisive.
• Combinatorial adult-onset A1 plus A2A deletion with careful controls for developmental compensation, in the specific circuits identified by Peng et al.
• Independent, well-powered replication (or definitive refutation) of the dnSNARE cell-specificity question, since a substantial part of the astrocytic-adenosine case rests on that one mouse line.
• A quantitative test of the metabolic premise: does the sleep-deprivation-associated adenosine rise track measured local ATP turnover (31P MRS or ATP biosensor) with the right magnitude and time constant? If adenosine is the readout of energy expenditure, the two should be tightly coupled; nobody has shown this at the required resolution.

11. QUICK-REFERENCE NUMBER TABLE (value, range, source)

• delta-G-naught-prime ATP hydrolysis: -30.5 kJ/mol conventional; -28 to -37 kJ/mol across conventions (Milo and Phillips, Cell Biology by the Numbers; BioNumbers 101989)
• delta-G in vivo: -50 to -60 kJ/mol typical; -47 to -70 kJ/mol full range (Milo and Phillips; E. coli -47, human muscle recovery -70)
• Human brain ATP: 3.0 mM (Ren, Sherry, Malloy, NMR Biomed 2015)
• Human brain PCr: 4.37 +/- 0.39 mM (Ren 2015) versus about 2.4 to 3.0 mM (Zhu, NeuroImage 2012) - SOURCES DISAGREE
• Human brain intracellular Pi: 0.85 +/- 0.11 mM (Ren 2015); 0.93 to 0.96 mM (Zhu 2012)
• Intracellular pH brain: 6.99 +/- 0.01 (Ren 2015)
• Presynaptic bouton ATP: 1.5 mM (Rangaraju, Cell 2014) versus 2.5 to 2.7 mM (Straub, J Neurochem 2025) - SOURCES DISAGREE
• Free cytosolic ADP: low micromolar, below 31P NMR detection, CK-equilibrium-derived only; total ADP much higher because most is protein-bound
• Human brain ATP flux: 9.9 +/- 2.1 mmol/min/kg (Ren 2015); prior saturation transfer 8.8 to 12.1 mmol/min/kg
• CMR_ATPase: 9.5 +/- 3.4 micromol/g/min grey, 3.0 +/- 2.3 white (Zhu 2012)
• CMR_CK: 68.8 +/- 13.3 micromol/g/min grey, 22.2 +/- 7.5 white (Zhu 2012)
• Whole brain: about 7 mmol ATP/min, about 4 x 10^21 molecules/min, about 5.7 kg ATP/day (Zhu 2012)
• Per neuron: 4.7 x 10^9 ATP/s (Zhu 2012, human MRS, resting); 3.29 x 10^9 ATP/s at 4 Hz (Attwell and Laughlin 2001, rodent bottom-up); 2.4 x 10^9 ATP per spike (Lennie 2003)
• Brain mass/energy: 2% of body weight, 20% of resting oxygen consumption; CMRO2 3.0 to 3.8 mL/100 g/min; glucose 5 mg/100 g/min; 30 to 36 ATP per glucose
• 2001 budget: AP 47%, postsynaptic 34%, resting 13%, glutamate recycling 3%, presynaptic/exocytosis about 3% (total exocytosis + recycling about 6%)
• 2012 cortex: postsynaptic 50%, AP 21%, resting 20%, presynaptic release 5%, recycling 4%
• 2012 cerebellum: resting 54%, postsynaptic 22%, AP 17%, presynaptic Ca/vesicles 4%, recycling 3%
• Housekeeping/non-signalling: about 25% of total grey matter energy (Engl and Attwell 2015); earlier budgets 25 to 50%; AP as fraction of TOTAL grey matter energy about 17%
• Basal forebrain adenosine rise with sleep deprivation: +140% of baseline (Porkka-Heiskanen 2000, single study) versus +74.7% (95% CI 54.1 to 95.3%) pooled (Leenaars 2018 meta-analysis)
• A1 receptor availability after 24 h wakefulness: +12.5% mean distribution volume, +15.3% orbitofrontal maximum (Elmenhorst 2007)
• Caffeine half-life: 4 to 5 h typical, 2.3 to 9.9 h documented range; more than 95% cleared by CYP1A2
• Caffeine A1 occupancy: 50% at plasma 67 micromolar, about 450 mg in a 70 kg adult (Elmenhorst 2012); 5 to 44% across ordinary doses
• Caffeine A2A striatal occupancy: 54.2% at 129.5 mg, 65.1% at 259 mg
• Caffeine Ki at A2A: 8.8 micromolar
• P2X ATP EC50: P2X1 and P2X3 nanomolar to sub-micromolar; P2X2, P2X4, P2X5, P2X6 low micromolar; P2X7 about 100 micromolar to low millimolar
• P2Y subtypes: eight - P2Y1, 2, 4, 6, 11, 12, 13, 14
