<result>OXYGEN AND GLUCOSE - THE DELIVERED SUBSTRATES

WEB-VERIFIED FINDINGS DUMP. All numbers below were retrieved in this session (approximately 30 searches/fetches). Where a source could not be opened directly (paywall, 403), that is stated and the value is attributed to the secondary source that reported it.


SECTION 1 - CEREBRAL BLOOD FLOW (CBF)

• Global CBF, classical textbook value: approximately 57 mL per 100 g of brain tissue per minute, equating to roughly 800 mL per minute for the whole brain. Source: Clarke and Sokoloff, "Regulation of Cerebral Metabolic Rate", Basic Neurochemistry 6th edition, NCBI Bookshelf NBK28194 (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK28194/), fetched this session.

• Global CBF, alternative classical value: 50 mL per 100 g per minute, with an original reported range of 50 to 102 mL per 100 g per minute, and a whole-brain total of 750 to 1530 mL per minute for a 1.5 kg brain. Attributed to Scheinberg and Stead 1949, as reported by Deranged Physiology, "Cerebral metabolic demand" (https://derangedphysiology.com/main/required-reading/neurological-intensive-care/Chapter-102/cerebral-metabolic-demand), fetched this session.

• Kety-Schmidt nitrous oxide method (Kety and Schmidt 1948, Journal of Clinical Investigation 27:476-483) is the historical reference method. The original full text at https://www.jci.org/articles/view/101994 could not be text-extracted this session (only metadata rendered), so the specific 1948 means are quoted below via a secondary source rather than from the original.

• METHOD DEPENDENCE - THIS IS A REAL AND IMPORTANT DISAGREEMENT. Madsen, Holm, Herning and Lassen 1993, Journal of Cerebral Blood Flow and Metabolism ("Average Blood Flow and Oxygen Uptake in the Human Brain during Resting Wakefulness: A Critical Appraisal of the Kety-Schmidt Technique", https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/abs/10.1038/jcbfm.1993.83) state explicitly that the widely quoted Kety-Schmidt normal values of 54 mL per 100 g per minute (CBF) and 3.5 mL per 100 g per minute (CMRO2) are OVERESTIMATES, because in practice diffusion equilibrium of the inert gas tracer between brain and its venous blood is never reached. From 57 measurements during electroencephalogram-verified wakeful rest in young healthy adults, they give corrected values of approximately 46 mL per 100 g per minute for CBF and approximately 3.0 mL per 100 g per minute for CMRO2.

• So the honest statement is: global CBF is quoted anywhere from 46 to 57 mL per 100 g per minute depending on method and on whether the Kety-Schmidt overestimation correction is applied. Textbooks use 50 to 57; the corrected inert-gas value is about 46; modern oxygen-15 water positron emission tomography (PET) gives whole-brain values around 43.

• MODERN IMAGING VALUES (these are LOWER than the textbook figures - a genuine discrepancy). Simultaneous oxygen-15 water PET versus pseudo-continuous arterial spin labelling (pCASL) magnetic resonance imaging: whole-brain CBF 43.3 plus or minus 6.1 mL per 100 g per minute by PET, and 51.9 plus or minus 7.1 mL per 100 g per minute by pCASL, in the same subjects. Grey-to-white matter ratio 3.0 by PET and 3.4 by pCASL. Source: comparison study indexed at https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4126098/ and the systematic review at https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4853843/.

• GREY VERSUS WHITE MATTER. The commonly repeated textbook pair is grey matter approximately 80 mL per 100 g per minute and white matter approximately 20 mL per 100 g per minute. This pairing is widely restated (for example the ScienceDirect topic page "Brain Blood Flow", https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/immunology-and-microbiology/brain-blood-flow, which gives "average CBF approximately 50 mL per 100 g per minute, grey matter 80, white matter 20").

• BUT MEASURED VALUES ARE LOWER. Clarke and Sokoloff (NBK28194) only commit to the statement that grey matter flow is "approximately four to five times" white matter flow, without absolute figures. Direct arterial spin labelling measurements give cerebral white matter perfusion of 15.8 to 27.5 mL per 100 mL per minute with grey-to-white ratios of 1.8 to 4.0 depending on spatial resolution (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3855805/). Cerebellar values by FAIR arterial spin labelling: grey 43.8 plus or minus 5.1 and white 27.6 plus or minus 4.5 mL per 100 g per minute (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4052111/). PET-based grey matter values cluster around 40 to 60, not 80.

• HONEST SUMMARY OF THE GREY/WHITE NUMBER: 80 versus 20 is a legitimate historical figure derived from autoradiographic and early inert-gas regional work and it correctly conveys the roughly fourfold ratio, but it should be presented as an idealised classical pair, not as what modern PET or arterial spin labelling actually returns. Arterial spin labelling systematically overestimates white matter flow because of long arterial transit times and partial-volume contamination; PET underestimates grey matter flow because of limited spatial resolution and partial-volume averaging. What would settle it: partial-volume-corrected, transit-time-corrected simultaneous PET/MR in the same subjects, which the 2016 comparison literature has begun but not closed.

• Brain fraction of cardiac output: approximately 15 percent of basal cardiac output. Brain mass: approximately 2 percent of body weight. Source: Clarke and Sokoloff, NBK28194.


SECTION 2 - CMRO2, CEREBRAL METABOLIC RATE OF OXYGEN

• Classical value: approximately 3.5 mL O2 per 100 g of brain per minute in normal conscious young adults. Source: Clarke and Sokoloff, NBK28194.

• Same value in molar units: 156 micromol O2 per 100 g of tissue per minute. This exact figure appears in Clarke and Sokoloff, "Substrates of Cerebral Metabolism", NCBI Bookshelf NBK28048 (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK28048/), fetched this session.

• UNIT CONVERSION VERIFIED. 156 micromol equals 1.56 times 10 to the minus 4 mol. At the molar gas volume of 22.4 L per mol (standard temperature and pressure), 1.56 times 10 to the minus 4 mol times 22,400 mL per mol equals 3.49 mL. So 156 micromol per 100 g per minute equals 3.5 mL per 100 g per minute. The conversion is internally consistent. Caveat worth stating in the book: this uses the ideal-gas molar volume at 0 degrees Celsius and 1 atmosphere (22.414 L per mol). If body temperature and pressure saturated conditions were used instead, the same molar rate would correspond to about 3.98 mL, so the textbook pairing implicitly uses standard temperature and pressure.

• Alternative CMRO2 figure with a range: 3.8 mL per 100 g per minute, range 3.1 to 5.2, giving 46.5 to 78 mL O2 per minute for a 1.5 kg brain. Source: Deranged Physiology chapter cited above, drawing on Scheinberg and Stead 1949.

• Corrected (Madsen 1993) value: approximately 3.0 mL per 100 g per minute.

• So the defensible range is CMRO2 approximately 3.0 to 3.8 mL per 100 g per minute, or approximately 134 to 170 micromol per 100 g per minute, with 3.5 mL (156 micromol) the canonical midpoint. Whole-brain oxygen consumption is therefore roughly 45 to 55 mL O2 per minute.

• Brain share of whole-body oxygen consumption: 20 percent of resting total body oxygen consumption in the adult; rising to approximately 50 percent in children during early development. Source: Clarke and Sokoloff, NBK28194.

• Energy equivalent of brain metabolism: approximately 20 watts, or 0.25 kcal per minute. Source: Clarke and Sokoloff, NBK28194.

• CMRO2 under pathological states, from the same source: insulin hypoglycaemia with confusion, 2.6 mL per 100 g per minute, which is 79 percent of normal; deep coma with arterial glucose at 8 mg per 100 mL, 1.9 mL per 100 g per minute, roughly a 50 percent fall.


SECTION 3 - CMRglc, CEREBRAL METABOLIC RATE OF GLUCOSE

• Human whole-brain value: 31 micromol per 100 g per minute. Source: Clarke and Sokoloff, NBK28048 (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK28048/), fetched this session. Note this is the measured value.

• The value PREDICTED from oxygen consumption alone, assuming complete oxidation of glucose, is 26 micromol per 100 g per minute. The measured 31 exceeds the predicted 26 - this excess is the classic evidence that some glucose carbon leaves the brain non-oxidatively (as lactate, or into amino acid and lipid pools) and it is the same arithmetic that anchors the lactate shuttle debate below.

• Oxygen-to-glucose utilisation ratio (the oxygen-glucose index): measured 5.5 micromol O2 per micromol glucose, versus the theoretical 6.0 for complete oxidation. Respiratory quotient 1.0, indicating carbohydrate is the near-exclusive substrate in the fed, normoglycaemic state. Source: NBK28048.

• Alternative mass-unit statement: 6.3 mg glucose per 100 g per minute, range 3.9 to 9.5, giving 58.5 to 142.5 mg per minute for a 1.5 kg brain. Attributed to Scheinberg and Stead 1949 via Deranged Physiology. Cross-check: 31 micromol times 180 g per mol equals 5.6 mg per 100 g per minute, so 6.3 mg and 31 micromol are consistent to within about 12 percent - a real but small discrepancy between the two classical datasets.

• SOKOLOFF DEOXYGLUCOSE NUMBERS (rat, autoradiographic 14C-2-deoxyglucose method). Sokoloff et al. 1977, Journal of Neurochemistry 28:897-916, "The [14C]deoxyglucose method for the measurement of local cerebral glucose utilization: theory, procedure, and normal values in the conscious and anesthetized albino rat" (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/j.1471-4159.1977.tb10649.x). In the conscious rat, grey matter structures range widely from 54 to 197 micromol per 100 g per minute; white matter is far more uniform at 33 to 40 micromol per 100 g per minute, i.e. roughly one quarter to one half of grey matter. The commonly quoted round figures of "about 100 grey, about 30 white" sit inside these measured ranges.

• Important caution for the book: rat values are two to four times higher than human values because of the much higher mass-specific metabolic rate of the rat. Human grey matter by 18F-fluorodeoxyglucose PET is roughly 30 to 50 micromol per 100 g per minute. Do not transfer Sokoloff's rat numbers to humans.

• The deoxyglucose method depends on the "lumped constant", an empirical correction relating deoxyglucose kinetics to glucose kinetics. It is not constant under all conditions - it changes in hypoglycaemia (see https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2347881/, "The lumped constant of the deoxyglucose method in hypoglycemia"). This is a genuine methodological limit on all absolute CMRglc figures.


SECTION 4 - OXYGEN EXTRACTION FRACTION (OEF)

• Normal OEF range: 0.35 to 0.45, relatively uniform through grey and white matter. Reported in the oxygen-15 gas PET technical review (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32634594/) and the MRI OEF review (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1155/2022/4554271), which states OEF is "about 40 percent in the normal condition".

• Regional detail, oxygen-15 PET (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10717292/, "Oxygen extraction fraction is not uniform in human brain"): cerebral neocortical regions 0.38 to 0.44 on average; lateral occipital cortex highest at 0.44 plus or minus 0.06; cerebellum 0.36 plus or minus 0.07; centrum semiovale 0.37 plus or minus 0.07; pons significantly lowest at 0.28 plus or minus 0.08.

• Clinical/physiological restatement: oxygen extraction ratio 25 to 35 percent of delivered oxygen; jugular bulb venous oxygen saturation normally 55 to 75 percent. Source: Deranged Physiology chapter cited above.

• DISAGREEMENT NOTED: the prompt's stated range of 0.3 to 0.4 is on the low side of the PET literature, which centres on 0.35 to 0.45 (roughly 0.40). The wider quoted band 0.3 to 0.45 is defensible if regional variation (pons at 0.28) and method differences (quantitative susceptibility mapping plus quantitative BOLD versus oxygen-15 PET) are included. The claim that OEF is uniform across the brain is itself now contested by the 2023 PET paper above.

• A useful derived check: OEF equals CMRO2 divided by (CBF times arterial oxygen content). With CBF 50 mL per 100 g per minute and arterial oxygen content approximately 20 mL O2 per 100 mL blood, oxygen delivery is 10 mL O2 per 100 g per minute; CMRO2 of 3.5 divided by 10 gives OEF equal to 0.35. The canonical numbers are internally consistent.


SECTION 5 - BRAIN SHARE OF WHOLE-BODY GLUCOSE AND OXYGEN

• Brain glucose consumption: approximately 110 g per day in the resting young adult, and approximately 25 percent of total body glucose use. Source: Mergenthaler, Lindauer, Dienel and Meisel 2013, Trends in Neurosciences, "Sugar for the brain: the role of glucose in physiological and pathological brain function" (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23968694/), as quoted in secondary sources retrieved this session.

• Alternative figure in circulation: approximately 120 g per day and approximately 20 percent of body glucose. Both figures appear in the literature; the 110 g / 25 percent and 120 g / 20 percent pairings are not fully reconcilable, which is itself worth flagging.

• Arithmetic check: 31 micromol per 100 g per minute times 14 (for a 1400 g brain) times 1440 minutes times 180 g per mol equals approximately 112 g of glucose per day. So 110 to 120 g per day is directly derivable from the canonical CMRglc, and the range simply reflects assumed brain mass (1350 to 1500 g) and rounding.

• Brain oxygen: 20 percent of resting whole-body oxygen consumption (Clarke and Sokoloff, NBK28194). This is the firmest number in this section; it is consistent across every source retrieved.

• The brain also consumes roughly 70 percent of total hepatic glucose output (hepatic output 100 to 200 mg per minute). Source: Deranged Physiology chapter.

• Context: the brain is approximately 2 percent of body mass, takes approximately 15 percent of cardiac output, and consumes approximately 20 percent of resting oxygen and 20 to 25 percent of glucose. This tenfold disproportion is the central quantitative fact of the chapter.


SECTION 6 - ISCHAEMIC THRESHOLDS

• PRIMARY CITATION: Astrup J, Siesjo BK, Symon L. "Thresholds in cerebral ischemia - the ischemic penumbra." Stroke 1981; 12(6): 723-725. PubMed identifier 6272455 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6272455/). IMPORTANT SOURCING CAVEAT: this is an editorial with NO ABSTRACT in PubMed, and the American Heart Association full text and PDF both returned HTTP 403 to automated fetching this session. All threshold numbers below are therefore taken from peer-reviewed reviews that quote the primary work, not from the 1981 text itself.

• Astrup et al. 1977 baboon middle cerebral artery occlusion model, paired measurements of regional CBF, evoked potentials, pH and extracellular potassium, gave TWO thresholds:
◦ Upper threshold, ELECTRICAL FAILURE: CBF approximately 15 mL per 100 g per minute. Neurons are functionally silent (evoked potentials and electroencephalogram lost) but structurally intact.
◦ Lower threshold, MEMBRANE / ION PUMP FAILURE: CBF approximately 6 mL per 100 g per minute. Massive release of intracellular potassium into the extracellular space; loss of viability.
Source for both figures: "Defining the ischemic penumbra using hyperacute neuro-imaging: deriving quantitative ischemic thresholds", https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4437623/, fetched this session.

• Expressed as percentages of baseline flow: electrical silence at a reduction of about 70 percent from baseline; massive potassium efflux at about 10 percent of baseline. Source: "Four Decades of Ischemic Penumbra and Its Implication for Ischemic Stroke", https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8557134/, fetched this session.

• PENUMBRA DEFINITION as given in the reviews: the region of ischaemic brain with CBF between the upper threshold of electrical failure and the lower threshold of energy and ion-pump failure - that is, roughly 6 to 15 mL per 100 g per minute in the baboon, or 10 to 30 percent of normal flow. It is perfused tissue that is functionally impaired but structurally intact and capable of recovery if perfusion is restored. Core, with ATP depletion, lies below 6 mL per 100 g per minute.

• DISAGREEMENT WITH THE PROMPT'S FIGURES. The prompt proposes "below 20 to 22 mL per 100 g per minute equals electrical failure, below 8 to 10 equals membrane failure". The Astrup/Symon primate numbers as reported in the reviews are LOWER: approximately 15 and approximately 6. The higher 20 to 22 and 8 to 10 figures are the CLINICAL/HUMAN thresholds that entered the stroke literature later, principally from Hossmann's synthesis and from human PET work by Baron and Heiss. Both sets are in use. State both.

• HOSSMANN 1994, Annals of Neurology 36:557-565, "Viability thresholds and the penumbra of focal ischemia" (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1002/ana.410360404) gives a GRADED CASCADE of thresholds, which is the most useful single formulation for a reference book:
◦ Approximately 55 mL per 100 g per minute - inhibition of protein synthesis (the earliest detectable failure).
◦ Approximately 35 mL per 100 g per minute - stimulation of anaerobic glycolysis.
◦ Approximately 20 mL per 100 g per minute - neurotransmitter release and disturbance of energy metabolism. This is the figure that matches the prompt's "20 to 22" electrical failure threshold.
◦ 6 to 15 mL per 100 g per minute - terminal (anoxic) depolarisation with potassium efflux. This is the membrane failure threshold.
Hossmann also shows, by combining autoradiographic CBF with bioluminescent ATP imaging, that the core progressively expands into the penumbra until after a few hours the penumbra has disappeared - the thresholds are time-dependent, not fixed.

• TIME-DEPENDENCE IS ESSENTIAL AND OFTEN OMITTED. CBF thresholds are time-dependent; a flow of 15 mL per 100 g per minute is survivable for minutes but not for hours. By contrast, Powers et al. established a CMRO2 threshold of 1.3 mL per 100 g per minute (37 to 39 percent of normal) that is INDEPENDENT of time after stroke onset. Source: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4437623/. This is why metabolic imaging rather than perfusion imaging is the more honest definition of irreversibility.

• The term "penumbra" was coined by Astrup in 1977 by analogy with the partially shadowed rim of a solar eclipse; the 1981 Stroke paper formalised it.


SECTION 7 - TIME TO LOSS OF CONSCIOUSNESS AND TO IRREVERSIBLE INJURY

• Loss of consciousness after complete interruption of cerebral blood flow: "consciousness is lost within less than 10 seconds". Source: Clarke and Sokoloff, Basic Neurochemistry, NBK28194, fetched this session. This is the most authoritative statement retrieved.

• PRIMARY HUMAN EXPERIMENT: Rossen R, Kabat H, Anderson JP. "Acute arrest of cerebral circulation in man." Archives of Neurology and Psychiatry 1943; 50: 510-528 (https://jamanetwork.com/journals/archneurpsyc/fullarticle/649750). Method: a specially designed inflatable cervical pressure cuff applied to the lower third of the neck, arresting cerebral circulation without obstructing the airway; the work was done to understand G-force-induced loss of consciousness in military pilots, on volunteers (including the investigators themselves) at Red Wing, Minnesota. Both PDF copies of this paper and the 2007 Smith re-analysis at litfl.com returned HTTP 403 to fetching this session, so the exact per-second tabulation could not be extracted directly. Secondary sources retrieved place loss of consciousness at approximately 6 to 7 seconds after complete arrest.

• So the defensible statement is: loss of consciousness in approximately 5 to 10 seconds, most commonly cited as 6 to 7 seconds from the Rossen 1943 human cuff data, and "less than 10 seconds" from Clarke and Sokoloff.

• Mechanistic underpinning: the brain holds essentially no oxygen reserve. Neuronal oxygen stores are exhausted within about 20 seconds of circulatory arrest; glucose and ATP stores are depleted within roughly 5 minutes; electroencephalographic activity flattens within 1 to 2 minutes.

• A separate empirical marker: jugular venous oxygen saturation below 25 percent correlates with loss of consciousness (Lennox et al. 1935, via Deranged Physiology).

• TIME TO IRREVERSIBLE INJURY - SOURCES DISAGREE, AND THIS SHOULD BE STATED PLAINLY.
◦ One line of sources: the brain tolerates anoxia for 2 to 4 minutes before irreversible damage begins.
◦ The most commonly cited clinical teaching: neurons die in significant numbers by 4 to 6 minutes.
◦ Wider textbook range: 4 to 8 minutes, and up to 10 minutes before uniformly poor outcome.
◦ There is no single agreed number. The disagreement is partly definitional: "irreversible injury" can mean first detectable selective neuronal necrosis (earliest, minutes), loss of the possibility of neurologically intact survival (later), or gross infarction (later still). Selective vulnerability means some populations - hippocampal CA1 pyramidal cells, cerebellar Purkinje cells, layers 3, 5 and 6 of neocortex - die after ischaemic durations that leave other regions intact, so "the brain dies at T minutes" is a category error.
◦ What would settle it: it cannot be settled as a single number. The honest framing for the book is that ischaemic injury is a dose (depth times duration) relationship modulated by temperature, not a cliff edge.

• TEMPERATURE EFFECT - LARGE AND WELL ESTABLISHED.
◦ CMRO2 falls with temperature: at 15 degrees Celsius, CMRO2 is 15 to 16 percent of the 37 degrees Celsius baseline, i.e. 0.5 to 0.8 mL O2 per 100 g per minute, or 7.5 to 12 mL per minute for the whole brain. This is the physiological basis of deep hypothermic circulatory arrest. Electroencephalographic burst suppression appears at about 24 degrees Celsius; electrocerebral silence at about 17 degrees Celsius. Source: Deranged Physiology chapter, citing McCullough et al. 1999.
◦ In the other direction, CMRO2 rises 5 to 6 percent per 1 degree Celsius up to 42 degrees Celsius (Carlsson et al. 1976, rat), so a fever of 41 degrees raises CMRO2 by roughly 20 to 24 percent - which is why fever worsens stroke outcome.
◦ "Small differences in intraischaemic brain temperature critically determine the extent of ischaemic neuronal injury", and relatively small temperature decreases produce marked neuroprotection. Source: Yenari and Han, "Neuroprotective mechanisms of hypothermia in brain ischaemia", Nature Reviews Neuroscience (https://www.nature.com/articles/nrn3174).
◦ Clinically, two randomised controlled trials showed therapeutic hypothermia at 32 to 34 degrees Celsius for 12 to 24 hours after ventricular fibrillation or ventricular tachycardia arrest improved neurological outcome and reduced death versus normothermia (https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/hc4001.097037). Note honestly that later large trials (TTM, TTM2, HYPERION) have substantially complicated this picture and current guidance favours targeted temperature management with fever avoidance rather than deep cooling - though those specific later trials were not retrieved in this session and should be verified separately before being asserted with numbers.
◦ The extreme illustrative cases are cold-water drowning survivals after 40 to 60 minutes of arrest, which are only explicable by the temperature dependence of CMRO2.


SECTION 8 - BRAIN GLYCOGEN

• Location: glycogen in the adult brain is located essentially exclusively in astrocytes, though its presence benefits neurons - implying export of a metabolite (glucose or lactate) from astrocyte to neuron. Source: "The Role of Brain Glycogen in Supporting Physiological Function", Frontiers in Neuroscience 2019 (https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnins.2019.01176/full).

• Human brain glycogen, in vivo 13C magnetic resonance spectroscopy: approximately 3.5 micromol per gram. Source: Oz et al. 2007, American Journal of Physiology - Endocrinology and Metabolism, "Human brain glycogen content and metabolism" (https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/ajpendo.00424.2006).

• Rodent brain, depending on extraction method and animal handling: 3 to 12 micromol per gram. The wide range is a genuine methodological artefact - post-mortem glycogenolysis is extremely fast, so any method that does not use microwave fixation or in vivo spectroscopy underestimates the true content.

• REVISED HIGHER HUMAN ESTIMATE: 7.8 plus or minus 0.3 micromol per gram in healthy human brain tissue, from extended measurements. Source: "Revisiting Glycogen Content in the Human Brain", Neurochemical Research (https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11064-015-1664-4). So human values disagree by more than twofold between the 2007 and the later estimate; give both.

• Astrocyte-referenced concentration: if bulk tissue glycogen is 8 to 12 micromol per gram and the astrocytic volume fraction is 15 percent, the concentration WITHIN astrocytes is 50 to 80 micromol per gram. This is a much less trivial store than the bulk figure suggests, and is worth stating explicitly.

• Turnover: bulk brain glycogen turns over at 0.16 micromol per gram per hour, implying complete turnover in 3 to 5 days (72 to 120 hours). Source: Oz et al. 2007. This slow turnover is a strong argument against glycogen being a routine minute-to-minute fuel and in favour of it being a reserve mobilised in emergencies (hypoglycaemia, intense activation, ischaemia).

• COMPARISON WITH LIVER AND MUSCLE - the point of the section. Glycogen is approximately 1 percent of skeletal muscle wet weight and approximately 5 percent of liver wet weight. Converting: 5 percent of liver wet weight in glucosyl units is roughly 300 micromol per gram, and 1 percent of muscle is roughly 60 micromol per gram. Against brain's 3.5 to 12 micromol per gram, the brain store is therefore roughly 25 to 90 times more dilute than liver and 5 to 17 times more dilute than muscle. Sources: Physiopedia glycogen entry (https://www.physio-pedia.com/Glycogen) and the ScienceDirect "Glycogen Muscle Level" topic page.

• Practical consequence: at a CMRglc of 31 micromol per 100 g per minute (0.31 micromol per gram per minute), a bulk store of 3.5 to 12 micromol per gram would sustain the whole brain for only about 11 to 39 minutes even if fully and instantly mobilisable - and it is not, because it sits in 15 percent of the tissue volume and glycogen phosphorylase flux is rate-limited. This calculation is derived here from the sourced figures above, not quoted from a source.


SECTION 9 - KETONE BODIES AS ALTERNATIVE FUEL

• PRIMARY CITATION: Owen OE, Morgan AP, Kemp HG, Sullivan JM, Herrera MG, Cahill GF Jr. "Brain metabolism during fasting." Journal of Clinical Investigation 1967; 46(10): 1589-1595. PubMed identifier 6061736 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6061736/). SOURCING CAVEAT: the JCI abstract page and the PDF (via CloudFront) were both fetched this session; the abstract contains no numbers and the PDF returned as unparseable binary. The quantitative figures below therefore come from the abstract text and from secondary sources, not from the paper's tables.

• Design as stated in the abstract: catheterisation of cerebral vessels (arteriovenous difference across the brain) in THREE obese patients undergoing 5 to 6 weeks of therapeutic starvation.

• Principal finding, verbatim from the abstract: beta-hydroxybutyrate and acetoacetate "replaced glucose as the predominant fuel for brain metabolism".

• THE HEADLINE NUMBER - AND THE DISAGREEMENT. Two different figures circulate and they are not the same claim:
◦ Approximately 60 percent: "ketone bodies were discovered to supply up to 60 percent of the energy needs of the brain in prolonged fasting or glucose shortage". This is the most widely repeated version.
◦ More than 50 percent: Clarke and Sokoloff, Basic Neurochemistry NBK28048, state that during prolonged fasting ketone bodies accounted for "more than 50 percent of the total cerebral oxygen consumption". Note this is expressed as a fraction of OXYGEN consumption, not of energy or of substrate carbon.
◦ Deranged Physiology, citing Owen 1967, states that by week 5 of starvation ketones account for approximately 50 percent of cerebral metabolism.
◦ The review at the Korean Journal of Physiology and Pharmacology (https://www.kjpp.net/journal/view.html?uid=2958&amp;vmd=Full) states ketones provide "up to two-thirds" (about 66 percent) of brain energy in extended fasting.
• HONEST SUMMARY: the range in the literature is 50 to 66 percent, most often quoted as "about 60 percent". The three-subject sample size of the original study, and the fact that the subjects were obese and therefore had unusually large fat stores and unusually deep ketosis, should be stated. The figure should be presented as "roughly half to two thirds", not as a precise 60 percent.

• Owen's own 2005 retrospective, "Ketone bodies as a fuel for the brain during starvation", Biochemistry and Molecular Biology Education 33: 246-251 (https://iubmb.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/bmb.2005.49403304246), is the accessible narrative source; it was identified but the full text is paywalled and could not be quantitatively extracted this session.

• Kinetics: circulating ketone body concentrations rise roughly exponentially during starvation and begin to plateau around the 18th day. In the normal fed state the arteriovenous difference for ketone bodies across the brain is essentially zero - ketone uptake is concentration-driven, limited by monocarboxylate transporter (MCT1) expression at the blood-brain barrier, which is itself upregulated by sustained ketosis. This is why ketosis takes days to weeks to become a quantitatively significant brain fuel and cannot rescue acute hypoglycaemia.

• Ketone bodies are used differentially by brain region: Hawkins et al., Science, "Ketone Bodies Are Selectively Used by Individual Brain Regions" (https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.451608). Cross-check: Pan et al. 2000, Journal of Cerebral Blood Flow and Metabolism, measured human brain beta-hydroxybutyrate directly by magnetic resonance spectroscopy in fasting-induced ketosis (https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1097/00004647-200010000-00012), confirming Owen's arteriovenous inference non-invasively.

• Critical caveat: ketones can substitute for glucose but not eliminate it. Even in deep starvation the brain retains an obligatory glucose requirement of roughly 25 to 40 g per day, supplied by gluconeogenesis, because acetyl-CoA from ketones cannot replenish tricarboxylic acid cycle intermediates removed for neurotransmitter synthesis (anaplerosis).


SECTION 10 - THE LACTATE SHUTTLE CONTROVERSY (REPORTED HONESTLY)

THE ORIGINAL CLAIM

• PRIMARY CITATION: Pellerin L, Magistretti PJ. "Glutamate uptake into astrocytes stimulates aerobic glycolysis: a mechanism coupling neuronal activity to glucose utilization." Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences USA 1994; 91(22): 10625-10629 (https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.91.22.10625, full text at https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC45074/, abstract fetched this session).

• Verbatim from the abstract: "Here we report that glutamate, in addition to its receptor-mediated actions on neuronal excitability, stimulates glycolysis - i.e. glucose utilization and lactate production - in astrocytes." The effect is mediated by activation of a sodium-dependent uptake system, NOT by interaction with glutamate receptors. Lactate release and concomitant glucose uptake in astrocyte cultures were stimulated by sodium-coupled glutamate uptake.

• SOURCING CAVEAT: the PMC version of the 1994 paper renders the results pages as scanned images, so the exact fold-changes in 2-deoxyglucose uptake and lactate release could NOT be extracted this session. Do not state a specific fold-change without opening the original PDF.

• THE ANLS (astrocyte-neuron lactate shuttle) MODEL AS IT DEVELOPED. Synaptically released glutamate is taken up by astrocytes with three sodium ions per glutamate; the sodium load activates the sodium-potassium ATPase; ATP demand drives astrocytic glycolysis; glucose is converted to lactate; lactate is exported and taken up by neurons and oxidised. The stoichiometric claim in its strong form is that one glucose is consumed per glutamate cycled, yielding two lactates.

MOLECULAR EVIDENCE OFFERED IN SUPPORT

• Differential lactate dehydrogenase isozyme expression: astrocytes express LDH5 (A4 tetramer, favouring pyruvate to lactate); neurons express LDH1 (B4 tetramer, favouring lactate to pyruvate).

• Complementary monocarboxylate transporter distribution: astrocytes express MCT1 and MCT4 (low affinity, exporters); neurons express MCT2 (higher affinity, i.e. lower Michaelis constant, importer). Source for both: the Korean Journal of Physiology and Pharmacology 2025 review (https://www.kjpp.net/journal/view.html?uid=2958&amp;vmd=Full), fetched this session. Note that the review does not give numerical Michaelis constants.

• Suzuki et al. 2011: inhibition of astrocytic glycogenolysis and lactate production disrupts long-term memory formation and long-term potentiation, and the deficit is rescued by lactate supplementation. This is the strongest single functional result cited in favour.

• Machler et al. 2016, Cell Metabolism, "In Vivo Evidence for a Lactate Gradient from Astrocytes to Neurons" (https://www.cell.com/cell-metabolism/fulltext/S1550-4131(15)00526-4). Using the genetically encoded FRET lactate sensor Laconic with two-photon microscopy in mouse cortex, the signal increase on lactate challenge was significantly smaller in astrocytes, indicating HIGHER basal lactate in astrocytes than in neurons; confirmed by one-point calibration. Trans-acceleration of the monocarboxylate transporter with pyruvate reduced intracellular lactate in astrocytes but not in neurons. This establishes the concentration gradient that is a necessary precondition for carrier-mediated astrocyte-to-neuron flux. Caveat: the paper reports the DIRECTION of the gradient; the absolute concentrations and the gradient magnitude in millimolar could not be extracted this session because the ScienceDirect and Cell full texts returned 403.

• Glutamate uptake both stimulates astrocytic glucose consumption AND inhibits neuronal glucose uptake, which would enforce the division of labour.

THE CASE AGAINST

• Dienel GA. "Lack of appropriate stoichiometry: strong evidence against an energetically important astrocyte-neuron lactate shuttle in brain." Journal of Neuroscience Research 2017 (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/jnr.24015). Full text returned HTTP 402 this session; the argument is reported from the abstract and from secondary summaries. The argument is arithmetic, and it is the strongest anti-ANLS argument:
◦ During activation, total CMRglc rises MORE than oxidative CMRglc.
◦ The CMRO2-to-CMRglc ratio FALLS during activation (this is the well-known uncoupling first shown by Fox and Raichle).
◦ Therefore the small increase in oxygen uptake during activation is arithmetically insufficient to support the oxidation of the quantity of lactate that the shuttle requires neurons to consume.
◦ Conclusion: lactate shuttling in the activated brain is not quantitatively substantial. Some of the extra glucose carbon leaves the brain as lactate, or enters other pools, rather than being oxidised by neurons.

• Dienel GA. "Brain lactate metabolism: the discoveries and the controversies." Journal of Cerebral Blood Flow and Metabolism 2012 (https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1038/jcbfm.2011.175) - the long-form critique.

• Diaz-Garcia CM, Mongeon R, Lahmann C, Koveal D, Zucker H, Yellen G. "Neuronal Stimulation Triggers Neuronal Glycolysis and Not Lactate Uptake." Cell Metabolism 2017; 26(2): 361-374 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28768175/). Using fluorescent NADH biosensors in neurons during excitation: pharmacological blockade of lactate transporters or of lactate dehydrogenase abolished the rise in cytosolic NADH in response to EXOGENOUS lactate, but did NOT abolish the rise in response to STIMULATION. This rules out lactate import as the source of the acute stimulation-evoked neuronal NADH transient, and shows neurons turn on their own glycolysis. This is the single most cited experimental challenge to ANLS.

• Follow-up: "Neurons rely on glucose rather than astrocytic lactate during stimulation", Journal of Neuroscience Research 2019 (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1002/jnr.24374).

• Bak LK and Walls AB. "CrossTalk opposing view: lack of evidence supporting an astrocyte-to-neuron lactate shuttle coupling neuronal activity to glucose utilisation in the brain." Journal of Physiology 2018 (https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1113/JP274945). Core argument: there is no good biochemical reason why neurons should PREFERENTIALLY consume lactate during activation; neurons in vitro produce lactate on activation and so may themselves contribute to the extracellular lactate surge. Crucially, they CONCEDE that neurons do metabolise lactate if it is present - their position is that lactate is an "opportunistic" substrate that supports energy metabolism when available, not an obligatory coupled shuttle.

• Barros LF and Weber B. "CrossTalk proposal: an important astrocyte-to-neuron lactate shuttle couples neuronal activity to glucose utilisation in the brain." Journal of Physiology 2018 (https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1113/JP274944). Position: there is net lactate transfer from astrocytes to neurons, this transfer matters for brain function, and astrocytes are the main source of extracellular lactate used by neurons for oxidative metabolism both at rest and during stimulation. NOTE: Barros is a PROPONENT, not a critic - the prompt lists him among critics, which is incorrect. Barros authored the CrossTalk PROPOSAL. He is a critic of some specific ANLS formulations and of over-interpretation, but his stated position is pro-shuttle.

• Similarly Hertz is best characterised as a critic of the STRONG form of ANLS on stoichiometric and thermodynamic grounds while accepting astrocyte-neuron metabolic cooperation; he is often grouped with Dienel. Dienel is the systematic quantitative opponent.

RECENT EVIDENCE, 2025

• Andersen JV et al. "Neurons in Need: Glucose, but Not Lactate, Is Required to Support Energy-Demanding Synaptic Transmission." Journal of Neurochemistry 2025 (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/jnc.70255). Full text paywalled (HTTP 402) this session; from the abstract as retrieved: energy-demanding synchronised synaptic transmission can only be sustained when glucose is freely available; blocking lactate transport had NO effect on neuronal signalling when glucose was present; lactate transport becomes important only when glucose concentration is too low and lactate is available as supplemental fuel.

• Soder J et al. "Lactate Transport via Glial MCT1 and Neuronal MCT2 Is Not Required for Synchronized Synaptic Transmission in Hippocampal Slices Supplied With Glucose." Journal of Neurochemistry 2025 (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12498266/). Same conclusion by an independent transporter-knockdown route.

• Andersen JV et al. "Embracing Scientific Debate in Brain Metabolism." Journal of Neurochemistry 2025 (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/jnc.70230) - an editorial acknowledging the field is still openly split.

• Korean Journal of Physiology and Pharmacology 2025 review, "Brain energy homeostasis: the evolution of the astrocyte-neuron lactate shuttle hypothesis" (https://www.kjpp.net/journal/view.html?uid=2958&amp;vmd=Full), fetched in full this session. Its position is a RECONCILIATION: cell-type-specific analyses detect significant LDHA expression in NEURONS, undermining the assumed strict metabolic division of labour; LDHA and LDHB can compensate for one another (only combined genetic disruption of both fully suppressed lactate secretion in tested cell lines); lactate alone is insufficient to sustain neuronal energy demands during high-frequency gamma oscillations. It also cites Rothman's alternative "glucose sparing by glycogenolysis" model, in which astrocytes conserve extracellular glucose FOR neurons during high activity rather than exporting lactate to them - a mechanistically different explanation for the same observations. The review's own conclusion: neurons possess metabolic flexibility through LDHA expression while still benefiting from efficient lactate utilisation through LDHB - a "nuanced model" rather than a verdict.

• A quantitative anchor from that review: the brain consumes approximately 20 percent of total body energy, and approximately 90 percent of brain ATP is produced by mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation. If 90 percent of ATP is oxidative, then any glycolytic shuttle can at most be a routing mechanism for the carbon, not an alternative to oxidation.

WHERE THIS ACTUALLY STANDS AS OF 2024-2026 - HONEST ASSESSMENT

• What both sides agree on: astrocytes take up glutamate and this is coupled to sodium and to ATP demand; astrocytes are glycolytic and export lactate; extracellular lactate rises during activation; neurons CAN oxidise lactate and will do so if it is present; there is a lactate concentration gradient from astrocyte to neuron in vivo (Machler 2016); activation produces a fall in the CMRO2-to-CMRglc ratio (aerobic glycolysis during activation is real).

• What is genuinely disputed: (1) whether the astrocyte-to-neuron lactate flux is quantitatively important as an energy supply, or merely permissive and opportunistic; (2) whether neurons preferentially use lactate over glucose during activation, or preferentially use their own glycolysis; (3) whether the memory and long-term potentiation effects of blocking astrocytic lactate production are METABOLIC (fuel) or SIGNALLING (lactate acting via HCAR1 receptor, redox state, or histone lactylation); (4) whether the ANLS is a resting-state mechanism, an activation mechanism, or neither.

• THE 2025 EVIDENCE LEANS AGAINST THE STRONG ENERGETIC FORM. Two independent 2025 Journal of Neurochemistry papers (Andersen, Soder) show synaptic transmission does not need lactate transport when glucose is available. Combined with Diaz-Garcia 2017 and Dienel's stoichiometry, the strong claim that neurons are FUELLED by astrocytic lactate during activation is now a minority position. The weaker claim - that lactate is a real, usable, sometimes important supplemental substrate and an important SIGNALLING molecule - is broadly accepted.

• WHAT THE DECISIVE EXPERIMENT WOULD BE. The field's own stated requirements:
◦ Cell-type-specific, in vivo, real-time simultaneous measurement of glucose flux, lactate flux and oxygen consumption in identified neurons and identified astrocytes in the awake behaving animal, with absolute (not relative) calibration. Current biosensors give relative or single-point-calibrated readings and are usually done in slices or anaesthetised animals.
◦ Conditional, cell-type-specific, acute (not chronic, to avoid compensation) genetic ablation of MCT1/MCT4 in astrocytes and MCT2 in neurons, with simultaneous readout of neuronal ATP and behaviour, and with rigorous demonstration that the manipulation did not perturb anything else. The LDHA/LDHB compensation finding above shows why chronic knockouts have been uninformative.
◦ Isotopomer analysis (13C labelling with position-specific mass spectrometry) that can trace whether the carbon oxidised in neuronal mitochondria during activation passed through an astrocytic pool. This is the only method that can settle the flux question directly, and it is hard because it requires cell-type separation after labelling.
◦ Cleanly separating the metabolic from the signalling hypothesis: repeating the Suzuki 2011 memory experiment with a non-metabolisable lactate analogue that activates HCAR1 but cannot be oxidised. If memory is rescued, the effect is signalling, not fuel.
• Until those are done, the honest statement for a reference book is: the ANLS is a well-posed, influential and partially supported hypothesis whose STRONG energetic form is not established and is contradicted by the most recent direct experiments, while astrocyte-neuron metabolic cooperation and a signalling role for lactate are well supported.


SECTION 11 - NEUROVASCULAR COUPLING AND THE HAEMODYNAMIC RESPONSE FUNCTION

• Canonical HRF shape, in order: a small initial dip; a rise to a tall positive peak; a fall; a post-stimulus undershoot; return to baseline.

• Onset: the BOLD signal begins to rise approximately 1 to 2 seconds after stimulus onset. This is the standard teaching but was the LEAST well-sourced number in this session - the sources retrieved emphasise the peak rather than the onset, and one source states signal onset has been observed "after 3 seconds but within 6 seconds of stimulus onset". Treat the 1 to 2 second onset as approximate and method-dependent (it depends heavily on echo time, field strength, vessel size and whether one is measuring in capillaries or draining veins - onset in large draining veins is later than in parenchyma).

• Peak: 4 to 6 seconds after event onset. The canonical SPM double-gamma HRF peaks at 5 seconds. Sources: SPM Haemodynamic Response Function wikibook (https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/SPM/Haemodynamic_Response_Function), the fmrihrf package vignette (https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/fmrihrf/vignettes/a_01_hemodynamic_response.html), and the MIT MindHive HRF FAQ (http://mindhive.mit.edu/book/export/html/72). This is the most consistently reproduced number across sources.

• Return towards baseline: approximately another 10 seconds after the peak; offset is reflected by a fall in BOLD contrast beginning within 6 seconds of stimulus cessation and returning to baseline in approximately 20 seconds.

• Post-stimulus undershoot: begins around 10 to 15 seconds, and the SPM canonical HRF places the undershoot MINIMUM at approximately 15 to 16 seconds. Total HRF duration approximately 30 seconds.

• Quantitative undershoot recovery time: BOLD signal recovery after the undershoot takes 28.1 plus or minus 5.5 seconds. Robust undershoots lasting LONGER than 30 seconds have been observed after 40 to 60 second stimulation blocks. Sources: van Zijl et al. and the review "The BOLD post-stimulus undershoot, one of the most debated issues in fMRI", NeuroImage 2012 (https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pubmed/22248572).

• So the prompt's "undershoot 10 to 30 s" is right in spirit: the undershoot BEGINS around 10 to 15 seconds and RESOLVES around 25 to 35 seconds, with longer stimuli producing longer undershoots.

• THE UNDERSHOOT'S CAUSE IS GENUINELY UNRESOLVED - REPORT AS SUCH. Three candidate mechanisms, with sources on each side:
◦ Delayed vascular compliance - venous (and arteriolar and capillary) blood volume stays elevated after flow returns to baseline, so deoxyhaemoglobin content stays high (the "balloon model").
◦ Sustained elevation of CMRO2 after the stimulus ends.
◦ Post-activation undercutting of CBF below baseline (generally regarded as the weakest of the three).
Hua, Stevens, Huang, Pekar and van Zijl 2011, Journal of Cerebral Blood Flow and Metabolism, "Physiological origin for the BOLD poststimulus undershoot in human brain: vascular compliance versus oxygen metabolism" (https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1038/jcbfm.2011.35) report that during the undershoot, post-arterial cerebral blood volume was only slightly elevated at 2.4 plus or minus 1.8 percent while CMRO2 was above baseline by 10.6 plus or minus 7.4 percent - favouring the metabolic explanation. Other analyses of combined MRI and optical data likewise conclude the predominant origin is a sustained CMRO2 increase. But the field's own summary is that "some contributions from delayed vascular compliance are likely, and relative contributions of different processes may vary with stimulus duration and type" - i.e. no single mechanism accounts for all observations. Also see "Cerebral blood volume changes during the BOLD post-stimulus undershoot measured with a combined normoxia/hyperoxia method" (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1053811918320032).

• The initial dip (a brief negative deflection in the first 1 to 2 seconds, attributed to oxygen consumption preceding the flow response) is REAL but small, inconsistently detected, and is more reliably seen at high field. It should be described as contested, not as a fixture of the HRF.

• The HRF is NOT universal. It is animal-specific and site-specific: Logothetis et al. 2001 showed by linear systems analysis on a trial-by-trial basis that the impulse response of the neurovascular system is both animal-specific and site-specific. HRF shape also differs in white matter - see "Anomalous and heterogeneous characteristics of the BOLD hemodynamic response function in white matter", Cerebral Cortex Communications 2022 (https://academic.oup.com/cercorcomms/article/3/3/tgac035/6671419). This matters: fitting a single canonical HRF across the brain and across subjects introduces systematic bias, and apparent between-region latency differences can be vascular rather than neural.


SECTION 12 - THE HONEST LIMITS OF BOLD

WHAT BOLD PHYSICALLY MEASURES

• BOLD is blood-oxygenation-level-dependent contrast. It measures the change in the concentration of PARAMAGNETIC DEOXYHAEMOGLOBIN in and around a voxel, via its effect on T2-star. It does not measure neural activity, does not measure oxygen consumption, does not measure blood flow, and does not measure glucose use. It is a mixed function of CBF, cerebral blood volume and CMRO2, and the positive BOLD signal arises because activation increases CBF PROPORTIONATELY MORE than it increases CMRO2, so venous blood becomes MORE oxygenated and deoxyhaemoglobin FALLS. The signal is therefore positive because oxygen delivery overshoots oxygen demand - an indirect, several-steps-removed, and sign-inverted proxy.

LOGOTHETIS 2001

• PRIMARY CITATION: Logothetis NK, Pauls J, Augath M, Trinath T, Oeltermann A. "Neurophysiological investigation of the basis of the fMRI signal." Nature 2001; 412: 150-157 (https://www.nature.com/articles/35084005, PubMed 11449264). SOURCING CAVEAT: neither the Nature page nor the wexler.free.fr PDF copy could be text-extracted this session, so the specific correlation coefficients and percentage-variance-explained figures could NOT be verified. Do not state a specific r value or percentage from this paper without opening the original.

• Design: simultaneous intracortical electrophysiological recording and high-resolution BOLD fMRI in the visual cortex of anaesthetised monkeys, comparing local field potentials (LFPs), multi-unit activity (MUA) and single-unit activity against the BOLD response.

• Verified claims, from the abstract and Nature's own summary as retrieved this session:
◦ "The largest magnitude changes were observed in LFPs."
◦ At recording sites characterised by TRANSIENT responses (that is, where spiking adapted away while the stimulus continued), LFPs were "the ONLY signal that significantly correlated with the haemodynamic response". This is the decisive dissociation: BOLD persisted where spiking had ceased.
◦ Linear systems analysis on a trial-by-trial basis showed the impulse response of the neurovascular system is both animal-specific and site-specific.
◦ "LFPs yield a better estimate of BOLD responses than the multi-unit responses."
◦ Conclusion, verbatim: "the BOLD contrast mechanism reflects the INPUT and INTRACORTICAL PROCESSING of a given area rather than its SPIKING OUTPUT."

• Interpretation for the book: LFPs are dominated by synaptic and dendritic currents - the input to and local processing within a region. Spiking is the output. Because BOLD tracks LFPs better than spikes, a BOLD activation tells you a region is RECEIVING and PROCESSING signals; it does NOT tell you the region is firing more. A region receiving strong INHIBITORY input can show increased BOLD while its output firing falls, because inhibitory synaptic activity is metabolically expensive too. This single point invalidates a large amount of casual "region X lit up therefore region X did the computation" reasoning.

• A companion accessible source is Logothetis and Wandell's and Logothetis's own follow-up, "The Underpinnings of the BOLD Functional Magnetic Resonance Imaging Signal", Journal of Neuroscience 2003; 23(10): 3963-3971 (https://www.jneurosci.org/content/23/10/3963) - identified but returned 403 this session.

LOGOTHETIS 2008

• PRIMARY CITATION: Logothetis NK. "What we can do and what we cannot do with fMRI." Nature 2008; 453(7197): 869-878, published 12 June 2008, doi 10.1038/nature06976 (https://www.nature.com/articles/nature06976). SOURCING CAVEAT: the Nature page redirected to an authentication endpoint and the Society for Neuroscience short-course PDF returned as unparseable binary, so the paper's internal quantitative claims could not be extracted verbatim this session. What follows is from the abstract and from secondary reporting retrieved this session.

• Verified claims:
◦ fMRI is "currently the mainstay of neuroimaging in cognitive neuroscience", and advances in scanners, acquisition protocols, experimental design and analysis promise to move fMRI "from mere cartography to the true study of brain organization".
◦ Critically: "fundamental questions concerning the interpretation of fMRI data abound, as the conclusions drawn often ignore the actual limitations of the methodology". The paper's stated purpose is to give "an overview of the current understanding of haemodynamic signals and the constraints they impose on neuroimaging data interpretation".
◦ The paper identifies three properties as decisive for interpretation: SIGNAL SPECIFICITY (does the signal come from the neurons of interest or from draining vessels), SPATIAL RESOLUTION, and TEMPORAL RESOLUTION.
◦ On future spatial resolution: fMRI "may soon provide images of a fraction of a millimetre (for example 300 by 300 micrometres with slice thickness of a couple of millimetres)", approximately 2 to 3 orders of magnitude smaller voxel volumes than those then used in human imaging.

• The substantive limits Logothetis argues for, as commonly summarised (flagged here as NOT verbatim-verified this session, and to be checked against the original before quoting):
◦ BOLD is a mass-action signal integrating over hundreds of thousands to millions of neurons per voxel and cannot distinguish excitation from inhibition, feedforward from feedback, or bottom-up from top-down.
◦ Neuromodulatory input (a diffuse projection carrying little information but high metabolic cost) can produce large BOLD changes with little change in local computation.
◦ The point-spread function of the haemodynamic response, and the draining-vein problem, limit EFFECTIVE spatial resolution well below the nominal voxel size.
◦ The temporal resolution is set by the haemodynamics (seconds), not the acquisition, and so is 2 to 3 orders of magnitude coarser than the neural events of interest (milliseconds).

• THE HONEST ONE-SENTENCE VERSION FOR THE BOOK: BOLD is an indirect, sign-inverted, spatially blurred, temporally smeared, mass-action measure of deoxyhaemoglobin concentration change, which tracks synaptic and dendritic input better than spiking output, and which by Logothetis's own account is adequate for cartography and for testing well-posed hypotheses about where processing occurs, but is not adequate on its own for inferring what a region computes, whether it is excited or inhibited, or in what direction information flows.

• The connection back to Sections 1 to 3 is the point of putting them in one chapter: BOLD exists at all only because CBF and CMRO2 are UNCOUPLED during activation - flow rises far more than oxygen consumption. That uncoupling is exactly the same phenomenon (the fall in the CMRO2-to-CMRglc ratio, "aerobic glycolysis during activation") that generates the excess lactate at the heart of the ANLS controversy in Section 10. The imaging signal that underpins modern cognitive neuroscience and the unresolved substrate controversy are two faces of one unexplained fact.
