PART 1 - NITRIC OXIDE (NO)


1.1 DIFFUSION COEFFICIENT

• The commonly quoted value of 3.3 x 10^-9 m2/s is CORRECT but must be labelled by medium and temperature, because the value differs between free solution and tissue.

• Zacharia and Deen (2005), Annals of Biomedical Engineering, measured nitric oxide diffusivity directly: (2.21 +/- 0.02) x 10^-5 cm2/s in pure water at 25 degrees Celsius, and (2.21 +/- 0.04) x 10^-5 cm2/s in phosphate-buffered saline (PBS) at 25 degrees Celsius.

• The same authors extrapolated to 37 degrees Celsius and obtained approximately 3.0 x 10^-5 cm2/s, which equals 3.0 x 10^-9 m2/s. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10439-005-8980-9

• Unit conversion note, because the literature switches units constantly: 3.3 x 10^-9 m2/s = 3.3 x 10^-5 cm2/s = 3300 micrometres squared per second = 3.3 micrometres squared per millisecond.

• Garthwaite (2016), "From synaptically localized to volume transmission by nitric oxide", states the aqueous diffusion coefficient as 3.3 micrometres squared per millisecond, and notes this means NO travels about 2.5 micrometres in 1 millisecond in aqueous solution. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4704503/

• The same source notes NO crosses the roughly 3 nanometre hydrophobic interior of a lipid membrane in about 3 nanoseconds, which is why membranes are treated as no barrier at all.

• IMPORTANT CAVEAT - tissue is not water. A Biophysical Journal study of the aortic wall found the NO diffusion coefficient there to be nearly fourfold smaller than the reported solution value at 37 degrees Celsius, concluding that NO diffusion in the vascular wall is "no longer free" and is markedly dependent on the tissue environment. https://www.cell.com/biophysj/fulltext/S0006-3495(08)70625-8

• Hall and Attwell (2008), working on brain tissue, used a diffusion constant of 1.98 x 10^-3 cm2/min, which equals 3.3 x 10^-5 cm2/s. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2538835/

• Reported values in water or buffer at 20 degrees Celsius span roughly 2000 to 4500 micrometres squared per second across the older literature, so a spread of better than twofold exists even for the "easy" case of free solution.

• BOTTOM LINE - quote 3.3 x 10^-9 m2/s as the aqueous value at body temperature, and state that in real tissue the effective value may be up to about fourfold lower.


1.2 HALF-LIFE IN TISSUE - GENUINELY CONTESTED

The claimed half-life of NO varies across roughly four orders of magnitude in the published literature.

The disagreement is not sloppiness; it is because the half-life depends entirely on which sink dominates in the compartment being measured.

• Intravascular half-life, dominated by consumption by red blood cells: approximately 2 milliseconds. NO diffuses into the red cell and reacts with oxyhaemoglobin to give nitrate.

• Extravascular half-life: reported as 0.09 seconds to more than 2 seconds, depending on the oxygen concentration and on the distance from the nearest blood vessel.

• Brain tissue specifically, from Garthwaite (2016): a half-life of 4.6 milliseconds in cerebellum, corresponding to a first-order decay rate constant of 150 per second; the plausible range given is 5 to 14 milliseconds depending on which inactivation mechanism is assumed. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4704503/

• The brain inactivation process is described as a saturable, enzyme-like process with an apparent Michaelis constant (Km) of 10 nanomolar and a maximum velocity (Vmax) of 1.5 micromolar per second.

• Hall and Attwell (2008) measured the brain NO-consumption Vmax in cultured slices at 1.11 micromolar per minute, and explicitly noted this is substantially LOWER than the prior published estimates of 60 to 120 micromolar per minute. That is a roughly 60- to 100-fold downward revision, and it directly lengthens the modelled NO lifetime. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2538835/

• Pacher, Beckman and Liaudet (2007), Physiological Reviews, give the biological half-life in vivo as less than 1 second, and about 6 to 8 seconds in a perfusion buffer saturated with 100 percent oxygen. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2248324/

• General clinical and cardiovascular sources quote "a few seconds", "3 to 5 seconds", or "seconds to a minute". These figures are for whole-blood or whole-organism contexts and should NOT be transplanted into a synaptic argument.

• The three chemical sinks that set the number: (a) oxyhaemoglobin in red cells, which is essentially diffusion-limited and dominates near any capillary; (b) superoxide, with a rate constant for the NO plus superoxide reaction of 6.7 x 10^9 per molar per second, forming peroxynitrite - this is near the diffusion limit and is why local superoxide production shortens NO life dramatically; (c) autoxidation with molecular oxygen, which is second order in NO and therefore is only relevant at micromolar NO and is negligible at the low nanomolar concentrations that are actually physiological.

• Cytochrome P450 oxidoreductase has been identified as a participant in NO consumption by rat brain, which is part of why the brain sink is saturable and enzyme-like rather than purely chemical. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2662488/

• HONEST SUMMARY - for brain tissue the best modern number is roughly 5 to 15 milliseconds. For blood it is roughly 2 milliseconds. For loosely defined "in vivo" it is often quoted as under 1 second up to a few seconds. A book should give the range with the compartment attached to each end of it, and should say that the sub-second-to-seconds figures come from vascular and whole-body work rather than from neuropil.


1.3 DIFFUSION RADIUS - THE CLASSIC CLAIM HAS BEEN LARGELY RETRACTED BY ITS OWN AUTHOR

This is one of the clearest cases in neuroscience of a famous number being publicly withdrawn.

• THE CLASSIC CLAIM. Wood J and Garthwaite J (1994), Neuropharmacology 33(11):1235-1244, "Models of the diffusional spread of nitric oxide: implications for neural nitric oxide signalling and its pharmacological properties". https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7870284/

• That model concluded that a single point source emitting for 1 to 10 seconds has a physiological sphere of influence about 200 micrometres in DIAMETER, that is roughly 100 micrometres in radius, enclosing a volume of brain containing approximately 2 million synapses.

• Modelling assumptions used: an NO point source surrounded by a sphere of 0.5 micrometre radius; point source strength 2.1 x 10^-17 moles per second; producing an NO concentration of 1 micromolar at the surface of that sphere; first-order decay of NO.

• THE PROBLEM. The 1 micromolar figure was taken from a single electrochemical report. Garthwaite's own later review states that older probes "have given improbably large variations in estimates of NO concentration, ranging across six orders of magnitude", and that the 1994 conclusion is now recognised as severely flawed.

• THE MODERN REVISION. Garthwaite (2016) re-ran the analysis with realistic synthesis rates that are "more than five orders of magnitude lower" than the 1994 assumption. Modelling a single synapse as a 400 nanometre diameter zone producing 40 NO molecules per second gives: peak concentration approximately 60 picomolar at the source, falling to 5 picomolar at 1 micrometre away. The conclusion is "very local spread of NO away from such a source, suggesting a high degree of synapse specificity". https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4704503/

• VOLUME TRANSMISSION IS NOT ABOLISHED, IT IS RESCALED. When many nNOS-expressing neurons fire together, NO sums. For sources spaced 2 micrometres apart, the intervening space holds roughly half the peak source concentration; a dense three-dimensional array reaches a steady-state tissue concentration of approximately 67 picomolar. Given that synapses in neuropil are spaced about 0.5 micrometres apart, immediate neighbours will receive a signal.

• HONEST STATEMENT FOR THE BOOK. The "NO signals over 100 to 300 micrometres and reaches 2 million synapses" figure should be presented as a historically important but superseded estimate, with the correction attributed to the original author. A single active synapse influences its immediate neighbours over roughly 1 to a few micrometres. Volume transmission over tens of micrometres requires a population of simultaneously active sources, not a single one. The 100 to 300 micrometre figure has NOT been experimentally confirmed and rests on an NO source strength now believed to be about 100000 times too large.


1.4 NITRIC OXIDE SYNTHASE ISOFORMS

Shared architecture, from Forstermann and Sessa (European Heart Journal 2012) and Pacher et al. (Physiological Reviews 2007). https://www.unimedizin-mainz.de/fileadmin/kliniken/cth/Dokumente/Publikationen/European_Heart_Journal_2011_Forstermann.pdf

• All three isoforms are homodimers. Each monomer has a C-terminal reductase domain and an N-terminal oxygenase domain, joined by a calmodulin-binding linker peptide.

• Reductase domain binds: NADPH (nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate, the electron donor), FAD (flavin adenine dinucleotide), FMN (flavin mononucleotide). This domain is homologous to cytochrome P450 reductase.

• Oxygenase domain binds: haem (iron protoporphyrin IX), BH4 (tetrahydrobiopterin, formally (6R)-5,6,7,8-tetrahydrobiopterin), and the substrate L-arginine.

• Electron path: NADPH to FAD to FMN in the reductase domain of ONE monomer, then across to the haem iron in the oxygenase domain of the OTHER monomer. This obligate cross-monomer electron transfer is why the dimer is the only active form.

• BH4 role: it binds at the haem active site at the dimer interface, stabilises the dimer, and increases L-arginine interaction. When BH4 is deficient the enzyme "uncouples" and produces superoxide instead of NO, which is the mechanistic basis of endothelial dysfunction.

• Product: NO plus L-citrulline from L-arginine, molecular oxygen and NADPH.

nNOS, also called NOS1 or NOS-I

• Calcium-dependent. Requires a rise in intracellular calcium so that calcium-calmodulin can bind to the linker.

• Localisation: has an N-terminal anchoring sequence (a PDZ domain) that localises the enzyme to the cytoskeleton in postsynaptic boutons; in hippocampal pyramidal neurones nNOS is concentrated POSTsynaptically.

• Pacher et al. state that nNOS produces approximately 20 times more NO than the entire vasculature does.

• Cofactors: haem, FAD, FMN, BH4, calmodulin.

eNOS, also called NOS3 or NOS-III

• Calcium-dependent, strongly activated by calcium entry through membrane receptors.

• Localisation: binds plasma membranes, typically associated with caveolin (myristoylated and palmitoylated, targeting it to caveolae).

• Additionally regulated by phosphorylation. Phosphorylation at serine 1177 in human eNOS (serine 1179 in bovine) enhances electron flux and reduces calmodulin dissociation, which is why phosphorylated eNOS is sometimes loosely described as "calcium-independent" - this is a kinetic shift, not true calcium independence. https://www.jbc.org/article/S0021-9258(18)30460-5/fulltext

• Cofactors: haem, FAD, FMN, BH4, calmodulin.

• Historical note: eNOS was the first NOS activity identified but the last isoform to be cloned.

iNOS, also called NOS2 or NOS-II

• NOT regulated by physiological changes in calcium. Calmodulin binds so tightly that it copurifies with the enzyme, so iNOS is fully active at basal resting calcium.

• Regulation is therefore transcriptional. It is induced by proinflammatory cytokines and by bacterial lipopolysaccharide, over hours.

• Consequence: once expressed, iNOS produces NO continuously and at far higher output than the constitutive isoforms, which is the basis of its role in inflammation and septic shock.

• Cofactors: haem, FAD, FMN, BH4, calmodulin.

• Calcium sensitivity comparison: iNOS binds calmodulin tightly at low calcium and is catalytically active at basal cellular calcium; eNOS and nNOS bind calmodulin reversibly in a calcium-concentration-dependent manner and require raised calcium for catalysis. eNOS is described as less stringent than nNOS or iNOS in requiring calcium binding at each of calmodulin's four calcium sites. https://febs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/j.1742-4658.2011.08395.x

• I did NOT find a single agreed-upon numerical calcium EC50 for nNOS or eNOS activation in the sources retrieved; the literature reports it as a calmodulin-concentration EC50 rather than a free-calcium EC50, and the values are assay-dependent. State this as not firmly established rather than quoting a round number.


1.5 SOLUBLE GUANYLYL CYCLASE - EC50 FOR NO

There is a genuine and important discrepancy here, spanning about two orders of magnitude, and it maps onto the in vitro versus in-cell distinction.

• PURIFIED OR IN VITRO ENZYME, HIGHER VALUES. Roy, Halvey and Garthwaite report a predicted EC50 of 120 nanomolar, "in good agreement with published estimates in the 80 to 250 nanomolar range". Original context: Bellamy and Garthwaite, PNAS 2002, "On the activation of soluble guanylyl cyclase by nitric oxide". https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.012368499 and https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11752394/

• INTACT CELLS, LOWER VALUES. Batchelor, Bartus, Garthwaite and colleagues, PNAS 2010, 107(51):22060-22065, measured an EC50 of 6.9 nanomolar in HEK 293T cells using the delta-FlincG cyclic GMP biosensor, against a model prediction of 6.0 nanomolar. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3009818/

• Garthwaite (2016) quotes a binding affinity (dissociation constant, Kd) of 20 to 50 nanomolar for isolated sGC protein, and a half-maximal activation EC50 of 10 nanomolar in cells.

• Hall and Attwell (2008) quote a still lower figure: "NO activates guanylyl cyclase with an EC50 of 1 to 4 nanomolar".

• WHY THE DISAGREEMENT. Purified enzyme assays use steady, high, sustained NO and measure the enzyme in isolation; the enzyme desensitises and the effective potency drops. In intact cells there is amplification: at low agonist concentrations each bound NO molecule drives synthesis of about 5000 molecules of cyclic GMP per second (Garthwaite 2016), and the cell's phosphodiesterase complement shapes the readout. The 2010 PNAS paper found the cellular EC50 to be roughly 3-fold lower than the purified protein's Kd.

• DETECTION THRESHOLD IS FAR BELOW THE EC50. Engineered detector cells with high sGC and low phosphodiesterase responded to 1 picomolar NO; the more conservative configuration including phosphodiesterase-5 still registered 10 picomolar. A 100 millisecond NO puff giving an estimated intracellular peak of 23 picomolar produced a detectable signal, and cells reached 90 percent of steady-state output in about 800 milliseconds. This is detection at concentrations roughly 10000-fold below the EC50.

• RECOMMENDED PHRASING - the EC50 for sGC activation by NO is low nanomolar in intact cells, best cited as 1 to 10 nanomolar, with 6.9 nanomolar as the single best-measured value; purified-enzyme preparations give substantially higher values of 80 to 250 nanomolar; and the threshold for a biologically detectable cyclic GMP response is in the single-digit picomolar range. Do not quote one number without the context.

• Other NO targets for comparison, from Hall and Attwell: half-maximal inhibition of mitochondrial respiration in synaptosomes or dissociated cerebellar cells occurs at 60 to 120 nanomolar NO; the derived competitive inhibition constant for cytochrome c oxidase is 2.9 nanomolar. At true physiological NO the inhibition of respiration is only 0.4 to 0.7 percent for nNOS-derived NO and 1.7 percent for iNOS-derived NO. So sGC is the physiological receptor and mitochondrial inhibition is a pathological-range effect.


1.6 PHYSIOLOGICAL NO CONCENTRATIONS - AND THE MEASUREMENT PROBLEM

THE MEASUREMENT PROBLEM, STATED HONESTLY

• Electrochemical NO microsensors in brain tissue have yielded values from less than 0.5 nanomolar to more than 1 micromolar - a spread of more than three orders of magnitude. Garthwaite's review puts the total spread at six orders of magnitude.

• The attributed cause is cross-reactivity of the sensors with other species, which causes overestimation of the true NO concentration. Sources: Hall and Attwell 2008 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2538835/) and Garthwaite 2016.

• Historically, "a decade ago a value of about 1 micromolar seemed reasonable based on early electrode measurements". That 1 micromolar figure is the one that propagated into the 1994 diffusion model and thence into textbooks. It is now regarded as an artefact.

BEST MODERN ESTIMATES

• nNOS-derived NO in vivo: maximum 0.6 to 1.1 nanomolar (Hall and Attwell 2008).

• iNOS-derived NO under pathological conditions: maximum 2.5 nanomolar (same source).

• nNOS in cultured brain slices: 6 to 23 nanomolar, and these are stated as UPPER DETECTION LIMITS, not measured values.

• Electrical stimulation of cerebellar slices with electrodes at the slice surface: 4 nanomolar NO.

• At a single synapse, modelled: approximately 60 picomolar at the source, 5 picomolar at 1 micrometre.

• Steady-state tissue concentration in a dense three-dimensional array of active sources: approximately 67 picomolar.

• Sub-nanomolar relevance: NO-dependent phosphorylation events have been reported after exposure to sub-nanomolar NO.

• A chemical consistency check supporting the low figures: at low nanomolar concentrations NO is remarkably unreactive. Its reaction with molecular oxygen is second-order in NO, so it is obvious at micromolar NO but negligible at low nanomolar NO. If NO really reached micromolar it would destroy itself far faster than observed.

• RECOMMENDED PHRASING - basal NO in brain is best stated as roughly 0.1 to a few nanomolar, with peak physiological signals in the low nanomolar range and single-synapse signals in the tens of picomolar. The frequently quoted "up to 100 nanomolar" and the older micromolar figures should be flagged as probably reflecting electrode cross-reactivity artefacts. Real-time electrical detection with sub-nanomolar sensitivity is only a recent capability (see Nature Communications 2013, https://www.nature.com/articles/ncomms3225).

• WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT - a genuinely NO-specific, calibrated, sub-nanomolar, sub-second in vivo sensor validated in nNOS-knockout tissue as a negative control. Genetically encoded cyclic GMP biosensors such as delta-FlincG currently give the most trustworthy indirect readout, but they report receptor occupancy, not concentration, and require an assumed EC50 to invert.


1.7 RETROGRADE SIGNALLING AND LONG-TERM POTENTIATION (LTP) - HOW SOLID IS IT?

Long-term potentiation (LTP) is a long-lasting strengthening of a synapse after high-frequency stimulation, and is the leading cellular model of memory.

EVIDENCE IN FAVOUR

• Anatomical fit. Garthwaite (2016): in pyramidal neurones nNOS is concentrated postsynaptically, whereas NO-activated guanylyl cyclase protein is found mainly presynaptically in excitatory axon terminals. This is exactly the arrangement a retrograde messenger requires.

• Arancio and colleagues (Cell 1996, and PubMed 9089501) reported that NO is produced in the postsynaptic neurone, travels through extracellular space, and acts directly in the presynaptic neurone to produce LTP in cultured hippocampal neurones. https://www.cell.com/fulltext/S0092-8674(00)81797-3

• Pharmacology: NOS inhibitors block LTP in many preparations. In a presynaptic glutamatergic LTP at paraventricular nucleus magnocellular neurosecretory cells, high-frequency-stimulation-induced LTP was completely prevented by a NOS inhibitor. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6610542/

• Both NO-sensitive guanylyl cyclase isoforms are required for hippocampal LTP, and two distinct cyclic GMP pathways appear to be needed (Journal of Neuroscience 29:9344). https://www.jneurosci.org/content/29/29/9344

EVIDENCE AGAINST, OR AT LEAST COMPLICATING

• The source of the NO is genuinely unresolved. NO-dependent LTP was preserved in nNOS-deficient mice but lost in eNOS knockouts in one study, whereas a previous study found that a COMBINED nNOS and eNOS deficit was required to eliminate NO-dependent LTP. Garthwaite's own words: "Where and how the active NO is formed remains unresolved, and this constitutes an important obstacle in understanding how the pathway functions." https://www.jneurosci.org/content/23/5/1941

• A tonic-plus-phasic model has been proposed to reconcile this: eNOS in blood vessels supplies a tonic NO signal and nNOS in neurones supplies the phasic one, with both required. https://www.jneurosci.org/content/26/45/11513

• But a different form of LTP contradicts even that. L-type voltage-gated calcium channel-dependent, NMDA-receptor-independent LTP requires NO but was UNAFFECTED in eNOS-knockout mice, implying a neuronal NO source. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4925670/

• The sGC-specific inhibitor ODQ did not completely block LTP induction, especially the early phase, even though the major fraction of LTP was cyclic GMP-dependent. Cyclic GMP analogues rescued LTP in one knockout strain but not another.

• LTP dependence on NO is pathway-selective and region-selective; for example in anterior cingulate cortex the dependence varies by pathway. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11118802/

• Historical weakness: much of the original evidence rested on NOS inhibitors and haemoglobin scavenging, both of which have off-target effects, and on the assumption that NO reached micromolar concentrations - an assumption now known to be wrong by about five orders of magnitude.

EVIDENCE GRADE FOR NO IN LTP - MODERATE TO STRONG, BUT NOT SETTLED

• Strong: NO signalling contributes to several forms of synaptic plasticity, the anatomy supports a retrograde direction, and the receptor (sGC) and downstream pathway (cyclic GMP, protein kinase G) are identified.

• Not settled: which NOS isoform supplies the NO, whether the NO is truly retrograde in the intact hippocampus as opposed to permissive or tonic, and whether NO is necessary or merely modulatory. Knockout results are mutually inconsistent across laboratories.

• WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT - conditional, cell-type-restricted nNOS and eNOS deletions combined with a real-time cyclic GMP biosensor imaged in the presynaptic terminal during LTP induction, so that the source, the destination and the timing are all observed simultaneously rather than inferred from pharmacology.


PART 2 - CARBON MONOXIDE (CO)


2.1 HAEM OXYGENASE AND THE REACTION

• Reaction stoichiometry: haem oxygenase oxidises haem (ferriprotoporphyrin IX) to biliverdin-IX-alpha, consuming 3 moles of molecular oxygen per mole of haem, with NADPH-cytochrome P450 reductase as the electron source. The products are equimolar biliverdin-IX-alpha, carbon monoxide, and ferrous iron (Fe2+). Source: Physiological Reviews, "Heme Oxygenase-1/Carbon Monoxide: From Basic Science to Therapeutic Applications". https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/physrev.00011.2005

• So the balanced summary is: haem plus 3 O2 plus NADPH-derived electrons gives biliverdin plus CO plus Fe2+ plus water. Note that the CO and the Fe2+ and the biliverdin come out in a strict 1 to 1 to 1 ratio - CO production is therefore rigidly coupled to haem turnover, unlike NO production which is coupled to a dedicated signalling enzyme. This is a structural reason to doubt CO as a fast messenger.

• Mechanism in four steps: (1) one electron reduces the haem-Fe3+-HO complex to haem-Fe2+-HO; (2) with oxygen and a second electron, haem-Fe2+ becomes alpha-meso-hydroxyhaem; (3) alpha-meso-hydroxyhaem is oxidised to verdohaem, and THIS is the step that releases CO; (4) with oxygen and a further electron, verdohaem becomes biliverdin and Fe2+ is released.

• Biliverdin is then reduced to bilirubin by biliverdin reductase, and the free Fe2+ induces ferritin. So the haem oxygenase reaction yields four bioactive products, not one.

HO-1 versus HO-2

• HO-1 is the INDUCIBLE isoform, also known as heat shock protein 32. In normal brain, HO-1 messenger RNA and protein are confined to small scattered populations of neurones and neuroglia; it is synthesised mainly in microglia and is strongly induced by oxidative stress, haem, heavy metals and inflammation.

• HO-2 is the CONSTITUTIVE isoform. In normal brain its expression is constitutive, abundant and fairly ubiquitous, in neurones and endothelial cells - specifically pyramidal cells, granule cells and endothelial cells, across forebrain, hippocampus, hypothalamus, midbrain, basal ganglia, thalamus, cerebellum and brainstem. https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/neuroscience/heme-oxygenase

• KEY ANATOMICAL ARGUMENT FOR CO AS A MESSENGER - HO-2 is found in discrete neuronal populations whose distribution closely parallels that of guanylyl cyclase. This parallel distribution is the strongest circumstantial evidence for the CO hypothesis.

• HO-2 is also described as committed to physiological haem turnover and to intracellular oxygen sensing, and as a buffer of labile ferric haem. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8808069/

• Regulated CO production: HO-2 is acutely activated by casein kinase 2 (CK2) phosphorylation. Odorant treatment of olfactory receptor neurones augments HO-2 phosphorylation and activity and raises cyclic GMP. Source: Boehning et al., Neuron 2004, "Carbon Monoxide Neurotransmission Activated by CK2 Phosphorylation of Heme Oxygenase-2". https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0896627303005968 This is the best mechanistic answer to the objection that CO production cannot be rapidly regulated.


2.2 CO AFFINITY FOR HAEMOGLOBIN - THE 210 VERSUS 250 QUESTION

• The relevant quantity is the Haldane relative affinity constant, conventionally written M, defined as the ratio of half-saturation partial pressures, that is M equals PO2 divided by PCO at equal saturations. Haldane's law is the assumption that M is a fixed constant.

• THE COMMONLY TAUGHT VALUE. M is usually assumed to be about 200 to 250.

• THE BEST MODERN NUMBER. A systematic review and meta-analysis of human data gives M equals 218 for adult haemoglobin (HbA) at physiological conditions, and M equals 175 for fetal haemoglobin (HbF). Sources: Journal of Evolutionary Biochemistry and Physiology comparative meta-analysis (https://link.springer.com/article/10.1134/S0022093019050028) and the human-specific systematic review and meta-analysis (https://www.researchgate.net/publication/341347059_Relative_Affinity_M_Constant_of_Adult_and_Fetal_Hemoglobin_for_Carbon_Monoxide_in_Humans_a_Systematic_Review_and_Meta-Analysis).

• The lower fetal value of 175 matters clinically, because it is often wrongly assumed that fetal haemoglobin is more avid for CO.

• WHY SOURCES DISAGREE - M IS NOT ACTUALLY A CONSTANT. Reported values differ between authors going back to Douglas et al. 1912, Sendroy et al. 1929 and Killick 1936. Measured partition coefficients are 263 +/- 27 at high saturation but substantially lower at low saturation. The dependence is conformational: the tense (T) state value is 123 +/- 34 and the relaxed (R) state value is 241 +/- 19. One study at 37 degrees Celsius reports K37 equals 230. M also depends on pH.

• RECOMMENDED PHRASING - carbon monoxide binds adult human haemoglobin with roughly 200 to 250 times the affinity of oxygen; the best meta-analytic value is 218. State explicitly that this ratio is not a true constant, that it varies with haemoglobin saturation (about 123 in the T state, about 241 in the R state), with pH, and with haemoglobin type (about 175 for fetal haemoglobin). Anyone quoting a single figure to three significant figures is overstating the precision.

• For scale within the sGC argument: CO's affinity for haemoglobin corresponds to a dissociation constant of 0.7 to 4.5 nanomolar for the high-affinity R state and 1.8 to 4.5 micromolar for the low-affinity T state.


2.3 ENDOGENOUS CO PRODUCTION AND BASELINE CARBOXYHAEMOGLOBIN

• Production rate: a healthy adult produces about 0.4 to 0.42 millilitres of CO per hour at standard temperature and pressure, dry, which is about 0.006 millilitres per hour per kilogram of body weight. Sources: ATSDR Toxicological Profile for Carbon Monoxide (https://www.atsdr.cdc.gov/toxprofiles/tp201-c2.pdf) and Journal of Applied Physiology, "The measurement of endogenous carbon monoxide production" (https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/japplphysiol.00174.2012).

• In molar terms this corresponds to roughly 16 to 20 micromoles per hour; the JAP method quotes a precision of +/- 2 micromoles per hour, described as about 10 percent of the mean normal rate, which is consistent with a mean normal rate near 16 to 20 micromoles per hour.

• Source of the CO: essentially all of it comes from catabolism of haem-containing proteins by haem oxygenase, principally in liver and spleen. Erythrocyte turnover accounts for about 80 percent of baseline CO production.

• Baseline carboxyhaemoglobin (COHb) in non-smokers with no occupational exposure: 0.5 to 1.5 percent. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK557888/

• One source states that endogenous production is responsible for less than 3 percent COHb, which is consistent with the 0.5 to 1.5 percent baseline plus haemolytic states.

• Haemolysis raises endogenous CO production measurably, which is the basis of using point-of-care COHb to detect haemolysis, for example in extracorporeal membrane oxygenation. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5799617/

• Tissue CO concentrations: gas chromatography measurements in rat tissue give 1 to 10 picomoles per milligram, corresponding to about 1 to 10 micromolar. Intracellular estimates in hepatocytes have been put as high as about 125 micromolar. Source: "Carbon Monoxide Signaling and Soluble Guanylyl Cyclase: Facts, Myths, and Intriguing Possibilities". https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10257856/

• Note the tension in those numbers: CO tissue concentrations are quoted in micromolar, that is roughly a thousand times higher than NO. That is because CO is not consumed - there is no CO equivalent of the NO scavenging machinery. High and slowly changing concentration is exactly the profile of a tonic modulator, not a fast messenger.


2.4 EVIDENCE GRADE FOR CO AS A NEUROTRANSMITTER - EXPLICITLY WEAKER THAN FOR NO

STATED PLAINLY - the evidence that carbon monoxide functions as a neurotransmitter is substantially weaker than the evidence for nitric oxide. It should be presented as a plausible but unproven hypothesis, not as established fact.

The reasons, each with a number attached:

• REASON 1 - POTENCY AT THE PROPOSED RECEPTOR IS TERRIBLE. The dissociation constant for CO binding to soluble guanylyl cyclase is approximately 240 micromolar. Compare NO, which activates sGC with an EC50 of about 1 to 10 nanomolar in cells. That is a difference of roughly four to five orders of magnitude in favour of NO. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10257856/

• REASON 2 - EFFICACY IS ALSO POOR. CO increases sGC catalytic activity by only about 3- to 6-fold. NO increases it by about 200-fold. So CO is both far less potent and far less efficacious at the same receptor.

• REASON 3 - THE THERMODYNAMIC OBJECTION. The review poses it directly: given that CO's affinity for haemoglobin is nanomolar to low micromolar while its affinity for sGC is about 240 micromolar, "how would CO go up the huge energy hill to be transferred from a high-affinity binder (haemoglobin) to a low-affinity target (sGC)"? In any tissue containing haemoglobin or myoglobin, those proteins should sequester CO long before sGC sees it.

• REASON 4 - THE PRODUCTION MECHANISM IS NOT PURPOSE-BUILT. CO is an obligate stoichiometric by-product of haem degradation. Unlike NOS, which exists solely to make NO on demand, haem oxygenase exists to dispose of haem. Regulated, rapid CO release requires the additional CK2-phosphorylation mechanism, which is established for HO-2 in olfactory neurones but not broadly.

• REASON 5 - THE REVIEW'S OWN CONCLUSION. In-vivo CO-sGC signalling "is yet to be convincingly established as playing generally critical roles" under normal physiological conditions. Whether CO's vasodilatory effects can even be attributed to sGC activation "is still a matter of debate".

EVIDENCE THAT DOES SUPPORT CO

• HO-2 knockout mice: in HO-2 null enteric preparations, responses to electrical field stimulation are nearly abolished despite persistent nNOS expression, and exogenous CO restores normal responses. This is the strongest single piece of genetic evidence, and it also implies the NO system does not function without CO generation, suggesting the two gases are interdependent. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC26525/

• HO-2 knockout mice show ejaculatory abnormalities, consistent with a peripheral neurotransmitter role. https://www.nature.com/articles/nm0198-084

• Olfactory receptor neurones are argued to generate micromolar CO locally, which is sufficient to activate endogenous sGC - this is the one context where the potency objection may be answered.

• The HO-2 and guanylyl cyclase distributions in brain parallel each other closely.

• CO has been implicated in long-term potentiation, olfactory receptor neurone activity, and circadian rhythms.

CAVEATS ON THAT SUPPORTING EVIDENCE

• Haem oxygenase produces four products - CO, biliverdin, bilirubin and ferrous iron. Bilirubin is a powerful antioxidant and free iron is a powerful pro-oxidant. Any HO-2 knockout phenotype could be caused by any of the four. Attributing it to CO requires rescue with CO specifically, which the enteric study did do but most studies do not.

• Widely used haem oxygenase inhibitors are metalloporphyrins, which also inhibit NOS and sGC directly. Much of the older pharmacological literature is therefore uninterpretable.

• WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT - a genuinely CO-selective, quantitative, real-time intracellular CO sensor, plus demonstration that physiological CO concentrations at the location of sGC actually reach levels capable of activating it, plus knockouts rescued by CO alone rather than by restoring haem catabolism generally.

• RECOMMENDED EVIDENCE GRADE - NO: strong, receptor identified, potency matched to measured concentrations, genetic and pharmacological evidence converge. CO: weak to moderate, receptor identity contested, a four-to-five-order-of-magnitude potency mismatch unexplained outside special cases, genetic evidence confounded by the other three haem oxygenase products.


PART 3 - HYDROGEN SULPHIDE (H2S)


3.1 THE ENZYMES AND THEIR BRAIN DISTRIBUTION

Three enzymes, all sulphur-amino-acid metabolising, are credited with H2S production.

• CBS - cystathionine beta-synthase. Cytosolic, pyridoxal-5-phosphate (PLP, vitamin B6-derived) dependent. In brain, CBS shows its highest enzymatic activity in GLIAL cells of the hippocampus and cerebellum. More specifically, CBS is localised to astrocytes, most intensively in cerebellar Bergmann glia. Sources: Kimura 2020, British Journal of Pharmacology (https://bpspubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/bph.15246) and the 2025 review "An Enigma of Brain Gasotransmitters: Hydrogen Sulfide and Depression" (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12334376/).

• ON THE CBS-IN-ASTROCYTES CLAIM. The astrocytic localisation of CBS is the current consensus in the H2S literature, and it is important because Abe and Kimura's founding 1996 paper attributed brain H2S to CBS. However, the claim rests substantially on immunohistochemistry, and antibody specificity for CBS has been a recurring problem in the field. Older work placed CBS in neurones. The safest statement for a reference book is: CBS is predominantly glial or astrocytic in the brain, most strongly in cerebellar Bergmann glia and in hippocampal glia, though the neuronal-versus-glial assignment has shifted over time and rests largely on immunostaining. Treat this as probable rather than certain.

• CSE - cystathionine gamma-lyase, also written CGL. Cytosolic, PLP-dependent. One source describes CSE as predominantly neuronal in brain; another, more specific, describes it as predominantly expressed in spinal cord microglia and cerebellar granule neurones. CSE is the dominant H2S enzyme in the cardiovascular system and liver rather than in brain. Note the disagreement between "predominantly neuronal" and "spinal cord microglia" - the field has not converged.

• Recent work argues CSE is a major regulator of cognitive function through neurotrophin signalling and neurogenesis (PNAS 2025), which if replicated would raise CSE's standing in brain. https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.2528478122

• 3-MST - 3-mercaptopyruvate sulfurtransferase. Mitochondrial, and NOT PLP-dependent. It works in tandem with cysteine aminotransferase (CAT), which converts cysteine to 3-mercaptopyruvate; 3-MST then transfers the sulphur. 3-MST is widely distributed in BOTH neurones and astrocytes throughout the brain. Sources: PLOS One, "Brain 3-Mercaptopyruvate Sulfurtransferase: Cellular Localization and Downregulation after Acute Stroke" (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3689812/).

• 3-MST is also itself a protein persulfidase, that is it transfers sulphur directly onto target protein cysteines rather than releasing free H2S. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10060159/ This matters enormously for section 3.3.

• A fourth pathway: H2S can also be produced from D-cysteine via D-amino acid oxidase feeding 3-MST, principally in cerebellum and kidney. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23882260/

• Summary triple that is safe to state: CBS is mainly glial or astrocytic, concentrated in hippocampus and cerebellum; CSE is mainly neuronal in brain but is quantitatively far more important outside the brain; 3-MST is mitochondrial and is present in both neurones and astrocytes throughout the brain.


3.2 BRAIN H2S CONCENTRATION - THE MAJOR HONESTY POINT

This is a textbook case of a physiological constant being wrong by three orders of magnitude for two decades.

THE OLD VALUES

• The classical figure quoted throughout the 1996 to 2008 literature is 50 to 160 micromolar H2S in the central nervous system.

• The primary sources for that figure are Goodwin et al. (1989) and Warenycia et al. (1989a), which measured sulphide in brain tissue of rats, largely in the context of sulphide poisoning.

• Abe K and Kimura H (1996), Journal of Neuroscience 16(3):1066-1071, "The possible role of hydrogen sulfide as an endogenous neuromodulator", cited exactly those two papers for the statement that "H2S in the brain is relatively high (50-160 micromolar)". https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8558235/

• That paper is the founding paper of the field. It showed that CBS produces H2S in brain and that H2S facilitates induction of hippocampal LTP by enhancing NMDA receptor activity, and that the H2S donor sodium hydrosulphide (NaHS) affected excitatory postsynaptic potentials in area CA1.

• The 50 to 160 micromolar figure is what justified using H2S donors at 100 micromolar and above in essentially all subsequent experiments. That is the reason the correction matters so much: an entire generation of pharmacology was performed at concentrations now known to be about 1000 to 10000 times higher than the real endogenous level.

THE MODERN VALUES

• Furne J, Saeed A, Levitt MD (2008), American Journal of Physiology - Regulatory, Integrative and Comparative Physiology 295(5):R1479-R1485, "Whole tissue hydrogen sulfide concentrations are orders of magnitude lower than presently accepted values". https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18799635/ and https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/ajpregu.90566.2008

• Their method: analysis of the gas space above RAPIDLY homogenised mouse brain and liver, that is headspace gas analysis rather than a colorimetric assay on acid-treated tissue.

• Their result: in-situ tissue H2S concentrations of only about 15 nanomolar in brain and liver. They stated explicitly that free H2S concentrations are three orders of magnitude less than conventionally accepted values, and characterised the prior paradigm as maintaining whole-tissue H2S of 30 micromolar to more than 100 micromolar.

• Independent confirmation of the low figure: a measurement of 14 +/- 3.0 nanomolar free H2S based on the brains of eight mice.

• Whitfield, Kreimier, Verdial, Skovgaard and Olson, American Journal of Physiology - Regulatory, Integrative and Comparative Physiology, "Reappraisal of H2S/sulfide concentration in vertebrate blood and its potential significance in ischemic preconditioning and vascular signaling": H2S gas was essentially UNDETECTABLE, defined as less than 100 nanomolar total sulphide, in all animals tested, contrary to then-current reports. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18417642/

• Cirino, Szabo and Papapetropoulos (2023), Physiological Reviews, "Physiological roles of hydrogen sulfide in mammalian cells, tissues, and organs": physiological steady-state concentrations of free H2S are in the LOW NANOMOLAR range, against glutathione at millimolar. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35435014/

• Levitt, Abdel-Rehim and Furne (2011) extended the work to free and acid-labile H2S across mouse tissues and reported anomalously high free H2S specifically in aortic tissue, so the correction is not uniform across all tissues. https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/abs/10.1089/ars.2010.3525

• Residual disagreement on blood: human plasma free H2S or HS-minus has been reported in the mid nanomolar range, 300 to 500 nanomolar, and mouse plasma in the low micromolar range, around 1.0 micromolar - these are higher than Olson's "undetectable, less than 100 nanomolar". So blood values are still contested even now, spanning roughly 10 nanomolar to 1 micromolar depending on method and species.

• A general modern summary: re-evaluations using improved methods that avoid releasing contaminant H2S from proteins give estimates of 20 nanomolar to a few micromolar in tissue and blood.

WHAT WENT WRONG METHODOLOGICALLY - THE MECHANISM OF THE ERROR

• THE METHYLENE BLUE ASSAY. The dominant old method reacts sulphide with N,N-dimethyl-p-phenylenediamine and ferric chloride in STRONG ACID to form methylene blue, which is read colorimetrically. The strong-acid pretreatment is the fatal flaw. Documented problems: interference from other coloured substances; methylene blue dimer and trimer formation; the strong-acid chemical pretreatment itself; and low sensitivity.

• ACID-LABILE SULPHUR. Iron-sulphur clusters in proteins such as ferredoxins and respiratory complexes release sulphide as H2S when acidified. The methylene blue protocol therefore liberates and counts this structural sulphur as if it were free signalling H2S. Acid-labile sulphide is released from brain homogenates with the release greatest at about pH 1.5, at roughly 160 nanomoles per gram of protein.

• BOUND SULPHANE SULPHUR. A second, separate storage pool consists of divalent sulphur bound mostly to cysteine sulphur in proteins - persulphides and polysulphides. This releases H2S under REDUCING conditions such as dithiothreitol (DTT). Brain homogenates actively absorb H2S into this pool. Any assay involving a reductant liberates it and counts it.

• HOMOGENISATION ITSELF. Disrupting tissue mixes compartments, exposes bound pools to reductants and to acid, and destroys the in-situ equilibrium. Furne, Saeed and Levitt's key methodological innovation was RAPID homogenisation with immediate headspace analysis, minimising the time for artefactual release.

• Ishigami and colleagues (2009) developed a gas-chromatography method for free H2S in rat brain homogenate but with a detection limit of only about 9 micromolar - which is itself above the true free concentration, illustrating how insensitive the older tools were. They reported acid-labile and bound forms in the micromolar range.

• SENSOR AND PROBE PROBLEMS PERSIST. Sulphide-sensitive polarographic electrodes and fluorescent probes each have issues; the 2025 review notes current fluorescent probe methods "still face limitations in specificity, stability, and quantification accuracy" and "lack the specificity needed to distinguish H2S from other similar compounds". Sulphide dibimane and monobromobimane (MBB) derivatisation with reverse-phase HPLC and fluorescence detection now reach low nanomolar limits of detection and are the current best practice, but MBB is a cell-permeable alkylating agent that reacts with both extracellular and intracellular sulphide pools AND with sulphydryl-containing biomolecules, so it too can over-report if not carefully controlled.

RECOMMENDED PHRASING FOR THE BOOK

• The endogenous free H2S concentration in brain is best stated as approximately 15 nanomolar, with a defensible modern range of about 10 nanomolar to a few micromolar depending on tissue and method.

• The widely cited figure of 50 to 160 micromolar, originating in Goodwin et al. 1989 and Warenycia et al. 1989 and propagated by Abe and Kimura 1996, is an OVERESTIMATE by approximately three orders of magnitude. It arose because the methylene blue colorimetric assay used strong acid, which liberates sulphide from iron-sulphur clusters (acid-labile sulphur) and from protein-bound sulphane sulphur, and counts it as free H2S.

• State the consequence explicitly: because the older figure was accepted, the great majority of published H2S pharmacology used donor concentrations of 50 to 200 micromolar, which are now understood to be pharmacological or toxicological rather than physiological. Findings from those experiments should not be assumed to describe endogenous signalling.

• WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT - genetically encoded, ratiometric, reversible H2S sensors validated in CBS, CSE and 3-MST triple-knockout tissue, capable of distinguishing free H2S from persulphides and polysulphides, and calibrated in intact tissue rather than homogenate.


3.3 PERSULFIDATION (S-SULFHYDRATION) AS THE REAL MECHANISM

Because the free H2S concentration is low nanomolar while its supposed targets require micromolar, the field has largely shifted from "free H2S is the messenger" to "persulfidation is the messenger". This is the intellectually honest resolution of the concentration problem.

• DEFINITION. Persulfidation, formerly called S-sulfhydration, is a post-translational modification in which a protein cysteine thiol (P-SH) is converted to a persulphide (P-SSH), that is an extra sulphur atom is inserted.

• Chemically, free H2S cannot directly attack a reduced thiol - the reaction is thermodynamically unfavourable. Persulfidation instead proceeds via oxidised cysteine intermediates reacting with H2S, or via direct sulphur transfer from an enzyme such as 3-MST, or via polysulphides (H2Sn). This is why 3-MST being a protein persulfidase directly is significant.

• THE CLASSIC EXTENT FIGURE. Mustafa AK et al. (2009), Science Signaling 2(96):ra72, "H2S signals through protein S-sulfhydration", from Solomon Snyder's laboratory at Johns Hopkins: about 10 to 25 percent of many liver proteins, including actin, tubulin and glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate dehydrogenase (GAPDH), are sulfhydrated under physiological conditions. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19903941/ and https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/scisignal.2000464

• For comparison, S-nitrosylation of proteins is typically well under 1 percent of any given protein. So persulfidation is claimed to be one of the most abundant cysteine modifications in the cell, which is a large claim.

• MECHANISTIC EXAMPLE. Persulfidation of GAPDH at cysteine 150 INCREASES its catalytic activity, in direct contrast to nitrosylation or glutathionylation of the SAME cysteine, which inhibit it. Persulfidation also enhances actin polymerisation. So the same residue is a switch read in opposite directions by NO and by H2S - a genuinely elegant point of contact between the two gasotransmitters.

• IMPORTANT CAVEAT ON THE 10 TO 25 PERCENT FIGURE - REPORT THIS. The 10 to 25 percent estimate was obtained with a modified biotin switch assay. That assay has since been shown to have LOW SELECTIVITY and to give false positives in most cases, so the extent of protein persulfidation "might be exaggerated". Source: the Antioxidants and Redox Signaling review "Protein Persulfidation". https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/pdf/10.1089/ars.2022.0064 The 10 to 25 percent number should therefore be quoted as the classic estimate WITH the methodological caveat attached, exactly as the free-H2S concentration should be.

• BETTER METHODS. The dimedone-switch method blocks all free thiols with NBF-Cl (4-chloro-7-nitrobenzofurazan) and then specifically tag-switches the persulfidated proteins with DCP-Bio1, a biotinylated dimedone. Developed and applied by Petrovic, Kouroussis, Vignane and Filipovic. https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/fnagi.2021.674135/full Other approaches include direct proteomic mapping of cysteine persulfidation (Fu et al., Antioxidants and Redox Signaling 2020, https://www.liebertpub.com/doi/10.1089/ars.2019.7777), quantitative persulfide site identification (qPerS-SID), and thioredoxin- and glutathione-based persulphide reduction assays (Science Advances, https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/sciadv.1500968).

• BRAIN AND AGEING. Persulfidation levels decline with age, leaving proteins vulnerable to oxidative damage. In brain extracts from Wistar rats, reduced protein persulfidation was observed beginning at 6 months of age, with approximately 50 percent lower persulfidation in aged animals. Age-dependent declines in persulfidation in neural tissue have been linked to Alzheimer's disease, Parkinson's disease and Huntington's disease, alongside age-dependent INCREASES in sulfenylation and sulfonylation. Sources: Frontiers in Aging Neuroscience 2021 "The Role of Protein Persulfidation in Brain Aging and Neurodegeneration" (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34248604/) and Neurotherapeutics 2025 "Profiling the landscape of cysteine posttranslational modifications in brain aging and neurodegeneration".

• Persulfidation has evolutionarily conserved anti-ageing effects across species (Cell Metabolism 2019, "Selective Persulfide Detection Reveals Evolutionarily Conserved Antiaging Effects of S-Sulfhydration"). https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31735592/

• A quantitative sulfhydration proteomic study found metabolic reprogramming during the integrated stress response (eLife). https://elifesciences.org/articles/10067

• THE STORAGE POOL. Brain homogenates absorb H2S as bound sulphane sulphur, which releases H2S again under reducing conditions such as DTT. H2S produced by 3-MST may be readily stored as bound sulphane sulphur and rapidly released on stimulation; cells expressing 3-MST plus CAT have elevated bound sulphane sulphur. This gives a coherent picture: free H2S is vanishingly low and transient, the functional currency is protein-bound sulphur, and the tissue holds a large releasable reservoir.

• HONEST FRAMING FOR THE BOOK - the modern position is that free H2S is a low-nanomolar, short-lived intermediate, and that the biologically meaningful entity is the persulphide or polysulphide modification of protein cysteines, plus the bound sulphane sulphur reservoir. State that the frequently quoted "10 to 25 percent of proteins persulfidated" comes from a method now known to over-report, and that a reliable proteome-wide number using the dimedone switch or direct mapping is not yet agreed.

• WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT - a head-to-head, site-resolved quantification of persulfidation stoichiometry in intact brain tissue by two independent chemistries (dimedone switch and direct proteomic mapping), with CBS, CSE and 3-MST knockouts as negative controls, reporting occupancy per cysteine site rather than "percent of proteins".


PART 4 - CROSS-CUTTING SUMMARY OF THE HONESTY POINTS


• All three gasotransmitter literatures share the same failure mode: an early, technically difficult concentration measurement was too high, everyone designed experiments around the wrong concentration, and the correction arrived one to two decades later.

• NO: the early electrode figure of about 1 micromolar is now believed to be roughly 0.6 to 2.5 nanomolar in vivo, an overestimate of about 400 to 1600 fold. Consequence: the 1994 diffusion model overestimated the source strength by more than five orders of magnitude and the "200 micrometre sphere, 2 million synapses" claim collapsed to a spread of a few micrometres per source.

• H2S: the early methylene blue figure of 50 to 160 micromolar is now believed to be about 15 nanomolar, an overestimate of roughly 3000 to 10000 fold. Consequence: essentially all pre-2010 H2S pharmacology used supraphysiological donor concentrations, and the mechanism has been reframed from free H2S to persulfidation.

• CO: the concentration was never the problem - tissue CO really is micromolar - but the RECEPTOR is the problem, with a dissociation constant at sGC of about 240 micromolar and only 3- to 6-fold activation, versus NO's 1 to 10 nanomolar and 200-fold. CO therefore has the weakest case of the three for being a genuine neurotransmitter.

• Ranking of evidence quality as a neuronal signalling molecule, based on the sources above: NO strong; H2S moderate but with its mechanism substantially rewritten; CO weak, plausible in specific peripheral and olfactory contexts, unproven as a general brain messenger.

• https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7870284/ - Wood and Garthwaite 1994, diffusional spread models
• https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4704503/ - Garthwaite 2016, from synaptically localized to volume transmission
• https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2538835/ - Hall and Attwell 2008, physiological NO concentration and targets in brain
