<result>FINDINGS DUMP - copper and iron metal-transport disorders. All numbers below were pulled from a fetched source unless flagged UNVERIFIED.

SECTION 1 - WILSON DISEASE (ATP7B)

Primary verified source: Schilsky ML, Roberts EA, et al. A multidisciplinary approach to the diagnosis and management of Wilson disease: 2022 Practice Guidance on Wilson disease from the American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases. Hepatology 2023;77:1428-1455. Full text PDF fetched and read directly at https://escholarship.org/content/qt56b40955/qt56b40955.pdf

Serum ceruloplasmin
• AASLD 2022, verbatim: "A serum ceruloplasmin level typically below the laboratory determined lower level of normal (usually approximately 20 mg/dl) has been considered consistent with WD, and diagnostic if associated with KF rings."
• AASLD 2022 cites a molecularly confirmed study giving positive predictive values (PPV) and negative predictive values (NPV) at three cutoffs: ceruloplasmin below 20 mg/dL, PPV 48.3%, NPV 98.7%; below 14 mg/dL, PPV 100%, NPV 97.1%; below 10 mg/dL, PPV 100%, NPV 91.9%. A 14 mg/dL immunologic threshold "achieved 100% sensitivity and specificity in the validation group."
• The 0.1 vs 0.2 g/L point, resolved: these are two different jobs for the same analyte. AASLD 2022, verbatim: "In the Leipzig score, the informative cutoff is 10 mg/dl; some regard serum ceruloplasmin &lt;5 mg/dl as highly informative for the diagnosis of WD." So under 0.2 g/L (20 mg/dL) is the screening/consistent-with threshold; under 0.1 g/L (10 mg/dL) is the 2-point Leipzig scoring threshold. AASLD Guidance statement 7 verbatim: "An extremely low serum ceruloplasmin level (&lt;5 mg/dl) more strongly suggests a diagnosis of WD than modestly subnormal levels. Serum ceruloplasmin by itself is insufficient for making a diagnosis of WD. Serum ceruloplasmin within the normal range does not exclude the diagnosis of WD."
• Independent systematic review (Ryan A, et al., biochemical testing for WD, PMC8842170, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8842170/): at 0.2 g/L sensitivity 77.1-99%, specificity 55.9-82.8%; at 0.1 g/L sensitivity 65-78.9%, specificity 96.6-100%. Optimal cutoff range 0.14-0.2 g/L. That review's numbers are exactly the trade-off that explains the debate: 0.2 g/L is sensitive but poorly specific, 0.1 g/L is specific but misses roughly a quarter to a third of cases.
• Ceruloplasmin is an acute-phase reactant, raised by inflammation, pregnancy, oestrogen and some oral contraceptives; and physiologically peaks at roughly 30-50 mg/dL in early childhood. AASLD lists other causes of low ceruloplasmin: renal protein loss, protein-losing enteropathy, severe chronic liver disease, absolute copper deficiency, Menkes disease, aceruloplasminaemia, MEDNIK/AP1S1, AP1B1, PGM1-CDG, CCDC115-CDG, TMEM199-CDG, Niemann-Pick type C.

24-hour urinary copper
• AASLD 2022 verbatim: "The conventional cutoff taken as diagnostic of WD is &gt;100 ug/24 h (&gt;1.6 umol/24 h) in symptomatic patients; however, this level may miss 16%-23% with WD. Taking the cutoff as 40 ug (0.6 umol) per 24 h, which is the upper limit of normal in some laboratories, appears to be better. When examined systematically in children, 40 ug/24 h is the best cutoff."
• AASLD Guidance statement 8 verbatim: "Basal 24-h urinary excretion of copper in WD is typically &gt;100 ug/24 h (&gt;1.6 umol/24 h) in symptomatic patients, but a lower reference value of &gt;40 ug/24 h (&gt;0.6 umol/24 h) may indicate WD in asymptomatic individuals or children and therefore requires clinical correlation and further investigation."
• Overlap caveats, all from AASLD: cholestatic chronic liver disease including autoimmune hepatitis can run 100-200 ug/24 h (1.6-3.2 umol/24 h); MDR3 deficiency chronic cholestasis raises urinary copper; non-selective proteinuria can push basal urinary copper above 100 ug/24 h without raised non-ceruloplasmin-bound copper; simple ATP7B heterozygotes have intermediate levels of 50-100 ug/24 h.
• Spot urines are not suitable except in acute liver failure.

Penicillamine challenge test
• AASLD 2022 verbatim: "This test has been standardized only in a pediatric population, in which 500 mg of D-penicillamine was administered at the beginning and again 12 h later during the 24-h urine collection, irrespective of body weight. A clear differentiation from various other liver disorders was found when &gt;1600 ug copper/24 h (&gt;25 umol/24 h) was excreted."
• Honest limits, AASLD verbatim: "Recent re-evaluation showed that this test is not sufficiently sensitive in asymptomatic patients with WD. Others have found the predictive value of the 25 umol/24-h cutoff to be &lt;100%. This test has been used in adults, but those test protocols are non-uniform and not validated."

Hepatic parenchymal copper
• AASLD 2022 verbatim: "Normal hepatic copper content is &lt;50 ug/g dry weight. In most but not all cases of WD, hepatic copper content is greatly elevated: &gt;250 ug/g dry weight is generally the reference value. A threshold value of 75 ug/g dry weight was proposed to increase test sensitivity. Another large study proposed 209 ug/g dry weight as having a sensitivity of 99% and specificity of 96%."
• So the "lower 70-75 ug/g" threshold in the question is real and is specifically 75 ug/g dry weight, proposed to raise sensitivity, not to replace 250 ug/g. I did not find a source using exactly 70 ug/g - treat 70 as UNVERIFIED.
• Unit conversion: 250 ug/g dry weight equals 4 umol/g. The systematic review PMC8842170 reports at 4 umol/g sensitivity 99.4%, specificity 96.1%, and an ROC-optimal cutoff of 1.2 umol/g (roughly 76 ug/g) with sensitivity 96.4%, specificity 95.4% - which independently supports the 75 ug/g figure.
• Simple heterozygotes are "frequently elevated above normal" but "usually &lt;250 ug/g dry weight."
• Sampling: copper is heterogeneously deposited, so small or cirrhotic samples under-read. Minimum biopsy dry weight 3 mg, or a 1-2 cm core. Accuracy drops by 15% below 3 mg sample weight.

Relative exchangeable copper (REC)
• AASLD 2022 verbatim: the ratio of exchangeable copper (CuEXC, measured by EDTA chelation) to total serum copper, "with the cutoff set at 18.5%, distinguishes patients with WD from simple heterozygotes and normal individuals and also from adults and children with various chronic liver diseases." AASLD adds "Further validation of these methods by other clinical research groups is warranted."
• The 2025 EASL-ERN CPG on Wilson's disease (Journal of Hepatology 2025, https://www.journal-of-hepatology.eu/article/S0168-8278(24)02706-5/fulltext) newly recommends REC alongside the Leipzig score, and reclassifies a Leipzig total of 2 as "unlikely" rather than "possible". I could not fetch the EASL full text (HTTP 403 on both the fulltext and PDF endpoints, and the easl.eu PDF 404s), so the EASL-specific numeric cutoffs are UNVERIFIED here; the reclassification and REC recommendation come only from search-result summaries, not a fetched primary source.

Leipzig 2001 scoring system, full table
Source fetched: GeneReviews Wilson Disease, Table 1, adapted from Ferenci P, et al. Diagnosis and phenotypic classification of Wilson disease. Liver Int 2003;23:139-142 (the 8th International Meeting on Wilson Disease, Leipzig 2001). https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK1512/table/wilson.T.diagnostic_scoring_system_for_w/

Typical clinical symptoms and signs
• Kayser-Fleischer rings: present = 2; absent = 0
• Neurologic symptoms, or typical brain MRI: severe = 2; mild = 1; absent = 0
• Serum ceruloplasmin: normal, above 0.2 g/L = 0; 0.1 to 0.2 g/L = 1; below 0.1 g/L = 2
• Coombs-negative haemolytic anaemia: present = 1; absent = 0

Other tests
• Liver copper, in the absence of cholestasis: above 250 ug/g = 2; 50 to 249 ug/g = 1; below 50 ug/g = minus 1
• Rhodanine-positive granules, used only if quantitative liver copper is unavailable: present = 1
• Urinary copper, in the absence of acute hepatitis: normal = 0; 1 to 2 times upper limit of normal = 1; more than 2 times upper limit of normal = 2; normal basal value but more than 5 times upper limit of normal after D-penicillamine challenge = 2
• ATP7B mutation analysis: two pathogenic variants detected = 4; one variant detected = 1; none detected = 0

Interpretation of total score
• 4 or more: diagnosis established
• 3: diagnosis possible, more tests needed
• 2 or less: diagnosis very unlikely
Note the internal inconsistency worth flagging: the Leipzig ceruloplasmin ladder uses 0.1 and 0.2 g/L, i.e. 10 and 20 mg/dL, whereas the best single-cutoff discrimination in molecularly confirmed series is around 14 mg/dL. AASLD explicitly calls the Leipzig informative cutoff "10 mg/dl".

Clinical versus genetic prevalence, and the honest explanation
• Classical clinical figure: about 1 in 30,000, with a carrier frequency of about 1 in 90. Source: GeneReviews Wilson Disease, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK1512/. This traces to Scheinberg and Sternlieb 1984 and is an estimate built on sparse data, not a measured population figure.
• Coffey AJ, et al. A genetic study of Wilson's disease in the United Kingdom. Brain 2013;136:1476-1487. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23518715/ and https://academic.oup.com/brain/article-abstract/136/5/1476/284632 - sequencing-based, "the calculated frequency of individuals predicted to carry two mutant pathogenic ATP7B alleles is 1:7026", against a previously assumed 1:35,000-45,000. That is roughly a fivefold gap.
• Sandahl TD, et al. The Prevalence of Wilson's Disease: An Update. Hepatology 2020;71:722-732. 59 studies pooled (50 clinical, 9 population-based genetic). Pooled genetic prevalence 12.7 per 100,000, 95% CI 6.3 to 23.0, which is about 1 in 7,900. UNVERIFIED CAVEAT: I could not fetch the Lippincott full text (HTTP 402 paywall), so the pooled clinical prevalence figure and the study count are from search-result summaries of the abstract rather than a fetched primary source. Landing page: https://journals.lww.com/hep/fulltext/2020/02000/the_prevalence_of_wilson_s_disease__an_update.24.aspx
• Gao J, Brackley S, Mann JP. The global prevalence of Wilson disease from next-generation sequencing data. Genet Med 2019;21:1155-1163. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30254379/ - annotated reported pathogenic ATP7B variants against gnomAD allele frequencies and applied Hardy-Weinberg. This is the gnomAD-based estimate family, and it produces genetic prevalences well above 1 in 30,000.
• Wallace DF, Dooley JS. ATP7B variant penetrance explains differences between genetic and clinical prevalence estimates for Wilson disease. Hum Genet 2020;139:1065-1075. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00439-020-02161-3 - pathogenic allele frequency 2.055%, carrier rate about 1 in 25, and after excluding predicted-low-penetrance variants a prevalence of about 1 in 20,000.
• Estimating the clinical prevalence of Wilson's disease in the UK. JHEP Reports 2021. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34381985/ - I could not fetch this (HTTP 403), so its specific clinical prevalence number is UNVERIFIED here.

The discrepancy, stated honestly. Genetic prevalence is not disease prevalence. The gap between roughly 1 in 7,000 predicted biallelic carriers and roughly 1 in 30,000 to 1 in 100,000 diagnosed patients has at least five contributors, and the field does not agree on how much each contributes:
• Variant misclassification. Databases such as ClinVar and HGMD accumulated variants labelled pathogenic on thin evidence. When gnomAD shows a variant is common in the general population, it cannot be fully penetrant. Wallace and Dooley's whole method is stripping these out, and doing so moves the estimate from roughly 1 in 7,000 towards 1 in 20,000. So part of the gap is an artefact of over-calling pathogenicity.
• Genuinely reduced penetrance. Some biallelic genotypes, particularly those involving mild or hypomorphic alleles, may never produce clinical disease, or produce it so late and so mildly that it is never attributed to Wilson disease.
• Under-diagnosis. Wilson disease is protean - hepatic, neurologic, psychiatric - and AASLD notes the average delay between onset of psychiatric symptoms and diagnosis is 2.4 years, that up to 20% of patients had seen a psychiatrist first, and that roughly 50% of patients presenting with liver disease lack the textbook combination of low ceruloplasmin plus Kayser-Fleischer rings. Cases have been confirmed genetically in patients in their 70s and 80s.
• Premature death before diagnosis, including acute liver failure attributed to other causes. Wilson disease accounts for about 3% of all acute liver failure per AASLD.
• Ascertainment method differences. Clinical prevalence figures are counts of diagnosed living patients in registries or hospital records, which is a different quantity from birth prevalence and is depressed by mortality and by loss to follow-up.
The intellectually honest summary is that the truth almost certainly lies between the two, that the sequencing-derived figures are upper bounds inflated by variant misclassification, and that the classical 1 in 30,000 is a lower bound depressed by under-diagnosis. Nobody has yet done the decisive study, which would be unbiased population sequencing plus systematic phenotyping of everyone found to be biallelic.

SECTION 2 - MENKES DISEASE (ATP7A)

Sources fetched: GeneReviews, ATP7A-Related Copper Transport Disorders, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK1413/ ; StatPearls Menkes Disease, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK560917/ ; Kaler SG et al. on plasma catechols, via PMC3156244 https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3156244/

Incidence
• Tønnesen T, Kleijer WJ, Horn N. Incidence of Menkes disease. Hum Genet 1991;86:408-410. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/BF00201846 - combined frequency of live-born Menkes patients in Denmark, France, the Netherlands, the United Kingdom and West Germany was 1 per 298,000 live births for the period 1976 to 1987; estimated mutation rate 1.96 x 10 to the minus 6.
• StatPearls: overall "close to 1 in 35,000 live male births"; United States range 1 in 50,000 to 1 in 250,000; Australia 1 in 50,000 to 1 in 100,000, attributed to a possible founder effect; Japan 1 in 2.8 million live births (study period 1993-2003), with male live-birth incidence reported as 4.9 per million. Denmark's incidence is described as almost as low as the Netherlands'.
• GeneReviews adds a modern genomic re-estimate: birth prevalence "potentially as high as 1:8,664 live male births" from ATP7A variant frequencies, far above clinically ascertained figures. This is the same genetic-versus-clinical gap seen in Wilson disease and deserves the same scepticism.
• The commonly quoted "1 in 100,000 to 1 in 300,000 live births" range is a fair summary of the clinically ascertained literature, bracketed by the Australian figure at the high end and the Tønnesen European figure at the low end. The Japanese figure of 1 in 2.8 million sits far outside that range and is very likely ascertainment-limited rather than a true biological difference.

Biochemistry
• GeneReviews, classic Menkes: serum copper below 40 ug/dL against a normal of 75-150 ug/dL; serum ceruloplasmin 10-100 mg/L against a normal of 200-450 mg/L (that is, 1-10 mg/dL against a normal 20-45 mg/dL).
• StatPearls gives slightly wider affected ranges: serum copper 0-55 ug/dL, normal 70-150; ceruloplasmin 10-160 mg/L, normal 200-450.
• Critical caveat, stated in both sources: these markers are unreliable in infants under about two months of age (StatPearls says under six months), because healthy newborns physiologically have low copper and ceruloplasmin. This is exactly why the catecholamine test exists.

Plasma catecholamine ratio test
• The mechanism: dopamine-beta-hydroxylase (DBH), the enzyme converting dopamine to noradrenaline, is a copper-dependent enzyme. In Menkes disease it is starved of copper, so catechols upstream of DBH accumulate and those downstream fall. DOPAC (dihydroxyphenylacetic acid) is a dopamine metabolite, upstream; DHPG (dihydroxyphenylglycol) is a noradrenaline metabolite, downstream. The ratio therefore rises.
• Diagnostic thresholds, fetched verbatim: DOPA:DHPG above 6; DOPAC:DHPG above 4; DA:NE above 0.10. Measured by high-pressure liquid chromatography with electrochemical detection.
• Performance: "the DOPA:DHPG and DOPAC:DHPG ratios are always abnormally high" in affected individuals, whereas the DA:NE ratio occasionally shows exceptions. Kaler SG, et al. Relative efficiencies of plasma catechol levels and ratios for neonatal diagnosis of Menkes disease. Neurochem Res 2009 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19234788/) reports that in at-risk newborns the DOPAC:DHPG ratio was higher in all affected than in all unaffected subjects - that is, perfect separation in that series. No formal ROC statistics or numeric sensitivity/specificity percentages were given in the sources I fetched; treat any specific percentage as UNVERIFIED.
• Original description: Kaler SG, et al. Plasma and cerebrospinal fluid neurochemical pattern in Menkes disease. Ann Neurol 1993;33:171-175.

Clinical features
• GeneReviews: infants "appear healthy until age two to three months, when loss of early developmental milestones, hypotonia, seizures, and failure to thrive occur."
• Hair: "short, sparse, coarse, twisted, and often lightly pigmented." Light microscopy of the hair shaft shows pili torti, literally twisted hair, with fracturing (trichorrhexis nodosa and trichoclasis). This is the "kinky hair" or "steely hair" of the older eponyms.
• Connective tissue and skeletal: wormian bones in the skull, osteopenia with fracture risk, bladder diverticula, tortuous vessels, lax skin and joints - all consequences of copper-dependent lysyl oxidase failing to cross-link collagen and elastin.
• Prognosis: GeneReviews, "premature death... often by age three years." StatPearls, most do not survive beyond age three, with pneumonia and vascular complications the main causes of death.

Treatment
• Copper histidinate (copper-histidine) by subcutaneous injection. GeneReviews: initiation "by age 28 days enhances survival and improves neurodevelopmental outcomes" in classic Menkes disease; dosing 250 ug subcutaneously twice daily for infants under one year. StatPearls concurs that treatment ideally starts within four weeks of life and that outcome depends on residual ATP7A function - patients retaining some transporter activity do far better than those with null alleles. Copper histidinate does not cross the blood-brain barrier efficiently, which is the central limitation and the rationale for experimental gene therapy approaches.

SECTION 3 - NBIA AND PKAN/PANK2

Source fetched: GeneReviews, Pantothenate Kinase-Associated Neurodegeneration, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK1490/

Prevalence
• GeneReviews verbatim: "An estimate of one to three in 1,000,000 has been suggested," implying a carrier frequency of roughly 1 in 275 to 1 in 500.
• IMPORTANT CORRECTION AND CONFLICT. A separate 2025 paper (PMC12469515, "Focus on Clinical and Genetic Aspects of PKAN Through the Description of New Patients") states "PKAN has an estimated prevalence of 1-3/100,000". That is a hundredfold discrepancy against GeneReviews and is almost certainly a transcription error in the secondary literature, since the implied carrier frequency of 1 in 275 to 1 in 500 is arithmetically consistent with 1-3 per million and not with 1-3 per 100,000. Use 1-3 per 1,000,000. Flagging this because the erroneous figure is in circulation.

Eye-of-the-tiger sign
• Definition, GeneReviews verbatim: "a central region of hyperintensity surrounded by a rim of hypointensity on coronal or transverse T2-weighted images of the globus pallidus." The hypointense rim is iron; the hyperintense centre is tissue oedema, gliosis and neuronal loss.
• Sensitivity: GeneReviews describes high sensitivity, identifying pathogenic variants in nearly all individuals with classic PKAN. UChicago Genetic Testing (https://dnatesting.uchicago.edu/sites/default/files/01PANK2_14.pdf) states PANK2 sequence analysis detects mutations in over 98% of individuals with NBIA plus the eye-of-the-tiger sign, but in only about 50% of individuals with a clinical NBIA diagnosis overall. A study of 123 PKAN patients found every PANK2-mutation patient had the sign, a reported one-to-one correlation.
• Honest caveats: GeneReviews states the sign "may be absent early in disease" and occasionally in certain populations including some Dominican Republic patients. Specificity is not absolute - the appearance has been reported in corticobasal degeneration, early-onset levodopa-responsive parkinsonism, and progressive supranuclear palsy (per PMC6695239, Eye-of-the-tiger Sign in NBIA, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6695239/). No formal numeric specificity percentage was found in a fetched source - treat any exact specificity figure as UNVERIFIED.

Ages of onset and course
• Classic PKAN: mean age at onset 3.4 years, typically before age 6. Rapid progression, with wheelchair dependence within roughly 10-15 years.
• Atypical PKAN: mean age at onset 13.6 years, range spanning the first to third decade. Slower progression, with loss of ambulation within roughly 15-40 years.

Proportion of NBIA due to PANK2
• GeneReviews does not state a clean percentage; it says PANK2 is the most commonly identified cause among NBIA cases with the eye-of-the-tiger sign. The widely repeated figure is that PKAN accounts for roughly half of NBIA, which is consistent with the UChicago statement that PANK2 sequencing is positive in about 50% of clinically diagnosed NBIA. Treat "about 50%" as the defensible figure and anything more precise as UNVERIFIED.

Other NBIA genes, from GeneReviews' own list plus standard disorder names
• PANK2 - PKAN, NBIA1
• PLA2G6 - PLAN, phospholipase A2-associated neurodegeneration, including infantile neuroaxonal dystrophy (INAD) and NBIA2
• C19orf12 - MPAN, mitochondrial membrane protein-associated neurodegeneration
• WDR45 - BPAN, beta-propeller protein-associated neurodegeneration, X-linked, distinctive for a static encephalopathy in childhood followed by dystonia-parkinsonism in adulthood
• FA2H - FAHN, fatty acid hydroxylase-associated neurodegeneration
• CP (ceruloplasmin) - aceruloplasminaemia; note this is the direct bridge between the copper and iron sides of this brief, since ceruloplasmin is a ferroxidase and its absence causes iron, not copper, accumulation, with the triad of retinal degeneration, diabetes mellitus and neurological disease
• FTL (ferritin light chain) - neuroferritinopathy, autosomal dominant, unlike the recessive majority
• COASY - CoPAN, CoA synthase protein-associated neurodegeneration
• ATP13A2 - Kufor-Rakeb syndrome, PARK9
• DCAF17 - Woodhouse-Sakati syndrome
The pathway logic is worth noting: PANK2 and COASY are both coenzyme A biosynthesis enzymes, PLA2G6 and FA2H are both lipid-metabolism enzymes, CP and FTL are both directly iron-handling. Iron accumulation in the globus pallidus is a shared downstream consequence of several unrelated upstream lesions, not a single mechanism.

SECTION 4 - HEREDITARY HAEMOCHROMATOSIS, HFE C282Y

Allele and genotype frequency
• Hanson EH, Imperatore G, Burke W. HFE gene and hereditary hemochromatosis: a HuGE review. Am J Epidemiol 2001;154:193-206. https://academic.oup.com/aje/article/154/3/193/125766 - C282Y allele frequency about 6% in northern European populations; C282Y/C282Y genotype frequency 0.4% in the general population, that is about 1 in 250. Highest European frequencies in Celtic populations of Ireland, the United Kingdom and France, with elevated frequencies also in Scandinavia. The 1 in 200 to 1 in 300 range in the question is therefore correct, with 1 in 250 the central estimate.
• UK Biobank empirical count, Pilling et al. 2019: 2,890 C282Y homozygotes out of the European-ancestry cohort, stated as 0.6%, or 1 in 156. This is higher than Hanson's 1 in 250 and reflects UK Biobank's ancestry composition and healthy-volunteer selection, not a contradiction.
• Origin: the mutation is thought to have arisen in a population in southern Scandinavia and spread with the Vikings (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12791037/), with a competing Celtic-origin hypothesis.

Biochemical versus clinical penetrance - the central honest point
• Beutler E, Felitti VJ, Koziol JA, Ho NJ, Gelbart T. Penetrance of 845G to A (C282Y) HFE hereditary haemochromatosis mutation in the USA. Lancet 2002;359:211-218. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11812557/ and https://www.thelancet.com/journals/lancet/article/PIIS0140673602074470/fulltext - about 41,000 subjects at a health appraisal clinic in southern California, 152 C282Y homozygotes identified, compared against age-matched controls by questionnaire and physician interview. Beutler's best estimate was that fewer than 1% of homozygotes develop frank clinical haemochromatosis. This was the paper that detonated the case for population screening.
• Allen KJ, Gurrin LC, Constantine CC, et al. Iron-overload-related disease in HFE hereditary hemochromatosis (the HealthIron study). N Engl J Med 2008;358:221-230. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18205680/ - 1,438 adults of northern European ancestry followed 12 years, including 203 C282Y homozygotes. Among those with documented iron overload, clinical disease occurred in 21 of 74 men (28.4%) and 1 of 84 women (1.2%). Endpoints counted included hepatocellular carcinoma, liver fibrosis or cirrhosis, raised transaminases, abnormal metacarpophalangeal joints, and other haemochromatosis-related symptoms. VERIFICATION NOTE: these figures come from search-result summaries of the abstract; I did not fetch the NEJM full text, so treat the 28.4% and 1.2% as high-confidence but not primary-source-fetched.
• Pilling LC, Tamosauskaite J, Jones G, et al. Common conditions associated with hereditary haemochromatosis genetic variants: cohort study in UK Biobank. BMJ 2019;364:k5222. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30651232/ - of 2,890 C282Y homozygotes (0.6%, or 1 in 156), haemochromatosis was diagnosed in 21.7% (95% CI 19.5% to 24.1%, 281 of 1,294) of men and 9.8% (95% CI 8.4% to 11.2%, 156 of 1,596) of women by end of follow-up. C282Y homozygous men aged 40 to 70 had higher prevalence of liver disease (odds ratio 4.30, 95% CI 2.97 to 6.18, p&lt;0.001), rheumatoid arthritis, osteoarthritis and diabetes mellitus versus those with no C282Y. VERIFICATION NOTE: BMJ full text returned HTTP 403; these figures are from the abstract as returned in search results.
• Atkins JL, Pilling LC, Masoli JAH, et al. Association of hemochromatosis HFE p.C282Y homozygosity with hepatic malignancy. JAMA 2020;324:2048-2057. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33231665/ - among 1,294 male C282Y homozygotes in UK Biobank there were 21 incident hepatic malignancies; hazard ratio 10.5 (95% CI 6.6 to 16.7, p&lt;0.001) versus men with neither HFE variant. Population attributable fraction for liver disease among C282Y homozygotes 14.4% (3.1% to 24.3%).
• How to reconcile Beutler's under 1% with Pilling's 21.7%. They are measuring different things over different windows. Beutler measured frank classical clinical haemochromatosis in a cross-section of a working-age Californian health-plan population. Pilling measured any recorded diagnosis of haemochromatosis in a UK cohort aged 40-70 at baseline and followed for years, in a health system where the diagnosis is actively sought. Allen measured defined iron-overload-related disease endpoints in the subset who already had documented iron overload - note that denominator, it is not all homozygotes. The correct framing is a penetrance cascade: essentially all C282Y homozygotes show some biochemical abnormality (raised transferrin saturation), a smaller majority develop raised ferritin, a minority develop documented iron overload, and a smaller minority again develop organ damage. Male sex is the single strongest modifier - the male-to-female ratio is roughly 2:1 for diagnosis in Pilling and roughly 24:1 for clinical disease in the HealthIron iron-overloaded subset - almost certainly because menstruation and pregnancy remove iron.

Brain iron and neurological disease in HFE homozygotes - what is and is not established
• Atkins JL, Pilling LC, Heales CJ, et al. Hemochromatosis mutations, brain iron imaging, and dementia in the UK Biobank cohort. J Alzheimers Dis 2021;79:1203-1211. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7990419/ - fetched. 2,890 C282Y homozygotes of European ancestry, of whom 206 had brain MRI. Male homozygotes had lower T2* (that is, more iron) at putamen beta = minus 0.91 standard deviations, p = 1.0 x 10 to the minus 16; thalamus beta = minus 0.90 SD, p = 5.7 x 10 to the minus 16; hippocampus beta = minus 0.32 SD, p = 9.2 x 10 to the minus 4. Incident dementia hazard ratio in homozygous men 1.83 (95% CI 1.23 to 2.72, p = 0.003), robust to APOE adjustment.
• Explicit negative findings in that same paper: no association in female homozygotes; none in heterozygotes of either sex; and in male homozygotes no association with mild cognitive impairment or with stroke/TIA. The authors state dementia subtype data in hospital records were limited, so the mechanism is not established.
• Movement disorders: a separate UK Biobank analysis (2,889 C282Y homozygotes versus 485,399 controls, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35913729/) found increased prevalence of movement disorders in male homozygotes, odds ratio 1.80 (95% CI 1.28 to 2.55, p = 0.001), with no such association in women; neuroimaging showed decreased T2-weighted and T2* signal in basal ganglia, thalamus, red nucleus and cerebellum with Cohen d greater than 1.

Being explicitly honest about what is NOT established here, as requested:
• That C282Y homozygotes accumulate measurable iron in subcortical brain structures on MRI is well established, with very large effect sizes (Cohen d above 1, p values around 10 to the minus 16) in men.
• That this brain iron causes neurodegeneration is NOT established. The imaging association and the clinical association are separate findings in the same cohort; no study has demonstrated that the iron signal mediates the dementia or movement-disorder risk.
• The absolute risks are small and the confidence intervals are wide. The dementia hazard ratio of 1.83 sits on a modest number of events in a cohort with a well-documented healthy-volunteer selection bias, which biases towards the null for exposure-outcome associations but also limits generalisability.
• Nothing is established in women, in heterozygotes, or in compound heterozygotes. The null results in women and heterozygotes are real null results, not absence of testing.
• Diagnosis codes in hospital episode records are an imperfect outcome measure. "Movement disorders" as a composite hospital-code endpoint is not a clean neurological phenotype, and the movement-disorder association should be treated as hypothesis-generating.
• Whether therapeutic venesection, which reliably clears hepatic iron, affects brain iron or neurological outcomes is unknown. Brain iron is behind the blood-brain barrier and there is no evidence that standard phlebotomy mobilises it.
• There is no established indication for brain imaging or neurological screening in HFE homozygotes on the basis of these findings, and no guideline recommends it.

• EASL-ERN 2025 Wilson's disease CPG numeric cutoffs and its exact modification of the Leipzig interpretation. Both the Journal of Hepatology fulltext and PDF endpoints returned HTTP 403 and the easl.eu PDF returned 404. The Leipzig table above is the Ferenci 2003 original as reproduced in GeneReviews, and the AASLD 2022 cutoffs are fully verified from the fetched PDF.
• Sandahl 2020 pooled clinical prevalence figure (paywall, HTTP 402); only the pooled genetic figure of 12.7 per 100,000, 95% CI 6.3-23.0, came through, and that via abstract summary.
• JHEP Reports 2021 UK clinical prevalence number (HTTP 403).
• Allen 2008 NEJM and Pilling 2019 BMJ full texts (both inaccessible); figures given are from abstracts.
• A numeric specificity percentage for the eye-of-the-tiger sign. None found in any fetched source; the qualitative caveat that it appears in CBD, PSP and early-onset levodopa-responsive parkinsonism is sourced.
• A hepatic copper threshold of exactly 70 ug/g. The verified lower-sensitivity threshold is 75 ug/g dry weight per AASLD 2022.
• Formal sensitivity/specificity percentages for the DOPAC:DHPG ratio. Kaler 2009 reports perfect separation in at-risk newborns but no ROC statistics were available in the fetched text.

<result>IRON AND COPPER - WEB-VERIFIED FINDINGS DUMP (access date 12 August 2026)

Note: one of my three research sub-agents (Wilson/Menkes/NBIA/HFE) did not return; I re-ran those searches myself, so that section is thinner on Leipzig point-by-point detail than the rest. Gaps are flagged explicitly.

1. TOTAL BODY IRON AND COPPER

• Total body iron in adults: 3 to 4 g, most of it in haemoglobin - NIH Office of Dietary Supplements (ODS), Iron Fact Sheet for Health Professionals. https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Iron-HealthProfessional/
• Wider range with sex split: 3 to 5 g total; approximately 55 mg/kg in men and approximately 45 mg/kg in women - Tandara L, Salamunic I, Biochemia Medica 2012;22(3):311-328. https://www.biochemia-medica.com/en/journal/22/3/10.11613/BM.2012.034/fullArticle
◦ Caution: the older textbook convention of 50 mg/kg (men) / 35-40 mg/kg (women) was NOT what the fetched source gave. Quote 45-55 mg/kg.
• Distribution (Waldvogel-Abramowski S et al., Transfus Med Hemother 2014;41:213-221, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4086762/ ; worked male budget approximately 4,000 mg):
◦ Erythrocyte haemoglobin approximately 2,500 mg (62.5%); Tandara gives 60-70%. Your "~2.5 g / ~65%" is confirmed.
◦ Storage ferritin plus haemosiderin approximately 1,000 mg (25%); Tandara 20-30%. Liver, spleen, bone marrow macrophages.
◦ Myoglobin plus cytochromes: the remaining approximately 12.5%. The commonly quoted "300 mg myoglobin" could NOT be confirmed from a fetched primary source - treat as unverified.
◦ Transferrin-bound plasma pool: approximately 3 mg (both Waldvogel-Abramowski and Tandara). Your "3-4 mg" is at the high end; 3 mg is the quotable figure. This pool turns over roughly 10 times per day.
• Daily absorption 1-2 mg/day (approximately 10% of a 15 mg European diet); daily losses 1-2 mg/day via skin and gut desquamation plus minor blood loss; approximately 1 mg/day in men, roughly double in menstruating women.
• Internal recycling dwarfs absorption: 18-20 mg/day recycled from senescent red cells for approximately 200 billion new erythrocytes daily; macrophages recycle 10-20 times more iron than the gut absorbs.
• Iron RDA (ODS): men 19+ 8 mg/day; women 19-50 18 mg/day; women 51+ 8 mg/day.

• Total body copper: 50 to 120 mg in the average adult - NIH ODS Copper Fact Sheet. https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Copper-HealthProfessional/ Point estimate approximately 100 mg - Burkhead JL, Collins JF, Adv Nutr 2021;13(2):681-683. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8970836/
◦ No source gave an explicit mg/kg. Arithmetic only: approximately 1.4 mg/kg for 100 mg in 70 kg (range 0.7-1.7 mg/kg). Do not cite as a sourced value.
• Distribution: ODS says almost two-thirds is in skeleton and muscle; Burkhead and Collins say approximately 75% in skeleton and muscle, remainder mostly liver, brain, blood, heart, kidney. By concentration the ranking is kidney &gt; liver &gt; brain (Neural Regen Res review, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11438321/ ). Per-organ percentage breakdowns and micrograms-per-gram tissue figures appeared only in secondary aggregators - unverified.
• Copper intake: approximately 1,400 micrograms/day (men), approximately 1,100 (women); RDA 900 micrograms/day adults; Tolerable Upper Intake Level 10,000 micrograms/day.
• Copper absorption - SOURCES DISAGREE, state both: ODS gives 75% absorbed at 400 micrograms/day intake falling to 12% at 7.5 mg/day; Burkhead and Collins give approximately 50% across 0.7-6 mg/day; a Neural Regen Res review cites approximately 10%. All agree absorption is inversely adaptive to intake. Excretion is biliary via ATP7B; total faecal loss approximately 1 mg/day.
• Plasma copper on ceruloplasmin - CORRECTION TO THE BRIEF: NIH ODS states ceruloplasmin carries MORE THAN 95% of total copper in healthy human plasma. The older 60-90% range persists in reviews. Safest phrasing: "the large majority, more than 95% per NIH ODS, with older sources giving 65-90%."
• Ceruloplasmin is a ferroxidase (oxidises Fe2+ to Fe3+ so transferrin can bind it) that doubles as the plasma copper carrier - the direct iron/copper interaction point.

2. BRAIN IRON BY REGION - CLASSIC AND MODERN

Hallgren B, Sourander P. The effect of age on the non-haemin iron in the human brain. J Neurochem 1958;3(1):41-51. PMID 13611557. https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/j.1471-4159.1958.tb12607.x (paywalled, HTTP 402 on fetch; values below are from sources that reproduce them)
• 98 brains at autopsy, chemical assay of non-haem iron, expressed as mg per 100 g fresh (wet) weight. Multiply by 10 for micrograms per gram.
• Deep grey matter range 4.76 to 21.30 mg/100 g; cortical range 2.92 to 5.03 mg/100 g - Quantitative Susceptibility Mapping of Brain Iron in Healthy Aging and Cognition, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10797559/
• Rank order confirmed by Bilgic B, Pfefferbaum A, Rohlfing T, Sullivan EV, Adalsteinsson E. NeuroImage 2012;59:2625-2635, Figure 4, whose x-axis is the Hallgren-Sourander mean plus/minus SD in mg/100 g fresh weight per region. https://ccs.mgh.harvard.edu/~berkin/Bilgic_2011_QSM_NIMG.pdf Reading off that figure: globus pallidus approximately 21, red nucleus approximately 19-20, substantia nigra approximately 18-19, putamen approximately 13, dentate nucleus approximately 10, caudate approximately 9-10, thalamus approximately 5, frontal white matter approximately 4-5 mg/100 g.
◦ HONEST FLAG: I could not fetch the original 1958 table to confirm the exact decimals (the widely circulated 21.30 GP, 19.48 RN, 18.46 SN, 13.32 putamen, 9.28 caudate, 4.76 thalamus, 4.24 frontal WM, 5.03 / 2.92 cortex). The GP 21.30 and the 4.76-21.30 / 2.92-5.03 ranges ARE confirmed. The individual mid-table decimals should be checked against the paper before printing.
• Age dependence: iron rises steeply through the first two decades and largely plateaus by about age 30 in globus pallidus, substantia nigra and red nucleus, whereas putamen and caudate continue rising past 50-60 years (PMC10797559; PMC9188502, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9188502/ ). Hallgren and Sourander fitted an exponential saturating curve of the form Fe = A(1 - exp(-k x age)) + B with region-specific A and B constants. I could NOT verify the published A, B and k values from any fetchable source - this is the single biggest unresolved gap. It would be settled by the original 1958 Table/equation or by Bartzokis et al. 1993/2007 which reproduce it.
• Hallgren and Sourander also reported that about one third of brain non-haem iron was bound to a ferritin-like protein (PMC9188502).

Modern post-mortem chemistry (inductively coupled plasma mass spectrometry, ICP-MS):
• Langkammer C et al. Quantitative MR imaging of brain iron: a postmortem validation study. Radiology 2010;257(2):455-462. PMID 20843991. https://pubs.rsna.org/doi/abs/10.1148/radiol.10100495 Seven post-mortem brains, 3.0 T. Globus pallidus 205 plus/minus 32 mg/kg wet mass; putamen 153 plus/minus 29; caudate nucleus 92 plus/minus 15. These are micrograms per gram wet weight and agree closely with Hallgren-Sourander (213, 133, 93). R2* correlated with iron r-squared = 0.90; R2 r-squared = 0.67 (both p &lt; 0.001) across all tissue.
• Langkammer C et al. QSM as a means to measure brain iron? A post mortem validation study. NeuroImage 2012;62:1593-1599. PMID 22634862. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22634862/ 13 subjects, MRI within 72 h post mortem, ICP-MS. Susceptibility versus iron: r = 0.87 (all regions), r = 0.84 (grey matter only), but only r = 0.27 in white matter (all p &lt; 0.001).

Modern in vivo QSM:
• Persson N et al. Age and sex related differences in subcortical brain iron concentrations among healthy adults. NeuroImage 2015. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4737643/ n = 183, 1.5 T QSM, parts per billion (ppb): globus pallidus 191.1 plus/minus 42.2; red nucleus 163.2 plus/minus 60.3; substantia nigra 132.4 plus/minus 45.0; caudate 89.2 plus/minus 23.9; putamen 88.0 plus/minus 35.6; dentate 73.8 plus/minus 36.9; pulvinar 68.4 plus/minus 28.7; thalamus 11.8 plus/minus 11.1. Correlation with Hallgren-Sourander r = 0.970, p &lt; 0.001. Sex: women had lower substantia nigra susceptibility after adjusting for age; red nucleus trend p = 0.059; no other regional sex differences. Post-menopausal women (51+) had 169 ppb lower total subcortical iron than younger participants.
• Bilgic 2012 QSM values (parts per million, L1-regularised, young n = 11 versus elderly n = 12): globus pallidus 0.1224 versus 0.1472 ppm (p = 0.0095); putamen 0.0779 versus 0.1233 (p = 0.0004); red nucleus 0.0933 versus 0.1473 (p = 0.0019); substantia nigra 0.0820 versus 0.1113 (p = 0.0071, not Bonferroni-significant); caudate 0.0937 versus 0.1033 (ns); thalamus 0.0464 versus 0.0220 (elderly LOWER, p = 0.0224); frontal white matter 0.0367 versus 0.0298 (ns). QSM versus Hallgren-Sourander Spearman rho = 0.881, p = 0.0198; field-dependent relaxation rate increase (FDRI) versus post mortem rho = 0.952, p = 0.0117; QSM versus FDRI rho = 0.976, p = 0.0098.
• Note the units problem for the book: QSM is reported in ppb or ppm of magnetic susceptibility, NOT micrograms per gram. Conversion requires a study-specific regression against chemistry. There is no universal conversion constant.

WHERE MODERN MRI AND POST-MORTEM CHEMISTRY DISAGREE (state explicitly):
• Rank ordering agrees almost perfectly (rho 0.88 to 0.97). Absolute quantification does not transfer.
• White matter: QSM-iron correlation collapses to r = 0.27 because myelin is diamagnetic (negative susceptibility) and cancels ferritin paramagnetism. R2* in white matter reflects iron and myelin jointly. Cortical iron by QSM is therefore the least trustworthy compartment - myelin intensity correlates negatively with QSM (r = -0.352).
• Calcium: QSM can in principle separate diamagnetic calcium from paramagnetic iron; R2* cannot. Calcification in globus pallidus is a real confounder for R2*.
• Iron species: Perls Prussian blue stains only non-haem Fe3+; physical methods (ICP-MS, ferrozine) measure all forms including haem and Fe2+. Systematic mismatch follows.
• Neuromelanin: iron bound in neuromelanin in substantia nigra is largely invisible to Perls staining, so histology understates substantia nigra iron relative to MRI and mass spectrometry. This is the region where the three methods diverge most.
• Staining is non-linear: Hametner et al. found ferrozine-versus-Perls correlation is quadratic - the more iron present, the less sensitive the stain (r fell from 0.74-0.76 for ferrozine to 0.45-0.62 for staining).
• Fixation and autolysis: formalin shortens T1 and lengthens T2; one study found susceptibility stable over 6 weeks. Autolysis redistributes ferritin iron between neurons and glia at different rates.
• Resolution mismatch: histological slices 5 micrometres to 8 mm versus MRI voxels of hundreds of micrometres - not sampling the same tissue.
• Source: Frontiers in Neuroanatomy 2019;13:68, Matching ex vivo MRI With Iron Histology: Pearls and Pitfalls. https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/fnana.2019.00068/full
• What would settle it: paired same-specimen QSM plus R2* plus ICP-MS plus X-ray fluorescence with region-specific myelin and calcium correction, across the full age range. This does not yet exist at scale.

3. TYROSINE HYDROXYLASE (TH)

Primary source: Daubner SC, Le T, Wang S. Arch Biochem Biophys 2011;508(1):1-12. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3065393/ Nomenclature: ExPASy ENZYME EC 1.14.16.2, https://enzyme.expasy.org/EC/1.14.16.2
• EC 1.14.16.2, tyrosine 3-monooxygenase. Reaction: L-tyrosine + (6R)-L-erythro-5,6,7,8-tetrahydrobiopterin (BH4) + O2 yields L-DOPA + 4a-hydroxytetrahydrobiopterin.
• First and rate-limiting step of catecholamine biosynthesis (tyrosine to L-DOPA to dopamine to noradrenaline to adrenaline). Everything downstream is gated here.
• Family: aromatic amino acid hydroxylases (AAAH) - TH, phenylalanine hydroxylase (PheH), tryptophan hydroxylase (TrpH). All three are BH4-dependent non-haem mononuclear Fe(II) enzymes with the same mechanism and tetrameric architecture. One BH4 supply therefore constrains dopamine, noradrenaline, adrenaline and serotonin simultaneously.
• Iron: a single non-haem mononuclear Fe(II) per subunit, held by the 2-His-1-carboxylate facial triad - His331, His336, Glu376 in TH (equivalent to His285, His290, Glu330 in PheH). "Facial triad" = the three ligands occupy one triangular face of the octahedral coordination sphere, leaving the opposite face open for O2, substrate and cofactor. Iron must be ferrous, Fe(II), for catalysis; Fe(III) is inactive. Crystal structure: Goodwill/Andersen rat TH catalytic plus tetramerisation domains at 2.3 A with iron and 7,8-dihydrobiopterin, PDB 2TOH, PMID 9753429. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9753429/
• Quaternary structure: homotetramer, subunits approximately 60 kDa, approximately 498 residues in human - approximately 155-residue N-terminal regulatory domain, approximately 330-residue catalytic domain, approximately 20-residue C-terminal coiled coil driving tetramerisation. One Fe(II) and one BH4 per subunit.
• Phosphorylation sites, all in the N-terminal regulatory domain: Ser8 (ERK1); Ser19 (CaMKII, MAPKAPK-2, PRAK - creates the 14-3-3 binding site); Ser31 (ERK1/2, Cdk5 - approximately 2-fold decrease in Km for BH4); Ser40 (PKA, CaMKII, MAPKAPK-2 - the master activating site).
• End-product feedback inhibition: dopamine, noradrenaline and adrenaline bind the active-site iron directly as catechols, trapping it as inhibited Fe(III) and competing with BH4. Dopamine binds approximately 1000-fold more tightly than L-DOPA; dihydroxyphenylacetate approximately 100-fold less tightly than L-DOPA.
• Ser40 phosphorylation decreases dopamine affinity approximately 300-fold and decreases Km for BH4 approximately 2-fold - a PKA signal simultaneously ejects the bound catecholamine and tightens cofactor binding.
• 14-3-3 binding: Kd 10 nM for doubly phosphorylated hTH1 pSer19/pSer40 with bovine 14-3-3 zeta; approximately 2 nM for the tighter site of singly phosphorylated pSer19.
• UNVERIFIED: the widely quoted Km for L-tyrosine of approximately 10 micromolar could not be confirmed from a fetchable primary source. Would be settled by BRENDA or the original kinetics papers.
• Corroborating structural work (not fetchable, cited only): Bezem MT et al., Nat Commun 2021;12:7385. https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-021-27657-y

Brain iron deficiency and dopamine (Beard/Connor lineage):
• Erikson KM, Jones BC, Beard JL. J Nutr 2000;130:2831-2837. PMID 11053528. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11053528/ Rats, 3 versus 35 mg Fe/kg diet for 4 weeks from postnatal day 21, n = 40. Dopamine transporter density fell approximately 30% in caudate putamen and approximately 20% in nucleus accumbens; synaptosomal dopamine uptake fell; KCl-stimulated release unchanged. FLAG: the 30% and 20% came from abstract summaries (PubMed direct fetch blocked) - verify before printing.
• Erikson KM et al. Pharmacol Biochem Behav 2001;69:409-418 - D1 and D2 receptor densities reduced in frontal cortex and caudate putamen.
• D2 receptor expression is directly iron-responsive at the cellular level in PC12 cells and rat brain. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3415866/
• Integrated claim: brain iron deficiency hits dopamine at synthesis (TH needs Fe(II)), clearance (DAT density) and reception (D1/D2 density). Early-life deficiency produces the most persistent changes.

4. DOPAMINE BETA-HYDROXYLASE (DBH) AND PAM - COPPER ENZYMES

• DBH (dopamine beta-monooxygenase, EC 1.14.17.1) converts dopamine to noradrenaline; PHM converts C-terminal glycine-extended peptides to alpha-hydroxyglycine products. Both are copper monooxygenases in the same family - Klinman JP, J Biol Chem 2006, The Copper-Enzyme Family of Dopamine beta-Monooxygenase and Peptidylglycine alpha-Hydroxylating Monooxygenase. https://www.jbc.org/article/S0021-9258(20)81684-6/fulltext (403 on direct fetch; content confirmed via indexed abstract text)
• TWO coppers per subunit, designated CuH and CuM. VERIFIED. Ligands, using PHM numbering: CuH = His107, His108, His172; CuM = His242, His244, Met314. The methionine at CuM is the family signature.
• PHM is a "non-coupled binuclear copper enzyme": the two coppers are essential but sit approximately 11 A apart in the resting structure and do not electronically communicate. CuH is the electron-transfer site accepting electrons from ascorbate; CuM performs the hydroxylation.
• Tetramer stoichiometry VERIFIED: DBH specifically binds 8 mol Cu per tetramer, and that stoichiometry is required for maximal activity; DBH requires two type-2 bound copper ions per subunit to be active. Source: kinetic and spectroscopic study, PMID 6323422, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6323422/ , and the human DBH crystal structure.
• Human DBH crystal structure at 2.9 A, PDB 4ZEL - Vendelboe TV et al., Sci Adv 2016;2:e1500980. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4846438/ https://www.rcsb.org/structure/4ZEL Each chain folds into four domains: DOMON, catalytic CuH domain, catalytic CuM domain, C-terminal dimerisation domain. Expressed DBH exists as both dimer and tetramer, separable by size-exclusion chromatography; the tetramer converts to dimer under denaturing conditions and to monomer on adding reductant. Two conformations seen - open active site, and a closed form in which the two copper sites approach 4 to 5 A, potentially a coupled binuclear site.
◦ NUANCE WORTH STATING: "tetramer" is the standard textbook description but the purified human enzyme is a dimer-of-dimers equilibrium, not an obligate tetramer. 8 Cu per tetramer = 2 per subunit either way.
• DBH is an ascorbate-dependent glycoprotein; ascorbate (vitamin C) is the physiological reductant supplying electrons at CuH.
• PAM (peptidylglycine alpha-amidating monooxygenase) is a bifunctional enzyme with two catalytic domains: PHM (the copper monooxygenase, hydroxylates the glycine alpha-carbon) and PAL (peptidyl-alpha-hydroxyglycine alpha-amidating lyase, cleaves to give the amidated peptide plus glyoxylate). PAM is the only enzyme that C-terminally amidates peptide hormones - so oxytocin, vasopressin, gastrin, calcitonin, neuropeptide Y and many others require copper for their final activating step. https://www.sciencedirect.com/topics/biochemistry-genetics-and-molecular-biology/peptidylglycine-alpha-amidating-monooxygenase
• Copper occupancy alters PHM's conformational landscape. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6123673/

5. IRON DEFICIENCY, COGNITION AND ADHD - EVIDENCE STRENGTH STATED HONESTLY

Lozoff work (observational cohort, Costa Rica):
• Lozoff B, Jimenez E, Wolf AW. NEJM 1991;325(10):687-694. PMID 1870641. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1870641/ Follow-up at 5 years, 163 of 191 children (85%). Index group: moderately severe iron-deficiency anaemia in infancy, haemoglobin 100 g/L or less. Woodcock-Johnson Preschool Cluster 448.6 plus/minus 9.7 (n = 30) versus 452.9 plus/minus 9.2 (n = 133), p &lt; 0.01 - a 4.3-point gap on a scale that is NOT an IQ scale, do not convert. Visual-Motor Integration 5.9 plus/minus 2.1 versus 6.7 plus/minus 2.3, p &lt; 0.05. Differences persisted after controlling for socioeconomic background.
• Lozoff B et al. Pediatrics 2000;105(4):E51. PMID 10742372. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10742372/ 162 children (87%) re-evaluated at 11-14 years, mean 12.3 years. Severe chronic infant iron deficiency n = 48 versus good iron status n = 114. Significantly lower arithmetic achievement, written expression, motor functioning, spatial memory, selective recall, tachistoscopic threshold. More grade repetition and special services. Parents AND teachers independently reported more anxiety/depression, social problems and attention problems.
◦ HONESTY FLAG: the widely circulated "8 to 9 IQ points lower" figure attributed to this paper could NOT be confirmed from the fetched abstract. Do not print it. Use the domain-specific findings.
• Lozoff B. Nutr Rev 2006;64(5 Pt 2):S34-S43. PMID 16770951. Narrative review, no pooled effect sizes: follow-up from preschool to adolescence reports poorer cognitive, motor and social-emotional function plus persisting neurophysiologic differences; proposed mechanisms are neurometabolism, myelination and neurotransmitter function.
• Lukowski AF et al. Nutr Neurosci 2010;13(2):54-70 - same cohort at 19 years, deficits in executive function and recognition memory (Trail Making, IED shift, Stockings of Cambridge, spatial working memory, RVIP, pattern and spatial recognition memory). Directional only; point values not obtainable free.
• CAUSALITY CAVEAT: this is an observational cohort. All children were treated with iron in infancy. The design cannot separate "iron deficiency caused the deficit" from "iron deficiency marks broader deprivation not fully captured by covariates."

Randomised supplementation trials and systematic reviews:
• Low M, Farrell A, Biggs BA, Pasricha SR. CMAJ 2013;185(17):E791-E802. PMID 24130243. https://www.cmaj.ca/content/185/17/E791 32 randomised or quasi-randomised trials, 7,089 children aged 5-12; 31 of 32 in low- or middle-income settings. Global cognitive score all children SMD 0.50 (95% CI 0.11 to 0.90, p = 0.01). IQ among children anaemic at baseline, mean difference 4.55 IQ points (95% CI 0.16 to 8.94, p = 0.04) - note the lower bound of 0.16 essentially touches zero. THE KEY LINE: global cognition SMD 0.29 (95% CI 0.07 to 0.51, p = 0.01) in children anaemic at baseline versus SMD 0.01 (95% CI -0.10 to 0.11, p = 0.9) in non-anaemic children. Benefit is confined to the already-anaemic. Anaemia risk fell 50%, iron deficiency risk 79%. Safety data limited.
• Wang B, Zhan S, Gong T, Lee L. Cochrane Database Syst Rev 2013, Issue 6, CD001444. PMID 23744449. https://www.cochranelibrary.com/cdsr/doi/10.1002/14651858.CD001444/full 8 RCTs, 385 children under 3 with iron-deficiency anaemia. Within 30 days: Bayley Psychomotor Development Index difference -1.25 points (95% CI -4.56 to 2.06, p = 0.65); Mental Development Index +1.04 points (95% CI -1.30 to 3.39, p = 0.79). Both null. Quality graded LOW. Beyond 30 days: 2 studies, 160 children, inconsistent - one gave 0.8 more skills (95% CI -0.18 to 1.78, p = 0.11), the other a PDI difference of 18.40 points at 4 months (95% CI 10.16 to 26.64, p &lt; 0.0001), which is implausibly large and should be treated as an outlier. Verbatim conclusion: no convincing evidence that iron treatment of young children with IDA affects psychomotor development or cognitive function within 30 days; longer-term effect unclear.
• Sachdev H, Gera T, Nestel P. Public Health Nutr 2005;8(2):117-132. Structured abstract https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK72234/ 17 RCTs, n = 3,646. Mental development overall SMD 0.30 (95% CI 0.15 to 0.46, p &lt; 0.001). Bayley MDI in children 27 months or younger, weighted mean difference 0.95 points (95% CI -0.56 to 2.46, p = 0.217) - NOT significant in the youngest. Same analysis restricted to baseline IDA, WMD 2.76 points (95% CI 0.26 to 5.25, p = 0.03). IQ in children 8 years or older, 4 trials, 1,666 children, SMD 0.41 (95% CI 0.20 to 0.62, p &lt; 0.001). Motor development, 10 trials, 1,246 children, SMD 0.09 (95% CI -0.08 to 0.26, p = 0.28) - no effect. The attached DARE quality assessment states the reliability of the authors' conclusions is unclear - quote that alongside the numbers.
• NO Cochrane review exists on iron supplementation for ADHD. State this plainly.

Ferritin and ADHD:
• Konofal E, Lecendreux M, Arnulf I, Mouren MC. Arch Pediatr Adolesc Med 2004;158(12):1113-1115. PMID 15583094. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15583094/ 53 children with ADHD (mean 9.2 plus/minus 2.2 years) versus 27 controls (9.5 plus/minus 2.8). Mean serum ferritin 23 plus/minus 13 ng/mL versus 44 plus/minus 22 ng/mL, p &lt; 0.001. Ferritin below 30 ng/mL in 84% of ADHD versus 18% of controls, p &lt; 0.001. Conners Parent Rating Scale severity r = -0.34 (p &lt; 0.02); cognitive deficits r = -0.38 (p &lt; 0.01). Small, unbalanced (53 versus 27), and the 84/18 split has never been reproduced. Hypothesis-generating.
• REPLICATION FAILURE: Donfrancesco R, Parisi P, Vanacore N, Martines F, Sargentini V, Cortese S. Iron and ADHD: time to move beyond serum ferritin levels. J Atten Disord 2013;17(4):347-357. PMID 22290693. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22290693/ 101 stimulant-naive ADHD children versus 93 controls - roughly twice Konofal's combined sample and the largest controlled ferritin study at the time. Serum ferritin did NOT differ between ADHD and controls, nor across subtypes; correlations with IQ measures and ADHD severity were not significant. Authors note peripheral ferritin does not measure brain iron.
• META-ANALYSIS: Wang Y, Huang L, Zhang L, Qu Y, Mu D. PLOS ONE 2017;12(1):e0169145. https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pone.0169145 Serum ferritin: 10 studies, 2,191 participants (1,196 ADHD), pooled SMD -0.40 (95% CI -0.66 to -0.14), I-squared = 84% (very high heterogeneity). Serum iron: 6 studies, 617 participants, SMD -0.026 (95% CI -0.29 to 0.24) - no association, I-squared 77%. Subgroup by assay: ELISA SMD -0.78 versus other methods -0.21 - a large part of the pooled effect tracks measurement technique, not biology.
• HONEST VERDICT: a small-to-moderate group-average ferritin difference with a CI approaching zero, severe between-study heterogeneity, partial explanation by assay method, and a failed larger replication. Serum ferritin is not a diagnostic or screening test for ADHD.
• Tseng PT et al. Sci Rep 2018;8:788 - full text behind auth redirect, no figures quoted.

Iron supplementation in ADHD:
• Konofal E et al. Pediatr Neurol 2008;38(1):20-26. PMID 18054688. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18054688/ 23 non-anaemic children aged 5-8 with ADHD (DSM-IV) and ferritin below 30 ng/mL, randomised 3:1 to ferrous sulfate 80 mg/day (n = 18) or placebo (n = 5) for 12 weeks. ADHD Rating Scale change -11.0 plus/minus 13.9 on iron (p &lt; 0.008) versus +3.0 plus/minus 5.7 on placebo (p = 0.308). Conners Parent p = 0.055 and Conners Teacher p = 0.076 - both NON-significant. CGI-Severity fell on iron (p &lt; 0.01).
◦ HONEST ASSESSMENT: placebo arm of 5 children. Within-group before-versus-after p values reported rather than a between-group treatment effect with a confidence interval - the weaker and more flattering analysis. SD of 13.9 on a mean change of -11.0 means wildly variable individual responses. The "comparable to stimulants" claim is author interpretation with no stimulant arm. Always label this a pilot.
• Pooled: J Neuropsychiatry Clin Neurosci 2024 systematic review and meta-analysis, https://psychiatryonline.org/doi/10.1176/appi.neuropsych.20230081 9 articles (7 ADHD, 1 autism, 1 Tourette). Hyperactivity severity, 3 RCTs, 124 participants: Cohen d = 0.76 placebo versus 1.70 supplement - between-group difference NOT significant. Inattention, 2 trials, 75 participants: d = 1.66 placebo versus 3.19 supplement - NOT significant. Note the large within-arm placebo effect sizes, which is exactly why uncontrolled before-and-after rating-scale improvements are unreliable. Quality of evidence and strength of recommendation rated VERY LOW.
• Nutrients 2021;13(11):4059, https://www.mdpi.com/2072-6643/13/11/4059 - 9 eligible RCTs of iron and zinc in ADHD, mixed results, benefit plausibly limited to specific subgroups.

Bottom line for the book: (a) strongest defensible claim - infant IDA is associated with worse cognitive, motor, academic and behavioural outcomes persisting to age 19 in the Costa Rica cohort, observational not causal; (b) supplementation improves cognition in school-aged children ANAEMIC at baseline (SMD 0.29) and does nothing measurable in non-anaemic children (SMD 0.01); (c) treating established IDA in under-threes does NOT demonstrably reverse the deficit within 30 days (Cochrane, both endpoints null, low-quality evidence) - prevention and reversal are not the same thing; (d) do not present iron as an ADHD treatment.

6. WILSON DISEASE (ATP7B)

• Serum ceruloplasmin: conventional threshold below 20 mg/dL (0.2 g/L), with the stricter 0.1 g/L used for high-confidence Leipzig points. Systematic review of biochemical testing (Ryan A et al., Hepatology 2021/2022), 5 studies, 4,281 participants: optimal cutoff range 0.14 to 0.2 g/L; at 0.2 g/L sensitivity 77.1% to 99%, specificity 55.9% to 82.8%. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8842170/ Ceruloplasmin is lower in neonates and RAISED by pregnancy, oestrogen/oral contraception and inflammation (it is an acute-phase reactant) - a major false-negative source.
• 24-hour urinary copper: above 40 micrograms/24 h (0.64 micromol/24 h) is the sensitive threshold, widely used in children and in the current EASL/AASLD framing; above 100 micrograms/24 h (1.6 micromol/24 h) is the classic specific threshold. Systematic review, 3 studies, 268 participants: best evidence cutoff 0.64 to 1.6 micromol/24 h; at 1.6 micromol/24 h sensitivity 50% to 80%, specificity 75.6% to 98.3%. SOURCES DISAGREE on which threshold to use - the 40 microgram cutoff buys sensitivity at real cost in specificity, which is why the Leipzig system awards graded points rather than a yes/no.
• Hepatic copper: above 250 micrograms/g dry weight (= 4 micromol/g) is the classic diagnostic threshold - sensitivity 99.4%, specificity 96.1%. A lower ROC-optimal cutoff of 1.2 micromol/g (approximately 75 micrograms/g dry weight) gave sensitivity 96.4% and specificity 95.4%. Both are in the same systematic review. Sampling error in a needle biopsy is the main practical limitation.
• Leipzig scoring system - Ferenci P et al., 8th International Meeting on Wilson Disease and Menkes Disease, Leipzig 2001; published Liver Int 2003. Items and point values: Kayser-Fleischer rings 2 points; neurological symptoms or typical brain MRI 2 points; serum ceruloplasmin up to 2 points; Coombs-negative haemolytic anaemia 1 point; 24-hour urinary copper up to 2 points; liver copper up to 2 points (with 1 point for rhodanine-positive granules); mutation analysis up to 4 points (2 mutations = 4, 1 mutation = 1). Interpretation: total 4 or more = diagnosis established; 3 = possible, more tests needed; 2 or less = diagnosis unlikely. Reported performance in paediatric cohorts: sensitivity 93-98%, specificity 92-97%. https://www.dovepress.com/usefulness-of-the-leipzig-score-in-the-diagnosis-of-wilsons-disease----peer-reviewed-fulltext-article-IMCRJ (403 on direct fetch; point values assembled from indexed summaries of that article and the Rare Disease Advisor and PediatricOncall reproductions - VERIFY the exact per-item point allocation against Ferenci 2003 before printing the table).
• There is NO gold-standard single test for Wilson disease; that is the explicit reason the Leipzig composite exists.
• Prevalence - THE CENTRAL DISCREPANCY: clinically diagnosed prevalence approximately 1 in 30,000 worldwide. Genetic prevalence from sequencing is far higher. Coffey AJ et al., A genetic study of Wilson's disease in the United Kingdom, Brain 2013;136(5):1476-1487, PMID 23518715, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3634195/ sequenced the whole ATP7B coding region plus splice sites in 1,000 UK controls; frequency of variants with in silico evidence of pathogenicity 0.056, or 0.040 restricted to variants previously reported in patients - giving a predicted genetic prevalence of approximately 1 in 7,026 in the UK, roughly four times the diagnosed prevalence. Gao J et al., The global prevalence of Wilson disease from next-generation sequencing data, PMID 30254379, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30254379/ reaches a comparable conclusion from population sequencing.
• HOW TO STATE THIS HONESTLY: the gap has three candidate explanations, not one - (a) genuine under-diagnosis of oligosymptomatic Wilson disease; (b) reduced or incomplete penetrance of some ATP7B genotypes; (c) over-calling of variant pathogenicity by in silico prediction in population sequencing. Coffey's own predicted-versus-observed gap does not by itself distinguish them. What would settle it: prospective biochemical phenotyping of unselected genotype-positive individuals from a biobank (the UK Biobank ATP7B genetic-versus-point-prevalence project is doing exactly this, https://www.ukbiobank.ac.uk/projects/exploring-the-genetic-landscape-of-atp7b-and-the-relationship-between-genetic-prevalence-and-point-prevalence-of-wilson-disease/ ).

7. MENKES DISEASE (ATP7A)

• X-linked recessive, caused by loss-of-function variants in ATP7A, encoding a transmembrane copper-transporting P-type ATPase - the mirror-image transporter to ATP7B. OMIM #309400, https://omim.org/entry/309400
• Incidence - SOURCES DISAGREE AND THE SPREAD IS LARGE: approximately 1 in 300,000 live births in Europe; up to approximately 1 in 40,000 in Australia (the historic Melbourne figure); commonly quoted global range 1 in 100,000 to 1 in 300,000. Tonnesen et al. European ancestry estimates cluster near 1 in 300,000. https://www.medlink.com/articles/menkes-disease-and-other-atp7a-related-disorders and https://www.nature.com/articles/ejhg2009187 The Australian outlier is not well explained and may reflect ascertainment.
• Biochemistry: LOW serum copper and LOW serum ceruloplasmin - the reliable screening pair. Note the diagnostic trap: these are also low in normal neonates, so a low result in the first weeks of life is not specific. https://emedicine.medscape.com/article/1180460-workup
• The specific early test is the plasma catecholamine profile - a raised DOPA/DHPG and DOPAC/DHPG ratio, reflecting deficient dopamine beta-hydroxylase activity because DBH is a copper enzyme (see section 4). This is the direct clinical link between the copper chapter and the DBH biochemistry.
• Clinical features: pili torti (hair twisted about its own axis on light microscopy) giving the "kinky hair" / "steely hair" appearance; profound hypotonia; failure to thrive; intractable seizures; developmental regression from around 2-3 months; connective tissue failure from deficient lysyl oxidase (another copper enzyme) giving skin laxity, bladder diverticula, tortuous vessels, occipital horn exostoses; death typically by age 3 in classic Menkes.
• Pili torti in the MOTHER's hair is considered proof of carrier status.
• Treatment: copper histidinate (copper chloride plus L-histidine), 350-500 micrograms/day subcutaneously or intravenously, normalises serum and cerebrospinal fluid copper within about 6 weeks and can normalise ceruloplasmin, improve tone and motor activity and prevent seizures - but only if started pre-symptomatically (neonatal). It bypasses the gut ATP7A block; it does not correct ATP7A-dependent cuproenzyme loading in all tissues. Systematic review and evidence-based guideline: Mol Genet Metab 2018, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S1096719218304104

8. NBIA AND PKAN (PANK2)

• PKAN is the commonest NBIA subtype. Prevalence approximately 1 to 3 per million worldwide; some sources give 1 to 2 per million. Incidence estimated at approximately 2 per 1 million live births globally outside Africa, and approximately 1 in 1.5 million in African-ancestry populations. Bioinformatic and population-based analysis of approximately 140,000 humans, PMID 31540697, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31540697/ ; GeneReviews, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK1490/ ; StatPearls, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK430689/
• Eye-of-the-tiger sign: bilateral central hyperintense signal surrounded by hypointensity in the MEDIAL globus pallidus on T2-weighted MRI. The hypointense rim is iron; the central bright spot is gliosis/oedema.
• Sensitivity and specificity - STATE HONESTLY: in selected PANK2-positive series 100% of cases showed the sign, which is why it is called the hallmark. But it is NOT pathognomonic. Chang CL et al., Eye-of-the-Tiger sign is not pathognomonic of pantothenate kinase-associated neurodegeneration in adult cases, Brain Behav 2011;1(1):55-56, PMID 22398981, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3217674/ Phenocopies reported in neuroferritinopathy, multiple system atrophy, corticobasal degeneration, carbon monoxide poisoning, and in healthy adults. It can also be ABSENT in early PKAN. No study I could fetch reports a formal sensitivity/specificity pair with confidence intervals - that is a genuine gap in the literature and what would settle it is a blinded read of consecutive NBIA-suspected MRIs against genetic confirmation.
• Classic PKAN: onset before age 6 in approximately 90%; range 6 months to 12 years; mean onset approximately 3 years 4 months, median approximately 3 years. Rapid progression, dystonia, rigidity, pigmentary retinopathy, loss of ambulation typically within 10-15 years.
• Atypical PKAN: onset after age 10, median approximately 18 years; prominent speech defects (palilalia, dysarthria), psychiatric disturbance, slower progression.
• Other NBIA genes to name: PLA2G6 (PLAN/INAD), C19orf12 (MPAN), WDR45 (BPAN, X-linked, de novo), FA2H (FAHN), CP (aceruloplasminaemia - the one NBIA that is genuinely a systemic iron-handling disease, with absent serum ceruloplasmin, low serum iron, high ferritin, diabetes and retinal degeneration), FTL (neuroferritinopathy, autosomal dominant, low serum ferritin). PANK2 accounts for roughly half of genetically defined NBIA in most series - I could not verify a precise proportion from a fetched source; flag as approximate.

9. HEREDITARY HAEMOCHROMATOSIS (HFE C282Y)

• HFE p.C282Y homozygosity accounts for more than 95% of hereditary haemochromatosis in European-ancestry populations.
• Homozygote prevalence in Northern European ancestry populations: 0.3% to 0.6%, i.e. roughly 1 in 165 to 1 in 330. (Implied allele frequency roughly 5-8%.) Source: Clinical Gastroenterology and Hepatology 2024 review, https://www.cghjournal.org/article/S1542-3565(24)01136-4/fulltext
• CLINICAL PENETRANCE IS LOW - this is the honest headline. UK Biobank, Pilling LC, Melzer D and colleagues: only 25.3% of male C282Y homozygotes and 12.5% of female homozygotes had a haemochromatosis diagnosis by age 65. Restated as morbidity: roughly 1 in 5 males and 1 in 10 females develop haemochromatosis-associated morbidity. Cohort: 1,294 male and 1,596 female European-ancestry C282Y homozygotes with up to 14 years of linked records. So biochemical penetrance (raised transferrin saturation and ferritin) is high, but CLINICAL penetrance - actual iron-overload disease - is a minority outcome, and the majority of homozygotes never develop the disease. https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.1101/2022.03.08.22272084v1
• BRAIN IRON AND NEUROLOGICAL EFFECTS - REVISE THE PRIOR: this is now BETTER established than the older literature suggested, at least for brain iron deposition, though causal neurological harm remains less certain.
◦ Atkins JL, Pilling LC, Heales CJ, Savage S, Kuo CL, Kuchel GA, Steffens DC, Melzer D. Hemochromatosis mutations, brain iron imaging, and dementia in the UK Biobank cohort. J Alzheimers Dis 2021;79(3):1203-1211. PMID 33427739. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7990419/ 78 male and 128 female C282Y homozygotes with brain MRI. Male homozygotes had significantly lower T2* (more iron): putamen beta = -0.91 SD (p = 1.0 x 10^-16); thalamus -0.90 SD (p = 5.7 x 10^-16); caudate -0.55 SD (p = 3.4 x 10^-6); hippocampus -0.32 SD (p = 9.2 x 10^-4). Females showed the same pattern with smaller effects. Incident dementia in male homozygotes HR 1.83 (95% CI 1.23 to 2.72, p = 0.003) over approximately 10.5 years mean follow-up; delirium HR 1.82 (p = 0.004). No association in heterozygotes or female homozygotes.
◦ Loughnan R et al. Association of genetic variant linked to hemochromatosis with brain MRI measures of iron and movement disorders. JAMA Neurology 2022. PMID 35913729. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35913729/ 488,288 UK Biobank participants including 2,889 C282Y homozygotes; imaging subset 165 homozygotes versus 671 matched controls. Decreased T2-weighted and T2* signal in subcortical motor structures (basal ganglia, thalamus, red nucleus, cerebellum) with Cohen d greater than 1 - a very large effect. Movement disorders: males OR 1.80 (95% CI 1.28 to 2.55, p = 0.001); females OR 1.09 (95% CI 0.70 to 1.73, p = 0.69) - null in women. Of 31 male homozygotes with movement disorders, only 10 had a documented haemochromatosis diagnosis.
◦ HONEST STATEMENT FOR THE BOOK: brain iron accumulation in male C282Y homozygotes is now well established by large-cohort MRI with very large effect sizes. The downstream clinical claims (dementia HR 1.83, movement disorder OR 1.80) are observational associations in a healthy-volunteer-biased cohort, are male-specific, and the authors themselves flag that they cannot distinguish direct brain iron toxicity from secondary mechanisms via liver disease, nor identify the molecular form of the excess brain iron. Hospital-record dementia subtyping is crude. What would settle it: whether early venesection prevents the neurological outcomes - untested.
• Beutler E et al. Lancet 2002 and Allen KJ et al. NEJM 2008 (HealthIron) are the classic low-penetrance references. I did not fetch them in this pass - their headline figures (Allen: approximately 28% of male homozygotes developed iron-overload-related disease) should be verified before printing.

10. THINGS EXPLICITLY NOT ESTABLISHED / NEEDING VERIFICATION BEFORE PUBLICATION

• The exact Hallgren-Sourander per-region decimals and the A, B, k constants of their exponential age equation - not obtained. Original 1958 paper is paywalled.
• Myoglobin iron approximately 300 mg - unverified.
• Copper mg/kg body weight - no cited source exists; the approximately 1.4 mg/kg figure is arithmetic.
• Ceruloplasmin share of plasma copper - 65-90% (older) versus greater than 95% (current NIH ODS). Genuine conflict, state both.
• TH Km for L-tyrosine (approximately 10 micromolar) - unverified from primary source.
• Erikson 2000 DAT reduction percentages (30% caudate putamen, 20% nucleus accumbens) - from abstract summaries only, direct fetch blocked.
• Leipzig per-item point allocation - assembled from secondary reproductions; verify against Ferenci et al. Liver Int 2003.
• Formal sensitivity and specificity with confidence intervals for the eye-of-the-tiger sign - does not appear to exist in the literature.
• Proportion of all NBIA attributable to PANK2 - commonly stated as about half, not verified from a fetched source.
• Beutler 2002 and Allen 2008 HFE penetrance percentages - not fetched this pass.
• Lozoff 2000 "8-9 IQ points" - NOT in the paper's abstract. Do not use.</result>
