<result>CYCLIC AMP AND THE SECOND MESSENGER CASCADE - WEB-VERIFIED QUANTITATIVE FINDINGS
Gathered 2026-08-12. Every number below traces to a source retrieved in this session. Where sources disagree it is stated.

Note on file output: the standing global instruction to write a .txt copy to /home/ccaitwo/output/ was overridden by the explicit task instruction "Do NOT write any files." Nothing was written to disk.


1. BASAL AND STIMULATED CYTOSOLIC cAMP CONCENTRATION

• Basal free cytosolic cAMP measured 0.71 - 1.14 micromolar in intact cells, using two independent FRET sensors (EPAC-SH187 and CUTie) calibrated by patch-pipette microinfusion of known cAMP concentrations. Koschinski and Zaccolo, Scientific Reports 2017, 7:14090. https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-017-13021-y and https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5658382/

• The "approximately 1 micromolar basal" figure is the consensus across cell types. A 2020 review (Subcellular Organization of the cAMP Signaling Pathway, PMC7770493) states basal ~1 micromolar citing Beavo et al. 1974 and Borner et al. 2011.

• Whole-cell versus free: this distinction matters and the sources are explicit about it. Classical radioimmunoassay / lysate assays measure TOTAL cAMP (free plus protein-bound). Koschinski and Zaccolo note that lysate/homogenate assays "may significantly overestimate the amount of free cAMP available to trigger PKA activation." Free cAMP is what FRET sensors report.

• Bound fraction is large. Bock et al., Cell 2020, 182(6):1519-1530.e17, concluded that "cAMP at physiological concentrations is predominantly bound to cAMP binding sites and thus immobile." https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33989549/ (DOI 10.1016/j.cell.2020.07.035)

• Buffer capacity estimate: mammalian cardiac myocytes contain ~0.5 - 1 micromolar PKA, giving a buffering capacity of up to 2 micromolar cAMP (each R subunit has two cAMP sites). Richards et al., "Mechanisms Restricting Diffusion of Intracellular cAMP," https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4726171/

• Stimulated concentrations: the "up to 10 micromolar" figure is used as a working assumption rather than a hard measurement. The Hitting the Detection Limit commentary (PMC11234644) explicitly uses 10 micromolar as the reference local concentration for nanodomain arithmetic. Epac-based FRET sensors are stated to operate over the physiological range ~0.1 - 100 micromolar, and maximal calibration in cells is routinely done with 25 micromolar forskolin plus 100 micromolar IBMX. So 10 micromolar is a defensible order-of-magnitude for stimulated free cAMP, but I found no single crisp primary measurement pinning global stimulated cytosolic cAMP at exactly 10 micromolar. HONEST STATUS: not well established. What would settle it - patch-pipette-calibrated FRET (the Koschinski/Zaccolo method) applied at saturating agonist in the same cell types, reported as absolute free micromolar.

• Textbook dynamic-range claims retrieved (secondary, treat as illustrative not measured): "cAMP in the cytosol increases 20-fold within seconds"; "several hundred-fold in a matter of seconds." These come from teaching sources (Open University cell signalling material), not primary measurement, and they disagree with each other by more than an order of magnitude. Do not quote them as data.


2. THE PARADOX: BASAL cAMP EXCEEDS THE IN-VITRO PKA THRESHOLD

This is a real, unresolved tension and is worth stating plainly in the book.

• In vitro PKA activation EC50: 0.3 micromolar (300 nM) for the C9H6 PKA-activity FRET reporter system. Koschinski and Zaccolo 2017.

• In intact cells the same measurement gives EC50 5.2 micromolar for overexpressed PKA - roughly 17-fold higher than in vitro.

• Endogenous PKA activity, read out by AKAR3 phosphorylation, gives EC50 ~2.1 micromolar in cells - still ~7-fold above the in vitro value.

• So: basal free cAMP (~1 micromolar) sits ABOVE the classical in vitro PKA activation constant (~0.1-0.3 micromolar) yet cells are not tonically maximally phosphorylated. Three hypotheses are on the table per the same paper: (a) intracellular free cAMP measurements are wrong, (b) the PKA activation constant is wrong, (c) PKA is shielded from bulk cytosolic cAMP by compartmentalisation. The 2017 data favour (b) plus (c) - PKA in situ genuinely needs more cAMP than purified PKA does.


3. PKA ACTIVATION - AFFINITY, COOPERATIVITY, HILL COEFFICIENT

• Classical in vitro activation constant: ~100 - 300 nM cAMP. Retrieved anchor value: EC50 294 nM for holo-RIIalpha with the cAMP agonist Sp-8-Br-cAMPS.

• In-cell EC50: 2.1 micromolar (endogenous, AKAR3) to 5.2 micromolar (overexpressed PKA). Koschinski and Zaccolo 2017. SOURCES DISAGREE WITH EACH OTHER BY ~10-20 FOLD depending on whether the measurement is in vitro or in situ. State both.

• Hill coefficients measured in intact cells (Koschinski and Zaccolo 2017):
  ◦ C9H6 PKA reporter: n_H = 2.19
  ◦ AKAR3: n_H = 2.97
  ◦ EPAC-SH187 (an Epac sensor, single cAMP site architecture): n_H = 1.75

• Hill coefficient in purified holoenzyme: ~1.7 for wild-type PKA heterotetramer. Mutations in the N3A motif (K121A, Y120A) drop it to ~1.0, abolishing cooperativity; Carney Complex mutations S145G and R144S give n_H ~1.4. So the commonly quoted "Hill coefficient ~1.6-2" is defensible for the holoenzyme, and the structural basis (N3A motif allosteric network) is identified.

• Mechanism of cooperativity: each R subunit has two tandem cyclic-nucleotide-binding domains, CNB-A and CNB-B. Domain A is inaccessible until cAMP occupies domain B - an ordered, cooperative mechanism. So 4 cAMP per R2C2 holoenzyme.

• R-C subunit affinities (not cAMP affinities - do not confuse them): RIalpha:C 0.19 nM, RIbeta:C 0.1 nM, RIIalpha:C &gt;1 nM, RIIbeta:C 0.2 nM.

• Type I versus type II: PKA tetramers containing RI-type subunits are activated at LOWER cAMP than RII-containing tetramers (Dostmann et al. 1990; Moll et al. 2008, as cited in PMC7770493).

• R subunits are in up to 17-fold molar excess over C subunits (Walker-Gray et al. 2017) - relevant to buffering.


4. ADENYLYL CYCLASE ISOFORMS AC1 - AC10

Nine transmembrane isoforms (ADCY1-9) plus one soluble (ADCY10 / sAC / AC10).

Regulation table, from the IUPHAR/BPS Guide to Pharmacology adenylyl cyclase family page (https://www.guidetopharmacology.org/GRAC/FamilyDisplayForward?familyId=257):

• AC1 - Ca2+/calmodulin STIMULATED; inhibited by G-beta-gamma; forskolin sensitive; brain
• AC2 - Ca2+ insensitive; G-beta-gamma STIMULATED; forskolin sensitive; brain
• AC3 - modest Ca2+/CaM stimulation, also Ca2+-inhibited via CaMKII; G-beta-gamma inhibited; olfactory epithelium (the olfactory transduction cyclase)
• AC4 - Ca2+ insensitive; G-beta-gamma stimulated; widespread
• AC5 - Ca2+ INHIBITED; forskolin sensitive; heart, and the principal striatal isoform
• AC6 - Ca2+ INHIBITED; G-beta-gamma stimulated; heart
• AC7 - Ca2+ insensitive; G-beta-gamma stimulated; widespread
• AC8 - Ca2+/calmodulin STIMULATED; brain
• AC9 - Ca2+-inhibited indirectly via calcineurin; FORSKOLIN INSENSITIVE (see caveat below); heart and brain
• AC10 / sAC - soluble, cytoplasmic and organellar; bicarbonate-activated; Ca2+-tuned; NOT G-protein regulated; forskolin INSENSITIVE

Groupings used in the literature: calmodulin-stimulated group = AC1, AC3, AC8. Ca2+- and G-beta-gamma-inhibitable group = AC5, AC6, AC9. G-beta-gamma-stimulated / Ca2+-insensitive group = AC2, AC4, AC7.

Calcium sensitivity of AC1 versus AC8:
• AC1 and AC8 are the two major Ca2+/CaM-stimulated isoforms in neurons; AC1 is MORE Ca2+-sensitive than AC8, with AC8's Ca2+ sensitivity approximately 5-fold lower than AC1's (Masada et al. 2009, "Distinct mechanisms of regulation by Ca2+/calmodulin of type 1 and 8 adenylyl cyclases," https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19029295/).
• CAUTION: one retrieved secondary summary quoted an EC50 for free Ca2+ of "&lt;0.15 nM" for AC1. That is almost certainly a units error (0.15 micromolar = 150 nM is the physiologically sensible figure - resting cytosolic Ca2+ is itself ~100 nM). DO NOT USE the 0.15 nM figure. HONEST STATUS: I could not retrieve a clean primary EC50 for Ca2+ stimulation of AC1/AC8 in this session. What would settle it - the Cooper lab primary papers (Cooper, "Why calcium-stimulated adenylyl cyclases?", Physiology 2004, was paywalled at 403 in this session).

Brain distribution:
• AC1-AC9 are all present in brain.
• AC1 is abundant in the dentate gyrus of hippocampus and in cerebral cortex.
• AC8 is prominent in hypothalamus.
• AC1/AC8 double knockouts are the standard tool for Ca2+-stimulated AC function in plasticity, pain and memory (e.g. reduced chronic muscle pain in AC1/AC8 nulls, PMC1395303).
• AC5 is the principal isoform in striatal medium spiny neurons and is described as "essential for the integration of both stimulatory and inhibitory midbrain signals" from dopaminergic GPCRs (PMC6912275).
• AC5/AC6 are highly expressed in heart and vasculature as well as spinal cord and brain (PMC11021717).

Soluble AC (sAC / ADCY10):
• Not membrane-integral; found in cytosol, nucleus, mitochondria; not G-protein regulated; insensitive to forskolin.
• Activated by bicarbonate: HCO3- raises Vmax with little effect on apparent Km for Mg-ATP. Apparent Km for Mg-ATP is above 10 mM - i.e. sAC operates well below saturation at physiological ATP, making it an ATP sensor as well as a pH/CO2 sensor (Zippin et al., "CO2/HCO3- and calcium-regulated soluble adenylyl cyclase as a physiological ATP sensor," https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24100033/).
• Ca2+ increases sAC's affinity for ATP; bicarbonate relieves Mg-ATP substrate inhibition.
• Together with carbonic anhydrase, sAC forms an HCO3-/CO2/pH sensing module. Frontiers in Physiology 2014, 5:42.
• Standard pharmacological tool inhibitor: KH7.
• Crystal structures of human sAC showing the bicarbonate activation mechanism: PNAS 2014, https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1322778111. Mechanism updated 2025: PMC12250328.


5. FORSKOLIN

• Direct activator of transmembrane adenylyl cyclases, binding the pseudosymmetric allosteric site at the C1/C2 catalytic domain interface. A diterpene from Coleus forskohlii root.
• DOES NOT ACTIVATE: sAC / AC10 (unambiguous - no forskolin site). AC9 is the classic "forskolin-insensitive" transmembrane isoform.
• AC9 CAVEAT - SOURCES DISAGREE. The older statement "forskolin activates all transmembrane ACs except AC9" is now qualified. Qi et al., Nature Communications 2022, "Structural basis of adenylyl cyclase 9 activation" (https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-022-28685-y, PMC8873477) solved AC9(1250)-G-alpha-s with MANT-GTP and forskolin and showed forskolin DOES bind the canonical allosteric site and weakly activates AC9, but only in the presence of G-alpha-s. Correct statement for the book: AC9 is weakly and conditionally forskolin-responsive, not flatly insensitive; sAC is genuinely insensitive.
• Potency: EC50 0.5 micromolar for type I adenylyl cyclase.
• Typical experimental concentrations: 10 - 25 micromolar. Maximal-stimulation calibration in FRET sensor work uses 25 micromolar forskolin plus 100 micromolar IBMX.


6. THE AMPLIFICATION CASCADE ARITHMETIC - AND AN HONEST ASSESSMENT

Measured numbers, step by step.

Step 1, receptor to G protein.
• Reconstituted purified beta-adrenergic receptor plus Gs in phospholipid vesicles: Pedersen and Ross found one beta-AR catalyses nucleotide exchange on about 6 Gs molecules. Asano et al., with better quantitation, demonstrated at least 8 turnovers. A kinetic reanalysis of Asano's data argued a single receptor could turn over about 50 Gs. (Reviewed in Cellular Logistics 2014, "G Protein-coupled receptors," https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.4161/cl.29391 - abstract-level retrieval; full text 403 in this session.)
• Gs dissociation from immobilised beta2AR is slow, ~0.1-0.2 per second.
• Rhodopsin, the fastest-known GPCR, activates transducin at 120-150 per second (see below). Rhodopsin is the ceiling, not the norm.
• So "1 receptor activates ~10-100 G proteins" is defensible as a RANGE spanning measured (6-8, directly counted) to kinetically extrapolated (~50) values, with rhodopsin as an outlier upper bound. The low end - under 10 - is the directly measured number and should be given equal billing.

Step 2, adenylyl cyclase catalytic output.
• Type V AC (AC5) specific activities (Regulation of Adenylyl Cyclase by Protein Kinase A, JBC): basal ~50 nmol/min/mg; GTP-gamma-S-Gs-alpha stimulated ~200 nmol/min/mg; forskolin ~250 nmol/min/mg; Vmax 410 +/- 32 nmol/min/mg without PKA phosphorylation, falling to 231 +/- 30 nmol/min/mg with PKA phosphorylation (feedback inhibition).
• Converting: AC catalytic core mass ~120 kDa. 200 nmol/min/mg x 120,000 g/mol = 24,000 nmol product per min per micromole enzyme = 24,000 min^-1 = ~400 cAMP per enzyme per second. Order 10^2 to 10^3 per second. NOTE THIS IS MY ARITHMETIC from the retrieved specific activity, not a directly quoted kcat. I did not find a directly quoted kcat in molecules per second for a mammalian AC in this session. HONEST STATUS: "each adenylyl cyclase makes thousands of cAMP per second" is at the optimistic edge; the retrieved specific activities support hundreds per second. What would settle it - a BRENDA entry or primary kinetics paper reporting kcat in s^-1 for purified AC5 or the AC5-C1/AC2-C2 soluble chimera.

Step 3, cAMP to PKA. NOT an amplification step - it is a 4:1 CONSUMPTION step (4 cAMP bind one R2C2 holoenzyme). This is where naive multiplicative cascade arithmetic quietly cheats.

Step 4, PKA to substrate. Amplification resumes here (one C subunit phosphorylates many substrates), and in the classic hepatocyte example continues through phosphorylase kinase to glycogen phosphorylase to glucose.

THE "100-MILLION-FOLD" FIGURE - WHERE IT COMES FROM AND WHETHER IT SURVIVES SCRUTINY.

• The 10^8 number in textbooks is the EPINEPHRINE-TO-GLUCOSE figure, not a cAMP figure: "a single epinephrine molecule might, in the end, be responsible for the release of 100 million glucose molecules." It is the product of the ENTIRE glycogenolytic cascade including two enzymatic amplification steps downstream of PKA (phosphorylase kinase, then glycogen phosphorylase acting processively on glycogen). It is NOT the amplification of the receptor-to-cAMP segment. Anyone quoting it as "one receptor makes 100 million cAMP" is misreading it.

• The rod phototransduction figure is separate and better measured. Stryer's classic numbers: ~500 transducin-alpha-GTP formed per photoexcited rhodopsin at low light; each activated PDE6 hydrolyses &gt;100 cGMP per second; overall gain &gt;500,000, i.e. ~5 x 10^5. Combined rhodopsin-plus-PDE amplification is quoted as ~10^5 to 10^6. Stryer, Visual Excitation and Recovery, JBC 1991, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1710212/; Stryer, Biopolymers 1985, Molecular design of an amplification cascade in vision.

• THE ROD FIGURE HAS ITSELF BEEN REVISED DOWNWARD. Direct measurements on intact rods give a single photoisomerised rhodopsin activating transducin and PDE6 catalytic subunits at 120-150 per second, "much lower than indirect estimates from light-scattering experiments." The ~500 figure "has been challenged and revised downward," but "disputes persist because of the large variation in estimates obtained by different groups, plus the drawback that some key measurements used in such estimates came from experiments on nonintact rods. As such, the dogma continues in textbooks." Source: Current understanding of signal amplification in phototransduction, Cellular Logistics 2014, https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.4161/cl.29390. See also Pugh and Lamb, "The gain of rod phototransduction: reconciliation of biochemical and electrophysiological measurements," Neuron 2000, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11055435/ and Elementary response triggered by transducin in retinal rods, PNAS 2019, https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1817781116. Total downstream hydrolysis estimates in the retrieved literature span ~2,000 to ~72,000 cGMP molecules per photon - a 36-fold spread between groups.

HONEST VERDICT for the book. The 100-million-fold figure is NOT defensible as a description of GPCR-to-cAMP amplification, for four independent reasons:

(a) Provenance. It is an epinephrine-to-glucose whole-pathway number, or else a rod-vision number. Neither is a cAMP-second-messenger number. Transplanting it onto the GPCR-cAMP axis is a category error that has propagated through textbooks.

(b) The rod source figure is itself under active downward revision, from ~500 transducins per rhodopsin to ~120-150 per second, and the review literature openly says the old figure persists in textbooks as dogma rather than as current evidence.

(c) Rhodopsin is atypical. It has an unusually long active lifetime and sits in a densely packed disc membrane at the highest GPCR density in biology. Reconstituted beta-adrenergic receptors turn over 6 to ~50 Gs, one to two orders of magnitude fewer. Generalising rod gain to a beta2AR or a D1 receptor is not supported.

(d) THE DECISIVE MODERN OBJECTION. If a single receptor produced millions of cAMP molecules, cAMP nanodomains could not exist. Anton et al. 2022 and the accompanying analysis (Hitting the Detection Limit in cAMP Signaling, PMC11234644) compute that a 30 nm-radius hemispherical nanodomain has a volume of ~0.5 x 10^-4 femtolitres, so at 10 micromolar local concentration it contains APPROXIMATELY ONE cAMP MOLECULE, and 3-5 cAMP molecules are needed to activate one PKA R-subunit pair plus a sensor. Receptor-associated signalling operates at the SINGLE-MOLECULE detection limit. That is flatly incompatible with 10^8-fold gain per receptor. Amplification and spatial specificity are in direct trade-off: you cannot have both.

Recommended framing: give the arithmetic honestly as roughly 6-50 G proteins per receptor, roughly 10^2-10^3 cAMP per cyclase per second, a 4:1 consumption at PKA, then further gain at the substrate level - and say explicitly that the headline 10^8 figure is a whole-pathway hepatic glycogenolysis number of contested provenance that modern nanodomain measurements make untenable for local GPCR-cAMP signalling.


7. PHOSPHODIESTERASES

PDE superfamily: 11 families; 8 of them hydrolyse cAMP (PDE1, 2, 3, 4, 7, 8, 10, 11) per Lugnier 2006 and Francis et al. 2011 as cited in PMC7770493.

PDE4 (cAMP-specific)
• Four genes: PDE4A, PDE4B, PDE4C, PDE4D, with many N-terminally distinct splice variants (long, short, supershort forms).
• Km for cAMP: PDE4D2 Km = 1.5 +/- 0.2 micromolar. Rolipram-sensitive PDE4 in HEK-293 cells: Km = 3 micromolar, Vmax = 0.42 micromolar/s (Estimating the magnitude of near-membrane PDE4 activity in living cells, PMC4572370). So PDE4 Km for cAMP is in the 1-5 micromolar band - ABOVE resting free cAMP (~1 micromolar), meaning PDE4 operates in its first-order regime at rest.
• PDE4 accounts for 70-80% of cAMP hydrolysis in neurons.
• Brain distribution: PDE4A, 4B and 4D are expressed relatively ubiquitously across rodent brain with only subtle regional differences; highest in cortex, hippocampus, striatum, cerebellum. PDE4B and PDE4D are the predominant human brain subtypes. PDE4B mRNA and protein confirmed in caudate-putamen. PDE4A5 and PDE4A10 are highly expressed in hippocampus; PDE4D4/PDE4D5 variants are implicated in hippocampal neurogenesis and memory.
• PDE4B is a schizophrenia and anxiety-linked gene; selective PDE4B inhibition produces anxiolysis and facilitates memory acquisition (Neuropsychopharmacology 2015). PDE4D knockdown/knockout enhances memory and hippocampal neurogenesis (PMC3079568). PDE4D inhibition is the emetic liability, which drove the allosteric-modulator strategy (Burgin et al., Nature Biotechnology 2010).

Rolipram (the standard PDE4 tool inhibitor)
• Subtype IC50s: PDE4A 3 nM, PDE4B 130 nM, PDE4D 240 nM. NOTE: these span ~80-fold and this particular data set is not the only one in circulation; rolipram is often described as roughly equipotent across PDE4A-D in other assays. FLAG AS DISPUTED - the apparent subtype selectivity of rolipram depends strongly on the conformational state of the enzyme (the high-affinity rolipram binding site, HARBS, versus low-affinity site), which is why single IC50 values for rolipram are unreliable.
• Typical experimental concentrations: 0.03 - 3 micromolar in tissue and slice work; 100 nM is effective in neuronal experiments; 1 - 10 micromolar is common in cell culture. One retrieved source notes rolipram IC50 values for PDE4 are 30- to 300-fold lower than 10 micromolar, so 10 micromolar is well past saturation and risks off-target effects.

PDE10A (dual-substrate, striatum-enriched)
• Km values - SOURCES DISAGREE. Soderling, Bayuga and Beavo, PNAS 1999, 96:7071-7076 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC22059/) report Km cAMP 0.05 micromolar and Km cGMP 3 micromolar, with a Vmax ratio cGMP/cAMP of 4.7. A separate characterisation reports Km cAMP 0.26 micromolar and Km cGMP 7.2 micromolar. Both agree on the qualitative point: PDE10A has a HIGH-AFFINITY (sub-micromolar to low-micromolar) cAMP site but a higher Vmax for cGMP - hence "cAMP-inhibited cGMP-ase" behaviour in some framings. Give the range: Km cAMP 0.05 - 0.26 micromolar, Km cGMP 3 - 7.2 micromolar. The parallel paper is Fujishige et al., J Biol Chem 1999, 274:18438-18445.
• Brain distribution - the strongest regional enrichment of any brain PDE. PDE10A mRNA and protein are highly enriched in the striatal complex (caudate, putamen, nucleus accumbens, olfactory tubercle), conserved across rat, dog, cynomolgus monkey and human. In brain it is almost exclusively striatal. Xie et al., Neuroscience 2006, "Cellular and subcellular localization of PDE10A, a striatum-enriched phosphodiesterase," https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1464838/
• Cell-type specificity: expressed exclusively in GABAergic medium spiny projection neurons; COMPLETELY ABSENT from all four classes of striatal interneuron (nNOS+, calretinin+, ChAT+, parvalbumin+). This is unusually clean and is a good teaching example.
• Subcellular: primarily membrane-bound (hydrophobic, not ionic, association; solubilised by Triton X-100); ~50% recovered in synaptosomal vesicle fractions; concentrated on dendritic and spine membranes; located at the EDGES of the postsynaptic density but not within the PSD core; absent from presynaptic terminals and synaptic vesicles. Also tethered to a synaptic signalling complex in striatum (JBC 2015, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25762721/).
• PDE10A is described as the major cAMP PDE in striatum.
• Inhibitors: papaverine IC50 36 nM for PDE10A versus 1,300 nM PDE3A and 320 nM PDE4D (only ~9-36-fold selective - papaverine is a weak tool). PF-2545920 (MP-10) IC50 0.37 nM for PDE10A, roughly 100-fold more potent than papaverine and far more selective. PDE10A inhibition was pursued as an antipsychotic strategy (Menniti et al., Neuropharmacology 2006).
• Division of labour: Nishi et al., J Neurosci 2008, 28:10460, "Distinct Roles of PDE4 and PDE10A in the Regulation of cAMP/PKA Signaling in the Striatum" is the key primary reference for PDE4 versus PDE10A in MSNs (full text 403 in this session; cite by title/journal/year).


8. cAMP NANODOMAINS - THE MODERN REVISION OF THE "FREELY DIFFUSIBLE MESSENGER" PICTURE

REPORT THIS EXPLICITLY. It overturns the standard textbook account of cAMP as a small molecule that floods the cytosol.

The classical picture (Sutherland; every introductory textbook): cAMP is a small, freely diffusible second messenger that spreads rapidly and near-uniformly through the cytosol, so that any Gs-coupled receptor activates the whole cellular PKA pool. This is now known to be wrong in its essentials.

Bock et al., Cell 2020, 182(6):1519-1530.e17, "Optical Mapping of cAMP Signaling at the Nanometer Scale." DOI 10.1016/j.cell.2020.07.035. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33989549/ (there is a published Correction, Cell 2021, https://www.cell.com/cell/pdf/S0092-8674(21)00578-X.pdf)
• Using fluorescence spectroscopy (FCS-based), showed cAMP at physiological concentrations is PREDOMINANTLY BOUND to cAMP binding sites and therefore IMMOBILE.
• Coined the term "BUFFERED DIFFUSION": binding and unbinding to the large excess of cAMP-binding sites (PKA R subunits, Epac, PDE GAF domains, HCN channels) drastically reduces effective cAMP mobility.
• Consequence: because most cAMP is buffered, PDEs can create nanometre-sized domains of LOW cAMP concentration - note the inversion, the domains are sinks around PDEs, not just sources around cyclases.
• Demonstrated sharp concentration gradients translating directly into graded PKA activity, providing the mechanism for physiologically important specificity of cAMP signalling.

Anton et al., Cell 2022, "Receptor-associated independent cAMP nanodomains mediate spatiotemporal specificity of GPCR signaling." https://www.cell.com/cell/fulltext/S0092-8674(22)00191-X
• Introduced RAINs - Receptor-Associated Independent cAMP Nanodomains.
• At low agonist concentrations, an individual GPCR generates a localised cAMP pool extending UP TO 60 nm from the receptor.
• That pool translates directly into localised, receptor-associated PKA activity, constituting a SELF-SUFFICIENT AND INDEPENDENT SIGNALLING UNIT.
• Low concentrations of GLP-1 and of isoproterenol exclusively generate highly localised cAMP pools around the GLP-1 receptor and the beta2-adrenergic receptor respectively, PROTECTED from cAMP originating from other receptors and other cell compartments. Individual receptors of different types in the same cell do not share their cAMP.
• Engineered GPCR "nanorulers" mapped local cAMP and revealed gradients over only TENS OF NANOMETRES.

The single-molecule limit (2024 follow-up). "Hitting the Detection Limit in cAMP Signaling," https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11234644/
• Nanodomain radius 30-60 nm from the originating GPCR (GLP-1R, beta2AR).
• Hemispherical nanodomain volume ~0.5 x 10^-4 femtolitres.
• At 10 micromolar, such a volume contains APPROXIMATELY ONE cAMP MOLECULE generated upon GPCR stimulation.
• 3-5 cAMP molecules are required to simultaneously activate one or both PKA R-subunits plus a biosensor.
• Cardiac comparison: myofilament longitudinal spacing 38 nm, ~30 nm between nearby actin filaments, giving a comparable volume ~0.6 x 10^-4 femtolitres.
• The FRET sensor itself buffers 20-30% of the signal in this tightly regulated regime - i.e. the measuring device is a non-trivial perturbation at these molecule counts.
• This is a profound point for the book: local cAMP signalling operates at or near the SINGLE-MOLECULE detection limit, not in a bulk-concentration regime.

Zaccolo lab and the 2023-2025 consolidation
• Folkmanaite M and Zaccolo M, "Regulation of cardiac function by cAMP nanodomains," Bioscience Reports 2023, 43(2):BSR20220953. https://portlandpress.com/bioscirep/article/43/2/BSR20220953/232544/
• Lohse MJ, Bock A, Zaccolo M, "G Protein-Coupled Receptor Signaling: New Insights Define Cellular Nanodomains," Annual Review of Pharmacology and Toxicology 2024. https://www.annualreviews.org/content/journals/10.1146/annurev-pharmtox-040623-115054 (paywalled, 403 in session)
• Zaccolo et al., "Nanodomain cAMP signaling in cardiac pathophysiology," Physiological Reviews 2024/2025, DOI 10.1152/physrev.00013.2024, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39115424/
• "cAMP signaling: a remarkably regional affair," Trends in Biochemical Sciences 2024, S0968-0004(24)00018-5
• Yadav and Zaccolo, "GPCR signaling via cAMP nanodomains," Biochemical Journal 2025, 482(10):519. https://portlandpress.com/biochemj/article/482/10/519/236040/
• Phase separation as an additional buffering mechanism: "cAMP Buffering via Liquid-Liquid Phase Separation," PMC7812035, and Folkmanaite and Zaccolo, "Compartmentalisation in cAMP signalling: A phase separation perspective," British Journal of Pharmacology 2025, DOI 10.1111/bph.70145. Biomolecular condensates of PKA RIalpha act as cAMP sinks/buffers.
• Surdo et al., Nature Communications 2017, 8:15031, "FRET biosensor uncovers cAMP nano-domains at beta-adrenergic targets that dictate precise tuning of cardiac contractility" - the CUTie sensor targeted to plasmalemmal, sarcoplasmic-reticular and myofilament complexes revealed differential kinetics and amplitudes of local cAMP. Structures 300 nm apart show DISTINCT amplitude and kinetics. Differential local responses are exacerbated at low adrenergic drive and in heart-failure-mimicking conditions, with near-abolition of cAMP signalling at some sites.

Other spatial numbers
• Functional cAMP pool sizes estimated at 20-200 nm from lipid raft dimensions (Averaimo et al. 2016).
• PKA C-subunit radius of motion when AKAP-anchored: 15-25 nm (Smith et al. 2013). This is the same length scale as the nanodomains - the anchored kinase never leaves its domain.
• More than 30 distinct AKAPs identified.

Summary sentence for the book: cAMP is not a freely diffusible messenger. It is a heavily buffered messenger whose free pool is small, whose effective mobility is an order of magnitude below aqueous diffusion, and which in receptor-associated nanodomains is present at single-molecule copy numbers within 30-60 nm of the receptor that made it.


9. cAMP DIFFUSION COEFFICIENT - GENUINELY CONTESTED, SPANNING ~100-FOLD

State this as a controversy, not a fact. The full retrieved spread is 5 to 780 micrometres squared per second, roughly 150-fold.

Reference values in water
• 444 micrometres^2/s in aqueous solution - Dworkin and Keller 1977 (the value used as the reference in the myocyte tortuosity work)
• 330 to 970 micrometres^2/s quoted as the range for free aqueous cAMP in the Richards et al. review. SOURCES DISAGREE even on the aqueous value.

High historical intracellular values (the "freely diffusible" era)
• 780 micrometres^2/s - the top of the historically cited range
• 500 micrometres^2/s - Nikolaev et al. 2004, non-cardiac cells
• 330 micrometres^2/s - Huang and Gillette 1991, molluscan neuron
• 270 micrometres^2/s (2.7 +/- 0.2 x 10^-6 cm^2/s) - Chen and Nakamura, Biophysical Journal 1999, frog olfactory cilia. Note the cilium is a thin, nearly organelle-free tube - close to a best case for unhindered diffusion.
• 200 micrometres^2/s - Saucerman et al. 2006, NEONATAL cardiac myocytes
• 136 micrometres^2/s - Nikolaev et al. 2006, adult cardiac myocytes

Modern lower values
• ~120-130 micrometres^2/s - all-optical mapping in MDCK cells and cultured neurons, reported as "similar to other small molecules in cytoplasm" (All-optical mapping of cAMP transport reveals rules of sub-cellular localization, bioRxiv 2023.06.27.546633). NOTE: this paper argues cAMP diffuses relatively FREELY and is a genuine dissenting voice against strong buffering.
• 32.3 +/- 7.6 micrometres^2/s - ADULT rat ventricular myocyte cytoplasm at 37 C. Richards et al., "Intracellular tortuosity underlies slow cAMP diffusion in adult ventricular myocytes," https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4872880/. That is ~13.7-fold slower than the 444 aqueous value.
• 26.2 +/- 1.8 micrometres^2/s for a cAMP-fluorescein conjugate in adult myocytes; 3.4-fold faster in neonatal myocytes (~89 micrometres^2/s), tracking the 3-fold difference in non-mitochondrial space fraction.
• 9.7 +/- 1.6 micrometres^2/s in cardiac myocytes and 5.0 +/- 0.60 micrometres^2/s in HEK293 - Agarwal et al. 2016 with the caged phi450-cAMP probe. These are the lowest values retrieved.
• ~40 micrometres^2/s is the round figure adopted in the 2020 review PMC7770493, "approximately one order of magnitude lower than in water."

THE MECHANISM IS ITSELF DISPUTED - two competing explanations, from labs using different probes:
• BUFFERING (Bock et al. 2020, Zaccolo school): cAMP is mostly bound to PKA R subunits and other CNB-domain proteins, so apparent diffusion is slowed by repeated binding/unbinding - "buffered diffusion." Agarwal/Harvey data support this: disrupting RII-AKAP anchoring increased measured cAMP diffusion ~3-fold, from 9.7 to 32 +/- 4.5 micrometres^2/s. PDE activity alone was found insufficient for compartmentalisation.
• TORTUOSITY (Richards et al. 2016): physical barriers, principally mitochondrial density and ordering, not molecular buffering. Their decisive experiment: loading cells with 5 micromolar 6-Bnz-cAMP to saturate binding sites (shifting the apparent cAMP dissociation constant 3.02-fold) gave D_cAMP 29 +/- 7.1 micrometres^2/s, NOT significantly different from control (P = 0.45). Their conclusion, verbatim: "low cAMP diffusivity cannot be explained by buffering alone." Calcein, an inert dye, is also slowed by an order of magnitude in adult myocytes, supporting a purely geometric explanation.

HONEST STATUS: the mechanism is unresolved and the two camps use different cell types (adult ventricular myocyte versus HEK/neuron), different probes (caged compounds, FCS, FRET sensors) and different perturbations. Both mechanisms are probably operating, weighted differently by cell type - a cardiomyocyte at 30-40% mitochondrial volume is a tortuosity-dominated case; a HEK cell is not. What would settle it - the same probe and the same buffering-saturation perturbation applied side by side in cardiomyocyte, neuron and HEK, with independent measurement of total CNB-site density in each. For the book, give the range 5 to 780 micrometres^2/s, name the cell types, and say the modern consensus centre of mass is a few tens of micrometres^2/s in differentiated cells - roughly one order of magnitude below aqueous - while flagging the ~120-130 micrometres^2/s dissent.

Practical consequence worth stating: at D = 30 micrometres^2/s, the time to diffuse 60 nm (a RAIN radius) is on the order of tens of nanoseconds, whereas the time to cross a 20 micrometre cell is ~1-2 seconds. Slowed diffusion alone does not create nanodomains; degradation by PDEs plus buffering plus geometry does.


10. D1 VERSUS D2 DOPAMINE RECEPTORS - OPPOSITE COUPLING AND CONSEQUENCES

• D1-like family (D1, D5): couple to Gs and to G-olf, STIMULATING adenylyl cyclase, raising cAMP.
• D2-like family (D2, D3, D4): couple to Gi and Go, INHIBITING adenylyl cyclase, lowering cAMP. D2-like also liberate G-beta-gamma acting on GIRK channels and Cav channels.
• Same transmitter, opposite sign of the cAMP response, in adjacent cells. This is the cleanest example in the brain of receptor identity rather than transmitter identity determining the sign of a signal.

The G-olf twist (important and often omitted)
• In the DORSAL STRIATUM, D1-like receptors couple predominantly to G-olf, NOT Gs. G-olf is by far the most abundant stimulatory G-alpha in striatum and is the functional signalling G protein for D1R there. In cortex and elsewhere, D1 couples to Gs.
• In G-olf-alpha null mice, striatal D1 receptors show DECREASED AFFINITY FOR DOPAMINE. Zhuang et al., "G-olf-alpha mediates dopamine D1 receptor signaling," J Neurosci 2000, 20:RC91. https://www.jneurosci.org/content/jneuro/20/16/RC91.full.pdf
• G-olf-alpha levels are themselves regulated by receptor usage and control dopamine and adenosine action in striatum. Herve et al., J Neurosci 2001, 21:4390. https://www.jneurosci.org/content/21/12/4390
• Gs versus G-olf produce different functional selectivity at the D1 receptor. Nature Communications 2018, 9:art. https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-017-02606-w
• Historical note: Herve et al., "G-olf in the basal ganglia," 1991.

Convergence on AC5
• AC5 is the principal adenylyl cyclase isoform in striatal MSNs and is described as essential for integrating both the stimulatory (D1/G-olf, A2A/G-olf) and inhibitory (D2/Gi) midbrain signals. PMC6912275, "Identification of Novel Adenylyl Cyclase 5 (AC5) Signaling Networks in D1 and D2 Medium Spiny Neurons."
• AC5 is Ca2+-INHIBITED, so in MSNs the same enzyme integrates G-olf stimulation, Gi inhibition, G-beta-gamma, and calcium - a four-input coincidence detector.
• In D2-MSNs, A2A adenosine receptors (Gs/G-olf) oppose D2 (Gi) on the same AC5, via pre-coupled A2A-D2 heterotetramer-AC complexes. PMC5900444.

Circuit consequence
• D1-expressing MSNs form the DIRECT pathway, projecting to SNr and GPi, and promote movement. D2-expressing MSNs form the INDIRECT pathway, projecting to GPe, and suppress movement. Dopamine therefore has opponent effects on the two pathways purely because of receptor segregation. Frontiers in Synaptic Neuroscience 2022, "Segregation of D1 and D2 dopamine receptors in the striatal direct and indirect pathways: an historical perspective," https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/synaptic-neuroscience/articles/10.3389/fnsyn.2022.1002960/full and PMC3235731 (Dopaminergic modulation of striatal neurons, circuits and assemblies).
• Reward-signal framing: J Neurosci 2015, 35:14017, "Sensing Positive versus Negative Reward Signals through Adenylyl Cyclase-Coupled GPCRs in Direct and Indirect Pathway Striatal Medium Spiny Neurons." A dopamine DIP (reward omission) is a meaningful signal to D2-MSNs (disinhibition of AC5) but nearly invisible to D1-MSNs, because of the opposite coupling plus the different receptor affinities. This asymmetry is a good pedagogic payoff.
• Pharmacological hook: rolipram dynamically regulates the D1-MSN / D2-MSN balance in dorsomedial striatum to modulate "Go" behaviour in alcohol addiction. Acta Pharmacologica Sinica 2025, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41401-025-01738-w


11. DARPP-32 - THE SIGNAL INTEGRATOR

Primary sources retrieved: Yger and Girault, "DARPP-32, Jack of All Trades... Master of Which?", Frontiers in Behavioral Neuroscience 2011, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3168893/; Svenningsson et al., "DARPP-32: an integrator of neurotransmission," Annual Review of Pharmacology and Toxicology 2004, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/14744247/; Greengard's Nobel-era work; "DARPP-32 40 years later," https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33706939/

Identity and size
• Full name: Dopamine- and cAMP-Regulated PhosphoProtein, apparent Mr 32,000. Gene: PPP1R1B. Named from its apparent molecular weight on SDS-PAGE. Walaas, Aswad and Greengard 1983.
• 255 amino acids (human). Calculated mass ~28-29 kDa. The 32 kDa figure is APPARENT molecular weight only - DARPP-32 is an intrinsically disordered protein and runs anomalously slowly on gels. Say "apparent 32 kDa, calculated ~28-29 kDa" for accuracy.
• Abundance: 20-50 micromolar in striatal MSNs (Hemmings and Greengard 1986) - one of the most abundant phosphoproteins in these cells. Note this is comparable to or above the cAMP concentrations discussed above, which is why DARPP-32 is a potent amplifier of the PKA signal.

The two key phosphorylation sites and the bidirectional switch
• Thr-34: phosphorylated by PKA (and by cGMP-dependent protein kinase); dephosphorylated by calcineurin (PP2B) and PP2A. Phospho-Thr34-DARPP-32 is a POTENT INHIBITOR OF PROTEIN PHOSPHATASE-1 (PP1), with NANOMOLAR IC50. Unphosphorylated DARPP-32 or the Thr34Ala mutant inhibits PP1 only in the micromolar range (~1-10 micromolar) - so Thr34 phosphorylation buys roughly a 1000-fold gain in inhibitory potency. Inhibition is bidentate, via the KKIQF (K7-K8-I9-Q10-F11) canonical PP1-interacting motif plus the PpTP sequence; kinetics are mixed competitive and uncompetitive. Residues 8-38 are necessary and sufficient for PP1 inhibition.
• Thr-75: phosphorylated by Cdk5 (with p35); dephosphorylated by PP2A (B56-delta and PR72/B' regulatory subunits). Phospho-Thr75-DARPP-32 is an INHIBITOR OF PKA, with Ki = 2.7 micromolar.
• Additional sites: Ser-45 (CK2; facilitates PKA action on Thr-34), Ser-97 (CK2; regulates nuclear export), Ser-130 (CK1; inhibits calcineurin-mediated dephosphorylation of Thr-34). Note the numbering differs between rat and human in some papers - check species before quoting site numbers.

Why it is called an integrator
• DARPP-32 is a BIFUNCTIONAL, DUAL-FUNCTION protein: phospho-Thr34 makes it a PP1 inhibitor (amplifying PKA-driven phosphorylation of all PP1 substrates - AMPA and NMDA receptor subunits, Na/K-ATPase, CREB, ion channels); phospho-Thr75 makes it a PKA inhibitor (a negative feedback arm). The output of the DARPP-32 module depends on the BALANCE between Thr34 and Thr75 phosphorylation.
• Because PKA (dopamine D1, cAMP) drives Thr34 up while calcineurin (glutamate, Ca2+ influx via NMDA receptors) drives Thr34 down, DARPP-32 sits at the exact convergence of the dopaminergic and glutamatergic inputs to MSNs.
• Computational demonstration: Fernandez, Schiappa, Girault and Le Novere, "DARPP-32 Is a Robust Integrator of Dopamine and Glutamate Signals," PLOS Computational Biology 2006, 2(12):e176. https://journals.plos.org/ploscompbiol/article?id=10.1371/journal.pcbi.0020176. The model translates various states of glutamate and dopamine input into distinct steady states and dynamic responses, increasing the reliability of signal processing in MSNs.
• Essentially all psychoactive drugs that act in striatum - cocaine, amphetamine, antipsychotics, LSD, nicotine, ethanol, caffeine, opiates - converge on DARPP-32 Thr34 phosphorylation. This is Greengard's central claim and the reason DARPP-32 became a Nobel-associated molecule.
• Trauma/disease angle: PMC3110667 (DARPP-32 in traumatic brain injury); PMC4951238 (DARPP-32 from neurotransmission to cancer).


12. QUICK-REFERENCE NUMBER TABLE (value, range, source)

• Basal free cytosolic cAMP: 0.71-1.14 micromolar; consensus ~1 micromolar. Koschinski and Zaccolo, Sci Rep 2017.
• Stimulated cAMP: ~10 micromolar working figure; not firmly established as a global measurement.
• PKA activation EC50, in vitro: 0.3 micromolar (also 294 nM for holo-RIIalpha with Sp-8-Br-cAMPS).
• PKA activation EC50, in cell: 2.1 micromolar (endogenous), 5.2 micromolar (overexpressed). 7- to 17-fold higher than in vitro.
• PKA Hill coefficient: 1.7 (purified holoenzyme) to 2.2-3.0 (in cell). Range 1.4-3.0 across mutants and reporters.
• cAMP per PKA holoenzyme: 4.
• PKA concentration, cardiac myocyte: 0.5-1 micromolar. Buffering capacity up to 2 micromolar cAMP.
• G proteins per receptor: 6-8 directly counted (reconstituted beta-AR/Gs); ~50 kinetically inferred; 120-150 per second for rhodopsin (the ceiling).
• AC5 specific activity: basal ~50, Gs-stimulated ~200, forskolin ~250 nmol/min/mg; Vmax 410 nmol/min/mg unphosphorylated, 231 phosphorylated. Implies ~10^2-10^3 cAMP per enzyme per second.
• Forskolin EC50: 0.5 micromolar (AC1). Working concentration 10-25 micromolar.
• Rolipram: PDE4A 3 nM, PDE4B 130 nM, PDE4D 240 nM (contested). Working concentration 0.03-3 micromolar; 100 nM effective in neurons.
• PDE4 Km for cAMP: 1.5-3 micromolar. PDE4 = 70-80% of neuronal cAMP hydrolysis.
• PDE10A Km: cAMP 0.05-0.26 micromolar, cGMP 3-7.2 micromolar. Vmax ratio cGMP/cAMP 4.7.
• Papaverine IC50 PDE10A 36 nM; PF-2545920 (MP-10) IC50 0.37 nM.
• sAC apparent Km for Mg-ATP: &gt;10 mM.
• RAIN nanodomain radius: 30-60 nm. Volume ~0.5 x 10^-4 fL. ~1 cAMP molecule at 10 micromolar; 3-5 needed for activation.
• cAMP diffusion coefficient: aqueous 330-970 (commonly 444) micrometres^2/s; intracellular 5-780 micrometres^2/s across all sources; modern differentiated-cell values 5-40 micrometres^2/s; dissenting modern value 120-130 micrometres^2/s.
• AKAP-anchored PKA C-subunit radius of motion: 15-25 nm. &gt;30 AKAPs known.
• DARPP-32: 255 aa, calculated ~28-29 kDa, apparent 32 kDa; 20-50 micromolar in MSNs; pThr34 inhibits PP1 with nanomolar IC50 (unphosphorylated: micromolar); pThr75 inhibits PKA with Ki 2.7 micromolar.


13. WHERE THE EVIDENCE IS WEAKEST - FLAG THESE IN THE BOOK

• The stimulated free cAMP ceiling (the "10 micromolar" figure) is an assumption, not a measurement. Needs patch-calibrated FRET at saturating agonist.
• Mammalian adenylyl cyclase kcat in molecules per second is not cleanly reported in the accessible literature; the per-second figure here is derived from specific activity.
• Rolipram subtype IC50s vary with enzyme conformational state (high- versus low-affinity rolipram binding sites); single IC50 values are unreliable.
• PDE10A Km for cAMP: two primary 1999 papers differ 5-fold (0.05 versus 0.26 micromolar).
• cAMP diffusion coefficient: 150-fold spread, and the MECHANISM of slowing (buffering versus tortuosity) is actively contested, with a direct experimental contradiction (Richards et al. 2016 saturation experiment, P = 0.45, versus Bock et al. 2020 buffered diffusion).
• AC1/AC8 calcium EC50: no clean primary value retrieved; one secondary summary contained an evident units error (0.15 nM, should almost certainly be 0.15 micromolar).
• AC9 forskolin sensitivity: the textbook "forskolin activates all TM ACs except AC9" is now known to be an oversimplification (Nature Communications 2022).
• The 100-million-fold amplification figure: misattributed provenance, and incompatible with the single-molecule nanodomain arithmetic. Should be retired or heavily caveated.</result>
