SODIUM AND POTASSIUM AS ELEMENTS - WEB-VERIFIED QUANTITATIVE FINDINGS
Access date 2026-08-12. Every number below is marked VERIFIED (retrieved from a primary or high-quality secondary source I actually fetched) or UNVERIFIED (search-level only - do not print as fact).

=====================================================================
1. IONIC RADII
=====================================================================

PRIMARY SOURCE, FULLY VERIFIED. Shannon RD (1976), "Revised effective ionic radii and systematic studies of interatomic distances in halides and chalcogenides," Acta Crystallographica Section A 32(5):751-767, DOI 10.1107/S0567739476001551. I retrieved the full PDF and read Table 1 directly (pages 752-753 of the paper).
https://journals.iucr.org/paper?a12967= ; https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1107/S0567739476001551 ; open PDF: https://pxrd.ucoz.ru/Radios_ionicos_de_Shannon_5291.pdf

Shannon Table 1, Na+ (electron configuration 2p6). Columns are CR (crystal radius) then IR (effective ionic radius), Angstroms:
• CN 4 - CR 1.13, IR 0.99
• CN 5 - CR 1.14, IR 1.00
• CN 6 - CR 1.16, IR 1.02
• CN 7 - CR 1.26, IR 1.12
• CN 8 - CR 1.32, IR 1.18
• CN 9 - CR 1.38, IR 1.24
• CN 12 - CR 1.53, IR 1.39

Shannon Table 1, K+ (electron configuration 3p6):
• CN 4 - CR 1.51, IR 1.37
• CN 6 - CR 1.52, IR 1.38
• CN 7 - CR 1.60, IR 1.46
• CN 8 - CR 1.65, IR 1.51
• CN 9 - CR 1.69, IR 1.55
• CN 10 - CR 1.73, IR 1.59
• CN 12 - CR 1.78, IR 1.64

ALL FOUR VALUES IN THE BRIEF ARE CONFIRMED EXACTLY: Na+ CN6 = 1.02 A, Na+ CN8 = 1.18 A, K+ CN6 = 1.38 A, K+ CN8 = 1.51 A.

INDEPENDENT SECOND CONFIRMATION. The CRC Handbook table "Ionic Radii in Crystals" (section 12-11/12-12), which cites Shannon 1976 as reference 1, lists identically: Na+ CN4 0.99, CN6 1.02, CN8 1.18, CN9 1.24, CN12 1.39; K+ CN4 1.37, CN6 1.38, CN8 1.51, CN12 1.64. Fetched from https://bionumbers.hms.harvard.edu/files/Ionic%20Radii%20in%20Crystals.pdf

CRITICAL TECHNICAL POINT THE BOOK MUST GET RIGHT. Shannon publishes TWO scales, and they differ by a constant 0.14 A for cations. Verbatim from page 759 of the paper: "In Table 1 two sets of radii are included. The first is a set of traditional radii based on r(VI O2-) = 1.40 A. The other set is based on r(VI O2-) = 1.26 and r(VI F-) = 1.19 A, and corresponds to crystal radii as defined by Fumi &amp; Tosi (1964)." And: "crystal radii differ from traditional radii only by a constant factor of 0.14 A." Shannon states his own preference against the popular one - "it is felt that crystal radii correspond more closely to the physical size of ions in a solid" - but retains the traditional (effective ionic radius, IR) values "because of their familiarity to crystal chemists and physicists." The numbers everyone quotes, including the 1.02 and 1.38 in the brief, are the IR / traditional set. The Na+/K+ DIFFERENCE is the same on both scales (0.36 A), which is what matters biophysically.

Na+/K+ radius difference, Shannon CN6: 1.38 - 1.02 = 0.36 A. On the Pauling scale it is 1.33 - 0.95 = 0.38 A. Both are commonly quoted; the ~0.38 A figure that appears throughout the channel-selectivity literature is the Pauling difference. State which scale you mean.

PAULING RADII - PARTIALLY VERIFIED, ONE GAP.
The specific values Na+ 0.95 A and K+ 1.33 A (Pauling LC, "The Nature of the Chemical Bond," 3rd ed., Cornell University Press, 1960; original radii Pauling 1927) are the standard textbook figures and are used throughout the ion-channel literature, but I could NOT confirm them against a primary or reliably-fetched source. Search returned an educational table giving "Pauling" values identical to Shannon's (Li+ 0.74, Na+ 1.02, K+ 1.38, Rb+ 1.52, Cs+ 1.67), which is a misattribution common in teaching materials - those are Shannon CN6 numbers. FLAG THIS: print Na+ 0.95 / K+ 1.33 with the Pauling 1960 citation but confirm against a physical copy before publication.

WHAT IS VERIFIED about the Pauling-versus-Shannon discrepancy, from Shannon's own text, page 765, retrieved directly - this is the better thing to cite anyway because it explains WHY they differ:
• Verbatim: "Covalence effects are useful in explaining certain differences between the effective ionic radii of Table 1 and the ionic radii of Pauling (1927) and Ahrens (1952). Pauling's radii for Cu+ (0.96 A) and Ag+ (1.26 A) are considerably larger than those in Table 1 (0.77 and 1.15 A respectively). Since these radii were derived from comparison of alkali halide distances, using an equation relating effective nuclear charge and screening constants (Pauling, 1927), they are valid in primarily ionic crystals. The smaller radii in Table 1 are applicable in the more covalent oxides."
• So the difference is not error but scope: Pauling's radii are derived from alkali halide interatomic distances via a theoretical effective-nuclear-charge argument; Shannon's are empirical, fitted to a very large body of measured distances, and weighted toward oxides. Shannon's own footnote, page 753: "Because of covalency differences in M-O and M-F bonds, oxide distances were emphasized. Therefore the radii in Table 1 are more applicable to oxides than to fluorides."
• Say explicitly in the book: Pauling radii and Shannon radii are DIFFERENT QUANTITIES on different scales, not competing measurements of one quantity. Never mix them in a single comparison.

ADDITIONAL VERIFIED DETAIL, useful and rarely quoted. Shannon page 760, verbatim: "Notable exceptions are IVNa+, VNa+, and IVK+. It is apparent that Na-O and K-O distances do not decrease as much as anticipated from the r-CN curve when the CN falls below six." Footnote: "Extrapolation of the Na curve gives r(IVNa+) = 0.90 A." This is why Na+ CN4 (0.99) is only 0.03 A smaller than Na+ CN6 (1.02), and K+ CN4 (1.37) only 0.01 A smaller than K+ CN6 (1.38) - anomalously flat. Shannon attributes it to reduced repulsive forces from fewer ligands, and states the Pauling repulsion expression "is not valid for four-coordinated Na+ or K+." Relevant to channel work, since filter coordination numbers are in this regime.

Also verified: Shannon states values marked with an asterisk in Table 1 are those where "at least five structural determinations resulted in radii differing by no more than +/- 0.01 A." So the practical precision floor on these radii is about 0.01 A, an order of magnitude smaller than the Na+/K+ difference - the radii themselves are not the uncertain part of the selectivity story.

=====================================================================
2. HYDRATION FREE ENERGIES
=====================================================================

THIS IS THE AREA WHERE SOURCES DISAGREE MOST, AND THE DISAGREEMENT IS LARGE AND SYSTEMATIC. Do not print a single value.

PRIMARY SOURCE, CITATION VERIFIED BUT TABLE NOT RETRIEVED. Marcus Y (1991), "Thermodynamics of solvation of ions. Part 5 - Gibbs free energy of hydration at 298.15 K," Journal of the Chemical Society Faraday Transactions 87(18):2995-2999, DOI 10.1039/FT9918702995. Compiles standard molar Gibbs free energies of hydration for 109 mainly inorganic ions, charges -3 to +4.
https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlelanding/1991/ft/ft9918702995 ; https://cris.huji.ac.il/en/publications/thermodynamics-of-solvation-of-ions-part-5-gibbs-free-energy-of-h/
I attempted the open PDF mirror at https://dasher.wustl.edu/chem478/labs/lab-09/jcs-faraday-87-2995-91.pdf twice; it returned HTTP 500 both times. The RSC copy is paywalled. So the specific Marcus values Na+ -365 kJ/mol and K+ -295 kJ/mol are UNVERIFIED at primary-source level (search-level corroboration only). They are, however, bracketed tightly by an independently verified compilation - see next.

VERIFIED INDEPENDENT COMPILATION. Joung IS, Cheatham TE III (2008), "Determination of alkali and halide monovalent ion parameters for use in explicitly solvated biomolecular simulations," J Phys Chem B 112(30):9020-9041, PMID 18593145. Fetched full text at https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2652252/ . Their adopted experimental targets, from Schmid et al. (2000):
• Li+  -113.8 kcal/mol  =  -476.1 kJ/mol
• Na+  -88.7  kcal/mol  =  -371.1 kJ/mol
• K+   -71.2  kcal/mol  =  -297.9 kJ/mol
• Rb+  -66.0  kcal/mol  =  -276.1 kJ/mol
• Cs+  -60.5  kcal/mol  =  -253.1 kJ/mol
(kJ conversions mine, 1 kcal = 4.184 kJ.)
These sit within about 2 percent of the -365 and -295 kJ/mol figures in the brief. So the brief's numbers are the right order and very nearly the right magnitude, and can be safely stated as "approximately -365 to -371 kJ/mol for Na+ and -295 to -298 kJ/mol for K+ on the conventional/Marcus-Schmid scale."

THE SPREAD, AND ITS CAUSE - THIS IS THE IMPORTANT PART.
Joung &amp; Cheatham, verbatim: "The hydration free energies fall into two groups with a constant ~12-15 kcal/mol difference in the overall free energies between the two groups." And: "This offset is largely determined by the estimation of the hydration free energy of the proton; the relative free energies among the monovalent cations or among the monovalent anions are mostly consistent." They rejected the Marcus values in favour of Schmid for their final parametrisation. Halide disagreement between the two compilations averaged about 8 kcal/mol.

The offset arises because single-ion hydration free energies are not directly measurable. Only neutral combinations (a salt, or KOH) are thermodynamically unambiguous. Splitting a salt's measured value into cation and anion parts requires assuming an absolute value for the proton, delta-hyd-G(H+), and different compilations assume different ones. Verified magnitudes, from https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11881599/ (fetched):
• Tissandier et al. convention: delta-hyd-G(H+) = -1104.5 kJ/mol
• CRC convention: delta-hyd-G(H+) = -1516.9 kJ/mol
• Offset between them: approximately 412.4 kJ/mol per unit charge
That same source tabulates, on the two conventions: Na+ -836.11 kJ/mol (CRC) versus -423.71 kJ/mol (Tissandier); K+ -764.28 kJ/mol (CRC) versus -351.88 kJ/mol (Tissandier). These look wildly different from the Marcus/Schmid numbers above because they are on a different standard-state and proton convention entirely. WARN THE READER: a hydration free energy quoted without its proton convention and standard state is meaningless.

A CLEAN, CONCRETE ILLUSTRATION OF THE DISAGREEMENT, FULLY VERIFIED. Rempe SB, Asthagiri D, Pratt LR (2003/2004), "Inner shell definition and absolute hydration free energy of K+(aq) on the basis of quasi-chemical theory and ab initio molecular dynamics," arXiv physics/0310158, LA-UR-03-8005 (published Phys Chem Chem Phys). I read the PDF directly. Verbatim from page 6: "The hydration free energy of K+, using the classical molecular dynamics calculation for the outer-shell contribution, is -70.5 +/- 2.1 kcal/mole for transfer of the solute from 1 mol (ideal gas) to 1 mol (ideally diluted solute) solution... the agreement between our absolute hydration free energies and the values of Coe and coworkers (-86 kcal/mole) is poor. The sign and magnitude of this discrepancy is in line with discrepancies already identified for H+, Li+, and Na+ and further validates our suggestion that the absolute hydration free energies estimated by Coe and coworkers contain a negative contribution from the potential of the phase."
• That is a 15.5 kcal/mol (65 kJ/mol) disagreement for K+ ALONE, between a quasi-chemical/AIMD estimate and the Tissandier-Coe cluster-pair experimental scale. "Coe and coworkers" is the Tissandier et al. 1998 lineage - Tissandier MD, Cowen KA, Feng WY, Gundlach E, Cohen MH, Earhart AD, Coe JV, Tuttle TR (1998), "The proton's absolute aqueous enthalpy and Gibbs free energy of solvation from cluster-ion solvation data," J Phys Chem A 102(40):7787-7794.
• Note the same paper's demonstration that the NEUTRAL combination is unambiguous: their computed KOH hydration free energy, -194.3 +/- 2.1 kcal/mol, agrees well with the experimental -191 kcal/mol from Coe et al. The single-ion split is where the disagreement lives, not the underlying thermodynamics.
• Related, and the reason this is still live: Duignan, Baer, Schenter, Mundy et al. (2020), "Absolute ion hydration free energy scale and the surface potential of water via quantum simulation," PNAS 117, https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.2017214117 and https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7720161/ - the surface potential of water term is still being argued about in 2020.

WHAT IS ROBUST DESPITE ALL THIS. The DIFFERENCE between Na+ and K+ is convention-independent, because the proton offset is identical for two ions of the same charge and cancels exactly. This is the number the channel field actually uses, and the book should lead with it rather than with the absolutes:
• From Schmid values (verified): -88.7 - (-71.2) = -17.5 kcal/mol = -73.2 kJ/mol. Na+ is more strongly hydrated than K+ by about 17.5 kcal/mol.
• From Marcus values as quoted in the brief: -365 - (-295) = -70 kJ/mol = -16.7 kcal/mol. Consistent.
• Independent confirmation from the channel literature: the eLife TMEM175 paper (Oh, Lee et al. 2022, eLife 11:e75122, https://elifesciences.org/articles/75122) states "the penalty for full dehydration is ~18 kcal/mol greater" for Na+ than for K+.
• So: Na+ costs roughly 17 to 18 kcal/mol (about 70 to 75 kJ/mol) MORE to strip of its water than K+ does. Quote this as approximately 17-18 kcal/mol with the range, not a point value.
• Scale check for the reader: thermal energy RT at 310 K is about 0.62 kcal/mol, so the Na+/K+ dehydration difference is roughly 28 RT. This is the energy the channel filter must repay if it is to conduct Na+, and it is why selectivity is possible at all.

RECOMMENDED WORDING. Absolute single-ion hydration free energies are not measurable and differ between compilations by 12-15 kcal/mol (about 50-63 kJ/mol) or, on some conventions, by over 400 kJ/mol; the discrepancy is entirely in the assumed absolute proton hydration free energy and the water surface-potential term, not in the underlying measurements. The Na+ minus K+ difference, about -17 to -18 kcal/mol, is convention-independent and is the physically meaningful quantity. WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: an agreed, independently determined value for the surface potential of water and hence for delta-hyd-G(H+); the 2020 PNAS quantum-simulation work is an attempt at exactly this and has not yet produced consensus.

=====================================================================
3. K+ CHANNEL SELECTIVITY FILTER - TVGYG AND THE SELECTIVITY RATIO
=====================================================================

SIGNATURE SEQUENCE - VERIFIED. Doyle DA, Morais Cabral J, Pfuetzner RA, Kuo A, Gulbis JM, Cohen SL, Chait BT, MacKinnon R (1998), "The structure of the potassium channel: molecular basis of K+ conduction and selectivity," Science 280(5360):69-77, DOI 10.1126/science.280.5360.69. Full PDF retrieved from https://lab.rockefeller.edu/chait/pdf/98/98_doyle_science.pdf ; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9525859/
• Figure 8 legend states verbatim that the chains shown "are comprised of the signature sequence amino acids Thr, Val, Gly, Tyr, Gly." Body text refers to "the V-G-Y-G sequence" and names Thr75, Val76, Gly77 and Tyr78 explicitly by number. Gly79 follows from the sequence and is universal usage but is not spelled out numerically in the retrieved passages. KcsA filter = Thr75-Val76-Gly77-Tyr78-Gly79.
• Abstract, verbatim: "Main chain carbonyl oxygen atoms from the K+ channel signature sequence line the selectivity filter, which is held open by structural constraints to co-ordinate K+ ions but not smaller Na+ ions." Filter length 12 A. Side-chain positions "preclude their direct participation in ion coordination, leaving this function to the main chain atoms." Throughput "up to 10^8 per second."
• CAUTION - the signature sequence is EIGHT residues, not five. Heginbotham L, Lu Z, Abramson T, MacKinnon R (1994), "Mutations in the K+ channel signature sequence," Biophysical Journal 66(4):1061-1067, DOI 10.1016/S0006-3495(94)80887-2, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8038378/ - abstract verbatim: "Potassium channels share a highly conserved stretch of eight amino acids, a K+ channel signature sequence." That is the TxxTxGYG consensus. TVGYG is its C-terminal five.
• MAJOR CORRECTION TO A COMMON TEXTBOOK CLAIM, VERIFIED. Heginbotham et al. 1994 found that "nonconservative substitutions of two threonine residues and the tyrosine residue leave selectivity intact," concluding that "hydroxyl groups at the third and fourth positions, and the aromatic group at position seven, are not essential in determining K+ selectivity." The tyrosine of GYG is NOT required for selectivity in Shaker. Do not write that it is.
• Variant filters: KcsA, Shaker and Kir carry TVGYG; hERG (Kv11.1) carries GFG not GYG, written SVGFG. VERIFIED at review level only - Frontiers in Physiology 2021, "Structural plasticity of the selectivity filter in cation channels," https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/physiology/articles/10.3389/fphys.2021.792958/full . The hERG residue number F627 and the Shaker numbering T439-G446 are UNVERIFIED (search-level only) - confirm before printing.

THE "&gt;1000:1" CLAIM - THIS IS NOT A MEASUREMENT. Three different quantities get conflated. Handle carefully.
• Doyle et al. 1998, introduction, verbatim: "Potassium is at least 10,000 times more permeant than Na+, a feature that is essential to the function of K+ channels," and "The 10^4 margin by which K+ is selected over Na+..." Note "at least." Doyle et al. report NO measurement of their own; their cited authority is Hille's textbook (Ionic Channels of Excitable Membranes, 2nd ed., Sinauer, 1992).
• Hille B (1973), "Potassium channels in myelinated nerve. Selective permeability to small cations," J Gen Physiol 61(6):669-686, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/4541077/ - measured permeability ratios from reversal potentials in frog myelinated nerve: P(Tl):P(K):P(Rb):P(NH4) = 2.3 : 1.00 : 0.92 : 0.13. Na+, Li+ and Cs+ were NOT MEASURABLY PERMEANT, i.e. below the detection floor, commonly quoted as P(Na)/P(K) less than 0.01. Hille's datum is therefore a lower bound of roughly 100:1, not 1000:1 and not 10,000:1. Hille also proposed there that the narrowest part of the K pore is a ring of oxygens about 3 A across.
• BEST DIRECTLY MEASURED VALUE FOR THE STRUCTURALLY DEFINED CHANNEL. LeMasurier M, Heginbotham L, Miller C (2001), "KcsA: it's a potassium channel," J Gen Physiol 118(3):303-314, DOI 10.1085/jgp.118.3.303, PMID 11524460, https://rupress.org/jgp/article/118/3/303/42440/ - abstract verbatim: "Selectivity sequences for permeant ions were determined by symmetrical solution conductance (K+ &gt; Rb+, NH4+, Tl+ &gt;&gt; Cs+, Na+, Li+) and by reversal potentials under bi-ionic or mixed-ion conditions (Tl+ &gt; K+ &gt; Rb+ &gt; NH4+ &gt;&gt; Na+, Li+). Determination of reversal potentials with submillivolt accuracy shows that K+ is over 150-fold more permeant than Na+."
• Egwolf B, Roux B (2010), "Ion selectivity of the KcsA channel: a perspective from multi-ion free energy landscapes," J Mol Biol, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3163611/ - fetched, verbatim confirmation: "the K+/Na+ permeability ratio is greater than 150," which "can be translated into a free energy for Na+ in the pore that is unfavorable relative to K+ by at least 3 kcal/mol minimum, but it could be more." Their computed barrier for Na+ at site S2 is about 5-6 kcal/mol. They also state plainly that "characterizing the selectivity of a very selective channel quantitatively using this method can be difficult."
• THE THREE QUANTITIES, KEEP THEM SEPARATE:
◦ (a) Permeability ratio P_K/P_Na from bi-ionic reversal potentials. This is what Hille 1973 and LeMasurier 2001 report. It SATURATES - once P_Na is small the reversal potential stops shifting - so the experiment can only ever return a lower bound. Every careful source writes "greater than."
◦ (b) Conductance ratio in symmetrical salt. Different sequence: Tl+ ranks top by reversal potential but far down by conductance. Quoting a conductance ratio as "the selectivity ratio" is a category error.
◦ (c) Na+ is a BLOCKER as well as a poor permeant, which contaminates both. Nimigean CM, Miller C (2002), "Na+ block and permeation in a K+ channel of known structure," J Gen Physiol 120(3):323-335, https://rupress.org/jgp/article/120/3/323/44406/ - intracellular Na+ produces voltage-dependent block relieved at high voltage by "punchthrough"; the blocking site is in the hydrated cavity, not the filter.
◦ Mechanistic corollary, VERIFIED: Thompson AN, Kim I, Panosian TD, Iverson TM, Allen TW, Nimigean CM (2009), "Mechanism of potassium-channel selectivity revealed by Na+ and Li+ binding sites within the KcsA pore," Nat Struct Mol Biol 16:1317-1324, https://www.nature.com/articles/nsmb.1703 - selectivity is NOT a higher binding affinity for K+; it is an energy BARRIER blocking Na+ and Li+ entry in the presence of K+. Na+ and Li+ are found at PLANES of carbonyl oxygens rather than in the canonical cage sites.
• RECOMMENDED WORDING FOR THE BOOK: "&gt;1000:1" is a rounded assertion, not a measurement. The defensible statements are: P_Na/P_K less than 0.01 in frog node (Hille 1973, a detection-limit bound); P_K/P_Na greater than 150 for KcsA (LeMasurier et al. 2001, explicitly a bound set by submillivolt reversal-potential precision); "at least 10,000-fold" asserted without measurement by Doyle et al. 1998. A recent review (Lee et al. 2025, Function 6(6):zqaf050, https://academic.oup.com/function/article/6/6/zqaf050/8307500) gives the range as 100-1000:1. Always name the quantity (permeability ratio from reversal potential) alongside any number. WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT: nothing currently can - the measurement method is intrinsically bounded. A true ratio would need a technique sensitive to Na+ flux at the 10^-3 to 10^-4 level, e.g. radiotracer flux or single-channel counting of Na+ events.

BINDING SITES AND OCCUPANCY - VERIFIED, AND A COMMON MISATTRIBUTION TO CORRECT.
• The four-site picture is NOT in the 1998 paper. Doyle et al. 1998, verbatim: "The selectivity filter contains two ions (inner and outer ions) located at opposite ends, about 7.5 A apart (center to center)," plus "a third weaker peak... below the selectivity filter at the center of the large cavity." One water modelled between the two filter K+.
• The four sites come from Zhou Y, Morais-Cabral JH, Kaufman A, MacKinnon R (2001), "Chemistry of ion coordination and hydration revealed by a K+ channel-Fab complex at 2.0 A resolution," Nature 414(6859):43-48, DOI 10.1038/35102009, PMID 11689936, PDB 1K4C. Abstract verbatim: the channel holds K+ "in a square antiprism of water molecules in a cavity near its intracellular entryway," and "carbonyl oxygen atoms within the selectivity filter form a very similar square antiprism around each K+ binding site, as if to mimic the waters of hydration."
• S1 (outermost) to S4 (innermost), formed by backbone carbonyls plus the Thr75 side-chain hydroxyl at S4. Beyond the filter, three more cation positions are resolved - S0 at the outer mouth, Sext further out, Scav in the cavity - giving seven total in that structure. "Four sites" and "seven sites" are counting different things, not disagreeing.
• Occupancy - Zhou Y, MacKinnon R (2003), J Mol Biol 333(5):965-975, PMID 14583193: "the average occupancy for each site in the selectivity filter is about 0.63 for Tl+ and 0.53 for K+," with about TWO ions total across the four filter sites. This reconciles the 1998 two-ion picture with the 2001 four-site picture - four sites, half occupied on time average.
• Morais-Cabral JH, Zhou Y, MacKinnon R (2001), "Energetic optimization of ion conduction rate by the K+ selectivity filter," Nature 414(6859):37-42, PMID 11689935: the filter alternates between the 1,3 configuration (ions at S1 and S3) and the 2,4 configuration (S2 and S4), separated by water, until a third ion knocks the far ion out. Key quantitative result: for K+ the free-energy difference between the two configurations is close to ZERO, which is the condition for maximum conduction rate. One K+ dehydrates, transfers and rehydrates on a timescale of order 10 nanoseconds.

=====================================================================
4. THE MECHANISTIC DEBATE - SNUG FIT VERSUS FIELD STRENGTH, AND KNOCK-ON
=====================================================================
This is a live controversy. The book must not take a side it cannot support.

4a. SNUG FIT / RIGID CAGE - the original position.
• Functional root: Bezanilla F, Armstrong CM (1972), "Negative conductance caused by entry of sodium and cesium ions into the potassium channels of squid axons," J Gen Physiol 60(5):588-608, https://rupress.org/jgp/article-abstract/60/5/588/31156/ - internal Cs+, Na+, Li+ interfere with outward K current; 100 mM NaF halved outward current at large depolarisations, producing negative conductance. Pre-structural evidence for a narrow, dimensionally specific tube that small ions enter but cannot traverse.
• Structural statement: Doyle et al. 1998 (above). Filter carbonyl oxygens are positioned to mimic the hydration shell of K+, so K+ pays little net cost swapping water oxygens for carbonyl oxygens, whereas the filter cannot equivalently coordinate the smaller Na+. The abstract's phrase "held open by structural constraints" is the load-bearing one.
• As its critics define it (Nimigean CM, Allen TW 2011, J Gen Physiol 137(5):405-413, https://rupress.org/jgp/article/137/5/405/42920/): the crystallographic K+ sites are cages of carbonyl ligands apparently of the right dimension for K+, which cannot effectively coordinate the smaller Na+, making Na+ binding thermodynamically unfavourable.

4b. THE THERMODYNAMIC CRITIQUE.
• Noskov SY, Berneche S, Roux B (2004), "Control of ion selectivity in potassium channels by electrostatic and dynamic properties of carbonyl ligands," Nature 431(7010):830-834, DOI 10.1038/nature02943, PMID 15483608, https://www.nature.com/articles/nature02943 . MD of KcsA shows the coordinating carbonyls are highly dynamic - their word is "liquid-like" - and that the INTRINSIC ELECTROSTATIC PROPERTIES of the ligands, not a fixed cage geometry, control selectivity.
• The objection stated plainly, and the alternative, from Roux B, Berneche S, Egwolf B, Lev B, Noskov SY, Rowley CN, Yu H (2011), "Ion selectivity in channels and transporters," J Gen Physiol 137(5):415-426, DOI 10.1085/jgp.201010577, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3082929/ - VERBATIM: "the fluctuating filter appeared to distort easily to cradle Na+ with little energetic cost," and "robust free energy differences controlling selectivity were observed to arise principally from the number and type of ion-coordinating ligands."
• THE CORE NUMERICAL ARGUMENT, which the book should state because it is simple and decisive: filter atomic fluctuations are of order 1 A, which is LARGER than the Na+/K+ radius difference of about 0.36-0.38 A. A mechanism requiring sub-angstrom geometric precision cannot survive thermal motion of that amplitude.
• Proposed replacement mechanism: a balance of favourable ion-ligand attraction against unfavourable ligand-ligand repulsion in a "confined microdroplet." Carbonyl ligands have high effective field strength (large dipole) unlike water, which is why a site built of carbonyls can be K+-selective where a site built of water is not.
• IMPORTANT CAVEAT THE AUTHORS THEMSELVES ADD - carry it, because "liquid-like" is routinely misquoted as "disordered": they clarify the term means fluctuations consistent with the Lindemann melting criterion, about 10-15 percent of neighbour distance, and they stress the protein architecture remains ESSENTIAL for spatial confinement even though exact geometry is not.

4c. THE OTHER CANDIDATE MECHANISMS - there are four, not two.
• Coordination number / over-coordination. Bostick DL, Brooks CL III (2007), "The predominant role of coordination number in potassium channel selectivity," Biophysical Journal, https://www.cell.com/biophysj/biophysj/fulltext/S0006-3495(07)71518-7 ; and PMID 19450462. THE SINGLE MOST HONEST SENTENCE IN THE WHOLE LITERATURE, verbatim from that work: their results are incompatible with the strong variant of snug fit, are consistent with an over-coordination hypothesis, and "neither confirm nor refute the field-strength hypothesis."
• Topological / architectural control. Varma S, Rempe SB (2007), "Tuning ion coordination architectures to enable selective partitioning" and "Selectivity in K+ channels is due to topological control of the permeant ion's coordinated state," PNAS, DOI 10.1073/pnas.0700554104, https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.0700554104 - neither pore size NOR intrinsic carbonyl dipole is the main discriminator; selectivity follows from architectural constraints on how many carbonyls can coordinate the ion simultaneously.
• NUMBER OF CONTIGUOUS SITES - the strongest experimental result in the whole debate, and it cuts against BOTH main camps. Alam A, Jiang Y, J Gen Physiol 2011, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3082925/ ; Nat Struct Mol Biol 2009, https://www.nature.com/articles/nsmb.1537 . NaK (two sites) is non-selective; NaK2CNG chimeras (three sites) remain non-selective; NaK2K, engineered to four sites identical to K+ channels, becomes highly K+/Na+ selective. Their words: this is "a key determinant, hitherto overlooked or otherwise unexplained by both the classical snug-fit model as well as current theoretical models," and "a simple energetic picture of snug fitting is not adequate to explain selectivity, and kinetic factors also play important roles." Note they ALSO say their individual site geometries "seem to favor the classical snug-fit model" - so this is not a clean win for anyone.
• Chemical perturbation tests. Valiyaveetil FI, Leonetti M, Muir TW, MacKinnon R (2006), "Ion selectivity in a semisynthetic K+ channel locked in the conductive conformation," Science, DOI 10.1126/science.1133415, PMID 17095703. Amide-to-ester backbone substitution removes a backbone NH donor while keeping the carbonyl, perturbing site 1 specifically; filter structure largely unperturbed, an ordered buried water is lost, K+/Rb+/Cs+ distribution and conduction change. Follow-ups: PNAS 2013 DOI 10.1073/pnas.1314356110 (ion occupancy and C-type inactivation); PNAS 2017 DOI 10.1073/pnas.1706983114. Significance: the filter is chemically tunable and locally perturbable WITHOUT collapse - selectivity is not a single rigid geometric switch.
• The 2011 J Gen Physiol "Perspectives on Ion Selectivity" series, edited by Andersen OS (137(5):393-395, https://rupress.org/jgp/article/137/5/393/42907/), is the best single place to see the protagonists argue side by side. Contributions: Nimigean &amp; Allen 137:405-413; Roux et al. 137:415-426; Dixit PD &amp; Asthagiri D 137:427-433 (quasi-chemical); Alam &amp; Jiang; Varma et al.; and "Design principles for K+ selectivity in membrane transport," 137(6):479-488. NOTE: rupress.org returns HTTP 403 to automated fetching, so these rest on journal listing pages and search metadata rather than retrieved full text.

4d. THE CONDUCTION MECHANISM - DIRECT VERSUS SOFT KNOCK-ON.
Definitions: SOFT knock-on = K+ ions in the filter separated by water, water co-permeates. DIRECT knock-on = water-free permeation, direct K+-K+ contacts, driven by Coulomb repulsion.
• Soft knock-on origin: Morais-Cabral et al. 2001 and Zhou et al. 2001 (above). The crystallographic density was INTERPRETED as a superposition of alternating K-water-K-water configurations, on the reasoning that two K+ in direct contact would be electrostatically prohibitive. That interpretive step is exactly what is now contested.
• Direct knock-on challenge: Kopfer DA, Song C, Gruene T, Sheldrick GM, Zachariae U, de Groot BL (2014), "Ion permeation in K+ channels occurs by direct Coulomb knock-on," Science 346(6207):352-355, DOI 10.1126/science.1254840, PMID 25324389, https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1254840 , open PDF http://www3.mpibpc.mpg.de/groups/de_groot/pdf/Kopfer_Science_2014.pdf . Over 1300 permeation events in applied-field MD; permeation via direct ion-ion contacts, Coulomb repulsion between adjacent K+ identified as the key to near-diffusion-limited conduction. Science commentary "Potassium ions line up," DOI 10.1126/science.1260555.
• Extension to selectivity: Kopec W, Kopfer DA, Vickery ON, Bondarenko AS, Jansen TLC, de Groot BL, Zachariae U (2018), "Direct knock-on of desolvated ions governs strict ion selectivity in K+ channels," Nature Chemistry 10(8):813-820, DOI 10.1038/s41557-018-0105-9, PMID 30030538. Over 400 microseconds of simulation, over 7000 permeation events; claims fast conduction AND K+/Na+ selectivity both follow from one principle. This is a strong unification claim - flag that not everyone accepts the premise, and note that if soft knock-on occurs at all, the unification fails as stated.
• EXPERIMENT SUPPORTING WATER-FREE: Oster C, Hendriks K, Kopec W, Chevelkov V, Shi C, Michl D, Lange S, Sun H, de Groot BL, Lange A (2019), "The conduction pathway of potassium channels is water free under physiological conditions," Science Advances 5(7):eaaw6756, DOI 10.1126/sciadv.aaw6756, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6669007/ . Solid-state NMR tailored to detect protein-water interaction.
• EXPERIMENT SUPPORTING WATER PRESENT - THE DIRECT REBUTTAL: Ryan MJ, Gao L, Valiyaveetil FI, Kananenka AA, Zanni MT (2024), "Water inside the selectivity filter of a K+ ion channel: structural heterogeneity, picosecond dynamics, and hydrogen bonding," JACS 146(2):1543-1553, DOI 10.1021/jacs.3c11513, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10797622/ . 2D infrared spectroscopy; water is definitively present in three heterogeneous populations (H-bonded to adjacent carbonyls, to non-adjacent carbonyls, and essentially free "gas-phase-like" water), and the authors state the results "are consistent with the soft-knock mechanism." Related 2D IR: Kratochvil HT et al. (2016), Science 353(6303):1040-1044, DOI 10.1126/science.aag1447; JACS 2023, DOI 10.1021/jacs.3c05339.
• INDEPENDENT ELECTROPHYSIOLOGICAL EVIDENCE FOR WATER CO-PERMEATION: Iwamoto M, Oiki S (2011), "Counting ion and water molecules in a streaming file through the open-filter structure of the K channel," Journal of Neuroscience 31(34):12180, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/31/34/12180 . Streaming potential measures the water-to-ion coupling ratio directly and returns non-zero water co-permeation. Also Oiki group, PNAS 2021, DOI 10.1073/pnas.2017168118.
• DIRECT-KNOCK-ON CAMP'S REPLY: Mironenko A, de Groot BL, Kopec W (2024), "Selectivity filter mutations shift ion permeation mechanism in potassium channels," PNAS Nexus 3(7):pgae272, DOI 10.1093/pnasnexus/pgae272, PMID 39015549, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11251424/ . Wild-type KcsA simulates as water-free direct knock-on; G77A and T75A filter mutants show "a complete change in the K+ permeation mechanism" to water co-permeation, with reduced conductance and impaired selectivity. Argument: crystal structures of such mutants must not be read back onto wild type.
• THE METHODOLOGICAL HEART OF THE DISPUTE - FORCE FIELDS AND WATER MODELS. Non-polarizable classical force fields overestimate ion-ion repulsion in condensed phase, which is precisely the quantity deciding whether two K+ can sit in contact.
◦ Hui et al. (2025), "Effective polarization in potassium channel simulations: ion conductance, occupancy, voltage response, and selectivity," PNAS, DOI 10.1073/pnas.2423866122. Electronic continuum correction charge scaling applied across three K+ channels: efficient permeation still occurs by direct knock-on, with HIGHER filter occupancy because polarization reduces ion-ion repulsion; ECC raises simulated conductance in Charmm36m to quantitative agreement with experimental single-channel conductance.
◦ THE MOST IMPORTANT RECENT RESULT FOR THE BOOK: Bosio S, Gazzoni D, Domene C, Masetti M, Furini S (2026), "Role of water models in simulations of ion conduction in potassium channels," Journal of Chemical Theory and Computation 22(2):1177-1186, DOI 10.1021/acs.jctc.5c01787, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12854744/ . Microsecond MD of KcsA and MthK with Markov State Model analysis comparing OPC and TIP3P water. With TIP3P, "no water molecules were observed in binding sites S2 and S3" under ANY condition - the simulation is FORCED into hard knock-on by the water model. With OPC, "both hard and soft knock-on mechanisms are accessible and can reversibly transition." The authors argue this "provides a reconciliation between structural data supporting hard knock-on and streaming potential measurements demonstrating water copermeation," and conclude both mechanisms may operate physiologically. IF THIS HOLDS UP, a large fraction of the fifteen-year MD literature was reporting a force-field property rather than a biological fact.
• Standard review entry point: Mironenko A, Zachariae U, de Groot BL, Kopec W (2021), "The persistent question of potassium channel permeation mechanisms," J Mol Biol 433(17):167002, DOI 10.1016/j.jmb.2021.167002, PMID 33891905. Abstract verbatim: "the molecular mechanism of K+ ions permeation through potassium channels remains unclear, with structural, functional and computational studies often providing conflicting data." Written by the direct-knock-on camp - read as an advocate's review, but its framing of the conflict is fair.

4e. WHAT IS SETTLED, WHAT IS NOT, AND WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT.
SETTLED:
• The filter's chemical identity - backbone carbonyl oxygens plus the Thr hydroxyl, four contiguous sites S1-S4 - is not in dispute. Doyle 1998 is structurally correct.
• The STRONG form of snug fit - a rigid cage with sub-angstrom precision mechanically excluding Na+ - is rejected by essentially all camps, because filter fluctuations of about 1 A exceed the 0.36-0.38 A radius difference.
• Selectivity is thermodynamic and multi-factorial: coordination number, ligand field strength, architectural constraint, and number of contiguous sites all contribute, with kinetic factors as well.
NOT SETTLED:
• WHICH factor dominates. Field strength (Noskov-Roux), coordination number (Bostick-Brooks), topological control (Varma-Rempe), and number of contiguous sites (Alam-Jiang) are all supported and none decisively falsified. Bostick &amp; Brooks' verdict - "neither confirm nor refute the field-strength hypothesis" - has not been superseded by any clean discriminating experiment.
• Whether a WEAK or LOCAL form of snug fit survives. Thompson/Nimigean's Na+ and Li+ plane sites keep geometry-based reasoning alive at the individual-site level even though the rigid-cage version is dead.
• Direct versus soft knock-on. ssNMR (Oster 2019) says water-free; 2D IR (Ryan/Zanni 2024) says water definitively present and consistent with soft knock-on; streaming potentials (Iwamoto &amp; Oiki 2011) measure real water co-permeation; large-scale MD (Kopfer 2014, Kopec 2018, Hui 2025) consistently produces direct knock-on. Not reconciled by any experiment.
• Whether the disagreement is even about biology rather than about simulation parameters (Bosio 2026).
• Whether bacterial model channels (KcsA, MthK, NaK) generalise to eukaryotic Kv, Kir and K2P. See "Model prokaryotic potassium channels: predictive power and relevance to eukaryotic channels," J Gen Physiol 158(5):e202614025, https://rupress.org/jgp/article/158/5/e202614025/282805/
WHAT WOULD SETTLE IT:
• A model-free measurement of the water-to-K+ coupling ratio in a single channel at physiological voltage and concentration, repeated across KcsA, MthK, NaK2K and a eukaryotic Kv, with stated error bars. Ratio indistinguishable from zero kills soft knock-on; robustly non-zero kills strict direct knock-on.
• Time-resolved, site-resolved S1-S4 occupancy DURING conduction, not at equilibrium - 2D IR or ssNMR under applied transmembrane voltage with simultaneous current measurement on the same sample. Without simultaneity, structural water and permeating water cannot be distinguished, which is the loophole both sides exploit.
• Neutron or joint X-ray/neutron structures, which see hydrogen directly, rather than inferring water from electron density equally well fit by K+; and anomalous X-ray scattering at the K edge to assign ion versus water density site by site - this discriminates exactly what the 2001 superposition interpretation could not.
• For selectivity specifically: a designed series varying coordination number, ligand field strength, and number of contiguous sites INDEPENDENTLY. The NaK to NaK2CNG to NaK2K series did this for site number; extending Valiyaveetil's amide-to-ester chemistry to every site with permeability ratios measured would separate field strength from coordination number - the variable pair no experiment has yet decoupled.
• Post-Bosio-2026 rule of thumb: no MD claim about this debate should be accepted unless its water-model dependence has been explicitly tested.

=====================================================================
5. Na+ CHANNEL SELECTIVITY FILTER - DEKA AND EEEE
=====================================================================

DEKA MOTIF - VERIFIED. Aspartate (D) from domain I, glutamate (E) from domain II, lysine (K) from domain III, alanine (A) from domain IV, each contributed by the P-loop (the re-entrant pore loop between transmembrane segments S5 and S6) of one of the four homologous domains of the single-polypeptide eukaryotic NaV1.x channel. Domain assignment confirmed in Stephens RF, Guan W, Zhorov BS, Spafford JD (2015), Frontiers in Physiology 6:153, https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/fphys.2015.00153 and Li Y, Liu H, Xia M, Gong H (2016), PLOS ONE 11(9):e0162413, https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0162413
• Residue numbers, rat/human skeletal muscle NaV1.4 (SCN4A, historically mu1): D400 (DI), E755 (DII), K1237 (DIII), A1529 (DIV). K1237 confirmed via the Sun/Favre/Schild/Moczydlowski 1997 designation.
• Residue numbers, rat brain II / NaV1.2 lineage: D384, E942, K1422, A1714. K1422 and A1714 are the ones Heinemann mutated and are well corroborated. D384 and E942 are LESS FIRMLY VERIFIED - I could not retrieve a single fetched primary table listing all four. Flag before printing.

CORRECTION TO THE BRIEF - IMPORTANT. Heinemann SH, Terlau H, Stuhmer W, Imoto K, Numa S (1992), "Calcium channel characteristics conferred on the sodium channel by single mutations," Nature 356(6368):441-443, https://www.nature.com/nature/journal/v356/n6368/full/356441a0.html . The paper's result was SINGLE mutations, NOT construction of a full EEEE ring. Changing ONE residue in the rat brain II sodium channel - either K1422 in repeat III or A1714 in repeat IV - to glutamate conferred calcium-channel-like selectivity. The key constructs were therefore DEEA (K1422E) and DEKE (A1714E). The full charge ladder DEKA to DEKE to DEEA to DEEE, giving progressively more calcium-selective channels, is the FOLLOW-UP literature (Schlief T et al. 1996, European Biophysics Journal, https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s002490050020), not the 1992 result. The book's draft claim that Heinemann mutated K and A together to give EEEE should be corrected. The paper's real message stands: adding negative charge at the DEKA locus converts Na+ selectivity into Ca2+ selectivity.

THE LYSINE IS THE CRITICAL RESIDUE - VERIFIED at secondary level.
• Favre I, Moczydlowski E, Schild L (1996), "On the structural basis for ionic selectivity among Na+, K+, and Ca2+ in the voltage-gated sodium channel," Biophysical Journal 71:3110-3125, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8968582/ . The domain-III lysine is the single most important residue for Na+ over K+ discrimination. Mutating it to alanine - or even to ARGININE, which is chemically similar and also positively charged - abolishes Na+/K+ selectivity. The arginine result matters: the requirement is not merely a positive charge but the specific geometry and protonation behaviour of lysine. NOTE: I could not fetch the Favre abstract itself (PubMed CAPTCHA, cell.com 403); this rests on Li et al. 2016 citing it.
• Sun YM, Favre I, Schild L, Moczydlowski E (1997), "On the structural basis for size-selective permeation of organic cations through the voltage-gated sodium channel: effect of alanine mutations at the DEKA locus," J Gen Physiol 110(6):693-715, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9382897/ . Lys(III) to Ala largely eliminated selectivity among the group IA monovalent alkali cations (Li+, Na+, K+, Rb+, Cs+), permitted inward current carried by group IIA divalents (Mg2+, Ca2+, Sr2+, Ba2+), and allowed permeation of large organic cations the wild type excludes. Lysine is the molecular sieve as well as the selectivity element.

P_Na/P_K PERMEABILITY RATIO - VERIFIED, AND IT IS A RANGE.
• The ~10-30:1 claim is SUPPORTED. Eukaryotic NaV P_Na/P_K approximately 10 to 30; bacterial NaV approximately 5 to 170. Source: Scientific Reports 9:15173 (2019), "Bases of bacterial sodium channel selectivity among organic cations," https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6813354/
• Hille B (1972), "The permeability of the sodium channel to metal cations in myelinated nerve," J Gen Physiol 59(6):637-658, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/5025743/ - abstract gives the permeability sequence Na+ approximately equal to Li+ &gt; Tl+ &gt; K+, with P_K/P_Na = 1/12. That is the origin of the commonly quoted 1 : 0.086 (1/12 = 0.083; the 0.086 is a more precise value from the paper's data tables, which I could not fetch).
• GENUINE DISAGREEMENT TO FLAG: Hille 1972 states permeabilities to Rb+, Cs+, Ca2+ and Mg2+ were TOO SMALL TO MEASURE. Widely reproduced textbook tables nonetheless assign Rb+ approximately 0.012 and Cs+ approximately 0.013. Those are upper bounds or later re-measurements, NOT values Hille reported as measured. Do not attribute them to Hille.
• Hille B (1971), "The permeability of the sodium channel to organic cations in myelinated nerve," J Gen Physiol 58(6):599-619, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/5315827/ - 21 organic cations tested. Reported sequence, VERBATIM from the abstract: sodium approximately equal to hydroxylamine &gt; hydrazine &gt; ammonium approximately equal to formamidine approximately equal to guanidine approximately equal to hydroxyguanidine &gt; aminoguanidine &gt;&gt; methylamine. Structural conclusion, verbatim: the sodium channel is an OXYGEN-LINED PORE ABOUT 3 A BY 5 A IN CROSS-SECTION, with one pair of oxygens acting as an ionised carboxylic acid group; methylated cations are excluded because the methyl group is too wide and cannot hydrogen-bond.
• GAP - DO NOT PRINT AS VERIFIED: the numeric per-ion table from Hille 1971 (hydroxylammonium ~0.94, hydrazinium ~0.59, ammonium ~0.16, formamidinium ~0.14, guanidinium ~0.13, aminoguanidinium ~0.06, methylammonium ~0.007). rupress.org returns HTTP 403 to automated fetching. These are consistent with the published rank order but were NOT verified against a fetched source. Needs a library copy.

BACTERIAL NaVAb - EEEE FILTER, VERIFIED.
• Payandeh J, Scheuer T, Zheng N, Catterall WA (2011), "The crystal structure of a voltage-gated sodium channel," Nature 475(7356):353-358, DOI 10.1038/nature10238, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21743477/ . From Arcobacter butzleri, captured in a closed-pore conformation with four activated voltage sensors at 2.7 A resolution.
• ABSTRACT VERBATIM: "the NavAb selectivity filter is short, approximately 4.6 A wide, and water filled, with four acidic side chains surrounding the narrowest part of the ion conduction pathway. This unique structure presents a high-field-strength anionic coordination site, which confers Na+ selectivity through partial dehydration via direct interaction with glutamate side chains."
• Use "PARTIAL DEHYDRATION" - that is the paper's own phrase and it is more precise than "partly hydrated." THE CONTRAST WITH KcsA IS THE HEADLINE: the K+ channel filter is long (12 A), narrow, and strips K+ of its hydration shell COMPLETELY, substituting backbone carbonyl oxygens for water oxygens. NaVAb's filter is short, wide, water-filled, and achieves only PARTIAL dehydration - the Na+ ion keeps part of its water shell as it passes, and the coordination is by glutamate SIDE CHAINS, not backbone carbonyls. Different chemistry, different geometry, different degree of dehydration.
• Glu177 confirmed as the residue forming the high-field-strength EEEE ring. The P-loop signature is TLESWSM and the E of that motif is Glu177. Confirmed in Stephens et al. 2015, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4436565/ and https://peerj.com/articles/16/ - the four identical P-loops give a filter of approximately 4.6 A by 4.6 A, with the four Glu177 side chains at the SAME SEQUENCE POSITION as the DEKA ring of eukaryotic channels.
• HOMOTETRAMER confirmed - four IDENTICAL subunits, each contributing one Glu177, so the ring is fourfold symmetric. The eukaryotic channel is a single polypeptide with four NON-IDENTICAL homologous domains, so it is pseudo-tetrameric and its DEKA ring is NECESSARILY ASYMMETRIC. This asymmetry is not incidental - it is what allows a single lysine to sit in the ring.
• RELATIVE WIDTH: the same Scientific Reports source states the DEKA filter is a few angstroms MORE CONSTRICTED than its prokaryotic counterparts. Consistent with Hille's 3 by 5 A estimate for the eukaryotic pore versus 4.6 by 4.6 A for NaVAb. The bacterial filter is the WIDER one.
• CONFLICTING NUMBER TO IGNORE: one Frontiers-sourced extraction gave the NaVAb filter as approximately 8-10 A at its narrowest, contradicting the 4.6 A in the Payandeh abstract. Trust the primary source, 4.6 A. The 8-10 A figure is almost certainly a different measurement plane (outer vestibule rather than the narrowest constriction).
• Bacterial echo of the Heinemann experiment: replacing NaVAb's TLESWSM with TLDDWSD (three added aspartates) converts the channel to calcium selectivity. https://peerj.com/articles/16/
• FIRST BACTERIAL NaV: Ren D, Navarro B, Xu H, Yue L, Shi Q, Clapham DE (2001), "A prokaryotic voltage-gated sodium channel," Science 294(5550):2372-2375, 14 December 2001, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11743207/ ; https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1065635 . NaChBac, from Bacillus halodurans, a single 6-transmembrane-segment subunit assembling as a homotetramer. Its pore-region sequence resembles that of voltage-gated CALCIUM channels rather than eukaryotic sodium channels - itself part of the argument that bacterial and eukaryotic sodium selectivity evolved separately.

DEKA AND EEEE ARE DIFFERENT STRUCTURAL SOLUTIONS - VERIFIED, WITH A SOURCE THAT SAYS SO OUTRIGHT.
• Finol-Urdaneta RK, Wang Y, Al-Sabi A, Zhao C, Noskov SY, French RJ (2014), "Sodium channel selectivity and conduction: prokaryotes have devised their own molecular strategy," J Gen Physiol 143(2):157-171, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24420772/ . The title makes the point. Prokaryotic channels achieve selectivity by tight binding of a single favoured ion together with inter-ion interactions, a strategy resembling CALCIUM and POTASSIUM channels rather than eukaryotic sodium channels. (rupress full text 403s; use the PubMed record.)
• THE SENTENCE TO CITE FOR THE "IMPERFECT MODEL" CLAIM: Li, Liu, Xia &amp; Gong (2016), PLOS ONE 11(9):e0162413 - the DEKA motif is COMPLETELY ABSENT in the prokaryotic counterparts, and the mechanism of cation selection in mammalian NaV channels lacks direct explanation at the atomic level because of the difference in amino acid sequences.
• Stephens et al. 2015 also frames DEKA as a different solution from EEEE, noting DEKA relies on a PROTONATABLE LYSINE rather than exclusively on acidic residues.
• BOOK WORDING: bacterial NaV structures are the only high-resolution voltage-gated sodium channel structures for much of this literature, but they are an IMPERFECT MODEL for the mammalian channel - fourfold-symmetric versus pseudo-tetrameric, EEEE versus DEKA, no lysine, wider and shorter filter, partial versus (in the K+ case) full dehydration. Do not transfer bacterial mechanism to human NaV1.x without saying so.

=====================================================================
6. SUMMARY OF EXPLICIT DISAGREEMENTS AND UNVERIFIED ITEMS
=====================================================================
SOURCES GENUINELY DISAGREE ON:
• Absolute single-ion hydration free energies - 12-15 kcal/mol systematic offset between compilations, or 412 kJ/mol per unit charge between proton conventions. The Na+ minus K+ DIFFERENCE (~17-18 kcal/mol) is convention-independent and is the number to use.
• K+ channel selectivity ratio - "at least 10,000" (Doyle 1998, asserted), "&gt;150" (LeMasurier 2001, measured bound), "&gt;100" (Hille 1973, detection floor), "100-1000:1" (2025 review). The disagreement is between what is measured and what is asserted, not between competing measurements.
• K+ conduction mechanism - spectroscopy points both ways (ssNMR water-free, 2D IR water present), electrophysiology says water co-permeates, MD says direct knock-on, and a 2026 paper shows the MD answer flips with the water model.
• Hille 1972 reports Rb+/Cs+ NaV permeabilities as unmeasurable; textbooks assign them finite values. Attribute finite values to later work.
• NaVAb filter width: 4.6 A (primary, Payandeh 2011) versus 8-10 A (secondary). Use 4.6 A.
CORRECTIONS TO THE BRIEF'S PREMISES:
• Heinemann 1992 used SINGLE mutations (K1422E or A1714E), not a full DEKA-to-EEEE conversion.
• The tyrosine of GYG is NOT essential for selectivity (Heginbotham 1994) - a very common textbook error.
• The four-site S1-S4 picture comes from Zhou et al. 2001, not from Doyle et al. 1998, which resolved two filter ions plus a cavity ion.
NOT VERIFIED - CONFIRM BEFORE PRINTING:
• Pauling radii Na+ 0.95 A and K+ 1.33 A (primary text not retrievable; teaching sources misattribute Shannon values to Pauling).
• Marcus 1991 tabulated values Na+ -365 and K+ -295 kJ/mol (Faraday PDF mirror returned HTTP 500 twice; RSC paywalled). Bracketed to within ~2 percent by verified Schmid et al. 2000 values (-371.1 and -297.9 kJ/mol).
• Numeric per-ion table from Hille 1971 for organic cations (rank order verified, numbers not).
• Rat brain II residue numbers D384 and E942.
• Shaker filter numbering T439-G446; hERG F627.
• Gly79 as an explicit number in Doyle et al. 1998 (Thr75, Val76, Gly77, Tyr78 are explicit).</result>

<result>Ravel D, Ben-Ari Y. "Bumetanide oral solution for the treatment of children and adolescents with autism spectrum disorder: results from two randomized phase III studies." Autism Research, October 2023, 16(10):2021-2034. doi 10.1002/aur.3005. https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/aur.3005

• CORRECTION TO THE BRIEF: the results were published in AUTISM RESEARCH, not Lancet Psychiatry. No Lancet Psychiatry publication of these trials was found.

• Numbers, from the abstract verbatim via Europe PMC:
◦ Each study enrolled 211 patients: bumetanide n equals 107, placebo n equals 104. TOTAL ACROSS BOTH STUDIES 422 PATIENTS.
◦ Design: international, multi-centre, randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled; bumetanide oral solution twice daily versus placebo twice daily; 6-month double-blind treatment period. Press reporting gives the dose as 0.5 mg twice daily.
◦ Primary endpoint: change in Childhood Autism Rating Scale 2 (CARS2) total raw score from baseline to Week 26.
◦ Key secondary endpoints: Social Responsiveness Scale-2, Clinical Global Impression scale, Vineland Adaptive Behavior Scale.
◦ Result: "Both studies were terminated early due to absence of any significant difference between bumetanide and placebo in the overall studied populations." CARS2 total raw score DECREASED FROM BASELINE IN BOTH ARMS - that is, a large placebo response - with no statistically significant between-group difference. No differences on ANY secondary efficacy endpoint in either study.
◦ Adverse events more frequent with bumetanide: thirst, polyuria, hypokalaemia (low blood potassium), dry mouth. Exact percentages not in the abstract; full text returned HTTP 402.
◦ Author conclusion, verbatim: "These large phase III trials failed to demonstrate a benefit of bumetanide for the treatment of pediatric ASD compared with placebo. Consequently, the sponsor has discontinued the development of bumetanide for the treatment of this condition."

• Post-hoc salvage attempt, flagged for honesty: "Treating autism with Bumetanide: Identification of responders using Q-Finder machine learning algorithm," Translational Psychiatry, doi 10.1038/s41398-026-03848-3. This is post-hoc subgroup mining on failed trial data and is hypothesis-generating only. Also a caregiver exit-interview paper: Frontiers in Child and Adolescent Psychiatry 2024, doi 10.3389/frcha.2024.1236340.

10.2 The earlier positive small autism trials

• Lemonnier E, Degrez C, Phelep M, Tyzio R, Josse F, Grandgeorge M, Hadjikhani N, Ben-Ari Y. "A randomised controlled trial of bumetanide in the treatment of autism in children." Translational Psychiatry (2012), article tp2012124. PubMed 23233021. https://www.nature.com/articles/tp2012124
◦ 60 children with autism or Asperger syndrome, aged 3 to 11 years. Double-blind, 3 months of placebo or bumetanide 1 mg daily, then 1 month washout.
◦ Results: bumetanide significantly reduced CARS, p less than 0.004 treated versus placebo; and Clinical Global Impression, p less than 0.017.
◦ Stated rationale: GABAergic synapses are altered in autism and GABA-acting benzodiazepines exert paradoxical effects in some patients, raising the possibility that GABA excites neurons because of elevated intracellular chloride; bumetanide should reduce intracellular chloride and restore GABAergic inhibition.

• Lemonnier E, et al. "Effects of bumetanide on neurobehavioral function in children and adolescents with autism spectrum disorders." Translational Psychiatry 7(3):e1056 (2017). doi 10.1038/tp.2017.10. PMC5416661.
◦ Multicentre, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomised phase 2B across four age groups (2-4, 5-9, 10-13, 14-18 years). 88 paediatric patients with ASD; bumetanide 0.5, 1.0 or 2.0 mg twice daily or placebo, for 3 months.
◦ Primary outcome, CARS mean reduction from baseline in completers (n equals 73): 0.5 mg minus 4.98 points, p equals 0.049 versus placebo; 1.0 mg minus 3.74 points, no significant p reported; 2.0 mg minus 5.35 points, p equals 0.017; placebo minus 1.79 points.
◦ RED FLAG WORTH REPORTING: the dose-response is NON-MONOTONIC - the middle dose performed worst. That is inconsistent with a real pharmacological effect.
◦ 23 bumetanide-treated participants showed greater than 6-point CARS improvement versus 1 placebo recipient.
◦ Social Responsiveness Scale: significant global improvement p equals 0.02; greatest reduction at 2.0 mg, minus 21.8 points; significant improvements in social communication and restricted/repetitive behaviour subscales.
◦ Clinical Global Impression-Improvement: p equals 0.0043; 17 bumetanide-treated versus 1 placebo participant rated "much improved."
◦ Hypokalaemia incidence by dose: 0.5 mg 30 percent; 1.0 mg 60.9 percent; 2.0 mg 76.2 percent; placebo 0 percent. Withdrawal rates: 0.5 mg 0 percent; 1.0 mg 17 percent; 2.0 mg 43 percent; placebo 14 percent.
◦ CRITICAL INTERPRETIVE POINT: near-universal hypokalaemia plus obvious diuresis (thirst, polyuria) creates a serious FUNCTIONAL UNBLINDING problem in all the small positive trials - participants and caregivers can tell which arm they are in, and every positive outcome measure in these trials is a subjective caregiver- or clinician-rated scale. This, together with regression to the mean and small-study bias, is the most plausible explanation for the discrepancy between the positive small trials and the null 422-patient phase 3 programme.

10.3 The NEMO trial in neonatal seizures

• Pressler RM, Boylan GB, Marlow N, Blennow M, Chiron C, Cross JH, de Vries LS, Hallberg B, Hellstrom-Westas L, Jullien V, Livingstone V, Mangum B, Murphy B, Murray D, Pons G, Rennie J, Swarte R, Toet MC, Vanhatalo S, Zohar S, on behalf of the NEonatal seizure treatment with Medication Off-patent (NEMO) consortium. "Bumetanide for the treatment of seizures in newborn babies with hypoxic ischaemic encephalopathy (NEMO): an open-label, dose finding, and feasibility phase 1/2 trial." Lancet Neurology, May 2015, 14(5):469-477. doi 10.1016/S1474-4422(15)00025-8.

• DATE CORRECTION TO THE BRIEF: the year is 2015, NOT 2021. European Union Framework Programme 7 project 241479, https://cordis.europa.eu/project/id/241479/reporting

• Numbers, from the abstract via Europe PMC:
◦ Population: full-term infants under 48 hours of age with hypoxic ischaemic encephalopathy and electrographic seizures unresponsive to phenobarbital loading, from eight European neonatal intensive care units.
◦ Recruitment September 2011 to September 2013. 30 infants SCREENED, 14 ENROLLED.
◦ Design: bumetanide at one of four dose levels, added on to phenobarbital, allocated by a Bayesian sequential design; continuous EEG to quantify seizure burden. (The four dose levels in mg/kg are not stated in the abstract and could not be verified.)
◦ Primary endpoint: greater than 80 percent reduction in electrographic seizure burden WITHOUT need for rescue antiepileptic drugs, in more than 50 percent of participants.
◦ RESULT: 5 of 14 infants met the seizure-reduction criterion, but ONLY 2 met it without rescue medication. THE PRIMARY ENDPOINT WAS NOT REACHED.
◦ TERMINATION: stopped early because of serious adverse reactions plus limited evidence for seizure reduction. Specifically, THE THIRD CASE OF HEARING LOSS TRIGGERED THE STOP.
◦ HEARING LOSS: 3 of 11 surviving infants, approximately 27 percent, had hearing impairment confirmed on auditory testing between 17 and 108 days of age.
◦ Interpretation: bumetanide with phenobarbital did not improve seizure control and potentially increased hearing-loss risk, underscoring the dangers of off-label neonatal drug use without controlled safety trials.
◦ Commentary: "Bumetanide for neonatal seizures - back from the cotside," Nature Reviews Neurology, doi 10.1038/nrneurol.2015.116.
◦ PARTISAN REBUTTAL, label it as such: Ben-Ari Y et al., "Failure of the Nemo Trial: Bumetanide Is a Promising Agent to Treat Many Brain Disorders but Not Newborn Seizures," Frontiers in Cellular Neuroscience 10:90 (2016), doi 10.3389/fncel.2016.00090.

10.4 The Soul et al. trial - the contradicting neonatal result

• Soul JS, Bergin AM, Stopp C, Hayes B, Singh A, Fortuno CR, O'Reilly D, Krishnamoorthy K, Jensen FE, Rofeberg V, Dong M, Vinks AA, Wypij D, Staley KJ, on behalf of the Boston Bumetanide Trial Group. "A Pilot Randomized, Controlled, Double-Blind Trial of Bumetanide to Treat Neonatal Seizures." Annals of Neurology (2021). doi 10.1002/ana.25959. PMC8122513. Registration NCT00830531.

• CORRECTION TO THE BRIEF: this is ANNALS OF NEUROLOGY 2021, not Pediatric Neurology.

• Screening funnel: 539 neonates screened, 191 eligible, 111 enrolled, 43 RANDOMISED - 27 bumetanide, 16 control.
• Doses: 0.1, 0.2 or 0.3 mg/kg intravenously in a dose-escalation design, added to standard phenobarbital 5 to 10 mg/kg. Continuous EEG for the first 4 hours after drug.
• Efficacy: significantly greater reduction in seizure burden with bumetanide. Total seizure burden median 3.1 min/h treatment versus 1.2 min/h control, p equals 0.006 - note this indicates the TREATMENT GROUP HAD HIGHER BASELINE SEIZURE BURDEN. Reduction at 0-4 hours post-drug greater in treatment, p less than 0.01, adjusted for baseline; same at 2-4 hours, p less than 0.01.
• Exposure, area under the curve 0-4 hours, median micrograms times hour per litre: 0.1 mg/kg gives 1,598; 0.2 mg/kg gives 3,050; 0.3 mg/kg gives 4,790.
• Hearing loss: 2 of 26 treatment survivors (8 percent); 0 of 13 control survivors (0 percent); 1 of 54 non-randomised infants (2 percent). No significant between-group difference was concluded, but the direction is unfavourable and the sample is tiny.
• Excess diuresis: 48 percent treatment versus 13 percent control, p equals 0.02.
• Deaths: 1 of 27 treatment (4 percent) versus 3 of 16 control (19 percent), p equals 0.14.
• Authors' conclusion: dose- and exposure-related benefit on seizure reduction without serious adverse effects beyond standard-therapy complications.

• DIRECT CONFLICT WITH NEMO: NEMO (n equals 14, open-label) found no efficacy and stopped for ototoxicity; Soul (n equals 43, double-blind, randomised) found dose-related efficacy and no significant ototoxicity difference. Both are small pilot studies. THE HONEST SUMMARY IS THAT NEONATAL SEIZURE EFFICACY REMAINS UNPROVEN AND THE OTOTOXICITY SIGNAL REMAINS UNRESOLVED. A pooled figure of approximately 12 percent permanent hearing loss across the two trials is cited in the Loscher and Kaila literature.

10.5 Other bumetanide clinical work

• "A Preliminary Study Evaluating the Safety and Efficacy of Bumetanide, an NKCC1 Inhibitor, in Patients with Drug-Resistant Epilepsy." CNS Drugs (2019), doi 10.1007/s40263-019-00607-5. Small and preliminary.
• "Effects of bumetanide on neonatal seizures: A systematic review of animal and human studies." Seizure - European Journal of Epilepsy (2023), S1059-1311(23)00238-8, PubMed 37690372. Full text returned HTTP 403, so its pooled estimates are not reported here.

10.6 THE PHARMACOLOGY OF FAILURE - why bumetanide arguably cannot work as given

Primary sources: Loscher W, Kaila K, "CNS pharmacology of NKCC1 inhibitors," Neuropharmacology, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0028390821004676 ; Kaila K, Loscher W, "Bumetanide for neonatal seizures: No light in the pharmacokinetic/dynamic tunnel," Epilepsia 63:1868-1873 (2022), doi 10.1111/epi.17279 ; Toepfer and Loscher group work at PMC9545618.

• CHEMISTRY: bumetanide is a relatively strong carboxylic acid, acid dissociation constant pKa equals 3.6, and is therefore MORE THAN 99 PERCENT IONISED at physiological pH. Ionised molecules do not cross the blood-brain barrier by passive diffusion.

• PLASMA PROTEIN BINDING - SOURCES DISAGREE: Kaila and Loscher 2022 give 97 to 98 percent; other Loscher-group text gives "greater than 95 percent." The 98 to 99 percent figure in common circulation is at the upper edge of what is verifiable. Report as approximately 97 to 99 percent and note the spread.

• BRAIN TO PLASMA CONCENTRATION RATIO: 0.01 to 0.02 or less. THIS IS THE SINGLE MOST DAMAGING NUMBER IN THE WHOLE STORY - brain parenchymal concentrations are 50 to 100-fold below plasma.

• ACTIVE EFFLUX compounds the problem: bumetanide is a substrate for efflux transport at the blood-brain barrier. Romermann K et al., "Multiple blood-brain barrier transport mechanisms limit bumetanide accumulation, and therapeutic potential, in the mammalian brain," Neuropharmacology, PubMed 28192112.

• NKCC1 POTENCY - SOURCES DISAGREE ON UNITS, AND IT MATTERS ENORMOUSLY:
◦ Loscher and Kaila 2022 state the inhibition constant Ki / half-maximal inhibitory concentration IC50 of bumetanide for NKCC1 is "often cited to be around 100 to 300 nanomolar," that is 0.1 to 0.3 micromolar, and explicitly note it is NANOMOLAR, not micromolar.
◦ PMC9545618 gives a threshold of approximately 0.1 micromole per litre for effects on immature hippocampal network events, and notes that in vitro studies typically apply approximately 10 micromole per litre - roughly 100-fold above threshold and 30 to 100-fold above the stated IC50.
◦ CONSEQUENCE FOR THE WHOLE LITERATURE: almost every in vitro experiment that "proves NKCC1 involvement" with bumetanide, INCLUDING Huberfeld 2007 at 5 to 10 micromolar and much of the developmental work, sits in this supra-selective range, where bumetanide also inhibits KCC2 and other targets. Any statement of "the IC50" must specify which figure is meant.

• MEASURED BRAIN CONCENTRATIONS VERSUS WHAT IS NEEDED:
◦ The clinically approved dose, 0.3 mg/kg intraperitoneally, produces a transient MAXIMUM BRAIN CONCENTRATION OF ONLY 1 TO 5 NANOMOLE PER LITRE.
◦ The concentration needed in brain tissue is approximately 0.1 micromole per litre, that is 100 nanomole per litre. THE GAP IS ROUGHLY 20 TO 100-FOLD.
◦ Reaching 0.1 micromole per litre in brain would require approximately 9 mg/kg intraperitoneally (Cleary et al. data in neonatal rats); approximately 2 mg/kg or more is needed for any central effect at all, and then only very briefly.
◦ Loscher and Kaila's conclusion in their own framing: brain concentrations at clinically used or even supra-therapeutic doses are "at least an order of magnitude lower" than needed for direct neuronal effects, and the reported central nervous system effects of bumetanide, experimental and clinical, are therefore NOT MEDIATED BY INHIBITION OF NKCC1 IN BRAIN PARENCHYMA. This directly undercuts the mechanistic rationale for every bumetanide trial listed above.

• DOSING CONTEXT: the low systemic doses associated with maximal DIURETIC effect in humans are approximately 0.05 to 0.3 mg/kg. So the therapeutic diuretic window and the putative NKCC1-in-brain window DO NOT OVERLAP.

• OTOTOXICITY, AND WHY IT IS STRUCTURAL RATHER THAN INCIDENTAL: significant ototoxic effects at approximately 2 mg/kg intravenously in cats and 0.5 mg/kg intravenously in dogs. Mice are strikingly less sensitive than other species, which is one reason rodent safety data under-predicted the human neonatal hearing loss. NKCC1 is expressed in the STRIA VASCULARIS of the inner ear, so ototoxicity is an ON-TARGET consequence of NKCC1 inhibition, not an idiosyncratic side effect. THE TOXICITY THEREFORE SCALES WITH EXACTLY THE PROPERTY ONE WOULD HAVE TO INCREASE TO GAIN EFFICACY. That is the central pharmacological trap.

• HUMAN SIDE-EFFECT NUMBERS: excess diuresis 48 percent versus 13 percent control (Soul 2021, p equals 0.02); hypokalaemia in 30 to 76 percent of children dose-dependently (Lemonnier 2017); thirst, polyuria, hypokalaemia and dry mouth in the SIGN phase 3 trials.

• PRODRUG AND ANALOGUE RESCUE STRATEGIES:
◦ Tollner K, Brandt C, Topfer M, et al. "A novel prodrug-based strategy to increase effects of bumetanide in epilepsy." Annals of Neurology (2014). PubMed 24615913. doi 10.1002/ana.24124. BUM5 is the N,N-dimethylaminoethylester of bumetanide, a lipophilic ester prodrug converted to bumetanide in vivo. Brain bumetanide levels after BUM5 increased by a factor of approximately 3 to 5 in rodents; in the rat kindling model BUM5 was more efficacious than bumetanide at potentiating phenobarbital.
◦ Erker T, Brandt C, et al. "The bumetanide prodrug BUM5, but not bumetanide, potentiates the antiseizure effect of phenobarbital in adult epileptic mice." Epilepsia (2016). PubMed 26921222. doi 10.1111/epi.13346.
◦ REALITY CHECK ON THE PRODRUG ARITHMETIC: a 3 to 5-fold increase applied to a brain-to-plasma ratio of 0.01 to 0.02 still yields only about 0.03 to 0.10. The prodrug narrows the gap but does not obviously close the 20 to 100-fold shortfall. The authors themselves position BUM5 as "a promising tool compound for target validation and proof-of-concept studies," not as a clinical candidate.
◦ IMPORTANT NEGATIVE EVIDENCE: bumepamine, a brain-permeant benzylamine derivative, does NOT inhibit NKCC1 yet is MORE potent than bumetanide at enhancing phenobarbital's anti-seizure efficacy. Neuropharmacology S0028390818306774. This dissociates the anti-seizure effect from NKCC1 inhibition entirely and supports Loscher and Kaila's position.
◦ Alternative scaffolds proposed by Loscher and Kaila: carboxyl-group-free loop diuretics such as torsemide. See also Loscher's comparison of bumetanide, azosemide and torasemide on seizure threshold, Neuropharmacology S0028390821000034.
◦ Structural biology now available: "Structural basis for human NKCC1 inhibition by loop diuretic drugs," EMBO Journal (2025), doi 10.1038/s44318-025-00368-6.


PART 11 - CONSOLIDATED LIST OF POINTS WHERE SOURCES DISAGREE

• Extracellular chloride: CSF 122-128 mM versus 120-132 mM clinically; ACSF actually used in the key experiments is 134-136 mM, and one commercial formulation is 155 mM. Choice of value shifts calculated E_Cl by several mV.

• Mature [Cl-]i: 6 plus or minus 2 mM by SuperClomeleon (Boffi 2018) versus 17 mM median by LSSmClopHensor (Sulis Sato 2017) in the same nominal preparation. Nearly threefold, about 27 mV of E_Cl.

• Mature E_GABA: the quoted minus 70 to minus 85 mV is a selective reading; across 20 gramicidin perforated-patch studies the group means span minus 85 to minus 60 mV.

• Immature E_GABA: the quoted minus 40 to minus 50 mV is not supported for typical P0-P7 pyramidal neurons; measured means cluster at minus 52 to minus 66 mV. Values near minus 40 mV belong to embryonic or precursor cells.

• Chloride imaging versus electrophysiology: in vivo sensor values (17 mM adult, 40-54 mM neonatal) predict E_Cl systematically more positive than any gramicidin-measured E_GABA. Genuinely unresolved.

• Does GABA ever become truly hyperpolarizing at the soma? Tyzio et al. 2008 say no - it stays slightly depolarizing even in adults, and mature somatic inhibition is shunting. This contradicts the Rivera 1999 narrative from within the same broad camp.

• Human switch timing: prenatal and complete before term (Vanhatalo 2005, Sedmak 2016) versus "perinatal human cortex is as chloride-immature as the rat" (Dzhala 2005) versus adult-like KCC2 only after the first year. Spread of well over a year. All evidence indirect.

• Energy-substrate artefact: Rheims 2009 and Holmgren 2010 versus Tyzio 2011, Ruusuvuori 2010, Kirmse 2010, Waddell 2011, Khakhalin and Aizenman 2012, Bos and Vinay 2012, Barkis 2010. Weight is against the artefact hypothesis on substrate grounds; partly for it on slicing-trauma grounds.

• Is neonatal hippocampal GABA excitatory in vivo? Murata and Colonnese 2020 (Science Advances) says yes at P3 in CA1; Valeeva 2016 and the Mortet 2025 bioRxiv preprint say no. This is the last live disagreement.

• KCC2 direction in human epileptic tissue: reduced (Huberfeld 2007, Palma 2006) versus ENHANCED (Karlocai 2016, Brain Structure and Function). Attributed to tissue type and epilepsy phase. Unresolved.

• Within Huberfeld 2007: about 30 percent of subicular pyramidal cells lack KCC2 messenger RNA but only about 10 percent lack detectable protein - a threefold internal mismatch.

• Bumetanide in neonatal seizures: NEMO 2015 (n equals 14) no efficacy, stopped for ototoxicity, versus Soul 2021 (n equals 43) dose-related efficacy, no significant ototoxicity. Directly contradictory.

• Bumetanide in autism: multiple small positive trials versus two 211-patient phase 3 trials with no effect on any endpoint. Functional unblinding via hypokalaemia (30-76 percent) and diuresis is the leading explanation for the earlier positives.

• Bumetanide plasma protein binding: 97-98 percent versus greater than 95 percent.

• Bumetanide NKCC1 potency: 100-300 nanomolar Ki/IC50 versus about 0.1 micromolar functional threshold versus about 10 micromolar routinely applied in vitro.

• Are bumetanide's central effects NKCC1-mediated at all? Loscher and Kaila say no on pharmacokinetic grounds, supported by the bumepamine result; Ben-Ari and colleagues (Frontiers in Cellular Neuroscience 2016, Trends in Neurosciences 2017) maintain that they are.


PART 12 - ITEMS THAT COULD NOT BE VERIFIED - DO NOT FILL THESE IN FROM MEMORY

• Yamada et al. 2004 Journal of Physiology 557:829-841 full text: HTTP 402 and 403 across publisher and mirrors. Only abstract-level content confirmed; do not quote per-age numeric values.
• Rheims 2009 and Holmgren 2010: exact E_GABA values in mV could not be recovered from open sources (the Rheims PDF is image-only). Check against the printed papers.
• Kirmse 2015 full text, Murata and Colonnese 2020 full text, Valeeva 2016 full text, Colonnese 2024 Nature Reviews Neuroscience: all paywalled or HTTP 403. Details drawn from abstracts, PubMed records, and quotations in commentaries.
• Doyon et al. 2016 Neuron; Journal of Neuroscience 41(23):4957 (2021): HTTP 403. Values attributed to them are from indexed search excerpts, flagged as such.
• Kaila K, Price TJ, Payne JA, Puskarjov M, Voipio J, Nature Reviews Neuroscience 15(10):637-654 (2014), https://www.nature.com/articles/nrn3819 : citation and scope confirmed only; no numeric values extracted.
• Huberfeld 2007: patient and slice counts, per-figure statistics. HTTP 403.
• Kahle 2014: idiopathic generalised epilepsy case and control cohort sizes, allele frequencies, odds ratios, association p-values. All mirrors inaccessible.
• Coull 2005: mV magnitude of the BDNF-induced E_anion shift; paw-withdrawal thresholds in grams. Nature paywalled.
• Coull 2003: the minus 70 mV to minus 49 mV pair is SECOND-HAND from Prescott, Sejnowski and De Koninck, Molecular Pain 2:32 (2006), citing Coull 2003. Not read in the Nature paper.
• Palma 2006: fold changes, oocyte reversal potentials, patient numbers. PNAS HTTP 403.
• Stodberg 2015: exact residue numbering comes from the Frontiers 2019 review table, which renders one variant as L331H where other sources give L311H. Verify before printing.
• SIGN phase 3 trials: exact CARS2 change values with confidence intervals, and adverse-event percentages. Autism Research HTTP 402.
• NEMO: the four dose levels in mg/kg are not stated in the abstract.
• Mapplebeck et al. 2018 (sex differences in the BDNF arm): seen in secondary literature only; primary not fetched.
• Karlocai et al. 2016: magnitudes and patient numbers unverified (Springer redirect; eScholarship mirror empty).


PART 13 - THREE CORRECTIONS TO THE ORIGINAL BRIEF

• Ben-Ari's rebuttal paper "Refuting the challenges of the developmental shift of polarity of GABA actions: GABA more exciting than ever!" is Frontiers in Cellular Neuroscience 2012, volume 6, article 35 - NOT 2011.
• The NEMO trial is Lancet Neurology 2015, volume 14, issue 5, pages 469-477 - NOT 2021. It was a phase 1/2 open-label dose-finding and feasibility trial, terminated after the third hearing-loss case, with the primary endpoint not reached.
• The Servier phase 3 autism results were published in Autism Research 2023, 16(10):2021-2034 - NOT Lancet Psychiatry. The discontinuation was announced 7 September 2021.</result>
