<result>VOLUME TRANSMISSION AND CIRCUMVENTRICULAR ORGANS - CITED FACT SHEET

No files were written, per the instruction. Where two figures conflict, both are given and the conflict is named.


TOPIC A - VOLUME TRANSMISSION


A1. EXTRACELLULAR SPACE GEOMETRY (Sykova and Nicholson 2008, Physiological Reviews 88:1277-1340, doi 10.1152/physrev.00027.2007)

• Volume fraction alpha, defined as the fraction of tissue volume that is extracellular space (ECS): approximately 0.20, that is about 20 percent of brain volume. Healthy range across regions and studies 0.15 to 0.28.

• Tortuosity lambda, defined as the square root of the factor by which the ECS slows diffusion relative to free solution: approximately 1.6. Range 1.4 to 1.8.

• The governing relation is D-star equals D divided by lambda squared. With lambda 1.6, lambda squared is 2.56, so the effective diffusion coefficient is reduced by a factor of about 2.5 to 2.6 relative to free solution.

• Foundational measurement: Nicholson and Phillips 1981, Journal of Physiology, rat cerebellum, tetramethylammonium (TMA-plus) real-time iontophoresis: alpha equals 0.21 plus or minus 0.02, lambda equals 1.55 plus or minus 0.05. TMA-plus free diffusion coefficient 1250 micrometres squared per second at 34.5 degrees Celsius.

• Representative Table-1 style values (rabbit caudate sucrose alpha 0.21 lambda 1.60; rat cortex TMA-plus alpha 0.18 to 0.22 lambda 1.54 to 1.65; mouse cortex TMA-plus alpha 0.23 lambda 1.67). Larger molecules are hindered more: 3 kDa dextran lambda 2.04, 70 kDa dextran lambda 2.69. Source: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3500962/

• Important nuance: pure geometrical packing at alpha 0.20 predicts only lambda-geometric equals 1.18. The excess hindrance (1.18 observed as 1.6) is attributed to dead-space microdomains and to binding/obstruction by the extracellular matrix. So lambda is not a purely geometric constant.

• Pathology: alpha falls to roughly 0.05 to 0.10 in ischaemia and spreading depression, with lambda rising above 2, which concentrates transmitters and slows their escape. This is a robust finding in the Sykova/Nicholson corpus.


A2. EFFECTIVE DIFFUSION COEFFICIENTS IN BRAIN ECS (micrometres squared per second)

• Dopamine, free aqueous D: 763 micrometres squared per second (Nicholson 1995 compilation; widely used in modelling).

• Dopamine, tortuosity-corrected effective D-star: 321.7 micrometres squared per second, using lambda 1.54 and alpha 0.21 (Rice and Nicholson 1991 parameters; used in the PLOS Computational Biology 2020 dopamine model, https://journals.plos.org/ploscompbiol/article?id=10.1371/journal.pcbi.1008410).

• A competing effective value of about 240 micrometres squared per second circulates for striatal dopamine. These two DISAGREE by a factor of about 1.34 and are not reconcilable from the same free D. The likely explanation is that cations bind glycosaminoglycans: Rice, Gerhardt, Hierl, Nagy and Adams 1985, Neuroscience 15:891-902, found catecholamine cations diffuse about three times more slowly than the purely geometric tortuosity correction predicts. Uptake contaminating diffusion-only fits is a second candidate.

• The often-quoted "6 times 10 to the minus 10 square metres per second" for dopamine is the same as 600 micrometres squared per second, that is a free-solution-like value, not a tortuosity-corrected one. Do not mix the two.

• Noradrenaline and serotonin: no clean absolute published effective D was located. Rice et al. 1985 gives only ratio statements relative to dopamine. This is a genuine gap. What would settle it: a real-time iontophoresis measurement of noradrenaline and serotonin diffusion in brain slices with simultaneous uptake blockade, which has not been published.

• Small ions for scale: TMA-plus free 1250, effective about 520 micrometres squared per second. Nicholson 2001, Reports on Progress in Physics 64:815, contains the most complete published table of free diffusion coefficients (paywalled).


A3. DOPAMINE AND NORADRENALINE DIFFUSION DISTANCES - THE SPHERE OF INFLUENCE

The classic Cragg/Rice numbers

• Cragg and Rice 2004, "DAncing past the DAT at a DA synapse", Trends in Neurosciences 27:270-277, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15111009/ ; and Rice and Cragg 2008, Brain Research Reviews 58:303-313, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18433875/

• Radius at which peak dopamine still reaches 1 micromolar, the concentration needed for LOW-affinity D1 receptors: about 2 micrometres, in both substantia nigra and striatum.

• Radius at which peak dopamine still reaches 10 nanomolar, needed for HIGH-affinity D2 receptors: 8.2 micrometres with diffusion alone, falling to 7.0 micrometres in striatum once uptake is included. Including uptake shrinks the sphere by about 40 percent by volume.

• Number of synapses enclosed: roughly 5 to 35 synapses inside the 2 micrometre D1 sphere, roughly 300 to 2,500 inside the 7 to 8 micrometre D2 sphere. A roughly 100-fold receptor affinity difference converts into a roughly 100-fold difference in the number of synapses reached. Receptor affinity, not anatomy, sets the reach.

• Cragg and Rice's central claim about the transporter: within about 1 micrometre of a release site, dilution by diffusion dominates and the dopamine transporter (DAT) is nearly irrelevant. DAT's real effect is on the sphere of influence and the lifetime of dopamine at a distance, shortening active lifetime by about 50 percent.

The modern counterweight - and this is a live disagreement

• Liu, Goel and Kaeser 2021, Nature Reviews Neuroscience 22:345-358, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41583-021-00455-7 : the contents of a single vesicle are diluted below 200 nanomolar within about 3 milliseconds, so the effective radius for a SINGLE quantal event is probably less than 1 micrometre. Micrometre-scale reach arises only from POOLING across many synchronised release sites (their "domain-overlap" model).

• Ozcete, Banerjee and Kaeser 2024, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11540752/ : concentration at the mouth of a fusing vesicle is hundreds of millimolar, dropping to nanomolar "within a few milliseconds and within micrometers".

• The two camps DISAGREE: 7 to 8 micrometres (Cragg and Rice) versus sub-micrometre per quantum (Kaeser). The disagreement turns on the assumed D2 affinity. Kaeser notes that intracellular GTP in medium spiny neurons collapses the D2 high-affinity state toward D1-like affinity, and that striatal D2 receptors are saturated only by about 100 micromolar dopamine. What would settle it: direct measurement of endogenous D2 occupancy as a function of measured distance from an identified single release site.

Release-site geometry

• Striatal dopamine varicosity density 1.0 to 1.7 times 10 to the 8 per cubic millimetre, that is about 0.14 varicosities per cubic micrometre, one per 7 cubic micrometres (Doucet et al. 1986, compiled in Rice and Cragg 2011, Neuroscience).

• Functional release sites are far sparser: one release site per about 4 micrometres of dopamine axon, equivalently one per about 25 cubic micrometres of striatum (Liu, Goel and Kaeser 2021). Dopamine axons occupy about 3 percent of striatal volume.

• DAT molecule density in striatum 175 to 350 (median about 237) per cubic micrometre, so mean DAT spacing is a few hundred nanometres.

• Over 97 percent of released molecules leave a dopamine cleft within 0.25 milliseconds; single vesicle content about 3,000 molecules; release probability about 6 percent per spike (PLOS Comput Biol 2020 model parameters).

Uptake kinetics

• Striatal DAT Michaelis constant Km about 0.2 micromolar (range 0.15 to 0.21 across sources). Maximal velocity Vmax spans 0.2 to 6 micromolar per second depending on method (iontophoresis versus evoked release), an about 30-fold spread that is NOT resolved. Primary: Wightman, Amatore, Engstrom et al. 1988, Neuroscience 25:513-523. Note some secondary sources miscite this as "Near et al. 1988".

• Noradrenaline transporter (NET) uptake rate in brain is about half the dopamine rate (Park, Takmakov and Wightman 2011, Journal of Neurochemistry, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3217157/ ). Purified human NET Km 0.17 plus or minus 0.08 micromolar (Nature 2024, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-024-07638-z ) versus 539 nanomolar in cultured rat cortical astrocytes (Inazu et al. 2003). These DISAGREE about threefold, and no per-volume Vmax for NET in brain tissue is authoritatively established.

Striatum versus prefrontal cortex - the key regional contrast

• Garris and Wightman 1994, Journal of Neuroscience 14:442-450, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8283249/ : dopamine terminal density is about 90-fold lower in medial prefrontal cortex than in caudate-putamen or nucleus accumbens; uptake rate about 50-fold lower; release rate constants about 8-fold lower. Their conclusion is that cortical dopamine regulation is "suitable for the long-range transfer of chemical information".

• Garris, Ciolkowski, Pastore and Wightman 1994, Journal of Neuroscience 14:6084-6093, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/14/10/6084 : a single stimulus pulse gives 0.25 micromolar extracellular dopamine in nucleus accumbens, about 60-fold above prior steady-state estimates; dopamine leaves the cleft essentially instantaneously and is removed by EXTRASYNAPTIC uptake. Average distance between striatal release sites taken as about 4 micrometres.

• Sesack, Hawrylak, Matus, Guido and Levey 1998, Journal of Neuroscience 18:2697-2708, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6793120/ : DAT in prefrontal cortex sits almost exclusively on INTERVARICOSE axon segments, not on the varicosities. Fraction of DAT-positive profiles of varicosity size: prelimbic cortex 12 percent, anterior cingulate 35 percent, dorsolateral striatum 56 percent. Tyrosine hydroxylase labelled 51 percent large profiles in prelimbic cortex, proving the varicosities exist but lack DAT. Consequence: dopamine diffuses much further in cortex.

• In prefrontal cortex and hippocampus, dopamine is cleared by NET, not DAT (Moron, Brockington, Wise, Rocha and Hope 2002, Journal of Neuroscience 22:389-395, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/22/2/389 ; Borgkvist et al. 2012).

• Noradrenaline spills over much further than dopamine: the functional range of noradrenaline diffusion in locus coeruleus was nearly FIVEFOLD greater than that of dopamine in midbrain, with substantial pooling across synchronised sites, whereas dopamine release sites operated largely independently. Courtney and Ford 2014, Journal of Neuroscience 34:7645, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/34/22/7645

• Noradrenaline diffusion range in rodent cortex is commonly quoted as up to about 100 micrometres (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9087804/ ). Treat this as an upper bound from a different measurement class than the Courtney and Ford slice work.

Basal extracellular dopamine - an order-of-magnitude disagreement

• Microdialysis with zero-net-flux: roughly 4 to 20 nanomolar. Voltammetry: roughly 95 to 220 nanomolar, with one report of 274 plus or minus 49 nanomolar. Chen 2005, Journal of Neurochemistry, https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/j.1471-4159.2004.02848.x , argues microdialysis underestimates because probe implantation destroys nearby release sites. Unresolved; would require a non-implanted absolutely calibrated in vivo measurement.

• Note the coincidence often invoked: 10 nanomolar is approximately the D2 equilibrium constant, so basal dopamine would sit at the D2 EC50. This argument depends on accepting BOTH the microdialysis figure and the nanomolar D2 affinity, both contested.

Mismatch between release sites and receptors

• Herkenham 1987, Neuroscience 23:1-38, "Mismatches between neurotransmitter and receptor localizations in brain" is the foundational statement. It is conceptual; it does not supply a single headline quantified mismatch fraction.

• Fuxe and Agnati's framework (Agnati, Fuxe, Zoli, Ozini, Toffano and Ferraguti 1986, Acta Physiologica Scandinavica; Fuxe et al. 2015, Neurochemical Research; Borroto-Escuela and Fuxe 2018, Frontiers in Synaptic Neuroscience 10:20) claims nigrostriatal and mesolimbic dopamine neurons communicate mainly by short-distance volume transmission "in the micrometre range". That is a qualitative bracket, not a measured radius.

• Modern ultrastructural restatement: even at dopamine varicosities that DO make synapse-like contacts, D1 and D2 receptors are usually NOT in the apposed postsynaptic membrane but perisynaptic (within 100 nanometres of the edge) or extrasynaptic (beyond 100 nanometres). Reported in Liu, Goel and Kaeser 2021.


A4. LOCUS COERULEUS

Human neuron number per side - the 15,000 versus 50,000 conflict

• About 15,000 per side: Ohm, Busch and Bohl 1997, Neurobiology of Aging, design-based unbiased stereology on neuromelanin-containing neurons, mean 15,731 plus or minus 3,408 standard deviation per side, range 11,737 to 25,319. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9330970/ This is the best-supported modern per-side figure.

• About 50,000: Sharma, Xu, Raghanti, Hof, Sherwood et al. 2010, Journal of Comparative Neurology, stereological counts of tyrosine-hydroxylase-immunoreactive neurons, human mean significantly higher than great apes, lesser apes and macaques (which fall at 10,000 to 30,000). https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20127761/

• CRITICAL UNRESOLVED POINT: it could not be determined from open sources whether the Sharma figure is per side or bilateral. If bilateral, 50,000 bilateral is 25,000 per side, which sits inside Ohm's upper range of 25,319 and the conflict largely dissolves. If per side, the two genuinely differ about threefold. Reading the Sharma methods section is what would settle it.

• Other primary counts: Manaye, McIntire, Mann and German 1995, Journal of Comparative Neurology 358:79-87, one side of brain: young (1 to 28 years) 21,084 plus or minus 653 standard error TH-immunostained cells; old (60 to 82) 16,502 plus or minus 921 pigmented cells; oldest (103 to 104) 9,493 plus or minus 1,236. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7560278/ German, Walker, Manaye, Smith, Woodward and North 1988, Journal of Neuroscience 8:1776-1788 (2D computer reconstruction, not stereology). Mouton, Pakkenberg, Gundersen and Price 1994, Journal of Chemical Neuroanatomy 7:185-190 (unbiased stereology, no age-related change). Pre-1990 literature spans about 8,000 to 34,000.

• Four separate reasons the numbers differ: 2D profile counting versus design-based stereology (profile counting misreads age-related cell shrinkage as loss); neuromelanin pigment versus tyrosine hydroxylase as the marker (pigment under-counts the young, TH misses neurons that survive but downregulate TH); whether the LC complex including subcoeruleus and pericoerulear cells is included, which roughly doubles the total; and unstated per-side versus bilateral reporting.

• Age-related loss is itself contested. Loss reported by Manaye et al. 1995 (about 55 percent young to centenarian, rostrally biased). No significant loss reported by Mouton et al. 1994, Ohm et al. 1997, and Kubis et al. 2000, Brain 123:366-373, https://academic.oup.com/brain/article/123/2/366/346067 . The pattern is that unbiased-stereology studies excluding incidental neuropathology find no loss.

• Disease calibration: Zarow, Lyness, Mortimer and Chui 2003, Archives of Neurology 60:337-341, about 80 percent noradrenergic LC loss in Alzheimer's disease, exceeding nucleus basalis and substantia nigra loss.

Arborisation

• Best hard number is from mouse whole-brain single-neuron reconstruction: mean total axon length per LC neuron 35.14 plus or minus 13.60 centimetres, largest 72.57 centimetres, branching sparsely at 17.8 branches per centimetre. Mouse, not human.

• Varicosities per LC neuron: NOT authoritatively established. No primary per-neuron count for a central LC neuron was found. The frequently repeated "over 25,000 varicosities" figure derives from PERIPHERAL sympathetic terminal work (Dahlstrom, 1960s) and should not be quoted as an LC number. An order-of-magnitude inference, using varicosity spacing of 5 to 10 micrometres along unmyelinated monoaminergic axons (Shepherd et al. 2002, PNAS 99:6340-6345, https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.052151299 ) times a 35 centimetre arbor, gives 35,000 to 70,000. That is arithmetic, not a published count.

• The claim that a single LC neuron innervates the entire cortex is an OVERSTATEMENT of modern evidence. Viral-genetic tracing and single-neuron reconstruction show modular, biased, non-uniform projections. Chandler, Jensen, McCall, Pickering, Schwarz and Totah 2019, Journal of Neuroscience 39:8239-8249, https://www.jneurosci.org/content/39/42/8239 ; Poe et al. 2020, Nature Reviews Neuroscience 21:644-659, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41583-020-0360-9 . Defensible statement: the LC as a NUCLEUS innervates essentially the whole neuraxis, and single LC neurons have unusually large widely collateralised arbors, but no single neuron covers the whole cortex.

Firing rates (all rodent - there are no single-unit recordings from human LC)

• Founding papers: Aston-Jones and Bloom 1981, Journal of Neuroscience 1:876-886 (sleep-wake), https://www.jneurosci.org/content/1/8/876 ; and 1:887-900 (phasic responses to non-noxious stimuli), https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7346593/

• Tonic overall range about 0.1 to 5.0 Hz. Quiet waking about 1 to 2 Hz, rising to about 3 to 5 Hz with active engagement. Slow-wave sleep about 0.5 to 1 Hz. REM/paradoxical sleep essentially 0 Hz, virtually silent. The REM silence is one of the most robust findings in the field and is not disputed.

• Phasic bursts: 2 to 3 spikes at about 10 to 20 Hz instantaneous rate on top of the tonic baseline, evoked by salient stimuli and task-relevant outcomes, followed by a post-activation inhibitory pause of a few hundred milliseconds.

• Consolidated figures and the adaptive-gain framework (low tonic plus large phasic equals focused exploitation; high tonic plus blunted phasic equals exploration; inverted-U performance relation): Aston-Jones and Cohen 2005, Annual Review of Neuroscience 28:403-450, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16022602/


A5. OTHER NEUROMODULATORY CELL COUNTS IN HUMANS

Serotonin

• Human dorsal raphe nucleus TOTAL neurons 235,000 plus or minus 15,000 (Baker, Halliday and Tork 1990, Journal of Comparative Neurology 301:147-161, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2262589/ ). Volume 71.3 plus or minus 4.5 cubic millimetres, density 3,300 plus or minus 200 per cubic millimetre.

• Human dorsal raphe SEROTONERGIC neurons 165,000 plus or minus 34,000 (Baker, Halliday, Hornung, Geffen, Cotton and Tork 1991, Neuroscience 42:757-775, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1720227/ ). So about 70 percent of dorsal raphe neurons are serotonergic. The common statement "the dorsal raphe has 235,000 serotonergic neurons" is a MISREADING of the 1990 total-neuron figure.

• Human pontine tegmentum outside dorsal raphe: about 125,000 serotonergic neurons, comprising median raphe (B8) 64,400, oral pontine reticular nucleus 30,700, supralemniscal B9 29,000 (Baker et al. 1991, Synapse 7:301-320, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2042112/ ).

• Human TOTAL - sources disagree by about twofold. Hornung 2003, Journal of Chemical Neuroanatomy 26:331-343, gives about 500,000 serotonergic neurons in the whole human brain, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/14729135/ . Summing the directly counted populations gives about 290,000 to 300,000 before adding the uncounted caudal medullary group (B1 to B3), for which no absolute human count exists. Both rest on 2D profile counting with correction factors, not modern stereology.

• Rodent comparison: rat dorsal raphe about 11,500 to 15,000 serotonergic neurons, rat whole brain about 26,000 (Jacobs and Azmitia 1992, Physiological Reviews 72:165-229). Mouse dorsal raphe about 9,000, whole brain about 26,000 to 28,000 (Okaty, Commons and Dymecki 2019, Nature Reviews Neuroscience 20:397-424, https://www.nature.com/articles/s41583-019-0151-3 ). Human exceeds rodent roughly 12 to 19 fold.

Basal forebrain cholinergic

• Nomenclature from Mesulam, Mufson, Levey and Wainer 1983, Journal of Comparative Neurology 214:170-197: Ch1 medial septum, Ch2 vertical limb diagonal band, Ch3 horizontal limb diagonal band, Ch4 nucleus basalis of Meynert / substantia innominata. Fraction actually cholinergic: Ch3 about 1 percent, Ch1 about 10 percent, Ch2 up to 70 percent, Ch4 about 90 percent.

• Standard figure: about 200,000 nucleus basalis neurons per hemisphere, about 90 percent in Ch4, traceable to Mesulam and Geula 1988, Journal of Comparative Neurology 275:216-240. HONEST CAVEAT: this is a consensus textbook number, not a design-based stereological estimate with a published coefficient of error. No modern optical-fractionator count of total human Ch4 choline-acetyltransferase-positive neurons in a decent normal cohort exists. This is the largest single gap in this section.

• Human Ch4 dimensions about 13 to 14 millimetres antero-posterior by 16 to 18 millimetres medio-lateral (Liu, Chang, Pearce and Gentleman 2015, Acta Neuropathologica 129:527-540, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4366544/ ).

• Alzheimer's loss estimates disagree by an order of magnitude and the METHOD predicts the answer more than the disease does. Arendt, Bigl, Arendt and Tennstedt 1983, Acta Neuropathologica 61:101-108: 70 percent loss in Alzheimer's, 77 percent in paralysis agitans, 47 percent in Korsakoff's, by profile counting. Vogels et al. 1990, Neurobiology of Aging 11:3-13: only 15.5 percent overall by whole-extent 3D sampling, explicitly attributing the discrepancy to earlier studies counting only magnocellular hyperchromatic neurons in part of the complex and reporting density rather than number. Lehericy et al. 1993: mean 50 percent. Zarow et al. 2003: 41.1 percent stereologically. Full published range compiled by Liu et al. 2015: 8 to 87 percent.

Dopamine

• Human substantia nigra: Pakkenberg, Moller, Gundersen, Mouritzen Dam and Pakkenberg 1991, JNNP 54:30-33, optical disector: controls averaged 550,000 pigmented and 260,000 non-pigmented neurons. In Parkinson's, pigmented reduced 66 percent. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2010756/

• Compiled healthy human SNc pigmented range 300,000 to 550,000; textbook figure 400,000 to 500,000 dopaminergic cells per side. TH-specific compiled range about 200,000 to 420,000 bilaterally. The Hardman et al. 2002, Journal of Comparative Neurology 445:238-255 figures could not be verified from open access.

• Human VTA: about 300,000 to 600,000 dopamine neurons (German and Manaye 1993 as cited downstream) versus a TH-specific compiled figure of about 60,000 to 65,000. The SNc-to-VTA ratio is NOT established: TH-specific numbers imply 3:1 to 6:1, German/Manaye-style numbers imply about 1:1. A fivefold discrepancy driven by marker, VTA boundary definition and counting method.

• Note a species inversion that undermines casual scaling: in rat (German and Manaye 1993, Journal of Comparative Neurology 331:297-309) and in macaque (2022 unbiased stereology: total 210,238 plus or minus 17,127, of which A10 110,319 and A9 87,399), VTA dopamine neurons OUTNUMBER substantia nigra dopamine neurons. In human the SNc is usually reported as larger.

• Age-related loss per decade - four peer-reviewed answers spanning zero to ten percent. Fearnley and Lees 1991, Brain 114:2283-2301: 4.7 percent per decade (dorsal tier 6.9, medial ventral 5.4, lateral ventral 2.1). Ma et al. 1999: 9.8 percent per decade for pigmented neurons, r equals minus 0.83. Ma et al. 1999 DAT study: 6.7 percent per decade for all DAT neurons, 11.2 percent for intensely stained type 1, while DAT-negative neuromelanin-only cells INCREASED with age, arguing for phenotype downregulation rather than death. Kubis et al. 2000: NO significant loss. The rate is not authoritatively established.

• Threshold at Parkinson's motor onset is contested. Traditional teaching 60 to 80 percent SNc loss. Fearnley and Lees extrapolation about 30 percent overall (about 68 to 70 percent of the vulnerable ventrolateral tier). Greffard et al. 2006, Archives of Neurology 63:584-588: modelled about 29 percent at onset, explicitly rejecting 60 to 80 percent. Kordower et al. 2013, Brain 136:2419-2431: TH-defined SNc loss 50 to 90 percent from the earliest timepoints but melanized-neuron loss only about 10 percent at 1 year. Best-established statement: putaminal terminal loss (roughly 50 to 70 percent at onset) exceeds cell-body loss.


A6. PERCENTAGE OF MONOAMINE VARICOSITIES FORMING CLASSICAL SYNAPTIC SPECIALISATIONS

Method note that governs every number. In a single thin section most junctions are invisible. Descarries' group plotted observed junction frequency against number of available serial sections and extrapolated. Seguela et al. 1988 estimated 9 to 12 serial sections are needed to see all definitive contacts; most published series average only 3.2 to 7.3 sections. Studies that did not extrapolate report much lower numbers. This is the single largest source of disagreement.

• Serotonin, rat neocortex, ORIGINAL: Descarries, Beaudet and Watkins 1975, Brain Research 100:563-588, tritiated-5-HT radioautography: junctional complexes on only 5 PERCENT of labelled boutons versus about 50 percent of unlabelled endings. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1192194/ This is the origin of the volume-transmission argument.

• Serotonin, rat neocortex, REVISED by the same laboratory: Seguela, Watkins and Descarries 1989, Journal of Comparative Neurology 289:129-142, serial-section immuno-electron microscopy: extrapolated synaptic incidence 28 percent (frontal layer VI) to 46 percent (parietal), interregional mean 38 PERCENT, extrapolation r above 0.95. Mean varicosity diameter 0.66 plus or minus 0.2 micrometres. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2808757/ The 1975 and 1989 values for the same transmitter in the same region differ about eightfold on METHOD, not biology. Anyone quoting "95 percent of cortical serotonin varicosities are asynaptic" is quoting the superseded number.

• Noradrenaline, rat neocortex: Seguela, Watkins, Geffard and Descarries 1990, Neuroscience 35:249-264: extrapolated synaptic incidence 17 PERCENT or 26 PERCENT depending on how stringently the junctional complex is defined. Randomly selected UNLABELLED terminals in the same material gave 98 percent. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2116602/ That 98 percent internal control is the strongest single argument for the school's position; the 17-versus-26 spread from criteria alone is the honest width of the uncertainty band. Descarries, Watkins and Lapierre 1977, Brain Research 133:197-222 is the earlier noradrenaline paper, commonly cited as giving a similarly low single-section frequency of order 5 percent, but this could NOT be verified from an open source.

• Dopamine, rat neostriatum: Descarries, Watkins, Garcia, Bosler and Doucet 1996, Journal of Comparative Neurology 375:167-186, verbatim: "Sixty to seventy percent were asynaptic, and 30-40 percent were endowed with a synaptic membrane differentiation." https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8915824/ All synaptic dopamine varicosities showed SYMMETRICAL junctions: 67 percent onto dendritic branches, 30 percent onto spines, 2 to 3 percent onto cell bodies. The often-quoted "20 percent" for striatal dopamine is wrong.

• Dopamine, rat CORTEX, which reverses the popular assumption: Seguela, Watkins and Descarries 1988, Brain Research 442:11-22: 93 PERCENT synaptic in anteromedial prelimbic prefrontal cortex versus 56 PERCENT in suprarhinal cortex. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3359247/ Cortical dopamine is therefore largely junctional, which cuts against a blanket volume-transmission story.

• Dopamine, monkey prefrontal cortex: 39 percent (Smiley and Goldman-Rakic 1993, Cerebral Cortex 3:223-238, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7686795/ ), with the authors explicitly treating this as a LOWER BOUND because small synapses may be undetectable. The rat 93 percent versus monkey 39 percent discrepancy has never been resolved in a single study; species and technique are fully confounded.

• Acetylcholine, rat parietal cortex: Umbriaco, Watkins, Descarries, Cozzari and Hartman 1994, Journal of Comparative Neurology 348:351-373: layer I 10 percent, layers II-III 14, layer IV 11, layer V 21, layer VI 14; overall 14 PERCENT. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7844253/

• Acetylcholine, rat hippocampus CA1 stratum radiatum: about 7 PERCENT junctional, with GABAergic varicosities in the same material essentially 100 percent (Umbriaco, Garcia, Beaulieu and Descarries 1995, Hippocampus 5:605-620). The hippocampal noradrenaline and serotonin figures from that paper could not be verified.

• Shape of the data worth noting: regional variation WITHIN one transmitter (dopamine 30 to 93 percent) exceeds variation between transmitters within one region. A uniform technical artefact should not produce 93 percent in one cortical field and 56 percent in the field next door. That is the strongest internal argument that the numbers measure something real.

• CITATION WARNING: no PubMed record for "Descarries and Mengual 2001, International Journal of Developmental Neuroscience" could be found. The verifiable Descarries reviews are Descarries and Umbriaco 1995, Seminars in the Neurosciences 7:309-318, and Descarries, Gisiger and Steriade 1997, Progress in Neurobiology 53:603-625.

Counter-evidence and the modern reframing

• Papadopoulos and Parnavelas 1991, Progress in Neurobiology 36:195-200, argued for much higher junctional incidence, objecting that dense peroxidase/DAB reaction product obscures thin symmetric membrane specialisations.

• Liu, Kershberg, Wang, Schneeberger and Kaeser 2018, Cell 172:706-718: super-resolution microscopy showed Bassoon, RIM and ELKS co-clustering in about 30 PERCENT of dopamine varicosities; conditional RIM knockout ABOLISHED dopamine release. Liu, Goel and Kaeser 2021 give about 25 percent of varicosities containing functional release sites and only about 20 percent showing detectable fluorescent-false-neurotransmitter release. Quote 20 to 30 percent as a range; the three numbers are the same phenomenon measured three ways.

• This result simultaneously vindicates and dissolves the Descarries claim. It vindicates it because most varicosities genuinely are not junctional release sites. It dissolves it because release is nonetheless millisecond-scale, calcium-microdomain-coupled and spatially organised at discrete molecular sites, the opposite of the slow diffuse undirected picture the term "volume transmission" originally implied. Nobody has shown that the junctional subset and the release-competent subset are the SAME subset.

• Ozcete, Banerjee and Kaeser 2024 state that "only about 1 percent of the dopamine varicosities have" synaptic appositions. This DISAGREES with Descarries' 30 to 40 percent by about 30-fold and is unresolved.

• Newest and most consequential datum: Lapios et al. 2025, Nature Communications 16:11467, cryo-correlative light and electron tomography of dopaminergic varicosities, which avoids chemical fixation and heavy-metal staining entirely and so removes the main artefact objection. Only 2 of 110 dopamine synaptosomes (1.8 percent) showed classical paired densities; only 37 percent contained a tethered vesicle versus consistent tethering in glutamatergic terminals; and 28 of 32 reconstructed dopamine hub varicosities directly ADHERED to a VGLUT1-positive corticostriatal presynaptic element. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12749485/ It supports both sides at once: junctions really are rare, and non-junctional varicosities really are physically targeted.

• Sarter, Parikh and Howe 2009, Nature Reviews Neuroscience 10:383-390, "Phasic acetylcholine release and the volume transmission hypothesis: time to move on": their objection is not to the electron-microscopy counts but to the inference. Amperometry shows phasic acetylcholine transients on a seconds timescale mapping to discrete cognitive operations, incompatible with a purely tonic ambient model. Low junctional incidence was over-interpreted as a claim about signalling TIMESCALE.

• What would settle the whole question: volume electron microscopy (focused-ion-beam SEM or serial block-face SEM) of large tissue blocks with genetically encoded EM-visible transmitter-specific labelling (APEX2 or miniSOG in TH-Cre, SERT-Cre, DBH-Cre, ChAT-Cre lines), reconstructing every varicosity completely at isotropic 5 to 10 nanometre resolution. This eliminates both incomplete series and extrapolation. No such census has been published for any monoamine system.


A7. NITRIC OXIDE

• No vesicular storage and NO reuptake transporter. This is not disputed. NO is synthesised on demand by nitric oxide synthase, and signal termination is controlled at the SOURCE, by calcium unbinding from NOS, plus chemical consumption and diffusion. Degradation routes: reaction with haemoglobin, with oxygen (autoxidation), and with superoxide.

• Autoxidation is NEGLIGIBLE at physiological nanomolar concentrations because the reaction is second order in NO. This is a key point often mis-stated.

• Diffusion coefficient in tissue: 848 micrometres squared per second from modelling versus 2,200 to 3,800 micrometres squared per second from in vivo voltammetry. These DISAGREE about fourfold. Complicated by the fact that NO partitions into membranes, which may invalidate the standard tortuosity correction entirely.

• Diffusion radius / sphere of influence - a major downward revision that is still contested. About 100 micrometre radius (Wood and Garthwaite 1994; Philippides et al. 2000, for LARGE sources) versus about 15 micrometres or less for synaptic-scale sources in modern Garthwaite-derived modelling, with sub-micrometre spread for a single synapse. The "approximately 30 to 50 micrometres" figure in circulation is an INTERPOLATION between these positions, not a published constant. The field revised downward by roughly an order of magnitude.

• Half-life / lifetime in tissue: published values span about 4.6 MILLISECONDS to about 15 SECONDS, more than three orders of magnitude. The Garthwaite lineage (4.6 to 60 milliseconds) is the most mechanistically grounded. The classic textbook "a few seconds" is at the top of the range and is not supported by the modern work. What would settle it: identifying the molecular identity of the brain NO-consuming sink, which remains UNKNOWN.

• Physiological NO concentration in vivo: revised DOWNWARD by two to three orders of magnitude, from about 1 micromolar to roughly 100 picomolar to 5 nanomolar. Hall and Garthwaite 2009, "What is the real physiological NO concentration in vivo?". The earlier high figure is now attributed to unreliable porphyrinic electrode measurements.

• Soluble guanylyl cyclase (sGC) EC50 for NO: published values span 0.28 nanomolar to 1.6 micromolar, a roughly 5,000-fold range, driven by sGC expression level, presence of GTP and ATP, desensitisation, and purified enzyme versus intact cells. Quote 1 to 10 nanomolar as the physiological bracket.


A8. TIMESCALES AND RECEPTOR AFFINITIES - WIRED VERSUS VOLUME

Synaptic (wired) transmission

• Synaptic cleft width about 20 to 22 nanometres, confirmed by cryo-electron microscopy of vitreous sections, not merely by chemically fixed tissue.

• Peak cleft glutamate is MILLIMOLAR. Absolute values disagree: 1.1 millimolar versus about 3 millimolar versus a 1 to 5 millimolar bracket. Decay constant disagrees too: 1.2 milliseconds versus a few hundred microseconds. Cleft clearance is at most about 1 millisecond.

• Glutamate molecules per vesicle: 2,000 versus 4,000 to 5,000 versus 8,000, a fourfold spread between inferential and direct-count methods.

• Ionotropic receptor affinities are LOW: AMPA, GABA-A and nicotinic receptors sit in the high-micromolar to millimolar range. Alpha-7 nicotinic acetylcholine EC50 is quoted as 30 versus 80 to 200 micromolar, method-dependent and inflated by desensitisation during slow oocyte perfusion. GABA-A GABA EC50 spans about 80-fold across isoforms from a single laboratory in a single expression system, 0.17 to 13.4 micromolar.

• The NMDA receptor is the outlier: glutamate EC50 0.51 to 3.3 micromolar depending on GluN2 subunit. This low-micromolar affinity is the mechanistic basis for spillover-selective NMDA recruitment, and it means the ionotropic/metabotropic affinity split is a CONTINUUM, not a clean dichotomy.

Volume transmission

• Timescale hundreds of milliseconds to seconds to minutes. D2-mediated inhibitory postsynaptic current lag about 50 milliseconds. Ambient extracellular concentrations nanomolar to picomolar.

• G-protein-coupled receptor affinities: classic figures are D2 high-affinity state Kd about 25 nanomolar and D1 low-affinity Kd about 1 micromolar (the Richfield lineage). IMPORTANT CITATION WARNING: Richfield, Penney and Young 1989 does NOT report Kd values in its abstract. It reports the PROPORTION of receptors in each affinity state: D1 high-affinity fraction 21 plus or minus 6 percent, D2 high-affinity fraction 77 plus or minus 3 percent. The widely repeated "about 10 percent D2 low-affinity, about 10 percent D1 high-affinity" attributed to this paper CONFLICTS with its own abstract. Do not cite Kd numbers to Richfield without checking the full text.

• Whether the receptor high-affinity state (D2-high, 5-HT2A-high, muscarinic-high) exists as a measurable population in vivo, or is an artefact of GTP-free membrane preparations, is NOT settled (Skinbjerg et al. 2012). Because many impressive nanomolar GPCR affinities come from GTP-free preparations, the wired-versus-volume affinity gap may be overstated.

One-paragraph synthesis

• Wired transmission: about 20 nanometres, 0.1 to 2 milliseconds, millimolar concentrations, low-affinity ionotropic receptors with EC50 of hundreds of micromolar to millimolar.

• Volume transmission: 1 to 20 micrometres for monoamines and single-digit to low-tens of micrometres for nitric oxide, hundreds of milliseconds to seconds, nanomolar to picomolar concentrations, high-affinity G-protein-coupled or guanylyl-cyclase-coupled receptors with EC50 of nanomolar or below.

• That is roughly 100 to 1,000 fold in distance, roughly 1,000 fold in time, and roughly 10,000 to 100,000 fold in concentration - and the receptor affinity difference is precisely what makes the concentration difference usable.

• Three honest caveats. The affinity dichotomy is a continuum, with NMDA and extrasynaptic GABA-A receptors sitting in between. Many nanomolar GPCR affinities are GTP-free artefacts. And the field has revised BOTH flagship volume-transmission numbers, nitric oxide's sphere of influence and lifetime, and dopamine's effective radius, substantially downward over two decades, with both revisions still contested.


TOPIC B - CIRCUMVENTRICULAR ORGANS AND THE NEUROHYPOPHYSIS


B1. THE CIRCUMVENTRICULAR ORGANS

• Sensory circumventricular organs (CVOs), which sample the blood and signal to the brain: area postrema (AP), subfornical organ (SFO), and organum vasculosum of the lamina terminalis (OVLT). The SFO and OVLT together with the median preoptic nucleus form the lamina terminalis.

• Secretory CVOs, which release substances into the blood: median eminence, neurohypophysis (posterior pituitary), and pineal gland.

• The subcommissural organ is the exception that HAS a barrier and is not permeable in the way the others are; it is often listed with the CVOs but should be flagged as different.

• All of the above except the subcommissural organ lack a conventional blood-brain barrier and have FENESTRATED capillaries, meaning the endothelium contains pores.

• Fenestra diameter: the figure in general use is 60 to 80 NANOMETRES, typically bridged by a thin diaphragm. HONEST FLAG: this value is borrowed from general fenestrated-endothelium morphology (kidney, endocrine tissue), not from a primary per-CVO measurement. Per-organ fenestra diameter distributions and fenestral densities for the AP, SFO, OVLT and median eminence have not been published to modern standards. What would settle it: cryo-electron tomography or high-pressure-frozen volume electron microscopy of all four organs in the same animals.

• Critically, the CVOs are not simply holes in the barrier. Tanycytes, specialised ependymal cells, form tight junctions at the interface between each CVO and the adjacent brain parenchyma and cerebrospinal fluid, so blood-borne molecules that enter a CVO are contained there and do not spread freely into the brain. The barrier is displaced, not abolished.


B2. AREA POSTREMA

• Function: the chemoreceptor trigger zone, in the dorsal medulla at the floor of the fourth ventricle, adjacent to the nucleus tractus solitarius. It detects circulating emetic toxins and initiates vomiting, and it is also a satiety and blood-pressure sensor.

• Receptors present, each with an antiemetic drug class mapped onto it:
◦ 5-HT3 (serotonin type 3, ionotropic) - target of ondansetron, granisetron, palonosetron; the principal route for chemotherapy-induced acute emesis via enterochromaffin-cell serotonin release.
◦ D2 (dopamine) - target of metoclopramide, domperidone, droperidol, prochlorperazine.
◦ NK1 (neurokinin 1, substance P receptor) - target of aprepitant and fosaprepitant; mediates delayed-phase chemotherapy emesis.
◦ GLP-1R (glucagon-like peptide 1 receptor) - mediates the nausea side effect of GLP-1 agonists such as exenatide, liraglutide and semaglutide.
◦ GFRAL (GDNF-family receptor alpha-like), the receptor for GDF15 (growth differentiation factor 15). Expression is restricted to the area postrema and nucleus tractus solitarius; it signals through the co-receptor RET. This is the pathway of cancer cachexia and probably of hyperemesis gravidarum. Discovered independently by FOUR groups in 2017, published in Nature Medicine, Nature and Nature within months of each other (Yang et al., Hsu et al., Emmerson et al., Mullican et al.). The four-way simultaneous discovery is itself a well-documented feature of this literature.
◦ Also mu-opioid and CB1 cannabinoid receptors, explaining opioid-induced nausea and the antiemetic effect of cannabinoids such as nabilone and dronabinol.

• Latency of emesis: SECONDS TO MINUTES after an intravenous emetogen. Precise, controlled human latency data by agent are sparse; most quoted latencies come from animal emesis models (ferret, shrew, dog) and vary by agent, dose and route. Treat any single number as agent-specific rather than a property of the area postrema.


B3. SUBFORNICAL ORGAN

• Location: on the ventral surface of the fornix at the roof of the third ventricle, at the interventricular foramen.

• SODIUM SENSING via the Nax channel (gene SCN7A), a sodium-concentration-sensitive rather than voltage-gated channel, expressed in SFO glia. Hiyama, Watanabe, Ono, Inenaga, Tamkun, Yoshida and Noda 2002 established the activation threshold: Nax activates at about 157 millimolar extracellular sodium with half-maximal activation at about 161 millimolar. Hiyama et al. 2004 showed by transgene rescue that the SFO is the PRIMARY sodium-sensing locus for body-fluid homeostasis - restoring Nax expression in the SFO alone of Nax-knockout mice restored appropriate salt-avoidance behaviour. This is a clean, well-established result.

• Angiotensin II sensing via AT1 receptors on SFO neurons, driving thirst, vasopressin release and sympathetic outflow. The SFO is the principal brain site at which circulating angiotensin II acts, because it is outside the blood-brain barrier.

• RAPID THIRST - the Zimmerman/Oka/Knight/Andermann work of 2016 to 2019. The headline finding is that thirst neurons are modulated within SECONDS, far faster than any change in blood osmolality could occur, by anticipatory signals from the mouth and gut. Drinking water suppresses thirst-neuron activity within seconds of the first swallow, before any osmotic correction; eating food ACTIVATES them, also within seconds. This overturned the classical view that thirst is a slow homeostatic feedback loop and established a feedforward, predictive architecture. Zimmerman et al. 2016, Nature (SFO thirst neurons anticipate drinking); Zimmerman et al. 2019, Nature (a gut-to-brain osmolality signal). Augustine, Oka and colleagues identified the complementary populations.

• The two SFO populations: excitatory glutamatergic neurons (SFO-GLUT, marked by nNOS/neuronal nitric oxide synthase) DRIVE thirst, and inhibitory GABAergic neurons (SFO-VGAT) SUPPRESS it. Optogenetic stimulation of SFO-GLUT neurons induces intense drinking in sated animals within seconds; stimulation of SFO-VGAT neurons suppresses drinking in water-deprived animals.


B4. OVLT AND OSMOSENSING

• The organum vasculosum of the lamina terminalis sits in the anterior wall of the third ventricle and is the principal central OSMORECEPTOR, meaning it detects the concentration of dissolved particles in plasma. Osmolality is measured in milliosmoles per kilogram (mOsm/kg).

• Vasopressin (AVP) osmotic threshold in humans: 280 to 285 mOsm/kg. Below this, plasma AVP is essentially undetectable; above it, AVP rises steeply and nearly linearly. Robertson 1976 and Thompson/Baylis 1986 are the primary human sources; a 2018 pooled review confirms the range.

• Slope: about 0.4 picograms per millilitre of AVP per mOsm/kg. That is a steep gain, which is why osmolality is regulated so tightly.

• SENSITIVITY: a change of roughly 1 PERCENT in plasma osmolality, that is about 2 to 3 mOsm/kg, is sufficient to produce a detectable change in AVP secretion. This is the number usually quoted for the exquisite sensitivity of the system, and it is well supported.

• The THIRST threshold is HIGHER than the AVP threshold, at roughly 290 to 295 mOsm/kg. The physiological logic is that the body first conserves water by concentrating urine (a cheap, involuntary response) and only then generates the conscious drive to seek water (an expensive, behavioural response). Normal plasma osmolality sits at about 280 to 295 mOsm/kg, so the AVP system is essentially always partly engaged while thirst is not.

• The MOLECULAR identity of the mammalian central osmosensor is NOT settled. A truncated splice variant of TRPV1 (delta-N-TRPV1) is the leading candidate but independent multi-laboratory replication with conditional OVLT-neuron-specific deletion has not been done, and WNK1 and TMEM63B are competing candidates. This is a genuine open question.


B5. VASOPRESSIN AND OXYTOCIN

• Both are nine-amino-acid peptides made in MAGNOCELLULAR ("large-celled") neurons of the supraoptic nucleus (SON) and paraventricular nucleus (PVN) of the hypothalamus. Their axons run down the infundibular stalk and terminate on capillaries in the posterior pituitary (neurohypophysis), where the peptides are released directly into the systemic circulation. The posterior pituitary is therefore not a gland in the usual sense but a bundle of axon terminals.

• Human magnocellular neuron number: NOT well established. Statements that "the human supraoptic nucleus contains about 3,000 neurons" are almost certainly a RAT figure misattributed to human, and are inconsistent with the also-quoted claim of over 100,000 magnocellular neurons in the human hypothalamus. No modern design-based stereological count of human SON and PVN magnocellular neurons exists post-1995. This is a clear gap.

• Basal plasma concentrations: both hormones circulate in the low PICOMOLAR range, typically about 1 to 5 picomolar (roughly 1 to 5 picograms per millilitre) under basal euhydrated conditions, rising several-fold with osmotic or haemodynamic stimulation. Oxytocin rises far higher during labour and suckling.

• PLASMA HALF-LIFE - THE LITERATURE GENUINELY DISAGREES, AND WIDELY. Published values span about 3 to 78 MINUTES for vasopressin and about 2 to 17 MINUTES for oxytocin. The textbook figures are "about 15 to 20 minutes" for AVP and "about 3 to 5 minutes" for oxytocin, but neither is defensible as a single number. The spread is driven by: radiolabelled tracer versus native hormone; whether plasma was extracted before assay; whether the reported value is the distribution (alpha) phase or the elimination (beta) phase; and pregnancy status, since placental oxytocinase dramatically shortens oxytocin half-life in pregnancy. What would settle it: a liquid-chromatography tandem-mass-spectrometry bolus-and-decay study of both hormones in the same healthy fasted subjects with full multi-compartment modelling. This has not been done.

• Stored pituitary content: human posterior pituitary oxytocin content is about 21 to 28 MICROGRAMS, which is a measured figure. The corresponding AVP content of roughly 15 to 30 micrograms is INFERRED by analogy with vesicle numbers, not directly measured. Absolute terminal counts in the human neurohypophysis are unknown.


B6. MEDIAN EMINENCE AND THE HYPOPHYSIAL PORTAL SYSTEM

• Anatomy: hypothalamic parvocellular ("small-celled") neurons release releasing and inhibiting hormones into a primary capillary plexus in the median eminence. Portal veins carry this blood DOWN the pituitary stalk to a secondary plexus in the anterior pituitary. Because the blood goes hypothalamus-to-pituitary directly without first being diluted in the systemic circulation, concentrations in portal blood are far higher than anywhere else in the body. This is the entire point of the design.

• Dopamine as prolactin-inhibiting factor is the ONE hormone with a true SAME-ANIMAL portal-versus-arterial measurement: rat portal blood dopamine 1.3 to 6 nanograms per millilitre versus 0.4 to 0.8 nanograms per millilitre arterial in the same animals. Ben-Jonathan et al. 1977; Gibbs and Neill 1978. That is a fold difference of roughly 3 to 10 measured directly.

• Corticotropin-releasing factor (CRF/CRH), rat portal blood: 430 picograms per millilitre basal, rising to 839 picograms per millilitre after haemorrhage. Plotsky and Vale 1984.

• Thyrotropin-releasing hormone (TRH), rat portal blood: about 1.0 to 2.8 nanograms per millilitre. Ching and Utiger 1983.

• PORTAL-TO-PERIPHERAL FOLD DIFFERENCES for CRH, TRH, GHRH and somatostatin are CROSS-STUDY INFERENCES only, not same-animal measurements. Figures of "10-fold to 100-fold" or higher circulate widely but do not rest on paired measurements. Only dopamine has a genuine same-animal ratio. This is an important honesty point: the qualitative claim that portal concentrations vastly exceed peripheral is certain, but a specific fold number for any given releasing hormone other than dopamine is not.

• GnRH pulses: Clarke and Cummins 1982 is the landmark demonstration that each pulse of gonadotropin-releasing hormone in hypophysial portal blood is followed by a pulse of luteinising hormone in peripheral blood, in ovariectomised ewes. CITATION WARNING: the paper describes "exact synchrony" qualitatively; a specific printed concordance percentage attributed to it could NOT be verified. Do not quote a percentage.

• Sheep hypophysial portal plasma flow is about 0.5 millilitres per minute, but this is DERIVED by dividing secretion rate by concentration, and two derivations give 0.64 and 0.44, which disagree.

• The commonly repeated "70 percent long portal vessels, 30 percent short" split is untraceable lore; competing versions give 80/20 and 90/10, and none is traceable to a primary measurement.


B7. BLOOD-BRAIN BARRIER

Molecular composition of the tight junction

• Claudin-5 is the dominant and functionally decisive claudin. Nitta et al. 2003 showed claudin-5 knockout mice have a size-selective leak: molecules UNDER 800 DALTONS cross freely, while larger molecules remain excluded. This is one of the cleanest results in barrier biology.

• Occludin (MARVEL-domain transmembrane protein), zonula occludens proteins ZO-1, ZO-2 and ZO-3 (PDZ-domain cytoplasmic scaffolds linking claudins and occludin to the actin cytoskeleton), JAM-A (junctional adhesion molecule A, gene F11R), and cingulin (couples the junction to RhoA signalling; least well characterised at the BBB specifically).

• Often omitted but present: tricellulin (MARVELD2), MarvelD3, and LSR/angulin-1 at tricellular contacts. Berndt et al. 2019 is the comprehensive source.

• Castro Dias et al. 2019 published two negative results: claudin-12 and claudin-3 are NOT required for barrier integrity, contradicting earlier assumptions.

Transendothelial electrical resistance (TEER), in ohm times square centimetre - an inverse measure of paracellular leakiness

• Crone and Olesen 1982, Brain Research 241:49-55, frog pial microvessels: about 1,870. Secondary sources cite this variously as 1,840 and as "1,500 to 2,000"; that spread is CITATION DRIFT, not two measurements. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6980688/

• Butt, Jones and Abbott 1990, Journal of Physiology 429:47-62, rat developmental series: about 310 at embryonic day 17 to 20, about 1,128 from 21 days gestation, about 1,462 in 28 to 33 day old rats. This is the classic demonstration that the high-resistance barrier is acquired PERINATALLY. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2277354/

• Butt and Jones 1992: rat arterial microvessels about 2,000, venous about 800, overall mean about 1,500.

• Outlier: Smith and Rapoport 1986 report about 8,000 for non-pial cerebral microvessels. FLAG AS UNREPRODUCED, far above every other in vivo estimate.

• For contrast, peripheral non-brain capillary endothelium is roughly 3 to 33 ohm times square centimetre, that is two to three orders of magnitude leakier.

• In vitro cultured monolayers give 92, 134, 250 and 386 in various reported arrangements, that is 10 to 100 times LEAKIER than in vivo. Reasons: loss of the neurovascular unit (astrocyte end-feet and pericytes are needed to induce the barrier phenotype), absence of shear stress in static culture, dedifferentiation with loss of claudin-5, and measurement artefacts in which a small number of leaky shunt cells dominate a parallel-resistance measurement. Micromachines 2021;12:685, https://doi.org/10.3390/mi12060685

Total capillary length in kilometres - EVERY VARIANT IS AN EXTRAPOLATION

• About 600 kilometres, the most-cited modern figure. Wong, Ye, Levy, Rothstein, Bergles and Searson 2013, Frontiers in Neuroengineering 6:7, state a microvessel density of about 500 metres per cubic centimetre "corresponding to a total microvessel length of about 600 km in the adult human brain", citing Zlokovic 2005. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3757302/ Note the arithmetic: 500 metres per cubic centimetre times about 1,200 cubic centimetres equals about 600 kilometres. The number is a MULTIPLICATION, not a measurement.

• About 400 miles, that is about 640 to 650 kilometres. Begley and Brightman 2003, Progress in Drug Research 61:39-78, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/14674608/ . The 600-versus-650 discrepancy is almost certainly the same underlying estimate rounded in two unit systems, not two independent results.

• About 400 kilometres circulates widely but is NOT TRACEABLE to any primary morphometric study. Treat as textbook lore.

• PROVENANCE VERDICT: rodent or small human-biopsy stereology gives a length density per unit volume; that density is multiplied by a nominal 1.3 to 1.4 kilogram human brain; the product is rounded. NO study has traced the total capillary length of an intact human brain. What would settle it: whole-hemisphere light-sheet imaging of a cleared human hemisphere with full vascular segmentation.

Total capillary surface area in square metres - the figures disagree, but for a traceable reason

• Stated RANGE 15 to 25 square metres (Wong et al. 2013, citing Crone 1963 and Gross et al. 1986). Every single-number figure below is a point inside this range.

• About 20 square metres is the most-quoted value (Pardridge, numerous papers; Begley and Brightman 2003 pairs "400 miles" with "about 20 square metres").

• About 12 square metres circulates in reviews; no clean primary attribution; likely reflects a smaller assumed capillary diameter or exclusion of non-capillary microvessels.

• 21 square metres is UNTRACEABLE and reads as a re-rounding.

• Per-gram figures: about 100 square centimetres per gram (Pardridge et al. 1990); 100 to 200 square centimetres per gram (Wong et al. 2013); up to about 240 square centimetres per gram for GREY MATTER specifically, reflecting its roughly twofold higher capillary density.

• CONSISTENCY CHECK: 100 to 200 square centimetres per gram times about 1,400 grams gives 14 to 28 square metres, which BRACKETS all of the 12, 15, 20, 21 and 25 figures. The entire spread is driven by which per-gram density and which brain mass an author chose, not by conflicting measurements. That is a satisfying resolution of an apparent disagreement.

• Cerebral capillaries occupy about 1 percent of brain tissue volume; the endothelial cells themselves about 0.1 percent (Pardridge et al. 1990). Pardridge's own headline figures are "over 100 billion capillaries" and "about 20 square metres".

Intercapillary distance and capillary dimensions

• Mean intercapillary distance in human brain about 40 MICROMETRES. Primary morphometric source is Duvernoy, Delon and Vannson 1981/1983, using cortical vascular casts and India-ink preparations.

• Measured DISTRIBUTION rather than a single value: 24 to 42 micrometres in human secondary cortex, versus 16 to 46 micrometres in a matched computational model. Safaeian and David 2013, Annals of Biomedical Engineering, using the Lauwers/Cassot human dataset. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3878164/ Reviews commonly quote a 20 to 60 micrometre range.

• ATTRIBUTION CORRECTION: Pawlik and Schlageter are commonly credited for intercapillary spacing, but that work is RODENT. The 40 micrometre HUMAN figure is properly Duvernoy's.

• Human cortical capillary diameter 6.66 plus or minus 1.70 micrometres. Lauwers, Cassot, Lauwers-Cances, Puwanarajah and Duvernoy 2008, NeuroImage 39:936-948, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17997329/ . This IS a measurement, from confocal microscopy of India-ink-injected human cortex, not an extrapolation, and is the single most reliable number in this section. Wong et al. quote 7 to 10 micrometres, and note about 7 micrometres in human versus about 4 micrometres in RAT. The commonly stated "4 to 8 micrometre lumen" for brain capillaries CONFLATES the rodent and human ends.

• Mean capillary segment length in human cortex 52.95 plus or minus 49.75 micrometres (note the enormous standard deviation, nearly equal to the mean). Capillary density 7,473 to 14,069 vessels per cubic millimetre; vascular volume fraction 2.43 to 3.02 percent; surface-to-volume ratio 10.19 to 12.85 square millimetres per cubic millimetre. That last figure equals about 120 square centimetres per cubic centimetre, which is internally consistent with the 100 to 200 square centimetres per gram whole-brain figures, since brain density is close to 1 gram per cubic centimetre. This internal consistency is a point in favour of the per-gram numbers.

• White matter is about 49 PERCENT LESS vascularised than cortex, consistently, in controls and in all dementia groups. Hase et al. 2019, Acta Neuropathologica Communications 7:16, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6366070/

Distance from capillary to neuron

• A neuron cell body typically sits 10 to 20 MICROMETRES from the nearest capillary.

• The geometric argument: if mean intercapillary distance is about 40 micrometres, no point in tissue can be more than about HALF that from a capillary, so about 20 micrometres; with the measured 24 to 42 micrometre spacing the half-distance is about 12 to 21 micrometres.

• The stronger claim "no neuron is more than about 25 micrometres from a capillary" has no traceable primary measurement. Treat as an upper-bound rule of thumb.

• Diffusion time: with a small-molecule diffusion coefficient of about 1,000 micrometres squared per second, a molecule crosses 20 micrometres in roughly 0.2 seconds and 10 micrometres in roughly 0.05 seconds, since diffusion time scales with the SQUARE of distance. The rate-limiting step is crossing the barrier, not diffusing through tissue; glucose requires the GLUT-1 transporter (gene SLC2A1) to cross the endothelium, after which interstitial equilibration is effectively instantaneous on physiological timescales.

• Untraceable lore to avoid: per-square-millimetre grey-versus-white capillary counts of "300 to 800" and "100 to 200". Per-square-millimetre counts are section-plane-dependent and less reliable than the per-cubic-millimetre length densities above.


CROSS-CUTTING NOTES ON RELIABILITY

Solid, backed by primary measurement
• alpha about 0.2 and lambda about 1.6, with D-star equals D over lambda squared (Nicholson and Phillips 1981; Sykova and Nicholson 2008).
• Synaptic cleft width 20 to 22 nanometres, confirmed by cryo-EM of vitreous sections.
• Peak cleft glutamate is millimolar; cleft clearance at most about 1 millisecond.
• Striatal DAT Km about 0.2 micromolar; prefrontal cortex dopamine uptake about 50-fold slower than striatum, with NET rather than DAT as the dominant clearance route and DAT located on intervaricose segments.
• 20 to 30 percent of striatal dopamine varicosities are release-competent; one release site per about 25 cubic micrometres.
• Nitric oxide has no vesicular storage and no reuptake transporter; physiological concentration was revised downward by two to three orders of magnitude.
• Claudin-5 knockout leak cutoff under 800 daltons (Nitta et al. 2003).
• In vivo brain endothelial TEER 1,462 to 1,870 ohm times square centimetre.
• Human cortical capillary diameter 6.66 micrometres, segment length 53 micrometres, intercapillary distance 24 to 42 micrometres (Lauwers et al. 2008).
• Nax activation threshold 157 millimolar with half-maximal 161 millimolar (Hiyama et al. 2002), and the SFO as the primary sodium-sensing locus by transgene rescue (Hiyama et al. 2004).
• AVP osmotic threshold 280 to 285 mOsm/kg, slope about 0.4 picograms per millilitre per mOsm/kg, roughly 1 percent sensitivity.
• Rat portal CRF 430 rising to 839 picograms per millilitre (Plotsky and Vale 1984); rat portal dopamine 1.3 to 6 versus 0.4 to 0.8 nanograms per millilitre arterial in the SAME animals.
• Human posterior pituitary oxytocin content about 21 to 28 micrograms.

Genuinely unresolved, with both sides peer-reviewed
• Human LC neurons per side: about 15,000 (Ohm 1997) versus about 50,000 (Sharma 2010), pending clarification of per-side versus bilateral reporting.
• Whether LC and SNc neurons are lost with normal ageing at all (Manaye/Ma versus Mouton/Ohm/Kubis), and if so at 0 to 10 percent per decade.
• Human total serotonergic neuron number: about 500,000 (Hornung 2003) versus about 290,000 to 300,000 by summation.
• Human SNc-to-VTA dopamine neuron ratio, spanning 1:1 to 6:1 depending on marker.
• Dopamine sphere of influence: 7 to 8 micrometres for D2 (Cragg and Rice) versus sub-micrometre per quantum with micrometre reach only from pooling (Kaeser).
• Fraction of striatal dopamine varicosities making synaptic appositions: 30 to 40 percent (Descarries 1996) versus about 1 percent (Ozcete 2024) versus 1.8 percent by cryo-tomography (Lapios 2025).
• Cortical dopamine synaptic fraction: 39, 56 and 93 percent in different regions and species.
• Nitric oxide half-life in tissue: about 4.6 milliseconds to about 15 seconds, over three orders of magnitude.
• Nitric oxide sphere of influence: about 100 micrometres versus about 15 micrometres or less.
• sGC EC50 for nitric oxide: 0.28 nanomolar to 1.6 micromolar, about 5,000-fold.
• Basal striatal dopamine: tens of nanomolar by microdialysis versus hundreds of nanomolar by voltammetry.
• Plasma half-life of vasopressin (about 3 to 78 minutes) and oxytocin (about 2 to 17 minutes).
• Whether receptor high-affinity states exist in vivo or are GTP-free preparation artefacts.
• The molecular identity of the mammalian central osmosensor.

Untraceable lore - do not repeat without a citation
• "400 kilometres" and "21 square metres" for brain capillaries.
• "No neuron is more than 25 micrometres from a capillary."
• Per-square-millimetre grey-versus-white capillary counts of 300 to 800 and 100 to 200.
• The 8,000 ohm times square centimetre TEER outlier.
• The 70/30 long-versus-short portal vessel split.
• "The human supraoptic nucleus contains about 3,000 neurons" - almost certainly a rat figure misattributed to human.
• "Over 25,000 varicosities per LC neuron" - a peripheral sympathetic figure, not central LC.
• A specific portal-to-peripheral fold difference for any releasing hormone other than dopamine.
• A concordance percentage attributed to Clarke and Cummins 1982.
• Kd values attributed to Richfield, Penney and Young 1989, which reports affinity-state PROPORTIONS, not Kd.

Citation corrections worth carrying forward
• The 9.8 percent per decade nigral loss figure is Ma et al. 1999, NOT Fearnley and Lees 1991 (whose figure is 4.7 percent per decade).
• The 1988 DAT kinetics paper is Wightman, Amatore, Engstrom et al. 1988, Neuroscience 25:513-523, not "Near et al. 1988".
• "Liu and Kaeser 2021" is a Nature Reviews Neuroscience REVIEW (Liu, Goel and Kaeser); the primary sparse-release-site research is Liu, Kershberg, Wang, Schneeberger and Kaeser 2018, Cell 172:706-718. These are frequently conflated.
• Some web summaries state D-star equals D over lambda. That is WRONG; the correct relation is D over lambda SQUARED.
• No PubMed record exists for "Descarries and Mengual 2001, International Journal of Developmental Neuroscience". Verify before citing.
• "235,000 serotonergic neurons in the human dorsal raphe" is a misreading; 235,000 is the TOTAL neuron count, 165,000 is the serotonergic count.

Highest-value experiments that would settle the biggest gaps
• Volume electron microscopy of large tissue blocks with genetically encoded EM-visible transmitter-specific labelling, reconstructing every varicosity completely at isotropic 5 to 10 nanometre resolution - would end the junctional-percentage dispute for all monoamines.
• Whole-hemisphere cleared-tissue light-sheet vascular segmentation of a human brain - would replace every extrapolated kilometre and square-metre figure with a measurement.
• Cryo-electron tomography of area postrema, subfornical organ, OVLT and median eminence in the same animals - would replace the borrowed 60 to 80 nanometre fenestra figure with real per-CVO numbers.
• Identifying the molecular identity of the brain nitric-oxide-consuming sink - would settle the NO half-life range.
• A modern design-based stereological count of human supraoptic and paraventricular magnocellular neurons - none exists post-1995.
• An LC-MS/MS bolus-and-decay study of AVP and oxytocin in the same healthy fasted humans with full multi-compartment modelling - would end the half-life disagreement.
• Direct measurement of endogenous D2 occupancy versus measured distance from an identified single dopamine release site - would settle the Cragg/Rice versus Kaeser sphere-of-influence dispute.
• Reading the methods section of Sharma et al. 2010 to determine whether its human LC figure is per side or bilateral - would likely dissolve the 15,000 versus 50,000 conflict outright.</result>

The hand-off is written and pushed, and one more document finished while I was writing it.

THE HAND-OFF — docs/neuroscience/LIBRARY_REPAIR_HAND_OFF.txt

It carries the state of every file, the banner convention with each file's removal test, what's still owed in priority order, and the traps the literature searches found.

ONE NAMING DECISION WORTH FLAGGING

I deliberately did not call it a Context Hand-Off. The corpus update run's cadence is evaluated by `find docs -name '*Context_Hand-Off*.txt' | wc -l` — a file in that directory carrying the string would have been counted and would have silently shifted a scheduled act by one, permanently. In a repository where a filename glob drives a scheduled act, a filename isn't a label; it's executable state. The file says so on its own face.

WHAT LANDED WHILE I WAS WRITING

THE LAYER 10 ADDENDUM IS COMPLETE — 1,730 lines, systems 45 to 48, chain audit written.

The agent that finished it removed the banner itself, having checked the banner's own stated condition. That is the first time the convention paid for itself: one session set a machine-checkable removal test, a concurrent session met it and cleared the flag without needing to ask anyone what the file's status was. It's the argument for never writing a removal test that's a matter of opinion, and I've recorded it in the hand-off as such.

Its chain audit also does the right thing under pressure — it names five honest gaps in the corpus's own parts roster rather than inventing entries to fill them, the real one being that no Layer 6 entry exists for the meningeal lymphatic endothelial cell.

WHERE THINGS NOW STAND

Finished and citable: the Interface Exception Catalogue, the Ontogenetic Axis, the Layer 10 addendum, Layer 12, the evidence fold-in.

Bannered: the Layers 1 to 3 pair (17 of ~22 chapters), the development-and-disease companion (front matter only).

Not started: the Layer 9 time constants companion, the cranial nerve roster, the transcriptomic cross-walk.

The hand-off's closing advice is the thing I'd most want carried forward: the temptation in this work is to finish documents, and it should be resisted at exactly one point — when the next paragraph would need a number nobody has measured, the correct output is a refusal with its reason, not a plausible figure. The Interface Exception Catalogue's two half-specified channels are the worked example, and they're the part of it a later session can actually trust.
